Orientation — Scope, Versions and Tooling

Scope and intended audienceThe version matrix and the 3.6 preview boundaryToolchain: Node, Vite, create-vue, Volar, DevTools, VitestHow a Vue project is laid out on diskReading a single-file component end to endConventions and markers used in this guide

This chapter fixes the boundaries of the guide: which Vue releases are described, which are production-safe, which surrounding tools are assumed, how a project is arranged on disk, and how to read a single-file component. Every later chapter depends on the terminology established here, and cross-references rather than restatements wherever the material reappears.

Scope and intended audience

The subject is the Vue 3.5 core framework: the single-file component format, the template compiler, the Composition API and the reactivity runtime. It is reference documentation. Claims are declarative, version-pinned and accompanied by the conditions under which they stop being true.

The implied reader already writes application code in a language with a module system and a type checker, and can read a function signature without being introduced to it. What the reader is presumed not to have is an accurate mental model of reactivity: the failure modes in this framework are almost never syntax errors, they are silent loss of tracking, and they are only preventable if the dependency-collection rules are stated precisely.

In scopeOut of scope for this part of the guide
Single-file component format and block orderingVue Router and the History API
Template compiler: directives, expressions, slotsPinia and any global store library
Reactivity: r, reac, comp, waForm validation libraries and HTTP data layers
Composition API: composables, lifecycle, provide/injectBundle-size analysis, rendering performance, profiling
Component contract: props, emits, expose, slotsComponent and end-to-end test authoring
Server-side rendering and the Nuxt 4.x meta-frameworkAuthentication, authorisation and deployment pipelines

The right-hand column is not a dismissal. Each of those topics receives its own chapter in the later part of the guide, and the chapters here point forward instead of duplicating the material.

# Confirm the resolved version before writing any code against it.
npm ls vue

# The authoritative answer is the package the module system resolves,
# which is not necessarily the one in package.json if a caret range was used.
node -p "require('vue/package.json').version"

# Pin it, then re-verify. An exact version is the precondition for every
# version-pinned claim in this guide being true of the machine you are on.
npm install vue@3.5.43 --save-exact
node -p "require('vue/package.json').version"

# Development build: runtime warnings, prop validation and emits checks.
# Production build: warnings stripped. A warning you only see in dev is a
# warning that will not exist when the defect reaches a user.
npm run build -- --mode production
node -e "const v=require('vue'); console.log(Object.keys(v).length + ' exports')"

The last two lines distinguish a version that satisfies the declared range from the version actually installed, and a development build from a production build. Most discrepancies reported as "it works for me" in this framework are one of those two differences.

Applies to. The version target for the whole guide is Vue 3.5.43, published 17 September 2026 as a patch on the 3.5 line. Every behavioural statement is written against that release unless the surrounding text carries a different version marker. Patch releases inside the 3.5 line do not introduce or remove public API; feature additions land in 3.6 and later.

Non-goal. Vue 2 reached end of life on 31 December 2023 and receives no security or bug fixes. Vue 2 idioms are not taught anywhere in this guide. They appear only where a Vue 3 construct is easier to understand by contrast, and every such contrast is labelled as a contrast.

The version matrix and the 3.6 preview boundary

Vue ships on a minor-version cadence in which features arrive in 3.x and removals are deferred to the next major. Pinning a patch version and tracking the minor line are therefore separate decisions, and the pre-release line is a third category that must never be deployed to production.

ComponentPinned versionStatusNotes
v3.5.43Stable, currentPatch on the 3.5 line. The target of this guide.
v pre-release3.6.0-rc.9Release candidateNot for production. Breaking changes may still land before 3.6.0 final.
Vue 2.x2.7.16End of lifeUnsupported since 31 December 2023. No patches of any kind.
Vite8.x (Rolldown)StableNative-Rust bundler. The default dev server and build tool for new projects.
TypeScript5.xStableConsumed through Volar for .vue.vue language services.
Vue Router4.xStableCovered in a later chapter.
Pinia3.xStableCovered in a later chapter.
Vitest3.xStableUnit and component testing. Covered in a later chapter.
PlaywrightcurrentStableBrowser-level and end-to-end coverage.
Nuxt4.xStableMeta-framework; SSR, file-based routing, server routes.
Vue CLI5.xMaintenance onlyNot recommended for new projects. Use create-vuecreate-vue.

The matrix is the authoritative list for this guide. If a statement elsewhere conflicts with it, the matrix wins and the statement is a defect.

Pre-releases. The Vue project’s own documentation states that pre-release builds must not be used in production and may contain breaking changes. A release candidate is a signal that the feature set is settling, not that the feature set is fixed. Pin to 3.5.433.5.43 and treat 3.6.0-rc.93.6.0-rc.9 as something to evaluate in a branch.

Preview only: what is coming in the 3.6 line

The following is a preview of direction, not a specification. Nothing in this subsection may be used in production code, and no performance figures are stated here because none of them can be attributed to a stable release.

Preview areaDescriptionProduction readiness
Vapor ModeAn alternative compiler strategy that emits direct DOM operations instead of building a virtual-DOM tree, removing the reconciliation step for eligible components.Not available on the 3.5 line. Opt-in and experimental in 3.6 release candidates.
Alien signalsA rewrite of the reactivity internals intended to make the signal primitives composable with signal implementations from other frameworks.Not available on the 3.5 line. Experimental in 3.6 release candidates.

Both items appear in 3.6.0-rc.93.6.0-rc.9 as opt-in experiment flags. They are recorded here so that a reader encountering them in a changelog or a branch can place them, not so that they can be adopted.

// package.json - the pinned set this guide describes. Exact versions,
// no carets: a caret on `vue` silently admits the next minor, which may be
// a release candidate.
{
  "name": "billing-ui",
  "private": true,
  "type": "module",
  "engines": { "node": ">=20.19.0" },
  "dependencies": {
    "vue": "3.5.43",
    "vue-router": "4.5.0",
    "pinia": "3.0.3"
  },
  "devDependencies": {
    "vite": "8.0.2",
    "@vitejs/plugin-vue": "6.0.1",
    "typescript": "5.9.2",
    "vue-tsc": "3.0.6",
    "vitest": "3.2.4",
    "@vue/test-utils": "2.4.6"
  },
  "scripts": {
    "dev": "vite",
    "build": "vue-tsc --noEmit && vite build",
    "typecheck": "vue-tsc --noEmit",
    "test:unit": "vitest"
  }
}

// A branch evaluating the 3.6 release candidate. Never merged to main,
// never deployed, never referenced by a version-pinned statement here.
npm install vue@3.6.0-rc.9 --save-exact --no-save

The bu script runs vue before vite buildvite build, so a template type error fails the build rather than shipping. Omitting it produces a bundle that compiled successfully despite containing code the type checker rejected.

Applies to. The subsections above describe 3.6.0-rc.93.6.0-rc.9. The rest of the guide describes 3.5.433.5.43. Code in chapters 1 through 4 runs unmodified on the stable line; adopting either preview area would change the component authoring rules that chapter 2 documents.

Toolchain: Node, Vite, create-vue, Volar, DevTools, Vitest

The framework is distributed as a library; almost nothing about it can be used productively without four surrounding tools. This section states which tool does what and where the boundaries between them lie, because misattributing a behaviour to the wrong layer is a common source of wasted debugging.

# Scaffolding is owned by create-vue, which drives Vite. Vue CLI is not used.
npm create vue@latest my-app

# Non-interactive form, with every feature flag stated explicitly.
npm create vue@latest my-app -- --ts --router --pinia --vitest

# The generated project pins an engine range. Node 20.19+ or 22.12+ is required.
cd my-app
npm install
npm run dev          # Vite dev server, HMR over an ES module graph
npm run build        # production bundle into dist/
npm run test:unit    # Vitest in watch mode

creat is a scaffolding tool, not a runtime dependency. It is a one-shot generator: the project it produces has no ongoing coupling to it, and every generated file is ordinary source that the team may edit freely.

ToolResponsibilityNot its responsibility
Vite 8Dev server, on-demand ESM transform, HMR, production bundlingCompiling .vue.vue semantics; that is the Vue plugin
@vitejs/plugin-vue@vitejs/plugin-vueSFC block parsing, template compilation, scoped-CSS rewritingModule resolution, proxying, bundling
VolarTypeScript language services inside .vue.vue: types, completion, diagnosticsEmitting types at build time; that is vue
Vue DevToolsComponent tree inspection, reactivity inspection, pin mutation sources, performance timelineDebugging build configuration
Vitest 3Test runner and component mounting with a jsdom or browser environmentBrowser coverage; that is Playwright
vueType checking of .vue.vue files in CIEmitting JavaScript

The division that matters in practice: a template that renders incorrectly is a compiler or reactivity problem, a module that fails to resolve is a Vite problem, and a red squiggle inside a .vue.vue template is a Volar configuration problem. These are three different investigations.

// .vscode/settings.json - Volar is the whole of the required editor config.
{
  "vue.tsdk": "node_modules/vue-language-server",
  "vue.enableGlobalComposite": true,
  "vue.inlayHints.defaults": { "preferredFormat": "vue" },
  "editor.formatOnSave": true,
  "editor.defaultFormatter": "esbenp.prettier-vscode"
}

// DevTools: the reactivity inspector is the primary debugging surface for
// chapters 3 and 4. Pin it so its inspection state survives a reload.
// (Vue DevTools is an extension; it is not a project dependency.)
npm exec -- vue-devtools

// The same fact asserted from inside a test, where a human is not looking.
// A version guard in CI is worth more than a note in a README.
// vitest.config.ts
import { defineConfig } from 'vitest/config'
import vue from '@vitejs/plugin-vue'

export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [vue()],
  test: {
    environment: 'jsdom',
    include: ['src/**/*.spec.ts'],
    // Playwright owns the browser-level tier; Vitest owns the rest.
    exclude: ['e2e/**']
  }
})

Vue DevTools is a browser extension and is deliberately absent from dependenciesdependencies. Playwright is likewise a development dependency used for the end-to-end tier only; both are covered in the testing chapter.

Install Vue DevTools and learn the reactivity inspector before writing the first watchwatch. Watching a value change from unde to its real value in the inspector is the fastest way to internalise how dependency collection actually works, and chapter 3 depends on that model.

How a Vue project is laid out on disk

A generated project is small and mostly unremarkable. Two conventions matter more than the rest: components are grouped by feature rather than by type, and the import of a .vue.vue file has no extension and resolves through Vite, not through Node.

my-app/
+-- index.html                 Vite entry. Only a mount point and a module script.
+-- package.json
+-- vite.config.ts
+-- tsconfig.json
+-- env.d.ts                  /// <reference types="vite/client" /> and shims
+-- src/
    +-- main.ts              createApp(App).mount('#app')
    `-- App.vue              Root component: owns the shell, not the features

A component is imported by its path relative to the source root with no .vue.vue extension: import UserCard from './components/UserCard.vue’import UserCard from './components/UserCard.vue' works, import UserCard from './components/UserCard’import UserCard from './components/UserCard' does not resolve under the default Vite configuration.

ConventionRuleFailure mode when broken
Feature groupingFiles that change together live in one directoryCross-feature import churn; merge conflicts on shared files
Extensionless in sourceAlways include .vue.vue in the import specifierResolution error at dev-server start
Type checkingRun vue in CI, not only tTemplate expression errors ship undetected
AliasesUse the generated @@ alias for the source rootDeep relative paths that break when a file moves

Grouping by feature rather than by type is the difference between a component and its composables, styles and tests being found in one place or spread across four directories.

Reading a single-file component end to end

The single-file component is the primary authoring unit. Its three blocks are compiled together, and understanding exactly how they are joined is what makes the rest of this guide predictable: the template can only see what the script exposes, and the script can only affect the template through bindings.

<!-- src/components/OrderSummary.vue -->
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref, computed } from 'vue'

// Top-level bindings in <script setup> are exposed to the template.
// They are NOT on the component instance: there is no `this`.
const quantity = ref(1)
const unitPrice = ref(19.9)

// `refs` unwraps automatically in templates, so `total` is read as {{ total }}.
const total = computed(() => quantity.value * unitPrice.value)
</script>

<template>
  <div class="summary">
    <label>
      Quantity
      <input type="number" v-model.number="quantity" />
    </label>
    <p>Total: {{ total.toFixed(2) }}</p>
  </div>
</template>

<style scoped>
.summary { border: 1px solid #ddd; padding: 1rem; }
</style>

The three blocks are compiled as a unit, but each has an independent job: script declares state, template projects state into markup, style is scoped with a generated attribute and never reaches the DOM tree of a child component.

Applies to. The top-level binding exposure described above is the behaviour of <script setup><script setup>, which has been the default in creat scaffolds since its introduction. Code written against the Options API instead exposes state through the component instance and requires th in the template. Chapter 1 states the difference precisely.

Read every .vue.vue file in this order: script, template, style. The script establishes the vocabulary, the template can only use names the script declared, and the style block has no bearing on either. Reading style first is the fastest way to waste time on a file.

Conventions and markers used in this guide

Four markers recur throughout the guide and carry fixed meaning. They exist so that a reader can decide whether a statement applies to their situation without reading the surrounding paragraph, and so that version-specific claims are never made implicitly.

MarkerMeaning
Applies to.The versions, platforms or configurations in which the described behaviour is valid
Requirements.A plugin, compiler option, browser capability or configuration without which the construct fails or degrades
Caveats.Edge cases, silent failure modes and the conditions under which the construct is unsafe
PreviewPresent only in a 3.6 release candidate. Explicitly not for production
Cross-referenceThe subject is documented in full in a later chapter and is not restated here

Every listing in this guide is complete and runnable within a generated project. Listings are annotated with comment styling rather than abbreviated, because a fragment that omits an import is a specification of nothing.

  2. Run npm ls vuenpm ls vue and node -p "require('vue/package.json').version"node -p "require('vue/package.json').version" and confirm both report the same version. Record the discrepancy if they do not.
  3. Install Vue DevTools, mount OrderSum from section 0.5, and in the reactivity inspector change quan from 11 to 55. Record whether to appears as a subscriber and whether it re-evaluates.
  4. Delete the lang="ts"lang="ts" attribute from the same component and run vue-tsc --noEmitvue-tsc --noEmit. Record the first diagnostic produced and whether it is reported at the template expression or at the script line.

1 · Project Setup and the Component Contract

1.1 Scaffolding with create-vue and the Vite dev server1.2 Anatomy of a .vue file and the block order rules1.3 Why <script setup> is the default1.4 Declaring props with defineProps1.5 Defaults, validators and the reactive destructure caveat1.6 defineEmits, defineExpose and defineComponent

The component contract is the interface between a component and everything around it. This chapter establishes how a project is generated, how a single-file component is compiled, and the three macros that define that contract: defin, defin and defineExposedefineExpose. Every prop and event decision made here is a public API commitment.

Scaffolding with create-vue and the Vite dev server

Scaffolding produces a working development loop in one command. The important part is not the file list but what the generated configuration already contains, because every one of those entries is a decision that would otherwise be re-litigated per project.

# 1. Generate. The generator is interactive; flags below are for scripted use.
npm create vue@latest billing-ui -- --ts --vitest --eslint-with-prettier

# 2. Install and start the dev server.
cd billing-ui
npm install
npm run dev

# 3. The generated build configuration, unabridged.
// vite.config.ts
import { fileURLToPath, URL } from 'node:url'
import { defineConfig } from 'vite'
import vue from '@vitejs/plugin-vue'

export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [vue()],
  resolve: {
    alias: { '@': fileURLToPath(new URL('./src', import.meta.url)) }
  },
  server: {
    port: 5173,
    proxy: { '/api': { target: 'http://localhost:8000', changeOrigin: true } }
  }
})

The /api/api proxy removes cross-origin requests from the development path entirely: the browser only ever talks to the Vite origin, and Vite forwards. This is the default arrangement for a separate API server and it is strictly better than enabling permissive CORS headers on the API for local work.

Requirements. Node 20.19 or later on the 22.x line, or 22.12 or later on the 24.x line. The Vite 8 dev server requires a runtime that supports native ES modules without a loader flag. Installing a project on an older Node produces an engine-check failure at npm installnpm install rather than a subtle runtime fault.

Caveats. vue-cli is in maintenance mode and receives no new features. A project still on Vue CLI should plan a migration to Vite; the migration is a build-configuration change, not an application-code change, because the component API is identical. Do not start a new project on Vue CLI.

Anatomy of a .vue file and the block order rules

A single-file component may declare at most one block of each kind, and some blocks may not be declared more than once. The order of the blocks is not significant to the compiler, but the tooling that formats and displays them imposes a convention, and a component that violates the block rules fails to compile rather than to render.

// A complete component exercising every legal top-level block.
<script setup lang="ts">
<script lang="ts">
  // A second <script> block alongside <script setup> is legal,
  // but it may NOT also be `setup`, and it runs in module scope,
  // outside the per-instance setup() function.
  export const CACHE_TAG = 'order-summary'
</script>

<script setup>
import { computed } from 'vue'
const props = defineProps({ items: { type: Array, default: () => [] } })
const count = computed(() => props.items.length)
</script>

<template><p>{{ count }} item(s)</p></template>

<style scoped>
p { margin: 0; }
</style>

<style>
/* A second style block is legal; each block is processed independently. */
:root { --summary-gap: 1rem; }
</style>

The conventional block order is <script><script>, <script setup><script setup>, <template><template>, then one or more <style><style> blocks. Order is not enforced by the compiler; the convention exists so that diffs stay small and tools can split blocks on fixed markers.

BlockRepeatableConstrained
<script><script>At most oneCannot also declare se when <script setup><script setup> is present
<script setup><script setup>At most oneMust be at the top level of the file, never nested
<template><template>At most oneRoot element count is unconstrained in Vue 3
<style><style>YesRequires lang="scss"lang="scss", lang="less"lang="less" or lang="stylus"lang="stylus" plus a matching Vite plugin

Multiple root elements are legal in Vue 3 and are attributed to a fragment, not to a synthetic wrapper element. The single-root requirement of Vue 2 no longer applies; the attribute fallthrough rules in section 2.6 are the one place this still has a visible consequence.

Caveats. A <style><style> block with a preprocessor language fails at transform time, not at runtime, and the error names the missing package. Adding lang="scss"lang="scss" without installing a preprocessor and registering it in the Vite plugins array produces a build failure on first page load.

Why <script setup> is the default

The Composition API has two authoring surfaces: the Options API, which organises code by option name, and <script setup><script setup>, which organises it by execution order. <script setup><script setup> is what creat generates, and the difference is not stylistic: it changes which bindings the template can see.

// The same component in both authoring styles.

<script setup>
// Every top-level binding is a closed-over constant the template closes over.
// Nothing is exposed dynamically. Nothing needs `expose` to be readable.
import { ref, computed } from 'vue'
const query = ref('')
const results = ref([])
const hasResults = computed(() => results.value.length > 0)
function search() { results.value = [] }
</script>

<template>
  <input v-model="query" />
  <button @click="search">Go</button>
  <p v-if="hasResults">{{ results.length }} found</p>
</template>

<script>
// Options API: the template reaches the component instance, so every
// name it uses must be returned from data(), methods, computed or props.
export default {
  data()      { return { query: '', results: [] } },
  computed:   { hasResults() { return this.results.length > 0 } },
  methods:    { search() { this.results = [] } }
}
</script>

In the Options API form the template is resolved against the instance proxy at render time, so an undeclared name is a silent render of nothing. In <script setup><script setup> the template is compiled to closures over specific setup-scope variables, so an undeclared name is a compile-time error.

Applies to. <script setup><script setup> compiles bindings to closures in the 3.5 line. The consequence worth internalising is that a template cannot read an imported binding that is not referenced, and that renaming a setup-scope variable is a template-wide rename that tooling performs correctly because the reference is lexical rather than dynamic.

Caveats. Because the template closes over setup scope, declaring l and reassigning it is the correct way to model a value that changes; declaring co and reassigning is an error the compiler will not catch for you if the reassignment happens inside a callback written in another file. Type checking with vue is the guard, and it belongs in CI.

Use the Options API only when maintaining a component that is already written that way, or when a library’s public API demands an options object for interop. Mixing the two in one component is supported but strictly worse than either alone: the Options API surface and the setup scope coexist, and the resolution order between them is a source of confusion rather than a feature.

Declaring props with defineProps

Props are the only sanctioned channel from parent to child. There are two declaration syntaxes, and they are not stylistically equivalent: a type-only declaration is erased at compile time and has no runtime representation, while a runtime declaration is compiled into an object that the runtime validates on every update.

// (a) TYPE-ONLY declaration. Erased by the compiler: zero runtime cost,
//     no validator, no default, no production-mode warnings.
<script setup lang="ts">
interface UserCardProps {
  name: string
  age?: number
  tags?: string[]
}
const props = defineProps<UserCardProps>()
</script>

// (b) RUNTIME declaration. Compiled to an object the runtime inspects.
<script setup lang="ts">
const props = defineProps({
  name:  { type: String, required: true },
  age:   { type: Number, required: false, default: 0 },
  tags:  { type: Array as PropType<string[]>, default: () => [] }
})
</script>

// (c) The hybrid form: type-only for the compiler, runtime object for the
//     runtime. `defineProps` accepts exactly one argument; this is the way
//     to have both without duplicating the prop list.
// (see section 1.5 for the full hybrid listing)

A type-only declaration is the preferred form when the project type checks in CI, because it produces a narrower, more accurate type than a runtime ty constructor and it costs nothing at runtime.

AspectType-only declarationRuntime declaration
Enforced byType checker only, at build timeType checker and runtime validator, on every prop update
DefaultsNot expressible; withDe is requiredDeclared inline
Custom validatorNot expressiblevali function
Runtime warning on wrong typeNoneYes, in development builds
Reflected in the devtools props panelPartially; types onlyFully, with resolved defaults
Bundle costNoneOne options object per component

The runtime declaration is a development-time safety net, not a validation layer. Do not branch on prop validity in production code; the validator is stripped and never runs there.

Caveats. Props are shallowly reactive. A nested object passed as a prop is not deeply watched, and mutating it from the child is a contract violation that produces no warning. Props flow in one direction only; the child must not write to them.

Defaults, validators and the reactive destructure caveat

Three mechanisms interact here and are frequently confused. A default makes a prop defined by omission; a validator warns at development time; and destructuring a prop in Vue 3.5 produces a reactive binding only because the compiler rewrites it, which means the same syntax behaves differently in a plain composable.

// The hybrid form: full type safety AND a runtime validator, no duplication.
<script setup lang="ts">
<interface PaginationProps {
  page?: number
  pageSize?: number
  total?: number
}

const props = defineProps({
  ...({} as PaginationProps),
  page: {
    type: Number,
    default: 1,
    validator: (v: number) => v >= 1 && v <= 1000
  },
  pageSize: { type: Number, default: 25 },
  total:   { type: Number, default: 0 }
})
</script>

// withDefaults is the type-only equivalent of a runtime default.
const props = withDefaults(defineProps<PaginationProps>(), {
  page: 1, pageSize: 25, total: 0
})

// Vue 3.5 reactive props destructure: the compiler rewrites `page` to
// `props.page` and keeps it live across parent updates.
const { page, pageSize } = defineProps<PaginationProps>()
watch(page, v => console.log('page is now', v))   // correct

The rewrite performed by the compiler is scoped to defin in <script setup><script setup>. The identical destructuring performed inside a composable is an ordinary JavaScript destructuring and produces a static snapshot with no reactivity at all.

Caveats. For an object or array prop, a def must be produced by a factory function, not a literal. default: []default: [] is a single shared array that every instance without an explicit prop will mutate, and the resulting cross-instance contamination is extremely difficult to trace. Type-based and factory-based validation cannot detect this.

Prop typeCorrect defaultIncorrect default and the failure it produces
Ardefault: () => []default: () => []default: []default: [] — one array shared by all instances
Objdefault: () => ({})default: () => ({})default: {}default: {} — one object shared by all instances
Funcdefault: () => () => {}default: () => () => {}default: () => {}default: () => {} — the factory itself becomes the value
Str, Num, Boodefault: 'x’default: 'x'— primitives are safe; no factory required

A Boo prop has a further rule: a Boolean-typed prop that is absent is cast to fa rather than left unde, unless a default of unde is declared explicitly. This is what makes the bare-attribute form work.

Applies to. Reactive props destructure requires <script setup><script setup> in Vue 3.5. In 3.4 and earlier, destructuring a props object is a static copy and is one of the most common causes of a component that renders correctly and then silently stops updating. Options API components are unaffected because they read through thisthis.

defineEmits, defineExpose and defineComponent

Props flow down and events flow up; both directions are typed contracts. define governs the third surface, the instance handle a parent obtains through a template ref. A fourth mechanism, defineC, exists for typing and interop and changes nothing about runtime behaviour.

// Two accepted emits declaration styles. (a) the type-only form,
// (b) the runtime form used when a validator is wanted.
<script setup lang="ts">
// (a)
const emit = defineEmits<{
  (e: 'change', value: string): void
  (e: 'close'): void
}>()

// (b) the call-signature map form, with named tuple payloads
const emit2 = defineEmits<{
  change: [value: string]
  close:  []
}>()
</script>

<script setup>
// Runtime validation of the event name, for development builds.
const emit = defineEmits({
  change: (value) => typeof value === 'string'
})

// Restricting the public surface of a component instance.
const reset = () => { /* ... */ }
defineExpose({ reset })
</script>

<template><button @click="emit('change', 'a')">x</button></template>

Without define, a template ref to a <script setup><script setup> component yields a handle that is closed over exactly what the parent needs and nothing more. That is a deliberate default: it is a feature, not an omission.

MacroDeclared withVisible to the parent as
definType argument or runtime objectRead-only attributes on the resolved vnode props
definType argument or runtime objectListeners the parent may attach with on
defineObject literal onlyProperties on the instance handle behind a template ref
defineObject literal onlyComponent options: na, inheri, custom options

A declared emits option also removes the corresponding on attribute from the fallthrough set, which is what prevents a listener from being applied twice: once as a declared emit and once as a fallthrough native listener on the root element.

// defineComponent adds types and nothing else. The runtime object it
// returns is what a plain `export default {}` would produce.
<script lang='ts'>
import { defineComponent } from 'vue'

export default defineComponent({
  name: 'LegacyWidget',
  props: { id: { type: String, required: true } },
  emits: ['activate'],
  setup(props, { emit, expose, slots, attrs }) {
    expose({ ping: () => emit('activate', props.id) })
    return { }   // nothing returned to the template
  }
})
</script>

defineC is required for inferring th types inside an options object and is the conventional shape for a component consumed by a library. Inside an application, a .vue.vue file with <script setup><script setup> is the equivalent and is preferred.

Requirements. All four macros are compiler macros, not runtime exports. They must be called at the top level of <script setup><script setup>, must not be imported from v, and must not be assigned to a variable before use. Calling one inside a function or a conditional is a compile-time error, not a runtime one.

Exercises 1

  1. Scaffold a project and create a UserCa that accepts na as a required prop and ta as an optional array defaulting to empty. Prove the shared-array failure by logging the identity of the default across two instances, then correct it.
  2. Declare the same component twice, once with a type-only prop declaration and once with a runtime declaration. Mount both and pass a numeric na from the parent; record which build warns and confirm the warning is absent in a production build.
  3. Use the reactive props destructure to write a wa on a prop, then perform the same destructure inside a composable in the same file. Record the difference in behaviour and explain the cause in terms of what the compiler rewrites.
  4. Add defineExpose({ reset })defineExpose({ reset }) to a component, obtain a template ref to it, and call resetreset. Remove the define call and record the failure. Then add it back and confirm the handle is restricted to what was exposed.
  5. Declare an emits option and attach a matching listener in the parent, then inspect the rendered element in DevTools. Record whether the listener appears on the root element as a native listener, and explain what changes when the emits option is removed.
  6. Attempt to call defin inside an i block in a <script setup><script setup> file and record the exact compiler diagnostic. Repeat with defin and with defineExposedefineExpose.

2 · Template Syntax and Directives

2.1 Interpolation and the expression grammar2.2 v-if, v-else-if, v-else and the v-show cost difference2.3 v-for, key, and the index-as-key failure mode2.4 Attribute binding, shorthands and dynamic arguments2.5 v-model: inputs, components and modifiers2.6 Event modifiers, v-html, slots and fallthrough attributes

The template is a declarative projection of state, compiled ahead of execution into render instructions. Its expression grammar is deliberately narrow, and the directives that extend it each have a cost model. This chapter covers the projection itself; the reactivity that drives it is chapter 3.

Interpolation and the expression grammar

Interpolation and directive bindings accept expressions, not statements. The compiler parses them with a JavaScript expression parser and rejects anything that is not an expression, which is why a ternary is legal and an assignment is not, and why v- is a directive with its own grammar rather than an expression.

<script setup>
import { ref } from 'vue'
const count = ref(3)
const items = ref(['a', 'b'])
</script>

<template>
  <p>{{ count }}</p>                          <!-- text interpolation -->
  <p>{{ count * 2 }}</p>                    <!-- expressions are allowed -->
  <p>{{ count > 2 ? 'many' : 'few' }}</p>  <!-- ternary is an expression -->
  <p>{{ items.join(', ') }}</p>              <!-- method calls are allowed -->

  <!-- NOT ALLOWED: statements, declarations, assignment, sequence -->
  <p>{{ count = 4 }}</p>
  <p>{{ const x = 1 }}</p>
  <p>{{ items; count }}</p>
  <p>{{ function() {} }}</p>
</template>

Each of the four rejected forms is a compile-time error reported with a source location, not a silently ignored binding. This is the single most useful property of the template compiler: illegal expressions cannot reach production.

Move logic into the script whenever an expression grows past a ternary or a method call. A template that computes a value has no place to document why, no place to unit test, and no way to be reused by another component. Every {{ }}{{ }} should be readable as a noun phrase.

Caveats. Interpolation is escaped. A string containing markup renders as text, not as elements. Producing markup from data is a deliberate act covered in section 2.6, and it is a security decision rather than a formatting one.

v-if, v-else-if, v-else and the v-show cost difference

Conditional rendering has two implementations with different costs. v- compiles to a branch that creates and destroys the subtree, involving a component instantiation on each toggle; v-s compiles to a style toggle and leaves the subtree mounted, paying its setup cost permanently.

<script setup>
import { ref } from 'vue'
const state = ref('idle')
const heavy = ref('a')
</script>

<template>
  <!-- v-if: a branch. The subtree does not exist while false. -->
  <button @click="state = 'loading'">Load</button>

  <div v-if="state === 'idle'">Nothing requested yet.</div>
  <div v-else-if="state === 'loading'">Loading…</div>
  <div v-else>Loaded: {{ heavy }}</div>

  <!-- v-show: always rendered, toggled with display:none. -->
  <p v-show="state === 'loading'">Spinner label</p>
</template>

<style>
/* v-show sets the inline `display` property. This rule loses to it. */
p[style] { display: block !important; }
</style>

The style block at the foot is a real failure mode. v-s writes an inline dis style, and inline styles outrank stylesheet rules that lack !important!important, so the element stays visible.

Aspectv-v-s
Initial costNone while falseFull render and setup cost incurred immediately
Toggle costCreate or destroy the subtree each timeA single style write each time
Memory while hiddenReleasedRetained, including any child component state
Appropriate forRarely displayed, expensive, or stateful childrenFrequently toggled, cheap, stateless content
TransitionsWorks with enter/leave hooksWorks with CSS transitions only

A component mounted under v-s and hidden for an hour still runs its setIn unless the component itself handles visibility. v- calls the unmount hooks; v-s does not.

Caveats. A v-el chain must immediately follow a v- or another v-el, with no intervening element and no intervening text of significance. A comment between them is tolerated; a wrapping element is not. Only one v-e is permitted, and it must be last.

Caveats. An empty string and the number 00 are falsy. v-if="message"v-if="message" will therefore hide a legitimately empty-but-present value, and v-if="items.length"v-if="items.length" correctly renders nothing for an empty list. Prefer message !== ''message !== '' or != null!= null when presence, not truthiness, is the intent.

v-for, key, and the index-as-key failure mode

List rendering is the one place where a key is not an optimisation but a correctness requirement. The key tells the diff algorithm which DOM nodes correspond to which items across renders; a key that is not stable and unique per item makes the algorithm attribute state to the wrong row.

<script setup>
import { ref } from 'vue'

// A stable, entity-derived id. Preferred key.
const people = ref([
  { id: 7, name: 'Ada',   note: '' },
  { id: 8, name: 'Grace', note: '' },
  { id: 9, name: 'Alan',  note: '' }
])

// WRONG: index as key. Reordering the list re-patches nodes in place,
// so the uncontrolled input below keeps its text under the wrong name.
const idxKeyed = ref(['a', 'b'])
</script>

<template>
  <!-- Preferred: key the entity -->
  <ul>
    <li v-for="person in people" :key="person.id">
      {{ person.name }}
      <input v-model="person.note" />
    </li>
  </ul>

  <!-- v-for with (value, key, index): index is the THIRD element -->
  <p v-for="(value, key, index) in object" :key="key">{{ index }}: {{ value }}</p>

  <!-- key on the <template v-for> when the output is several elements -->
  <template v-for="n in [1, 2]" :key="n">
    <dt>{{ n }}</dt><dd>value {{ n }}</dd>
  </template>
</template>

The (value, key, index)(value, key, index) parameter order differs from the array form, where the second parameter is the index. Reading a map as if it were an array produces an off-by-one that is silent until a rendering bug is traced.

Key choiceValidFailure when violated
Stable entity id from the data source✓—
Index, for a list that is append-only and never reordered, filtered or sorted✓—
Index, for a sortable, filterable or reorderable list✗Input state, focus and component state follow the position, not the item
Random value generated per render✗Every node is torn down and recreated on every update
A primitive that can duplicate✗Duplicate-key warning; ambiguous patching

Duplicate keys produce a development warning and an undefined order in the patched output. The warning names the duplicates, which makes this failure easier to diagnose than the index case.

Caveats. v- and v- on the same element is permitted by the compiler, and v- takes precedence in evaluation, which means the v- alias is not yet in scope when the condition is evaluated. Referencing the alias in the v- therefore fails. Wrap the pair in a <template><template> instead.

Attribute binding, shorthands and dynamic arguments

Attribute binding projects state into element attributes, and it is how a template stays declarative. The shorthand forms exist for ergonomics only; they compile to exactly the same instructions as the long form, so a codebase should pick one and be consistent.

<script setup>
import { ref } from 'vue'
const cls = ref('btn')
const disabled = ref(false)
const attrs = ref({ 'aria-label': 'close', 'data-testid': 'x' })
const anchor = ref('href')
const onToggle = () => (disabled.value = !disabled.value)
</script>

<template>
  <!-- long and short forms are equivalent -->
  <button :class="cls" @click="onToggle">A</button>
  <button v-bind:class="cls" v-on:click="onToggle">B</button>

  <!-- `.` shorthand for v-bind; `@` shorthand for v-on -->
  <button .disabled="disabled" .aria-busy="disabled">C</button>

  <!-- multiple classes: array, object or string -->
  <div :class="[cls, { active: disabled, 'is-off': !disabled }]" />

  <!-- style binding: object syntax, kebab or camel key both work -->
  <div :style="{ backgroundColor: disabled ? '#eee' : '#fff' }" />

  <!-- dynamic argument: the attribute name itself is an expression -->
  <a v-bind=[anchor]="'#'" <v-bind=[anchor]="attrs">link</a>

  <!-- spreading an object of attributes -->
  <span v-bind="attrs">spread</span>
</template>

Boolean attributes are handled specially: binding fa or nu removes the attribute, and binding tr writes it with an empty value. Non-boolean attributes bind their value as a string, so binding a number to tabi is safe while binding to i yields a stringified number.

Caveats. A dynamic argument expression must evaluate to a string or nullnull. It cannot be used to construct a spread operator, and a value containing a space produces an attribute the browser will not parse as intended. Dynamic argument names are not known to the type checker in a .vue.vue template, so a typo there is a silent no-op.

Applies to. The .. shorthand for v-b is parsed by the template compiler and is not valid HTML attribute syntax. It is safe in a .vue.vue template and is a syntax error in any template processed by something other than the Vue compiler, such as a server-rendered string that is not compiled.

v-model: inputs, components and modifiers

v-m is a two-way binding sugar over a va prop and an in event. Its expansion differs per input type, and its component form is a convention rather than a language feature: any component can participate by honouring that contract.

<script setup>
import { ref } from 'vue'
const text   = ref('  spaced  ')
const amount = ref('42.50')   // string until .number parses it
const lazy   = ref('')
const agree  = ref(false)
const plan   = ref('pro')
</script>

<template>
  <input v-model="text" />                        <!-- string -->
  <input v-model.trim="text" />                 <!-- trims on input -->
  <input v-model.number="amount" />              <!-- toNumber() applied -->
  <input v-model.lazy="lazy" />                  <!-- commits on change, not input -->
  <input type="number" v-model="amount" />  <!-- no .number needed -->

  <!-- checkbox: v-model is bound to the ARRAY, not the boolean -->
  <input type="checkbox" v-model="agree" />
  <!-- a group with a shared ref, or a bound :value on each -->
  <input type="radio" v-model="plan" value="free" />
  <input type="radio" v-model="plan" value="pro" />
  <select v-model="plan">
    <option value="free">Free</option>
    <option value="pro'>Pro</option>   <!-- deliberate typo: see caveat -->
  </select>
</template>

The mismatched va attribute on the second option is intentional and demonstrates a live bug: with a v-m on the <select><select>, selecting that option assigns the literal string pro’pro' to the model, and no diagnostic is produced because the value is a perfectly valid string.

ElementModel receivesWrite-back
<input><input> text, password, searchString on inSets va
<input type="number"><input type="number">Number when parseable, otherwise the raw stringSets va
<input type="checkbox"><input type="checkbox"> singleBooleanSets che
<input type="checkbox"><input type="checkbox"> with array modelArray, toggling membership of the vaChecks membership
<input type="radio"><input type="radio">The va attribute of the selected inputSets che per member
<select><select>The selected <option><option> vaSelects by value
<select multiple><select multiple>Array of selected valuesSelects each member

Custom modifiers are defined with v-m arguments and read back with getModel in the child. This is how a wrapper adds trimming, debouncing or numeric coercion without the parent knowing.

// A component that participates in v-model, three ways.
<script setup>
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, watch, computed, getCurrentInstance } from 'vue'

// (a) The default convention: `modelValue` prop + `update:modelValue` emit.
const props = defineProps({ modelValue: { type: String, required: true } })
const emit  = defineEmits(['update:modelValue'])

// (b) Named models via the `v-model:name` argument.
const title  = defineModel<string>('title', { default: '' })
const draft  = defineModel<string>()

// (c) A custom modifier, read from the parent's v-model.modifier args.
const instance = getCurrentInstance()
const modifiers = computed(
  () => instance?.vnode.props?.modelModifiers ?? {}
)
watch(() => props.modelValue, v => emit('update:modelValue',
  modifiers.value.trim ? String(v).trim() : v), { immediate: true })
</script>

<script setup>
<template>
  <h1>{{ title }}</h1>
  <input :value="draft" @input="draft = ($event.target as HTMLInputElement).value" />
</template>

defin is the concise form of the default convention and is the preferred way to declare a two-way binding. It returns a ref that is writable from inside and whose writes emit update:nameupdate:name to the parent.

Caveats. v-m on a component binds to the model prop. A component that declares a prop named va and expects the native convention will receive nothing and will appear not to update. Additionally, v-m and an explicit :value:value on the same element produce conflicting writes to the same property; the compiler reports this as a duplicate-attribute error at build time.

Event modifiers, v-html, slots and fallthrough attributes

The final four template features share a theme: they are the escape hatches. Event modifiers change propagation, v-h bypasses escaping, slots project parent markup into a child, and fallthrough attributes project parent attributes onto a child root. Each has a narrow correct use and a wide misuse potential.

<script setup>
<script setup>
function onSubmit() { /* no event.preventDefault() needed */ }
</script>

<template>
  <!-- .prevent calls preventDefault; .stop stops propagation; -->
  <!-- .self fires only when the target IS the bound element; -->
  <!-- .once removes the listener after the first call; -->
  <!-- .capture registers the listener in the capture phase.       -->
  <form @submit.prevent="onSubmit">
    <div @click.self="onBackground">
      <button @click.stop.once="onFirst">Go</button>
    </div>
  </form>

  <!-- v-html: the ONLY way to render markup from data. Unescaped. -->
  <div v-html="sanitisedHtml" />

  <!-- Slot projection: parent content into child layout -->
  <Card>
    <template #header><h2>Title</h2></template>
    Body text
    <template #footer><small>Fine print</small></template>
  </Card>
</template>

<script setup>
// The child declares where each slot is rendered, and a fallback.
<template>
  <section>
    <header><slot name="header">Untitled</slot></header>
    <main><slot /></main>
    <footer><slot name="footer" /></footer>
  </section>
</template>

// Fallthrough: undeclared attributes land on the child's root element.
<script setup>
<script setup>
const attrs = defineAttrs({ id: String })
defineOptions({ inheritAttrs: false })
</script>

<template>
  <div v-bind="attrs" class="'card ' + (attrs.class ?? '')"></div>
</template>

When a component has more than one root element, fallthrough attributes cannot be applied automatically and the compiler emits a development warning. Either give the component a single root or set inheritAttrs: falseinheritAttrs: false and bind deliberately.

Caveats. v-h performs no sanitisation. Assigning a string that contains an attacker-controlled payload executes it. Every use requires sanitisation upstream, and a component that accepts a ht prop and renders it with v-h must document that contract on the prop itself. Escaped interpolation is the default and the correct choice in almost every case.

Event modifierEffectTypical use
.stop.stopCalls stopProNested clickable regions inside a clickable parent
.prevent.preventCalls preventForm submission, link navigation
.self.selfFires only when event.target === elementevent.target === elementOverlay backdrop dismissal
.capture.captureRegisters on the capture phaseIntercepting an event before descendants handle it
.once.onceRemoves the listener after the first invocationOne-shot confirmation dialogs
.passive.passiveDeclares the listener non-blockingScroll and touch handlers that never call prevent
.exact.exactSystem modifier must be the only one pressedCtrl-click that must not fire for Ctrl-Shift-click

System modifiers are named as a suffix group, for example @keyup.ctrl.enter@keyup.ctrl.enter and @keyup.page-down@keyup.page-down. The alias .esc.esc, .tab.tab, .enter.enter, .space.space, .up.up, .down.down, .left.left and .delete.delete are provided for the common key names.

Exercises 2

  1. Build a list keyed by index containing uncontrolled text inputs, then reorder the underlying array at runtime. Record what happens to each input’s text. Repeat with a stable entity key and record the difference.
  2. Place v- and v- on the same element and reference the loop alias in the condition. Record the compiler diagnostic, then rewrite using a wrapping <template><template>.
  3. Apply v-s to an element with a display: block !importantdisplay: block !important rule and confirm the element remains visible. Replace the rule and confirm the toggle works.
  4. Create a multi-root component, pass a cl and a data-*data-* attribute to it, and record the development warning. Then set inheritAttrs: falseinheritAttrs: false and bind the attributes manually.
  5. Construct a <select><select> with a mismatched va attribute on one option and record the model value after selecting it. Explain why no diagnostic appears.
  6. Bind a v-h value containing a scr element and a img onerrorimg onerror payload, and record the execution. Then install a sanitiser, repeat, and record the result.
  7. Declare a component with defin bound as v-model:titlev-model:title from the parent, and write to it from inside the child. Confirm the parent observes the update and that the declared name matches.

3 · Reactivity Fundamentals

3.1 ref versus reactive and the selection rule3.2 .value, template unwrapping and nested refs3.3 computed: caching, invalidation and purity3.4 watch: sources, deep, flush and immediate3.5 watchEffect and the difference from watch3.6 Conversion utilities, shallow variants and the tracking model

Reactivity is automatic dependency tracking through a Proxy and an effect stack. Everything in this chapter follows from two rules: a reactive read inside a running effect subscribes that effect, and a write invalidates the subscribers. Every failure mode listed here is a case where a read happened outside an effect.

ref versus reactive and the selection rule

There are two ways to declare reactive state. r wraps a value in an object with a .value.value accessor and works for any type, including primitives. reac proxies an object and has no accessor, so it cannot hold a primitive at all.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, reactive } from 'vue'

// ref: the default choice. Holds primitives, objects, arrays, anything.
const count  = ref(0)
const name   = ref('anonymous')
const items  = ref([])            // type inferred as never[] without annotation
const user   = ref({ id: 1, name: 'Ada' })  // deeply reactive
count.value

// reactive: object identity is preserved; no .value accessor exists.
const form = reactive({ email: '', tags: [] })
form.email                    // mutate in place; do NOT reassign the proxy

// These two do not work and fail in characteristic ways:
const bad1 = reactive(0)       // runtime warning: cannot make a primitive reactive
// form = { email: 'x' }  // TS error, and at runtime the proxy is orphaned

The selection rule is short enough to state as a policy: use r for primitives and for any single value, and use reac only for a cohesive object whose shape is fixed and whose identity is meaningful. Everything else follows from the two mechanical differences.

Aspectrreac
Accepts a primitiveYesNo — development warning, value returned unwrapped
Access in script.value.valueDirect property access
Access in templateUnwrapped automaticallyDirect
ReplaceableYes: count.value = 1count.value = 1No: reassigning the variable orphans the proxy
Return type from a functionConsistent, always unwrapped by template typingLoses its reactive type through many TypeScript inference paths
Identity stabilityStable wrapper; the object behind it can changeOne proxy for the object lifetime
Spread / destructure in scriptProduces a static copyProduces a static copy

Both are implemented on the same proxy-based target, and both are deep by default: a nested object inside either becomes reactive on access.

Caveats. reactive()reactive() returns a proxy that must be held in a variable declared with co or l in setup scope. Destructuring a reac object breaks reactivity completely, because the destructured value is a plain snapshot, not a live read. Returning it from a composable, or storing it in a r, both work; destructuring it does not.

.value, template unwrapping and nested refs

Ref unwrapping is the most frequent source of a Vue reactivity bug, and it has three different rules depending on where the access happens. The template is the permissive case, the script is the strict case, and a ref placed inside another ref is the case where the strict rule applies even in the template.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, computed } from 'vue'

const count = ref(1)

// SCRIPT: .value is REQUIRED. `count` here is a RefImpl object.
count.value          // 1
count                // RefImpl { _value: 1 } -- not a number
count.value++         // the only correct increment

// TEMPLATE: unwrapped. Text interpolation and top-level bindings only.
// {{ count }}  ->  1

// ARRAYS AND COLLECTIONS are NOT unwrapped in templates.
const list = ref([ref(1), ref(2)])
// {{ list }} renders "[object Object],[object Object]"
// {{ list[0] }} renders "[object Object]"  -- unwrapping stops here

// NESTED REFS: a ref inside a ref is NOT unwrapped, in script or template.
const inner = ref(5)
const outer = ref(inner)
outer.value.value     // 5 -- correct

// The same rule breaks a computed that returns an object of refs:
const state = computed(() => ({ a: ref(1), b: ref(2) }))
// {{ state.a }} renders "[object Object]"; state.a is a Ref, not 1.

The general statement of the rule: unwrapping is applied to a ref only when it is a top-level property of a template render context, or a top-level entry of a reactive object. Every other position yields the ref itself.

When a value must survive a boundary that does not unwrap, normalise it at the boundary with unrefunref. unref(state.a)unref(state.a) is correct wherever sta is not, and costs nothing when the value was already a plain primitive.

Caveats. Assigning a component instance to a r causes the instance’s internal state to be unwrapped into the ref. Templates and component refs must therefore be held in shall or marked raw, or the instance proxy is corrupted. The same applies to objects that carry their own .value.value semantics, such as a class instance with a value accessor.

// The sanctioned fixes for a computed that must return nested refs.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, computed, shallowRef, unref } from 'vue'

// (a) Return plain values from the computed.
const flat = computed(() => ({ a: inner.value, b: outer.value.value }))

// (b) Use a reactive object: refs placed in a reactive object ARE unwrapped
//     on property access, in script and in template alike.
const bag = computed(() => reactive({ a: ref(1), b: ref(2) }))
bag.value.a            // 1 -- a number

// (c) Use toRefs to build a reactive object from separate refs.
const x = ref(0), y = ref(0)
const both = computed(() => toRefs({ x, y }))
both.value.x.value     // toRefs returns refs; unwrap explicitly

// (d) Mark the holder as shallow so no deep conversion occurs.
const holder = shallowRef({ instance: null as unknown })

Option (b) is the one most often needed: a reac container restores unwrapping for nested refs, at the cost of one extra proxy layer and the loss of the ability to replace the container wholesale.

Applies to. The template unwrapping rules described here have been stable since Vue 3.0 and are unchanged in 3.5. Reactive props destructure, added in 3.5, removes one class of mistake from this chapter by keeping a destructured prop live; it does not change any rule about arrays, nesting or instances.

computed: caching, invalidation and purity

A computed ref caches its value and re-evaluates only when a dependency it actually read has been written. That caching is the entire reason to prefer it over a method, and it is also the reason it must be a pure function: a side effect inside a computed is replayed unpredictably and cannot be reasoned about.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, computed, watch } from 'vue'

const first  = ref('Ada')
const last   = ref('Lovelace')
const logged = ref(0)

// PURE: reads only. No writes, no console output, no fetch, no DOM access.
const full = computed(() => {
  logged.value++            // WRONG: a write inside a computed
  return `${first.value} ${last.value}`
})

// With a writable ref, the cache is recomputed at most once per invalidation
// and is re-read many times between invalidations.
let evaluations = 0
const slow = computed(() => {
  evaluations++
  return items.value.map(i => i.price * 2)
})
slow.value; slow.value; slow.value   // evaluations === 1
items.value = [...]                  // invalidate
slow.value                          // evaluations === 2

// A computed with no reactive dependency is evaluated ONCE and then cached
// forever, for the lifetime of the component.
const constant = computed(() => Math.random())

// A writable computed forwards writes to a ref, or rejects them.
const firstWritable = computed({
  get: () => first.value,
  set: v => (first.value = v.trim())
})

Caching is lazy as well as memoised: nothing runs until the computed is read. A computed declared in a component that is never rendered is never evaluated, which makes it cheap but also means a bug in it may never surface in a test that does not render the component.

Caveats. A computed that writes to any reactive state is unsafe. If the write invalidates a dependency of the same computed, the scheduler re-runs it; the result is either an infinite update loop reported in the console or a silently dropped write, depending on timing. A computed that performs an asynchronous operation returns a Promise that resolves to whatever the value was at the time of resolution, not at call time.

Requirement for a computedConsequence when violated
No writes to reactive stateRe-entrant re-evaluation or an infinite update loop
No awThe value becomes a Promise; downstream consumers read a stale snapshot
Deterministic for a given dependency stateCache holds a value that no longer corresponds to its inputs
Reads every value it depends on, synchronouslyConditional reads narrow the dependency set; a value read inside a branch that did not execute is not tracked

The last row is the subtle one and is not a bug. A computed that reads bb only when aa is truthy tracks bb only while aa is truthy. This is correct dependency tracking, but it means a later change to bb will not invalidate the computed unless aa has already made the read happen.

watch: sources, deep, flush and immediate

A watcher is an effect with explicit sources. Unlike a computed it is not cached and it re-runs on every invalidation, which makes it the right tool for side effects and the wrong tool for derivation. Its scheduling options control when the callback runs relative to the component update cycle.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, reactive, watch } from 'vue'

const query   = ref('')
const form    = reactive({ email: '', age: 0 })
const results = ref([])

// 1. A ref source: new and old values are passed. Arrays compare by identity,
//    so a source must be a getter or array source to react to mutation.
watch(query, (value, previous) => { ... })

// 2. A getter source. This is the correct form for a ref holding an object.
watch(() => form.email, (value, previous) => { ... })

// 3. Multiple sources, compared as a flat array of results.
watch([query, () => form.age], ([q, a], [pq, pa]) => { ... })

// 4. Deep watching a reactive object: every property is a source. Cost grows
//    with the size of the object, because it is tracked by traversal.
watch(form, cb, { deep: true })

// 5. Deep watching a getter: same traversal, but only fires on a change the
//    getter actually observed.
watch(() => form, cb, { deep: true })

// 6. immediate fires the callback once at setup, before any change.
watch(query, cb, { immediate: true })

// 7. flush timing. 'pre' (default) runs before the owner component re-renders;
//    'post' runs after DOM updates; 'sync' runs synchronously on every write.
watch(results, cb, { flush: 'post' })   // measure the rendered DOM
watch(form,   cb, { flush: 'sync' })   // no batching, no dedup

A watcher created inside a component is bound to that component’s lifetime and is stopped automatically on unmount. A watcher created outside any component is never stopped and leaks; see section 4.6.

OptionValuesDefaultEffect
immeBooleanfaInvokes the callback once at registration, with the current value and unde as the old value
deBoolean or a numberfaTraverses nested values and compares by reference
fl'pre''pre', 'post''post', 'sync''sync''pre''pre'Chooses the scheduler queue the callback joins
onBooleanfaStops the watcher after its first invocation
onTrFunction—Development-only diagnostics on the dependency that triggered

A numeric de limits traversal to that many levels, which is the correct fix for a large tree that does not need full comparison. deep: Infinitydeep: Infinity is the documented equivalent of deep: truedeep: true.

Caveats. Deep watching is not free. Because Vue 3 traverses the object when the source is registered, a watcher with deep: truedeep: true over a large object adds a traversal on every dependency invalidation and holds strong references to everything visited. Prefer explicit getters over deep traversal, and use watch with a narrow body when the natural set of dependencies is small.

Caveats. flush: 'sync’flush: 'sync' disables the scheduler. A synchronous callback runs for every individual write, so a loop that mutates a watched value in its own callback recurses on the JavaScript stack rather than being collapsed into one update. Use it only where another system requires the value to be current at write time.

Applies to. The on option is available on wa in the 3.5 line. Use it instead of a let fired = falselet fired = false guard inside the callback: the guard prevents the body running but does not release the reactive subscriptions, so the watcher keeps being triggered forever.

watchEffect and the difference from watch

wa declares what to observe; watch declares what to do and infers the dependencies by running the body. The distinction is not stylistic. The inferred set is whatever the body reads synchronously before its first await, which makes the dependency list a function of the code and therefore fragile to edit.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, watch, watchEffect, onMounted, nextTick } from 'vue'

const id      = ref(1)
const version = ref(0)
const items   = ref([])

// watch: sources stated up front. Stable dependency set.
watch([id, version], ([newId, newVersion], [oldId, oldVersion]) => {
  load(newId, newVersion)
})

// watchEffect: dependencies discovered by execution. Runs immediately,
// synchronously, before the component mounts.
watchEffect(() => {
  load(id.value, version.value)     // id and version are tracked
}, { flush: 'post' })

// The tracking boundary: only synchronous reads before the first await
// are tracked. Everything after it is invisible to the dependency set.
watchEffect(async () => {
  const snapshot = id.value         // TRACKED
  await tick(snapshot)
  items.value = await fetch(snapshot) // version.value here is NOT tracked
})

// A local const is not reactive, so assigning it is invisible. This is the
// single most common watchEffect bug: a variable that "should" trigger.
watchEffect(() => {
  const enabled = props.enabled       // props.enabled is tracked, enabled is not
  if (enabled) start()
})

// A watcher created in a lifecycle hook is still owned by the component.
onMounted(() => {
  watch(id, () => { ... })   // auto-stopped on unmount
})

Because the body runs immediately, watch should be used for synchronising an external system with local state, not for fetching. Deferring the fetch to onMo, or using wa with an explicit source, makes the timing obvious to the next reader.

Aspectwawatch
First runOnly with immediate: trueimmediate: trueAlways, synchronously at registration
Dependency sourceDeclared: getters, refs, reactive objects, arrays of theseInferred from synchronous reads in the body
Callback signature(newValue, oldValue, onCleanup)(newValue, oldValue, onCleanup)None; values are read from scope
Survives a code edit that adds a readYes; the source list is explicitThe dependency set changes silently
Correct for async workYes; old value is available for comparisonOnly the pre-await reads are tracked
Correct useFetching, persisting, reacting to a change with contextSyncing a DOM or third-party API to state

Read the body of a watch and list every reactive value it touches before its first await. If that list is not what the function name suggests, the function is doing more than one job. The inference is invisible at the call site, which is the whole argument for using wa when the dependency set matters.

Conversion utilities, shallow variants and the tracking model

The remaining reactivity surface is a set of conversion and inspection utilities plus two shallow variants. They are not decorative: each exists because a specific, common operation cannot be performed correctly with the default deep behaviour.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import {
  ref, shallowRef, reactive, shallowReactive, toRef, toRefs,
  toRaw, unref, isRef, isReactive, isProxy, markRaw
} from 'vue'

// toRef: a ref that FOLLOWS one property of a reactive object.
const state = reactive({ count: 0 })
const count = toRef(state, 'count')
count.value          // reads and writes go through to state.count

// toRefs: one ref per property, preserving the property connection.
const { count: c, label } = toRefs(state)
c.value++             // state.count is now incremented

// toRaw: the underlying target behind a proxy. Bypasses reactivity.
const raw = toRaw(state)          // mutations to `raw` are NOT tracked

// unref / isRef / isReactive: normalisation and inspection at boundaries.
unref(count)          // number, whether count is a ref or not
isRef(count)          // true
isReactive(state)     // true
isProxy(state)        // true -- also true for a shallowReactive or a writable computed

// markRaw: opt an object out of conversion permanently.
// Correct for large immutable payloads, class instances with their own
// semantics, and component instances.
const chart = markRaw({ engine: null })

// shallowRef: .value is tracked; the object inside is NOT converted.
const big = shallowRef({ nested: { deep: 1 } })
big.value = { ... }    // triggers, because the ref identity changed
big.value.nested.deep = 2  // does NOT trigger

// shallowReactive: top level is tracked, nested objects are not converted.
const bag = shallowReactive({ a: 1, deep: { x: 1 } })
bag.a = 2           // triggers
bag.deep.x = 2        // does NOT trigger

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// The dependency-tracking model, demonstrated end to end.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
const source = ref(1)
const log: string[] = []

// An effect subscribes to a ref by READING it while the effect runs.
// Reading `source` above this line would have subscribed nothing.
watchEffect(() => { log.push('read: ' + source.value) })

source.value = 2
// log === ['read: 1', 'read: 2']

// What invalidates a computed is a write to a ref it READ.
const parity = computed(() => (source.value % 2 === 0 ? 'even' : 'odd'))
log.push('parity reads: ' + parity.value)  // subscribes nothing new

// Mutating a raw target bypasses the trigger entirely.
const obj = reactive({ n: 1 })
const target = toRaw(obj)
target.n = 99          // no effect runs; obj.n reads 99 but nothing was notified

// A ref read inside a callback that is NOT itself an effect subscribes
// nothing: a plain setTimeout callback is not a tracking context.
setTimeout(() => { log.push('late: ' + source.value) }, 0)
// The read happens; the write above will not re-run this callback.

shall is not a faster refref. It is a different contract: only assignment of the .value.value itself is reactive, and a shall holding a component instance or a large third-party object avoids the deep conversion cost entirely. The second half of the listing is the model everything above depends on: a read subscribes, a write invalidates, and a read that escapes a tracking context subscribes nothing.

Caveats. toR and to work on a reac object, not on a refref. Applied to a r they return refs whose .value.value is the ref itself, which produces a double-wrapped value that renders as an object. The same applies to toR on a plain object, which converts nothing.

Caveats. Destructuring a reac object takes a static snapshot of every property, so const { a, b } = objconst { a, b } = obj produces values that never update. Destructuring a r has the same effect on the ref itself. Both are correct JavaScript and both are silent failures in Vue; use toR, or read through the source object.

Exercises 3

  1. Declare the same counter as a r and as a reac object. Record what reactive(0)reactive(0) returns and what warning it produces, and explain why the two APIs differ on primitives.
  2. Build a r containing an array of refs and a comp returning an object of refs. Render both in a template and record the exact output, then correct each with a reac container and with unrefunref.
  3. Write a computed that performs a fe, then a wa that performs the same fetch. Record how many times each body runs across a sequence of dependency changes, and identify which values each one reads.
  4. Compare watch(form, cb, { deep: true })watch(form, cb, { deep: true }) with watch(() => form.a, cb)watch(() => form.a, cb) for a form of twenty fields, changing one field at a time. Record the callback count for each and account for the difference.
  5. Write a watch whose body contains an aw before a second reactive read, then change the second value. Record whether the effect re-runs, and restructure it so that every dependency is read before the first await.
  6. Compare r with shall holding a three-level nested object. Mutate a leaf at depth three and record whether a dependent computed re-evaluates, then use trigg and confirm it does.
  7. Mutate a value obtained from to and record the behaviour of every dependent effect. Then write the same mutation through the proxy and record the difference.

4 · The Composition API in Depth

4.1 The Composition API and why it exists alongside the Options API4.2 Composables: the use prefix and the setup-time rule4.3 Lifecycle hooks and the before/after pairing4.4 provide and inject, including reactivity caveats4.5 The component-ownership model for shared state4.6 Cleanup of subscriptions, timers and observers

The Composition API is not a replacement for the Options API; it is a second organisational axis. Its subject is reuse: logic that leaves a component without leaving the framework’s lifecycle, and state whose lifetime is expressed in terms of ownership rather than in terms of a single component instance.

The Composition API and why it exists alongside the Options API

Both APIs compile to the same internal component instance. The difference is where logic is grouped: the Options API groups by option category, which splits a single feature across da, comp, met and wa; the Composition API groups by feature, which keeps a feature’s state, derivation and effects adjacent.

// The same feature, in both styles. The Composition API version is not
// shorter; it is COHESIVE, which is what makes extraction possible.

<script>
// Options API: `useSearch` functionality scattered across four options.
export default {
  data() { return { query: '', results: [], loading: false } },
  computed: { active() { return this.results.filter(r => r.id !== this.query) } },
  methods: { async fetch() { this.loading = true; this.results = await api.search(this.query) } },
  watch: { query() { this.fetch() } },
  mounted() { this.fetch() }
}
</script>

<script setup>
// Composition API: one cohesive function, callable from anywhere.
function useSearch() {
  const query = ref('')
  const results = ref([])
  const loading = ref(false)
  const active = computed(() => results.value.filter(r => r.id !== query.value))
  async function fetch() { loading.value = true; results.value = await api.search(query.value) }
  watch(query, fetch)
  onMounted(fetch)
  return { query, results, loading, active, fetch }
}
</script>

The Composition API version is extractable without modification. That is the design goal: logic that does not know which component happens to call it can be tested, shared, and composed, and a mixin cannot achieve the same because its state is merged into the component rather than returned from it.

ConcernOptions APIComposition API
Code organisationBy option categoryBy feature
Reusable logicMixins, which merge state and risk name collisionsComposables, which return namespaced values
Name collisionsSilent: a mixin property is overwritten by the componentImpossible: a return value is destructured by the caller
State source visibilityDeclared across several optionsAdjacent to the logic that uses it
TypeScript inferenceRequires defineC to infer thNative, with no wrapper
Instance accessth available in every optionNo th; use getCurren as a last resort
Recommended for new codeNoYes

The Composition API and the Options API can coexist in one component. A component that is largely options-based and needs one composable can call it from setup()setup() and return its values, which is the correct incremental migration path.

Caveats. getCurren is a documented escape hatch and not a supported foundation. Anything obtained from it — the instance proxy, appCo, prov — is an internal structure, and code that depends on it is coupled to the runtime implementation rather than to a public contract.

Applies to. Every claim in this chapter holds for both APIs in Vue 3.5. The Options API is not deprecated and receives the same fixes; it is simply not the recommended surface for new application code, while remaining entirely appropriate for maintaining existing components and for component libraries with an options-based public API.

Composables: the use prefix and the setup-time rule

A composable is a function that calls other Composition API functions and returns reactive state. The u prefix is a naming convention that marks the call as a lifecycle-boundary call, and it is what makes the setup-time requirement legible at the call site.

// src/composables/useCounter.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, computed, readonly, Ref, ComputedRef } from 'vue'

// The contract: ALL state lives in this closure. Nothing is module scope,
// so two callers get two independent instances.
export function useCounter(initial = 0, step = 1) {
  const count = ref(initial)
  const doubled = computed(() => count.value * 2)

  function inc()     { count.value += step }
  function dec()     { count.value -= step }
  function reset()   { count.value = initial }
  function set(n: number) { count.value = n }

  // `readonly` is a contract, not a convention: writes throw a warning.
  return {
    count: count as Ref<number>,
    doubled: doubled as ComputedRef<number>,
    inc, dec, reset, set
  }
}

// A composable that also owns a subscription MUST clean up. `onScopeDispose`
// fires for the effect scope, which is broader than a component.
export function useInterval(handler: () => void, ms: number) {
  let id: ReturnType<typeof setInterval> | undefined
  const start = () => { if (id === undefined) id = setInterval(handler, ms) }
  const stop  = () => { clearInterval(id); id = undefined }
  onScopeDispose(stop, true)   // true = also on component unmount
  return { start, stop, readonly: readonly({ start, stop }) }
}

Declaring state in module scope is the error this structure exists to prevent. A module-level r is a single shared value: every component that calls the composable reads and writes the same one, and the bug is invisible until two instances are mounted.

Caveats. A composable that registers a lifecycle hook or a watcher must be called synchronously during se, before the first awaitawait. After an aw the active instance has already been popped from the stack, the registration silently binds to nothing, and the resulting watcher is never stopped.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import { onMounted, watch, getCurrentInstance } from 'vue'

// CORRECT: synchronous, inside setup, before any await.
const { count } = useCounter(0)

// CORRECT: inside a composable that itself was called during setup.
onMounted(() => {
  // The component is still the current instance here, so this is safe.
  watch(count, v => console.log(v))
})

// WRONG: after the first await, `getCurrentInstance()` is null and the
// watcher is never associated with any component scope.
async function setupLater() {
  await Promise.resolve()
  // getCurrentInstance() === null from here on
  watch(count, v => console.log(v))   // LEAKS: never stopped
}

// A composable that must tolerate deferred use declares the requirement:
function useLaterThing() {
  if (!getCurrentInstance()) {
    throw new Error('useLaterThing must be called during setup')
  }
}
