The guard at the end is the pattern for a composable that has a hard ordering requirement. Failing loudly at the call site is far cheaper than failing silently as a leak that is discovered during a memory profile weeks later.

ConventionRuleReason
Prefixu followed by a PascalCase nounMarks the call as registering framework-managed effects
File nameMatches the exported function, lowercaseOne composable per file; importable by path alone
State locationInside the function bodyGuarantees per-instance isolation
Return valueAn object of refs, computeds and functionsCallers destructure; no name collisions
Framework importsInside the composable file, not the callerThe caller should not need to know which API a composable uses
CleanuponScope for anything registeredSurvives use outside a component, such as in a store

A composable that only reads props and emits is technically a composable by shape but has no lifecycle. Those are better expressed as an inj plus a small local helper, and they are covered in section 4.4.

Lifecycle hooks and the before/after pairing

Every component instance traverses the same ordered sequence. Hooks exist in bef and plain forms, and the plain form is a deferred promise: in the default flush it runs after the DOM has been updated, which is the only point at which reading rendered output is meaningful.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import {
  onBeforeMount, onMounted, onBeforeUpdate, onUpdated,
  onBeforeUnmount, onUnmounted, onActivated, onDeactivated,
  onErrorCaptured, onRenderTracked, onRenderTriggered, onServerPrefetch,
  nextTick, getCurrentInstance
} from 'vue'

// MOUNT. before: component instance exists, DOM does not.
onBeforeMount(() => {
  // Read state, register global listeners. Do NOT touch the DOM.
})

// MOUNT. after: DOM is present. Reading layout belongs here.
onMounted(() => {
  // Measure, focus, initialise a third-party library against an element.
  el.value?.focus()
})

// UPDATE. before: reactive state has changed, DOM is still the old tree.
onBeforeUpdate(() => { snapshot = readState() })

// UPDATE. after: DOM reflects the new state. Guard against an infinite
// loop: this hook fires on every update caused by the component itself.
onUpdated(() => {
  // if (changed()) { mutateSomething() }   -- this is a loop
})

// UNMOUNT. before: teardown that may still need the instance and the DOM.
onBeforeUnmount(() => { controller.abort() })

// UNMOUNT. after: all children are already unmounted.
onUnmounted(() => { window.removeEventListener('resize', onResize) })

// KeepAlive. Fires on every entry into and exit from the cache.
onActivated(() => { resumePolling() })
onDeactivated(() => { pausePolling() })   // NOT called on unmount

// Error boundary. Return false to stop propagation to a parent boundary.
onErrorCaptured((err, instance, info) => {
  report(err, info)
  return false
})

// Development-only render diagnostics.
onRenderTracked(e => console.debug('tracked', e))
onRenderTriggered(e => console.debug('triggered', e))

A component inside <KeepAlive><KeepAlive> is unmounted never: it is deactivated. Its onUnm does not fire, which means any cleanup placed there is skipped for cached components and the polling in a cached view continues to run.

HookFiresDOM state at that moment
onBefoBefore the first renderNot created
onMoAfter the first renderCreated and patched
onBeforBefore a re-renderPresent, still the previous tree
onUpAfter a re-renderPatched to the new state
onBeforBefore teardownPresent
onUnmAfter teardownRemoved
onAct / onDeacKeepAlive entry and exitPreserved, not destroyed
onErrorOn a descendant errorVaries; the tree may be partially patched
onServerDuring server renderingNot applicable; no browser DOM

onUp is deferred to after the DOM update in the default flush, but it is still scheduled. If a measurement must happen synchronously with the write, use next or flush: 'post’flush: 'post' on a watcher and read the element there instead.

Caveats. onBefor does not fire for a component removed by a parent that is itself unmounting, because the teardown is initiated from the parent and the child hooks are not reached. Do not rely on it for release of resources shared with a still-mounted sibling. onUnm does fire in that case.

Caveats. The component instance identifier used in onError is not the component name; it is the internal uid, which changes between builds. Log the component’s declared na instead. onRende and onRender are development-only hooks and are removed from production builds.

Requirements. All hooks must be registered synchronously during setupsetup. Registering one inside an aw, a callback or a setTi produces a component-instance warning and no hook. Registration inside onMo is valid because the instance is still current.

provide and inject, including reactivity caveats

Provide and inject establishes an ancestor-descendant relationship that is resolved at component-creation time and is not reactive by itself. It is the right mechanism for a value a subtree genuinely owns and the wrong mechanism for a value that changes; a reactive value provided through it is reactive, and that distinction is the whole caveat.

<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, provide, inject, InjectionKey, Ref, computed } from 'vue'

// A typed key is required. A bare string key is untyped and, because every
// ancestor in the chain participates in the lookup, is collision-prone.
export const ThemeKey = as InjectionKey<Ref<string>>('theme')

// Providing a REF is what makes the injected value reactive. The provider
// keeps ownership; consumers read and write the same ref.
const theme = ref('light')
provide(ThemeKey, theme)

// A plain value works and is correctly typed:
provide('app-name', 'billing-ui')

// An injection key carrying an object plus its methods.
const cartKey = as InjectionKey<{ items: Ref<string[]>; add: (s: string) => void }>('cart')
const items = ref<string[]>([])
provide(cartKey, { items, add: (s) => items.value.push(s) })

<!-- consumer -->
// No default: inject() returns undefined and the cast hides it. Prefer this
// form when a missing provider is a genuine programming error.
const theme = inject(ThemeKey)!

// With a default, for an optional dependency. The default is returned
// verbatim if no ancestor provided the key.
const name = inject('app-name', 'fallback')
const cart = inject(cartKey)
// cart is undefined for a subtree rendered outside the provider

// Recommended: narrow explicitly rather than asserting.
const cart = inject(cartKey, null)
if (!cart) throw new Error('cartKey was not provided')

// An inject() called in the SAME component that provides it resolves to
// its own provide. It is a legitimate way to share within one component.
const local = inject(localKey)   // == the value passed to provide() above

Resolution walks the parent chain from the instantiating component upward, so an intermediate component that also provides the same key shadows every provider above it for that subtree. This is the mechanism behind scoped theming, and it works precisely because shadowing is local and not global.

Provided asReactive in the consumerCorrect use
A r or reac objectYes, for the consumerShared state with one owner
A comp or read refYes, read-onlyDerived state with one owner
A functionNo; the reference is stableCommands and callbacks
A plain object literalNo; the reference is stableA frozen, immutable configuration
A primitive, or a value that is later reassigned by the providerNo; the injected binding keeps the originalNever. Wrap it in a ref

The last row is the defect this table exists to prevent. let theme = 'light’let theme = 'light' provided as a string, then reassigned in the provider, leaves every consumer holding 'light''light' forever. There is no warning and no diagnostic.

Caveats. inj is resolved once, when the consumer component is created. It is not re-evaluated if a provider is unmounted and a different one takes its place, and it does not follow a component that is moved in the tree after creation. Two components can therefore hold injected references to providers that are no longer related to them.

Caveats. Because the lookup is by ancestor relationship, a component mounted into a different tree — a modal portalled to bo, a component rendered by a test harness — does not see the provider, even though both are in the same application. This is a frequent source of a modal that cannot find its store.

Choosing between provide/inject and a store. provide/inject is correct when there is exactly one provider, a known set of descendants, and a scope that is a subtree: a theme, a form context, a table’s column configuration, a slot-like capability. It also makes a local override trivial, because a deeper pro shadows the one above it for that subtree alone. A store is correct when there are many unrelated writers and readers, when the state must outlive any single component, or when it requires cross-route persistence, devtools time-travel, or a single shared instance enforced by contract. Both are the same shape — a ref plus methods — so the choice is about how many owners the state should have, not about reactivity features. Pinia is itself built on this primitive: a store is a module-level reactive object with a lifecycle that the application owns.

The component-ownership model for shared state

Reactive state in Vue has an owner. The owner is the component instance whose setup scope created it, and the owner is what causes automatic teardown. Choosing an owner deliberately is the difference between state that is disposed and state that survives its consumers, sometimes for the rest of the session.

// OWNER 1: the component. Created in setup, disposed on unmount.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, onScopeDispose, effectScope, getCurrentScope } from 'vue'

// Two instances of the same component have two independent states.
const local = ref(0)          // per-instance

// Owner 2: an explicit effect scope. Everything created inside is
// disposed together, independent of any component.
const scope = effectScope()
scope.run(() => {
  // a computed and a watch created here belong to the scope
})

// Detached scope: stop() does NOT dispose computeds. This is the correct
// mode for state that must survive its creator.
const detached = effectScope(true)

// Owner 3: the application. The root scope lives as long as the app.
// A module-level ref belongs to no scope and is disposed by nothing;
// it must be a deliberate singleton, not an accident.

// Disposal semantics: stopping a scope runs every registered cleanup.
scope.stop()          // watchers stop, onScopeDispose callbacks run
onScopeDispose(() => { teardown() }, true)

// Membership check, for a library that must know whether it is
// currently inside a component or a scope.
getCurrentScope()     // undefined at module top level

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// The ownership mistake, and the two correct repairs.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// WRONG: module scope. One ref, shared by every caller, for the life of
// the module. Two routes now show the same selection.
const selected = ref<number | null>(null)
export function useSelection() { return { selected } }

// REPAIR A: per-caller state. Each call returns a new ref. This is the
// correct default for any composable whose state belongs to a view.
export function useSelection() {
  const selected = ref<number | null>(null)
  const pick = (i: number) => (selected.value = i)
  return { selected, pick }
}

// REPAIR B: a deliberate singleton, created inside an app-level scope so
// its lifecycle is explicit and its disposal is testable. Note the
// scope is a PARAMETER, not an import: the lifetime belongs to the caller.
let session: { token: Ref<string | null> } | undefined
export function useSession(scope: EffectScope) {
  if (!session) session = scope.run(() => ({ token: ref<string | null>(null)) })!
  return session
}

Disposal is not merely about watchers. It is what runs the cleanup functions registered with onScope, and those are the functions that release timers, observers, abort controllers and event listeners — which is what makes an effect scope the correct owner for a composable that must work outside a component. Repair B accepts the scope as a parameter rather than importing one, so a test can create a scope, obtain a fresh singleton, and dispose it at the end.

OwnerCreated byDisposed byUse when
Component instancese in a .vue.vue fileUnmount of that instanceThe state is meaningful only while this component is on screen
Effect scopeeffectScope()effectScope()scope.stop()scope.stop()Grouping several related reactive resources with a shared lifetime
Application rootapp.runWi or the root scopeUnmount of the root componentCross-cutting singletons, plugins, anything the app owns
No scopeModule top levelNothing. EverOnly a deliberate, documented singleton

The last row is the design decision that must be made explicitly. A module-level r in a file that also exports a composable is almost always a mistake, and it is invisible in review because the declaration looks ordinary.

Caveats. A composable that creates state at module scope and is called from two routes will silently share that state between them, including across an unmount and a later remount. The symptom is data from a previous visit reappearing, which is diagnosed as a caching problem and repaired in the wrong place.

Applies to. Effect scopes, onScope and the automatic disposal of component-owned watchers are stable in the 3.5 line. The relationship between a watcher and the scope that owns it is the mechanism behind every automatic-cleanup guarantee in this chapter; nothing in chapter 5 or later supersedes it.

Cleanup of subscriptions, timers and observers

Everything registered outside the reactive system outlives the component by default: a timer, a subscription, a ResizeO, an abort controller, a global listener. Each must be released explicitly, and the release belongs in a scope-disposal hook so that it is correct for the component, the scope and the test harness alike.

// src/composables/useElementSize.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, onMounted, onScopeDispose, watchEffect, Ref } from 'vue'

export function useElementSize(target: Ref<HTMLElement | null>) {
  const width  = ref(0)
  const height = ref(0)

  const observer = new ResizeObserver(([entry]) => {
    // Guard: entries can report a zero-size box for a display:none element.
    if (!entry) return
    width.value  = entry.contentRect.width
    height.value = entry.contentRect.height
  })

  // A watchEffect re-observes whenever `target` changes and disposes
// the previous observation, because the effect's cleanup runs first.
  watchEffect((onCleanup) => {
    const el = target.value
    if (!el) return
    observer.observe(el)
    onCleanup(() => observer.unobserve(el))
  })

  // Scope disposal covers the case where the target is never cleared,
// and the component is torn down with the element still observed.
  onScopeDispose(() => observer.disconnect(), true)

  return { width, height }
}

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// The same contract applied to every other resource that outlives the
// component by default.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// 1. Timers. clearInterval on disposal, and a guard against double-start.
let ticker: ReturnType<typeof setInterval> | undefined
const startTicker = () => { if (!ticker) ticker = setInterval(tick, 1000) }
const stopTicker  = () => { clearInterval(ticker); ticker = undefined }
onScopeDispose(stopTicker, true)

// 2. Global listeners. Remove the SAME function reference that was added.
const onResize = () => { /* ... */ }
window.addEventListener('resize', onResize, { passive: true })
onScopeDispose(() => window.removeEventListener('resize', onResize), true)

// 3. AbortController. One call cancels every fetch it was passed to.
const ac = new AbortController()
const data = ref([])
fetch('/api/items', { signal: ac.signal }).then(r => r.json()).then(d => (data.value = d))
onScopeDispose(() => ac.abort(), true)

// 4. Third-party subscriptions. Dispose the handle, not the constructor.
const chart = shallowRef<unknown>()
let unsubscribe: (() => void) | undefined
onScopeDispose(() => unsubscribe?.(), true)

// 5. Detached DOM nodes. A directive-like manual insertion needs its node removed.
onScopeDispose(() => el.value?.remove(), true)

// 6. onUnmounted is the component-only spelling of the same contract, and is
// correct when the resource genuinely belongs to one component instance.
onUnmounted(() => stopTicker())

Two mechanisms are in play. The onCl callback passed to a watch runs before the next execution and before disposal, and is the correct place to release something the effect itself acquired. The onScopeDispose(fn, true)onScopeDispose(fn, true) registration covers everything else: passing tr extends the registration to the owning component as well, without which the callback is skipped in exactly the case where cleanup is wanted.

Caveats. A setTi scheduled in a callback fires after unmount unless it is cleared, and its body will write to a r that no longer has a subscriber. The write is harmless; the side effect inside the callback is not. A debounce implemented with setTi must therefore clear the pending timeout on disposal, or the debounced function fires against a torn-down component.

Caveats. onUnm does not fire for a child unmounted as part of a parent teardown, and onBefor does not fire for a child whose parent is being removed. A composable that must release resources in every teardown order belongs to a scope with onScopeDispose(fn, true)onScopeDispose(fn, true) rather than to either component hook.

ResourceRelease mechanismMissed release symptom
setIn / setTiclearI / clearT on disposalCallback runs against a destroyed component; unbounded log growth
Global event listenerremoveEve with the original referenceHandler runs once per mount; the max-listener warning in the console
In-flight feAbortController.abort()AbortController.abort()Late response writes state nobody reads; connection held open
ResizeO / Intersectidisconnect()disconnect()Callback fires on layout changes for detached nodes
Third-party subscriptionThe handle returned at subscription timeDuplicate updates; the library’s own memory report grows
wa / watchAutomatic on scope disposal, or .stop().stop()Retained closure keeps the component’s data alive
Watcher created outside any scopeManual .stop().stop(); there is no automatic ownerPermanent leak; no warning is emitted

The final row is worth stating as a rule: a wa created at module top level, in a setTi callback, or after an aw in setup has no owner and is never stopped. Storing its handle and stopping it is the only remedy.

Make cleanup structural rather than remembered. A composable that returns { start, stop }{ start, stop } and registers onScopeDispose(stop, true)onScopeDispose(stop, true) internally cannot be used in a way that leaks, because the caller never has to remember anything. That property is the real argument for the composable pattern over copying reactive code between files.

Exercises 4

  1. Write a component that registers a setIn and a win listener without cleanup. Mount and unmount it twenty times, then count the listener warnings in the console and the interval invocations. Add the cleanup and repeat.
  2. Author a useSel composable with module-scope state, use it in two routes, and record how selection carries across navigation. Apply both repairs from section 4.5 and confirm the behaviour for each.
  3. Call a composable that registers an onMo hook after an aw in setupsetup. Record the warning, then confirm with the reactivity inspector that the resulting watcher has no owner.
  4. Provide a plain string and then reassign it in the provider, and record what a descendant sees. Replace it with a r and confirm the descendant updates.
  5. Build a nested provider chain with three levels overriding the same typed key, and record which value each level’s consumer receives after mounting and after unmounting the middle provider.
  6. Create an effect scope, register three watchers and an onScope callback inside it, then call stop()stop(). Record which of the four registrations ran, then repeat with effectScope(true)effectScope(true).
  7. Create a component that is inside <KeepAlive><KeepAlive>, starts polling in onMo, and releases the timer in onUnmountedonUnmounted. Deactivate and reactivate it, and record whether polling continues while deactivated.

5 · Component Communication and Slots

5.1 One-way data flow: props down, events up5.2 Typed props, withDefaults and the Props helper5.3 defineEmits: validators and the type-versus-runtime trade-off5.4 Event naming conventions and how the two spellings reconcile5.5 v-model on a component and the defineModel macro5.6 Slots, useSlots, useAttrs, fallthrough and dynamic components

The public surface of a component is exactly three things: the props it declares, the events it emits, and the slots it renders. Everything else in the file is private. This chapter states the contract in each direction, the typing idioms for each half, the naming rules that the template compiler reconciles between script and template, the two-way binding contract and why it is not a violation of one-way flow, and the two projection mechanisms that let a parent own markup a child only lays out. Component resolution rules close the chapter, because a component that cannot be found is a component that cannot receive any of the above.

One-way data flow: props down, events up

A parent passes data to a child as props, which the child must treat as read-only. A child signals something to its parent by emitting an event, which the parent handles with a listener that owns the resulting state change. There is no third channel. The rule is not a style preference: it is what makes the parent the only place a piece of state is written, which in turn is what makes the change traceable, testable and undoable.

// src/components/UserRow.vue -- the child. Two inputs, two outputs, and
// no reference of any kind to the parent that renders it.
<script setup lang='ts'>
interface User {
  id: number
  name: string
  role: 'admin' | 'member' | 'viewer'
}

const props = defineProps<{
  user: User
  selectedId: number | null
  readonly?: boolean
}>()

const emit = defineEmits<{
  // The child reports what happened and carries the identity of
  // what happened, so the parent never has to close over a loop
  // variable to know which row was acted on.
  select: [id: number]
  rename: [id: number, name: string]
}>()

function onRowClick() {
  if (props.readonly) return
  emit('select', props.user.id)
}

function onNameInput(e: Event) {
  if (props.readonly) return
  const value = (e.target as HTMLInputElement).value
  emit('rename', props.user.id, value)
}
</script>

<template>
  <tr :class="{ 'is-selected': props.selectedId === props.user.id }"
      @click="onRowClick">
    <td>{{ props.user.name }}</td>
    <td>{{ props.user.role }}</td>
    <td>
      <input :value="props.user.name"
             :readonly="readonly || undefined"
             @input="onNameInput" />
    </td>
  </tr>
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/views/UsersView.vue -- the parent. It owns the state, it is the
// only writer of that state, and every change is one line long.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref } from 'vue'
import UserRow from '../components/UserRow.vue'

const users = ref<User[]>([])
const selectedId = ref<number | null>(null)

function onSelect(id: number) {
  selectedId.value = id          // the parent's own state, the parent's own write
}

function onRename(id: number, name: string) {
  const row = users.value.find(u => u.id === id)
  if (row) row.name = name
}
</script>

<template>
  <table>
    <tbody>
      <UserRow v-for="u in users" :key="u.id"
                :user="u"
                :selected-id="selectedId"
                @select="onSelect"
                @rename="onRename" />
    </tbody>
  </table>
</template>

The ren case is the one that proves the rule. The child holds a te value it does not need to keep; it emits the string upward and the parent decides whether to accept it. A child that kept a local copy of the text would need to reconcile that copy against the prop on every parent update, which is the re-introduction of shared mutable state that the rule exists to prevent.

DirectionMechanismDeclared byWritable by the child
Parent → childProp, read-only in the childdefinNo. Mutating a prop is a silent contract violation.
Child → parentEmitted event, handled by a parent listenerdefinOnly the em call itself
Parent → child, markupSlot content, projected into the child layoutdefin or useSNo. Slot content is compiled in the parent’s scope.
Parent → child, undeclared attrsFallthrough to the child’s single root elementNobody; implicitNo
Child → parent, imperativeTemplate ref plus definedefineOnly the exposed members

Caveats. Props are shallowly reactive. A nested object or array passed as a prop is not deeply watched by the child, and mutating it in place is a contract violation that produces no warning in any build. A child that must transform a prop should derive a value with comp and emit the result, not edit the argument. Similarly, an emitted event is not guaranteed to be handled: a parent that omits a listener produces no diagnostic, and the child’s assumption that someone is listening is false.

Applies to. The reactivity guarantees in this subsection are the 3.5 line. Reading a prop through the pr object rather than a destructure is the portable form; the reactive props destructure described in section 1.5 is a 3.5-only optimisation and produces identical behaviour here.

Typed props, withDefaults and the Props helper

A prop list is an interface, and it should be written as one. Four declaration forms exist, differing in what they cost at runtime and what they can express. The Pr naming convention is not a framework feature: it is the ordinary TypeScript practice of giving the interface a name so that it can be referenced from more than one place, which matters as soon as a composable, a wrapper component or a test needs the same shape.

// (1) TYPE-ONLY. The interface is named so it can be reused. No runtime
//     representation, so no defaults and no validator are expressible.
<script setup lang='ts'>
export interface Props {
  user: User
  size?: 'sm' | 'md' | 'lg'
  disabled?: boolean
  onDismiss?: () => void
}

const props = defineProps<Props>()
</script>

// (2) TYPE-ONLY PLUS DEFAULTS. withDefaults is the only way to express a
//     default against a type-only declaration, and the only place where
//     the literal types above are widened to their declared unions.
<script setup lang='ts'>
<interface Props {
  user: User
  size?: 'sm' | 'md' | 'lg'
  tags?: string[]
}

const props = withDefaults(defineProps<Props>(), {
  size: 'md',
  // A factory for the object and array case. A literal default is a
  // single value shared by every instance that omits the prop.
  tags: () => [] as string[]
})

// Vue 3.5: with a type-only declaration the destructure is reactive. The
// compiler rewrites each name to props.size / props.tags, so a watcher
// written on the destructured name tracks parent updates.
const { size, tags } = defineProps<Props>()
watch(size, v => console.log('size is now', v))   // correct in 3.5
</script>

// (3) HYBRID. One list, two representations: a runtime object for the
//     runtime, intersected with the type for the type checker. This is the
//     form to use when a runtime validator is genuinely required.
<script setup lang='ts'>
<interface PaginationProps {
  page?: number
  pageSize?: number
}

const props = defineProps({
  ...({} as PaginationProps),
  page: {
    type: Number,
    default: 1,
    validator: (v: number) => Number.isInteger(v) && v >= 1
  },
  pageSize: { type: Number, default: 25 }
})
</script>

// (4) CONSUMING the declared shape from outside the file. A component's
//     $props type is the authoritative description of what a wrapper,
//     a test factory or a composable must supply.
import type UserRow from './UserRow.vue'

type UserRowProps = InstanceType<typeof UserRow>['$props']

function mountUserRow(overrides: Partial<UserRowProps> = {}): UserRowProps {
  return { user: { id: 1, name: 'Ada', role: 'admin' }, ...overrides }
}

Form (4) is the one that prevents drift. A hand-written mirror of a prop list in a test helper is a second source of truth that is guaranteed to become wrong, and it is wrong silently, because the test compiles and the assertion still passes.

FormType-checkedRuntime defaultsRuntime validatorProduction cost
defineProps<Props>()defineProps<Props>()Yes, preciselyNoNoNone; erased
withDefaults(defineProps<Props>(), {})withDefaults(defineProps<Props>(), {})Yes, preciselyYesNoOne options object
defineProps({ ... })defineProps({ ... })Only if written in TypeScriptYesYesOne options object
Hybrid spread intersectionYes, preciselyYesYesOne options object
InstanceType<typeof C>['$props']InstanceType<typeof C>['$props']Yes, precisely——None; type-only

Caveats. withDe is a compile-time macro, not a runtime call, and it must wrap the defin call directly. It cannot be applied to the result of a function, spread conditionally, or supplied from an imported object, and doing any of those is a compile error. Separately, a default declared against a type-only prop list is not validated against the declared type at runtime: a default that contradicts the interface is a type error and nothing else, and it will not be caught in a production build.

Requirements. Every form in this subsection requires <script setup><script setup>. In the Options API the equivalent is the pr option, which is a runtime object by construction and gains nothing from the Pr interface except a Prop annotation per entry.

defineEmits: validators and the type-versus-runtime trade-off

An emits declaration is half a type and half a runtime option. The type half documents the payload at every call site; the runtime half validates the event name and argument types during development. Deciding which half to write is a project decision with a defensible answer, and the two halves are combinable in the same hybrid way as props.

// (a) TYPE-ONLY, call-signature form. The most precise spelling: each
//     overload is one event with one arity and one payload type.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const emit = defineEmits<{
  (e: 'change', value: string): void
  (e: 'open'): void
  (e: 'select', index: number, meta: { shiftKey: boolean }): void
}>()
</script>

// (b) TYPE-ONLY, named-tuple form. Equivalent typing, written as a map.
//     Preferable when the event list is long, because a missing entry is
//     visible in the declaration rather than implied by an overload.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const emit = defineEmits<{
  change: [value: string]
  open: []
  select: [index: number, meta: { shiftKey: boolean }]
}>()
</script>

// (c) RUNTIME. The values are validators, not type constructors. A
//     failing validator logs a development warning; it does not throw and
//     it does not stop the emit from reaching the parent.
<script setup>
const emit = defineEmits({
  change: (value) => typeof value === 'string' && value.length <= 64,
  select: (index) => Number.isInteger(index) && index >= 0
})
</script>

// (d) HYBRID, with an object validator for the production-checked case.
//     The runtime object must be written where the compiler can see it.
<script setup lang='ts'>
<interface Emits {
  (e: 'select', index: number): void
}

const emit = defineEmits({
  ...({} as Emits),
  select: (index: number) => Number.isInteger(index)
})
</script>

// (e) Declaring the event name as a const. This is what makes an unknown
//     event a compile error rather than a runtime no-op.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const EVENTS = {
  change: 'update:modelValue',
  dismiss: 'dismiss'
} as const

type EmitName = typeof EVENTS[keyof typeof EVENTS]
const emit = defineEmits<{
  (e: EmitName, ...args: unknown[]): void
}>()
</script>

The runtime form has one production consequence that is easy to miss: declaring select: (index) => Number.isInteger(index)select: (index) => Number.isInteger(index) produces a validator that runs on every emit in development, and that is the entire benefit. The validator is not stripped in a production build, because the option object is still a real object; it is simply that production code paths that pass a wrong value are bugs you will now pay for on every call rather than only during development.

AspectType-onlyRuntime validatorsHybrid
Catches a wrong payloadAt the em call site, in the editor and in CIAt runtime, in development builds, with a console warningBoth
Catches an undeclared event nameAt the em call siteAt runtime, in development buildsBoth
Enforced when a library consumer is wrongOnly if they type-checkYes, in developmentBoth
Cost per emit in productionNoneOne validator invocationOne validator invocation
Removes the matching on from fallthrough attrsYesYesYes
Exposes the event in the devtools component treeName onlyName and declared shapeName and declared shape

Caveats. A declared emits option is not a contract enforcement mechanism. Because a validator logs and returns, an event emitted with the wrong payload still reaches every listener, including code that will then throw. Validators are a diagnostic. If a payload must be trusted, validate it where the value is produced, or type the producer and rely on the type checker.

Caveats. A component with a single root element that emits a native event name will also have that listener applied to the root element through the fallthrough path, so a click handler fires twice: once through the emit and once as a native listener. Declaring the event in em removes it from the fallthrough set, which is the actual reason declaring every emitted event name is mandatory rather than optional.

Event naming conventions and how the two spellings reconcile

An event has two spellings: the string passed to em, written in the script, and the attribute written in the template, which the compiler reads. The two are not required to be textually identical, and the reconciliation is a documented transform rather than a coincidence. The rule that produces the fewest bugs is to emit camelCase and to listen in kebab-case, always.

// The reconciliation, stated exactly. A listener prop is compiled to
// onUpdate:modelValue. The runtime then compares the emitted name
// 'update:modelValue' against prop keys with hyphens removed, so
// @update:model-value resolves to the same handler.

// SCRIPT: camelCase, and for a namespaced event the namespace
//         itself camelCased while the member keeps its exact name.
<script setup>
<script setup lang='ts'>
const emit = defineEmits<{
  (e: 'update:modelValue', value: string): void
  (e: 'close-drawer', reason: 'escape' | 'outside'): void
  (e: 'row-select', index: number): void
  (e: 'update:searchQuery', q: string): void
}>()
</script>

// TEMPLATE: kebab-case is the only spelling that is guaranteed to
//          match. All four of these listeners resolve to the emits above.
<template>
  <Drawer v-model="text"
          @update:model-value="text = $event"
          @close-drawer="onClose"
          @row-select="onRowSelect"
          v-model:search-query="query" />
</template>

// The one case where the exact camelCase spelling is required in the
// template: an event whose name contains capitals that are part of
// the name rather than a word boundary. Prefer renaming such an
// event over relying on this exception.
<script setup>
emit('update:searchQuery', 'vue')   // 5.2 pair above: searchQuery
emit('closeDrawer', 'escape')      // 5.3: @close-drawer also matches
</script>

The hyphen-removal transform is applied per event name, not per character, so a name containing a hyphen cannot be produced by a camelCase emit in the naive sense. In practice the two rules together are: emit the name you want, and write the template attribute in the form toHand would produce, which is the same as writing every capital as a hyphen followed by its lowercase form.

Emitted nameListener in templateProp key after compileMatches
close@close-drawer@close-draweronClosYes
close@closeDrawer@closeDraweronClosYes, and in-DOM templates require the lower-case form
row-s@row-select@row-selectonRow- → hyphen-stripped to onRowYes
update:modelValueupdate:modelValue@update:model-value@update:model-valueonUpdate:model-valueonUpdate:model-valueYes, the v-m default
update:searchQueryupdate:searchQueryv-model:search-queryv-model:search-queryonUpdate:search-queryonUpdate:search-queryYes, see 5.5
update:searchQueryupdate:searchQuery@update:searchQuery@update:searchQueryonUpdate:searchQueryonUpdate:searchQueryOnly in an SFC template, never in an in-DOM template

Caveats. Attribute names in HTML are lower-cased by the parser before the template compiler sees them. An in-DOM template therefore cannot express @update:searchQuery@update:searchQuery at all, and camelCase attribute spellings are silently lost rather than reported. This is the reason the creat scaffold ships no in-DOM entry point, and the reason an event name containing internal capitals is a design smell rather than a legitimate choice.

Choose one convention and enforce it mechanically. A lint rule banning capital letters in @@ and v-bind:v-bind: attribute names costs nothing to run and removes an entire class of bug that only reproduces in one of the two build modes.

v-model on a component and the defineModel macro

Component v-m is not a second data channel. It is the compiler expanding to a prop plus an event, and the write still happens in the parent. The expansion is a convention any component can opt into, which is why a component with a model prop and an update:modelValueupdate:modelValue emit participates without any framework registration. defin generates that boilerplate and nothing else.

// (1) THE EXPLICIT CONTRACT. What `v-model="x"` expands to. Nothing in
//     the child ever writes the parent's binding target.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const props = defineProps<{ modelValue: string }>()
const emit = defineEmits<{ (e: 'update:modelValue', v: string): void }>()

// The child emits a new value. The parent's own handler assigns it.
function onInput(e: Event) {
  emit('update:modelValue', (e.target as HTMLInputElement).value)
}
</script>
<template><input :value="modelValue" @input="onInput" /></template>

// (2) MULTIPLE MODELS. `v-model:title` expands to :title plus
//     @update:title. `v-model:search-query` expands to :search-query plus
//     @update:search-query, which the hyphen-strip rule matches to the
//     emitted name 'update:searchQuery'.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const props = defineProps<{
  title: string
  searchQuery: string
  draft: string
}>()
const emit = defineEmits<{
  (e: 'update:title', v: string): void
  (e: 'update:searchQuery', v: string): void
  (e: 'save', payload: { title: string; draft: string }): void
  (e: 'cancel'): void
}>()
</script>

// (3) defineModel(). Added in 3.4, no longer experimental. The ref is
//     writable by the child and, on assignment, emits the paired event.
<script setup lang='ts'>
// Default model: the ref replaces the modelValue prop and the
// update:modelValue emit. `local` is the name of the local ref.
const model = defineModel<string>({ default: '' })
// Required, with no default: absence is an error at both ends.
const title = defineModel<string>('title', { required: true })
// Options shared with a prop declaration of the same name.
const open = defineModel<boolean>('open', {
  default: false
})
// A validator runs in development, exactly as a prop validator does.
const size = defineModel<'sm' | 'md' | 'lg'>('size', {
  validator: (v) => ['sm', 'md', 'lg'].includes(v)
})
// A transform applied to the value on the way out, and optionally to
// the value coming in. Added in 3.4 with the macro.
const tags = defineModel<string[]>('tags', {
  default: () => [],
  get: (v) => v.filter(Boolean),
  set: (v) => v
})
</script>
<template>
  <input v-model="model" />
  <input v-model="title" />
  <button @click="open = !open">{{ open }}</button>
  <Editor v-model:title="title" v-model:search-query="query" />
</template>

// (4) WRITING FROM THE PARENT SIDE. The child's ref is a computed with
//     a setter: reading it reads the prop, writing it emits. There is no
//     path by which the child mutates the parent's state object.
//     Expanded form of what defineModel() compiles to:
const _modelValue = defineProps<string>()
const _emit = defineEmits<{ (e: 'update:modelValue', v: string): void }>()
const model = computed({
  get: () => _modelValue,
  set: (v) => _emit('update:modelValue', v)
})

The expanded form in (4) is the whole mechanism, and it is the reason the rule in 5.1 survives v-modelv-model. The child’s binding is a computed whose setter emits an event. Assigning to it performs no write to the parent’s r; the parent performs the write, in the parent’s own listener, where the parent’s own validation and derived state live.

AspectExplicit props and emitsdefineModel()defineModel()
Available since3.03.4, stable
Lines of boilerplate per modelTwo per model, plus a handler per modelOne per model
Local g and s transformRequires a hand-written computedDeclared as options
Default value and validatorProp declaration optionsSecond-argument options, same shape
Additional events alongside the modelOrdinary defin entriesOrdinary defin entries; combine both macros
Read the incoming prop without emittingRead the prop directlyRead the prop by its declared name; the ref only reflects it
Prevent a write entirelySimply do not emitDeclare the model as read in the options

Caveats. defin is a compiler macro and must be called at the top level of <script setup><script setup>, once per model name, with a literal name. Two calls for the same name, a computed name, or a call inside a conditional are compile errors. Because the macro declares both a prop and an emit, declaring an update:modelValueupdate:modelValue entry in defin as well is a duplicate declaration and produces conflicting types.

Caveats. A def on a model with no local write is misleading: the child will show the default while the parent’s bound ref is still unde, and the two disagree until the first edit. For a two-way binding, prefer required: truerequired: true or give the parent’s ref a default, so that the value shown is always the value held.

Slots, useSlots, useAttrs, fallthrough and dynamic components

A slot hands the child a piece of the parent’s template, compiled in the parent’s scope. The child decides where it goes; the parent decides what it is. This is the inversion that separates a layout component from a content component, and it is the only sanctioned way for a parent to control the markup inside a child’s boundary. Two further mechanisms govern the opposite problem: useA for deliberate control of what falls through, and <component :is><component :is> for choosing a component at runtime.

// src/components/Panel.vue -- the layout component. It owns structure and
// chrome, and it has no opinion about the content it lays out.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { useSlots, useAttrs, computed } from 'vue'

const props = withDefaults(defineProps<{
  title?: string
  collapsible?: boolean
}>(), {
  title: '',
  collapsible: true
})
const open = defineModel<boolean>('open', { default: true })

// Typed slot declaration. defineSlots is available from 3.3 and is
// erased at runtime; useSlots is the runtime equivalent.
interface Slots {
  // A scoped slot: the parent renders, the child supplies the argument.
  default(props: { collapsed: boolean }): any
  header?(props: { title?: string }): any
  footer?(): any
  empty?(): any
}
defineSlots<Slots>()

// useSlots() is the runtime object. The two are complementary: the
// type declaration checks the parent's slot content, the runtime
// object answers questions about which slots were filled.
const slots = useSlots()
const hasBody = computed(() => !!(slots.default || slots.empty))
</script>

<template>
  <section class="panel">
    <header v-if="props.title || slots.header" class="panel__head">
      <slot name="header" :title="props.title">
        <h2 v-if="props.title">{{ props.title }}</h2>
      </slot>
      <button v-if="props.collapsible" type="button"
              :aria-expanded="String(open)"
              @click="open = !open">
        {{ open ? 'Hide' : 'Show' }}
      </button>
    </header>

    <div v-show="open" class="panel__body">
      <slot :collapsed="!open">
        <p v-if="!hasBody" class="panel__empty">Nothing selected.</p>
      </slot>
    </div>

    <footer v-if="slots.footer" class="panel__foot">
      <slot name="footer" />
    </footer>
  </section>
</template>

// The parent's side. Slot content is compiled in THIS scope, so it closes
// over this component's refs and needs no props to reach them.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const invoices = ref<Invoice[]>([])
const heading = computed(() => `Invoices (${invoices.value.length})`)
</script>
<template>
  <Panel title="heading" class="'panel--wide'">
    <<!-- a scoped slot: Panel supplies `title`, the parent    -->
    <<!-- renders. A real button, a real parent handler.     -->
    <template #header="{ title: t }">
      <strong>{{ t }}</strong>
      <button @click="exportCsv">Export</button>
    </template>

    <<!-- v-if on the template, not on the Panel: the slot simply  -->
    <<!-- is absent, so Panel's slots.default is falsy and its   -->
    <<!-- fallback renders. This is the recommended condition.   -->
    <template v-if="invoices.length" #default="{ collapsed }">
      <InvoiceTable :rows="invoices" :compact="collapsed" />
    </template>

    <template #empty>
      <EmptyState title="'No invoices'" @create="createInvoice" />
    </template>

    <template #footer>
      <small>{{ invoices.length }} records</small>
    </template>
  </Panel>
</template>

// useAttrs and fallthrough. A single-root component with no explicit
// binding of $attrs receives every undeclared attribute, class and
// style included, on its root element, automatically merged.
<script setup lang='ts'>
// inheritAttrs: false switches the automatic pass-through off. It does
// not clear $attrs; it only stops the automatic application.
defineOptions({ inheritAttrs: false })

const attrs = useAttrs()

// The collision case: the component sets its own class on the root,
// and the parent's class is in attrs. Concatenating both is the fix.
const rootClass = computed(() => [
  'card',
  attrs.class,                       // the parent's class, string or array
  { 'card--muted': attrs.disabled }
])
</script>
<template>
  <<!-- one root: the table wrapper. v-bind="$attrs" is deliberate, -->
  <<!-- not automatic, and it does not clobber rootClass.       -->
  <div v-bind="attrs" :class="rootClass" id="'account-panel'">
    <table>...</table>
  </div>
</template>

// Component resolution. Imported in script setup: available in the
// template with no registration, matched case-insensitively.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { shallowRef, markRaw, resolveComponent, resolveDynamicComponent } from 'vue'
import InvoiceTable from './InvoiceTable.vue'
import PaymentTable from './PaymentTable.vue'

// A registry keyed by a string is the maintainable form of a dynamic
// component. markRaw is required: without it the component object is
// made reactive and every instance is re-created on any change.
const registry = {
  invoice: markRaw(InvoiceTable),
  payment: markRaw(PaymentTable)
} as const

type Kind = keyof typeof registry
const kind = shallowRef<Kind>('invoice')
const current = computed(() => registry[kind.value])

// Resolving a name at runtime, for a component that is not imported.
// Resolution order: locally registered, then globally registered via
// app.component, then a development-mode unresolved-component
// warning. There is no filesystem lookup and no auto-import.
const Dynamic = resolveComponent('InvoiceTable')

// resolveDynamicComponent is what `<component :is="expr">` compiles to.
// It accepts an object, a string name, or a function, and returns the
// value unchanged when given an object. Calling it directly is only
// necessary outside a render function, for example inside a store action.
const AlsoDynamic = resolveDynamicComponent(() => import('./LegacyPanel.vue'))
</script>
<template>
  <<!-- :is accepts a component object, a string name, or an -->
  <<!-- async component factory. A string goes through the same  -->
  <<!-- resolution as resolveComponent.                      -->
  <component :is="current" :rows="rows" />
  <component :is="'InvoiceTable'" />  <!-- must be registered -->
  <component :is="Dynamic" />
  <KeepAlive>
    <component :is="current" />
  </KeepAlive>
</template>

A named slot with fallback content and a v- on a <template #default><template #default> are two different techniques for the same decision, and the second is the one to prefer when the condition depends on the child’s data. Conditional slot content declared by the parent is simply absent, and slots. is then falsy, which is what has above tests.

MechanismDirectionDeclared byCollides with
Slot contentParent markup into the childdefin, or nothingNothing; a named slot takes precedence over fallback
Fallthrough attributesParent attributes onto the child rootNobody; automatic for a single rootThe child’s own cl and st, which are merged not replaced
inheritAttrs: falseinheritAttrs: falseSuppresses the automatic pass-throughdefineNothing, but silently stops $attrs$attrs being applied
useAManual application, for split placementNothing; always availableDouble application if combined with automatic pass-through
Template refsParent reaches a child instance handledefine on the childNothing, but it inverts the data flow and should be rare

Caveats. Fallthrough attributes cannot be applied automatically to a component with more than one root node, and the compiler emits a development warning in that case. A multi-root component that genuinely needs to accept arbitrary attributes must set inheritAttrs: falseinheritAttrs: false and place v-bind="$attrs"v-bind="$attrs" on the one element that should receive them, accepting that every other root element silently discards them.

Caveats. Storing a component in a r without mar or shall makes the component object reactive. The proxy is not a valid component, and the symptom is a component that re-mounts from scratch on every unrelated state change rather than an error. The same applies to a component stored in a reac object, and to an object literal of components held in refref.

Applies to. defin is available from Vue 3.3 and is type-only. defin is stable from 3.4. resolveC and resolveDyna are runtime exports of the v package, not compiler macros, and are the programmatic forms of the <component :is><component :is> contract. A component used only in a template must be imported in <script setup><script setup>, registered with compo in the Options API, or installed globally with app.co; there is no automatic discovery.

Exercises 5

  1. Build a Use that receives a us object prop and mutates props.u from a text input. Record the resulting behaviour in development and in a production build, then replace the mutation with a ren emit and confirm the change.
  2. Write the same prop list four ways: type-only, withDe, runtime object, and hybrid. Set a default array with a literal on the runtime form, mount two instances, mutate the array in one, and record what the other sees.
  3. Declare an emits option that names cha, attach @change@change on a single-root component, and inspect the rendered element in DevTools. Remove the emits declaration and record how many times the handler fires on one click.
  4. Emit an event named close and listen with @close-drawer@close-drawer, then with @closeDrawer@closeDrawer in a single-file component template and in an in-DOM template. Record which forms survive each build mode.
  5. Declare two models with defineModel('title')defineModel('title') and defineModel('searchQuery')defineModel('searchQuery'), bind them with v-model:titlev-model:title and v-model:search-queryv-model:search-query, and confirm both round-trip. Then declare a duplicate update:titleupdate:title entry in defin and record the compiler diagnostic.
  6. Build a Pa with a default slot, a named hea slot with fallback content, and a scoped slot passing a coll flag. Then hold a component in a plain r and render it with <component :is><component :is>, mutating unrelated state, and record the remount behaviour before and after markRawmarkRaw.
  7. Create a two-root component, pass a cl and a data-*data-* attribute, and record the development warning. Add inheritAttrs: falseinheritAttrs: false and place v-bind="$attrs"v-bind="$attrs" on one root only, then confirm the attribute reaches that root.

6 · Vue Router

6.1 Installing the router and the isReady requirement6.2 Route records: path, name, component, props, children, meta6.3 router-link versus router.push, and the active-class contract6.4 Dynamic segments, useRoute, params, query and hash6.5 Navigation guards and the unsaved-changes prompt6.6 Lazy routes, scroll behaviour, catch-all and 404 handling

Vue Router is a separate package on the 4.x line and is the routing layer for every single-page application built on Vue 3. It owns a reactive current route, a matcher that maps paths to records, a history adapter that reads and writes the address bar, and a set of navigation guards that run around every resolution. The chapter covers the install sequence, the record shape, the two ways to navigate, the two ways to read a route, the guard taxonomy, and the production requirements that are easy to omit: code splitting, scroll restoration, a catch-all, and awaiting the first navigation before mount.

Installing the router and the isReady requirement

A router is created from a history implementation and a route table, then installed on the application with app.useapp.use. Installation registers the Route and Route components globally and injects the router, which is what makes useR and useR work in any descendant. The install sequence has one non-obvious requirement: the application must not be mounted until the router has resolved its initial navigation.

// src/router/index.ts -- the whole of the router definition.
import { createRouter, createWebHistory } from 'vue-router'

const router = createRouter({
  // 1. History adapter. Three implementations, differing only in how
  //    the URL is stored. HTML5 history is the default choice and
  //    requires exactly one server-side rule, given in 6.6.
  //    createWebHistory    /path, ?query, #hash  -- server rewrite
  //    createWebHashHistory /#/path             -- no server rule
  //    createMemoryHistory  no URL at all       -- SSR, tests
  history: createWebHistory(import.meta.env.BASE_URL),

  // 2. The route table. Discussed in 6.2.
  routes: [
    { path: '/', name: 'home', component: () => import('../views/HomeView.vue') },
    { path: '/invoices', name: 'invoices', component: () => import('../views/InvoicesView.vue') },
    { path: '/invoices/:id', name: 'invoice-detail', component: () => import('../views/InvoiceDetail.vue'), props: true },
    { path: /:pathMatch(.*)*', name: 'not-found', component: () => import('../views/NotFound.vue') }
  ],

  // 3. Global defaults, applied to every RouterLink that does not
  //    override them. The class names are the contract with CSS.
  linkActiveClass: 'router-link-active',
  linkExactActiveClass: 'router-link-exact-active',

  // 4. Scroll behaviour. Discussed in 6.6.
  scrollBehavior(to, from, savedPosition) {
    if (savedPosition) return savedPosition
    if (to.hash) return { el: to.hash, behavior: 'smooth' }
    return { top: 0 }
  }
})

export default router

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/main.ts -- the install sequence, and the requirement that is
// not optional.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
import { createApp } from 'vue'
import { createPinia } from 'pinia'
import App from './App.vue'
import router from './router'

const app = createApp(App)

app.use(createPinia())
app.use(router)   // registers RouterLink, RouterView and the injections

// Awaiting isReady() before mount() prevents the application from
// painting the wrong route first. The initial navigation is
// asynchronous: a lazy route component is a dynamic import, and
// RouterView has nothing to render until that import settles.
// Without this await, a deep link paints an empty shell, then
// the real view, and the scroll position is computed twice.
router.isReady().then(() => {
  app.mount('#app')
})

// A route-level failure inside the initial navigation rejects here.
// Catching it here is the only place that can show a real message;
// the catch-all route in 6.6 does not cover a rejected guard.
router.isReady().catch((err) => {
  console.error('initial navigation failed', err)
  app.mount('#app')
})

// The component that receives the resolved route. v-slot gives the
// route object when the transition needs it; Component and :key are
// what force a remount when the matched record changes.
// src/App.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
</script>
<template>
  <AppHeader />
  <main id="main">
    <RouterView v-slot="{ Component, route }">
      <component :is="Component" :key="route.path" />
    </RouterView>
  </main>
</template>

The :key="route.path":key="route.path" is not a requirement and is usually wrong. It forces a full remount on every navigation that changes the path, which discards local state, restarts in-flight requests and defeats transitions. The correct key is the matched route name when the intent is to force a remount between records, and no key at all when the intent is to reuse the instance across parameter changes.

History adapterURL shapeServer configurationUse when
createWe/invoices/42?tab=lines#top/invoices/42?tab=lines#topRewrite all unmatched paths to indexThe normal choice for a modern deployment
createWebH/#/invoices/42/#/invoices/42NoneA static host that cannot be configured, or an embedded document
createMemNoneNoneServer-side rendering and component tests

Caveats. With createWe, a direct request for a deep path such as /invoices/42/invoices/42 reaches the web server before the application loads. Without a rewrite rule the server returns 404 and no client-side router can help. With createWebH the path before the hash is always //, which is why that adapter works on an unconfigurable static host at the cost of an uglier URL and an analytics consequence: every page view is reported against a single path by tools that ignore the fragment.

Caveats. Calling app. before router.isReady()router.isReady() resolves is the single most common router defect, and the symptom is a blank flash rather than an error. The two isReady()isReady() calls in the listing are illustrative: in production code, call it once, attach a ca, and mount in both branches.

Applies to. This chapter describes the vue-r 4.x API as pinned in the version matrix. router.isReady()router.isReady(), the three history constructors, the me field and the guard signatures are unchanged across the 4.x line. Vue Router 5 is a separate major line with its own migration requirements and is outside the scope of this guide.

Route records: path, name, component, props, children, meta

A route is a plain object. The matcher builds a ranked tree from the array of records, and the six fields that matter in application code are pa, na, comp, pr, chil and metameta. Every other field in the type is a matching or lifecycle option that this guide does not use. The two structural decisions that cause the most rework later are the choice between a named route and a positional path, and the choice to nest children or to use a flat table with layout records.

// The complete record shape used in this guide.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import type { RouteRecordRaw } from 'vue-router'

const routes: RouteRecordRaw[] = [
  {
    // A layout record: a pathless parent that contributes a <RouterView>
    // and a name, and nothing else. Children are matched beneath it.
    path: '/admin',
    name: 'admin',
    component: () => import('../layouts/AdminLayout.vue'),
    // Guards declared here run only when LEAVING this record entirely,
    // and on entering it, not on every child navigation.
    beforeEnter: [requireAdmin],
    meta: {
      // meta is untyped by the framework. It is extended by the app
      // through declaration merging, which is what makes meta.title
      // a checked string rather than an any.
      requiresAuth: true,
      title: 'Administration'
    },
    children: [
      { path: '', name: 'admin-overview', component: () => import('../views/admin/Overview.vue') },
      { path: 'users', name: 'admin-users', component: () => import('../views/admin/Users.vue'), props: true },
      { path: 'users/:userId', name: 'admin-user',
        component: () => import('../views/admin/UserDetail.vue'),
        // The function form. Route params and query are merged and
        // passed as props, so the view declares no useRoute at all.
        props: (route) => ({ userId: route.params.userId, tab: route.query.tab ?? 'profile' }),
        meta: { title: 'User detail', requiresAuth: true }
      }
    ]
  }
]
</script>

// Reading a typed meta. Declaration merging is what turns `meta.title`
// from any into a string, and it is the only supported mechanism.
// src/router/meta.d.ts
import type { RouterMeta } from 'vue-router'

declare module 'vue-router' {
  interface RouteMeta {
    requiresAuth?: boolean
    title?: string
    hideInNav?: boolean
    breadcrumb?: 'Dashboard' | 'Settings'
  }
}

// Consuming meta, in the two places it is normally used.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { useRoute, watchEffect } from 'vue'

const route = useRoute()
watchEffect(() => {
  document.title = route.meta.title ? `${route.meta.title} -- Billing` : 'Billing'
})

// useRouter().currentRoute is the router-side spelling of the same
// reactive route. Prefer useRoute() inside a component: it is
// the same object, and it does not require holding a router.
</script>

// Programmatic route table construction. A static array is a literal;
// these three functions are for routes that depend on runtime data
// or that plugins add at runtime. addRoute returns the removal
// callback, which is how a dynamically installed plugin is
// uninstalled without leaving a dead record in the matcher.
import router from './router'

router.addRoute({ path: '/admin/audit', name: 'admin-audit', component: () => import('../views/admin/Audit.vue') })

const removeAudit = router.addRoute('admin', {
  path: 'audit', name: 'admin-audit-2', component: () => import('../views/admin/Audit.vue')
})
removeAudit()                    // removes the record; an in-flight route to it now 404s

if (router.hasRoute('admin-audit')) {
  router.removeRoute('admin-audit')
}

// A component definition may also be given as an object rather
// than a lazy function, which is what a plugin that ships several
// small routes usually does.
router.addRoute({
  path: '/status', name: 'status',
  component: { template: '<div>All systems nominal</div>' },
  meta: { title: 'Status', hideInNav: true }
})

Every record in this listing has a na, and that is a policy rather than a convention of taste. A named route is referenced by identity, so a path can be restructured without a search-and-replace across templates; a path reference is a string that must be edited everywhere at once, and a typo in one of them is a navigation to a route that does not exist rather than a type error.

FieldTypeEffectFailure when omitted or wrong
pastrThe URL pattern the matcher usesNo record matches; the catch-all handles it
nastrIdentity for router.push({ name })router.push({ name }) and for removPaths must be repeated literally; renames break silently
compComponent or lazy factoryWhat Route rendersA record with no component renders nothing and is only useful as a layout parent
prboo or functionPasses params and query to the view as propsThe view must call useR and re-derive everything
chilRouteRecordRaw[]RouteRecordRaw[]Nested records beneath this oneA flat table; layout components then repeat
meArbitrary, typed by declaration mergingCarried on the resolved route; not merged across the chainUntyped a access and no single place to declare the contract
beforGuard or array of guardsRuns when entering this recordA guard that also fires on unrelated child navigations

Caveats. me is not merged across the matched chain. route is a merge of the matched records with later records overriding earlier ones, but a parent that sets meta.requiresAuth = truemeta.requiresAuth = true and a child that sets meta. produce both; a child that sets meta.requiresAuth = falsemeta.requiresAuth = false overrides the parent for that route. A guard that reads to.meta.re therefore reads the deepest value, not the strictest, and negating it in one child silently exempts that child. Set the flag on every record that should require it, or use a guard that walks to.matchedto.matched.

Caveats. A child path must begin with a slash, and a child’s path: ''path: '' matches the parent path exactly rather than as a prefix. An empty child path therefore does not make every sibling a child of it: /admin/admin with a child at '''' matches /admin/admin and not /admin/anything/admin/anything, which is the behaviour a redirect default requires.

router-link versus router.push, and the active-class contract

There are two ways to navigate. route renders an anchor and delegates to the router on click, adding active classes and an aria-c attribute. route performs a navigation from code and returns a promise. The choice between them is not stylistic: only the link form is correct for a destination that a user should be able to open in a new tab, bookmark, or share, and only the programmatic form is available when there is no user gesture to attach to.

// (1) The declarative form. Renders an <a> with a real href, so it is
//     keyboard-reachable, middle-clickable, and crawlable.
<template>
  <<!-- by name: immune to a path restructure -->
  <RouterLink :to="{ name: 'invoice-detail', params: { id } }">
    Invoice {{ id }}
  </RouterLink>

  <<!-- by path, with a query object rather than a string -->
  <RouterLink :to="{ path: '/invoices', query: { tab: 'overdue', sort: 'due' } }">
    Overdue
  </RouterLink>

  <<!-- replace: the new entry replaces the current entry, so  -->
  <<!-- Back does not return to the page just left. Right  -->
  <<!-- for a filter that is not a destination.          -->
  <RouterLink :to="{ query: { tab } }" replace>Filter</RouterLink>

  <<!-- active-class / exact-active-class override the    -->
  <<!-- router-level defaults for this link only.        -->
  <RouterLink to="'/admin/users'" active-class="'is-open'" exact-active-class="'is-current'">
    Users
  </RouterLink>
</template>

// (2) The programmatic form, for navigation without a link.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { useRouter } from 'vue-router'

const router = useRouter()

// After a successful mutation, leave the page the user was on.
async function onSaved(id: string) {
  await api.invoices.create({ id })
  // The returned promise rejects on a navigation failure, including
  // a redundant navigation and an aborted one. Swallowing it silently
  // hides a real error, so the type is narrowed before use.
  const failure = await router.push({ name: 'invoice-detail', params: { id } }).catch(undefined)
  if (failure) {
    // failure is a NavigationFailure, not an Error.
    console.warn('navigation failed', failure.type, failure.from.fullPath)
  }
}

// The canonical redundant-navigation guard. Pushing the current
// location resolves as a failure, not as success, and an unhandled
// rejection in a click handler surfaces in the console as an error.
async function go(path: string) {
  if (router.currentRoute.value.fullPath === path) return
  await router.push(path).catch(undefined)
}
</script>

// (3) A link that is currently disabled. RouterLink has no disabled
//     prop in 4.x, and a click handler on the anchor is not a
//     substitute: the href remains, the element remains focusable, and
//     the link still opens in a new tab. The correct treatment is to
//     render a button, or to render nothing at all.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const props = defineProps<{
  to: RouteLocationRaw
  disabled?: boolean
  reason?: string
}>()
</script>
<template>
  <span v-if="disabled" class="'link link--disabled'"
        role="'link'" aria-disabled="'true'"
        :title="reason"
        tabindex="'-1'">
    <slot />
  </span>
  <RouterLink v-else :to="to" class="'link'">
    <slot />
  </RouterLink>
</template>

// (4) `custom` renders no anchor at all and exposes the resolved link
//     state through the default slot. This is the escape hatch for a
//     styled element that is still semantically a link, and for a
//     link that must navigate imperatively on its own trigger.
<template>
  <RouterLink v-slot="{ href, route, isActive, isExactActive, navigate }"
                custom :to="{ name: 'invoices' }">
    <a :href="href"
       :aria-current="isExactActive ? 'page' : undefined"
       :class="{ 'is-active': isActive, 'is-current': isExactActive }"
       @click="navigate">
      {{ route.name }}
    </a>
  </RouterLink>
</template>

The distinction that matters for accessibility is that aria-current="page"aria-current="page" is set automatically by Route when the link is exactly active, and active is a purely visual hook. A navigation that is indicated only by a background colour communicates nothing to a screen reader, and the aria-c attribute is the mechanism that does. A custom link must therefore set it explicitly, as the fourth listing does.

Class or attributeApplied whenSet byAccessibility role
router-liThe target and the current route share matched recordsRouteVisual only
router-link-The current path is exactly the targetRouteVisual only
aria-current="page"aria-current="page"Exactly activeRoute; a manual job under cusThe semantic equivalent, and the one that must be preserved
aria-disabled="true"aria-disabled="true"A disabled navigationThe applicationAnnounces unavailability; the element must also be removed from the tab order

Caveats. router-li is not an equality test. A link to /invoices/invoices is active while the application is on /invoices/42/invoices/42, because both resolve to records that share the /invoices/invoices prefix. A navigation menu that must highlight only the current page needs exact-act, and a menu that must highlight the section needs active-classactive-class. Using the wrong one is the usual cause of two items appearing selected.

Caveats. route and router. both return a promise that rejects on a failure, and a redundant navigation is a failure with type 16. A ca handler is therefore required at every call site that is not inside a router guard, or the rejection is reported as an unhandled promise rejection. In a guard, the equivalent is a return falsereturn false or a returned location, never an unhandled promise.

Dynamic segments, useRoute, params, query and hash

A path segment prefixed with a colon is a dynamic segment. A segment suffixed with ?? is optional, and one suffixed with ++ is repeatable. The resolved route exposes four distinct sources of input, and confusing them is a frequent source of state that does not update on navigation: par are part of the path identity, qu and ha are not.

// The path grammar, in one table.
const routes = [
  { path: '/users',                    name: 'users' },
  { path: '/users/:id',               name: 'user' },        // required segment
  { path: '/users/:id?',              name: 'user-optional' },// optional segment
  { path: '/users/:id/orders/:orderId', name: 'user-order' },// two segments
  { path: '/files/:pathMatch(.*)*',    name: 'files' },       // repeatable, zero or more
  { path: '/chapters/:parts+',        name: 'chapters' },     // repeatable, one or more
  { path: '/:locale?',                name: 'root' }          // only valid as a tail
]

// Naming a dynamic segment with a regex constrains it. A named
// capture group inside the regex is exposed under its group
// name, which is how a single segment yields two values.
{ path: '/invoices/:id(\\d+)', name: 'invoice' }  // digits only
{ path: '/i/:date(\\d{4}-\\d{2}-\\d{2})', name: 'invoice-date' }

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// Reading the route. useRoute() returns a reactive route object.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { computed, watch } from 'vue'
import { useRoute } from 'vue-router'

const route = useRoute()

// Every field is a string or an array of strings. There is no parsing
// and no coercion, which is why a computed is the right tool.
const id     = computed(() => route.params.id)              // string | string[]
const tab    = computed(() => (route.query.tab as string) ?? 'lines')
const page   = computed(() => Number(route.query.page ?? '1'))
const anchor = computed(() => route.hash)                 // '#line-42'
const flags  = computed(() => route.query.flags)           // string[] for ?a&flags=x&flags=y

// The object identity of the route changes on every navigation,
// including a navigation that changed only the query. Watching
// the whole object therefore refires on any URL change at all.
// watch(() => route.query, cb, { deep: true })   // narrower
watch(() => route.query.tab, (next, prev) => {
  // A guard for the case where the change came from a
  // back/forward navigation and is not ours to accept.
  if (next === prev) return
  load(next)
})
</script>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// `props: true` versus useRoute(). The same information, two
// placements, and the choice is a real one.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------

// A. With props: true the view receives them as props. It has no
//    import of the router, so it can be mounted in a test with a
//    plain object, and it can be reused under a different route.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const props = defineProps<{
  id: string
  tab?: string
}>()

// Note: this view no longer knows that a URL exists. It cannot
// build a link to a sibling record, which is the real cost.
watchEffect(() => load(props.id))
</script>

// B. With useRoute() the view can both read and write the route,
//    which is what a view that also builds its own links needs.
<script setup lang>
const route = useRoute()
const router = useRouter()

// Writing to the query is a navigation. It is not a state update:
// it pushes a history entry by default and therefore produces a
// Back entry for every keystroke of a filter.
function setTab(tab: string, { replace = true } = {}) {
  router[replace ? 'replace' : 'push']({
    name: route.name as string,
    params: route.params,
    query: { ...route.query, tab }
  })
}
</script>

// The distinction that catches people: params are part of the route
// identity and cannot be changed without a new route record; query
// and hash can. Two visits to /users/1?tab=a and /users/1?tab=b are
// the same component instance with a changed route object, and no
// remount occurs unless a key is placed on the component.

A repeatable segment is exposed as an array and never as a joined string: :parts+:parts+ matching /chapters/a/b/c/chapters/a/b/c yields ['a', 'b', 'c']['a', 'b', 'c']. A repeatable segment with ** may be empty, in which case the value is an empty array. A dynamic segment always yields a string, including a numeric-looking one, and the conversion belongs in a comp where it can be observed rather than in the template where it will be recomputed on every render.

FieldTypePart of route identitySurvives a back navigationReactive
parstr or string[]string[]YesYesYes
quLocati: string or array or nullNoYesYes
hastr, empty when absentNoYesYes
pastr, the resolved full pathYesYesYes
fullstr, path plus query plus hashYesYesYes
nastr or sym or undeYesYesYes
matRouteRecordNormalized[]RouteRecordNormalized[], outermost firstYesYesYes

Caveats. An optional dynamic segment is valid only as the last segment of a path. /users/:id?/orders/users/:id?/orders is rejected at route-table construction with an explicit error, because the matcher cannot decide whether the segment was present. A param that is absent is unde on the resolved route, and with props: trueprops: true the prop is unde rather than an empty string, so a required prop declared on the view will produce a development warning for a path the record itself allows.

Caveats. Query values are untyped strings. A query is part of the URL and therefore part of the bookmarkable, shareable, user-editable contract of the page, so a value from route is untrusted input. Passing it straight to a typed API call is the mechanism behind injection bugs that survive a purely client-side type checker.

Navigation guards and the unsaved-changes prompt

A navigation guard is a function that runs around a route resolution and may allow, block or redirect it. There are three registration points and one in-component hook, and the order in which they run is fixed. The two calling conventions, the legacy ne form and the return-value form, are both supported in 4.x; the return-value form is correct in new code because it cannot leak a ne call that never happens.

// The full order for a single navigation, and which registration point
// each line belongs to. Only navigation guards are listed; component
// lifecycle hooks and per-route beforeEnter are shown separately.
//
//  1. beforeEach        (global, registered on the router)
//  2. beforeResolve     (global, registered on the router)
//  3. beforeEnter       (per-record, declared in the route table)
//  4.   ... the async components of the matched records resolve ...
//  5. beforeRouteUpdate  (in-component, only on update, not on enter)
//  6. beforeRouteEnter  (in-component, only on enter)
//  7. the navigation is confirmed and the URL changes
//  8. afterEach         (global, registered on the router)
//  9.   ... the new components mount and their DOM updates ...
// 10. afterEnter / next  (in-component)
// 11. scrollBehavior is applied

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// Global guards, registered once in the router module.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
router.beforeEach((to, from) => {
  // RETURN-VALUE FORM. Three legal returns:
  //   true / undefined  -- allow
  //   false             -- abort, URL untouched
  //   a location        -- redirect, the navigation is retried
  const needsAuth = to.matched.some(r => r.meta.requiresAuth)
  if (!needsAuth) return true

  if (auth.isAuthenticated) return true

  // Preserve the destination so the login page can return to it.
  // A redirect to the same path would loop; the guard cannot detect
  // that for you.
  return { name: 'login', query: { redirect: to.fullPath } }
})

router.afterEach((to, from, failure) => {
