  // `failure` is non-null for an aborted or redirected navigation.
  // Analytics must not record a page view that did not happen.
  if (failure) return
  analytics.page(to.fullPath)
})

router.onError((error, to) => {
  // Errors thrown by an async component loader or by a guard.
  console.error('navigation error', to.fullPath, error)
})

// beforeResolve is the last global gate before the async components
// are resolved, which makes it the right place for a check that
// needs the resolved data of the target route.
router.beforeResolve((to) => {
  if (to.meta.title && !document.title.startsWith(to.meta.title)) {
    // no-op branch kept for shape
  }
  return true
})

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// The next() form, and why new code should not use it.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
router.beforeEach((to, from, next) => {
  if (auth.isAuthenticated) { next() } else { next('/login') }
  // Two obligations: exactly one next() call, and no fall-through.
  // Calling next() twice resolves the navigation twice. Forgetting
  // it leaves the navigation pending forever and the URL frozen.
  // An async guard that forgets next() and uses await instead hangs
  // in the same way. Return values cannot be got wrong this way.
})

// An async guard. The promise IS the decision; no next() is used and
// the router waits. An uncaught rejection aborts the navigation.
router.beforeEach(async (to) => {
  await i18n.loadLocale((to.meta.locale as string) ?? 'en')
  // a second way to express a redirect from an async guard
  if (!i18n.supported(i18n.locale)) return { name: 'not-found' }
})

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// Per-record beforeEnter, and in-component guards.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
{
  path: '/billing/:id',
  name: 'billing',
  component: () => import('../views/Billing.vue'),
  // Runs when ENTERING this record only. Navigating between two
  // of its own children does not re-run it, which is the whole
  // reason to prefer it over a global guard with a name check.
  beforeEnter: [
    async (to) => {
      const account = await api.accounts.get(to.params.id as string)
      if (account.status === 'archived') return { name: 'account-archived', params: { id: to.params.id } }
      return true
    }
  ]
}

// In-component guards. useRoute/useRouter are not available inside
// onBeforeRouteLeave, so the router instance must be imported.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { onBeforeRouteLeave, onBeforeRouteUpdate } from 'vue-router'
import { ref, watch } from 'vue'

const form = ref({ name: '', email: '' })
const saved = ref({ name: '', email: '' })

// Fires only on UPDATE: same record, different params. Without this,
// navigating /users/1 to /users/2 reuses the instance and the form
// keeps showing user 1's values.
onBeforeRouteUpdate(async (to, from) => {
  if (isDirty() && !(await confirmDiscard())) return false
  await loadUser(to.params.id as string)
  saved.value = { ...form.value }
  // Returning true is implicit; the navigation continues.
})

// The unsaved-changes prompt. Fires on LEAVE, in either direction,
// and also on a router.push, unlike an onBeforeUnmount check.
onBeforeRouteLeave(async () => {
  if (!isDirty()) return true
  return await confirmDiscard()
})

function isDirty() {
  return form.value.name !== saved.value.name || form.value.email !== saved.value.email
}

// The prompt itself. window.confirm is synchronous and cannot be
// cancelled by the browser's own chrome; a bespoke dialog must be
// awaited and must resolve to a boolean, because a guard that
// returns undefined ALLOWS the navigation.
function confirmDiscard(): Promise<boolean> {
  return window.confirm('Discard unsaved changes?')
}
</script>

The single most important line in this section is the implicit one: a guard that returns nothing allows the navigation. An i branch with no ret therefore permits the transition, so a guard written to block one case and forget the allow case blocks nothing. Write the allow explicitly.

RegistrationScopeFires onHas access toTypical use
router.bGlobalEvery navigationOnly t and frAuthentication
router.befGlobalEvery navigation, last global stept, frChecks that need resolved data
router.aGlobalEvery navigation, after confirmationA fai argumentAnalytics, logging
router.GlobalA thrown error in a guard or loaderThe error and the targetGlobal error reporting
beforOne recordEntering that recordOnly tPer-route preconditions
onBeforeROne componentSame record, different paramsThe router, t, frReload on a param change
onBeforeROne componentLeaving the componentThe routerUnsaved-changes prompt

Caveats. A befor guard does not run when navigating between the children of its own record, and a beforeRo guard does not run when the record itself changes. Each covers one of the two cases; a precondition that must hold for every visit needs both, or a global guard keyed on a meta flag.

Caveats. An in-component guard is registered against the component instance and is removed on unmount, so it does not fire for a navigation that unmounts the component through a parent transition rather than through the router. It also does not fire on a browser-level page unload; that requires a before listener, which is a browser behaviour with its own restrictions and which must be added and removed inside the scope as described in section 4.6.

Prefer redirecting over blocking. A guard that returns a location gives the user a way forward; a guard that returns fa on an unauthenticated navigation to a protected route strands them on the page they came from with no explanation. Send them to a login route with the destination preserved, which is what the first listing does.

Lazy routes, scroll behaviour, catch-all and 404 handling

Four production concerns close the chapter. Every route component above is declared as an arrow function returning a dynamic import, which is what makes the bundler emit one chunk per route. Scroll restoration is a function of the history implementation, not of the router. A catch-all record is what turns an unmatched path into a rendered component rather than a blank shell. And the first navigation must be awaited, which is stated in 6.1 and repeated here because it is the requirement most often missed.

// (1) Code splitting. The component field is a function returning a
//     promise of a module. The bundler emits one chunk per record and
//     the router awaits it before the navigation is confirmed.
const routes = [
  { path: '/', name: 'home', component: () => import('../views/HomeView.vue') },
  { path: '/invoices', name: 'invoices', component: () => import('../views/InvoicesView.vue') },
  { path: '/reports', name: 'reports', component: () => import('../views/ReportsView.vue') }
]

// Naming the chunk makes the output readable in the build report and
// makes the mapping from record to chunk explicit.
{
  path: '/reports', name: 'reports',
  component: () => import(
    /* webpackChunkName */ '../views/ReportsView.vue'
  )
}

// A second function, not a second route: an async component that is
// reused across several routes. defineAsyncComponent adds a loading
// state and an error state, which a bare import() does not.
import { defineAsyncComponent } from 'vue'

const Chart = defineAsyncComponent({
  loader: () => import('../components/HeavyChart.vue'),
  loadingComponent: SkeletonChart,
  errorComponent: ChartError,
  delay: 200,        // do not flash the loader for a fast chunk
  timeout: 10000       // a chunk that never arrives must not hang
})

// (2) Scroll behaviour. The signature returns a position, a
//     selector to scroll to, or a promise of either.
scrollBehavior(to, from, savedPosition) {
  // A back or forward navigation restores the position the
  // browser recorded. This branch takes priority over all others.
  if (savedPosition) return savedPosition

  // An in-page anchor. `el` accepts a selector, and Vue Router
  // retries the query on the next tick when the element is
  // rendered by a component that mounts after the navigation.
  if (to.hash) return { el: to.hash, behavior: 'smooth', top: 88 }

  // Returning a selector for an element rendered by the view is
  // more reliable than scrolling in the view's own code, because
  // it runs after the DOM update.
  if (to.name === 'invoices') return { el: '#invoice-list', top: 88 }

  // A returned position scrolls a bare element or the window.
  if (to.name === 'invoice-detail') return { top: 0 }

  // Anything else: the top of the page. Omitting a return here
  // leaves the scroll offset wherever the previous page left it.
  return { top: 0 }
}

// scrollBehavior runs after the DOM update, so measuring here is
// valid. Returning a number scrolls the window; returning an
// element scrolls that element's scroll container.

// (3) The catch-all record. It must be LAST: the matcher ranks
//     static segments above dynamic ones and catch-alls lowest, but
//     the array order is what the ranking falls back to.
{
  path: '/:pathMatch(.*)*',          // 4.x form, matches any depth
  name: 'not-found',
  component: () => import('../views/NotFound.vue'),
  // hideInNav keeps the record out of a navigation that is
  // generated from the route table itself.
  meta: { hideInNav: true, title: 'Not found' }
}

// The 4.1 and earlier form was '/:pathMatch(.*)*' with a single
// star group. The repeatable-parameter form above is the 4.x
// spelling and also matches a path with no segments at all.

// (4) 404 handling, in three layers. A catch-all record covers an
//     unmatched path inside the application. The other two cases are
//     outside the router and must be handled separately.

//     (a) An unmatched path: the catch-all renders. Its status code is
//     still 200, which is correct for a client-rendered application and
//     is what search engines will index unless a prerender step
//     emits a real status.
<script setup lang='ts'>
<script setup>
<template>
  <section class="not-found">
    <h1>404</h1>
    <p>No page matches {{ $route.fullPath }}

A catch-all record is not the same thing as a server 404. The record turns an unmatched path into a rendered component, which is what the user needs; it does not change the status code, which is what a crawler and a monitoring system need. A single-page application cannot produce a 404 for a route it serves, so any requirement for a real status code is a server requirement and must be met there or by prerendering.

CaseWhere it is handledMechanismStatus code
Unmatched path inside the appRouterCatch-all record matched by the router200200 from the server
Matched route, missing resourceViewThe data layer reports a not-found error; the view renders a state200200
Request the server cannot routeServerRewrite rule to index, or a server 404 pageWhatever the server returns
Chunk load failure after deployRouterrouter.; force a reload because the old asset hashes are gone200200

Caveats. A dynamic import that fails because the deployment replaced the assets while the tab was open produces a rejected navigation and a blank view that no amount of retrying will fix, because the chunk filename no longer exists. The recovery is a hard reload. router. plus a location.reload()location.reload() is the standard handling, and it must not retry the import, which would loop.

Caveats. A scrollB that returns { el: to.hash }{ el: to.hash } for a hash pointing into a lazily loaded component can fail to find the element on the first attempt. Vue Router retries once after the next tick, which covers a component that mounts synchronously, and does not cover an element that only appears after a data fetch. Scrolling to a target that the view has not rendered is a data-layer concern; the view should scroll, not the router.

Requirements. Every production deployment of a createWe application needs exactly one server behaviour: any path that is not a real file and does not carry a file extension is answered with index.htmlindex.html. Everything else in this section assumes that rule is in place. A single-page application that ships without it works on every client-side navigation and fails on every bookmark, every shared link and every refresh.

Exercises 6

  1. Scaffold a router with a deep route, comment out the isReady()isReady() await in mai, and hard-reload the deep URL. Record what is painted and for how long, then restore the await and repeat.
  2. Declare the same record with props: trueprops: true and again with useRoute()useRoute(). Write a unit test for the view in each form and record how the mount differs. Then add a link to a sibling record to the props form and record what the view now needs.
  3. Add a befor that returns a redirect for unauthenticated navigation, and a befor on one record that performs the same check. Navigate between two children of that record and record how many times each guard runs.
  4. Write a guard with a ne parameter, return from only one branch, and record the behaviour. Then add a second next()next() call in the same guard and record the diagnostic.
  5. Build a form with an onBeforeR prompt. Navigate away with the form dirty, then perform a browser reload with the form dirty, and record which of the two the guard catches.
  6. Add a catch-all record, then a scrollB returning a saved position, a hash, and a plain top offset. Record which branch applies on a forward navigation, a back navigation, and a click on an in-page anchor.
  7. Deploy a build to a static host with no rewrite rule and request a deep path directly. Record the response, then add the rule and repeat.

7 · Pinia State Management

7.1 When state belongs in a store rather than a composable or provide7.2 defineStore: the options style and the setup style7.3 State, getters and actions7.4 storeToRefs, direct destructuring, $reset and $patch7.5 Multiple stores, cross-store use and stores outside components7.6 Plugins, $subscribe and $onAction

Pinia is the official store for Vue 3. A store is a reactive object created once per application and addressed by an identifier, together with the mutations and derived values that belong to it. The library’s contribution over a hand-rolled module of refrefs is typing, the devtools integration, a plugin surface, and a set of subscription hooks; its cost is a dependency and a set of rules about how state may be read. This chapter covers when a store is the right container, the two definition styles, the three member kinds, the destructuring rules, and the extension points.

When state belongs in a store rather than a composable or provide

Four mechanisms can hold state shared by more than one component: component-local state, pro and inj, a module-scope composable, and a store. They are not interchangeable and the differences are structural rather than stylistic. The decision is made from three questions: how many components read it, does it outlive the component that created it, and does anything outside a component need to reach it.

// (1) Component-local. The default, and correct more often than not.
//     Two instances of the same component have two independent states.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const open = ref(false)   // disposed with the component, section 4.5
</script>

// (2) provide / inject. A typed, immutable HANDOVER down a subtree.
//     The provider owns the ref; the consumer must not reassign it.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, provide, inject, type InjectionKey } from 'vue'

interface ThemeContext {
  theme: Ref<'light' | 'dark'>
  setTheme: (t: 'light' | 'dark') => void
}

export const ThemeKey: InjectionKey<ThemeContext> = Symbol('theme')

const theme = ref<'light' | 'dark'>('light')
function setTheme(t: 'light' | 'dark') { theme.value = t }
provide(ThemeKey, { theme, setTheme })

// A descendant. Note the required-key form: a missing provider is a
// developer error and should throw rather than yield undefined.
const { theme, setTheme } = inject(ThemeKey)   // typed, non-null
</script>

// (3) A module-scope composable. A deliberate singleton with a real
//     module-level ref. Nothing disposes it, which is exactly why it
//     is a decision rather than an accident (section 4.5).
// src/composables/useSession.ts
import { ref, computed } from 'vue'

const token = ref<string | null>(null)
const isAuthed = computed(() => token.value !== null)

export function useSession() {
  return { token, isAuthed, signIn, signOut }
}
function signIn(t: string) { token.value = t }
function signOut() { token.value = null }

// (4) A Pinia store. The differences from (3) that matter:
//     typed to $state / $getters, disposable, in the devtools,
//     and with no module-scope state to reason about.
// src/stores/session.ts
import { defineStore } from 'pinia'
import { ref, computed } from 'vue'

export const useSessionStore = defineStore('session', () => {
  const token = ref<string | null>(null)
  const isAuthed = computed(() => token.value !== null)
  function signIn(t: string) { token.value = t }
  function signOut() { token.value = null }
  return { token, isAuthed, signIn, signOut }
})
</script>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// The decision, stated as a rule with an order of preference.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
//
// 1. Read by exactly one component -> component state. Nothing else is
//    warranted. A store for a single reader is indirection with no gain.
//
// 2. Read by a subtree, and the provider stays mounted for the life of
//    that subtree, and nothing outside needs it -> provide/inject. It
//    needs no dependency and it is scoped to exactly the right tree.
//
// 3. Read across unrelated subtrees, or must survive every component
//    that reads it, or must be reachable from a router guard, an axios
//    interceptor, a test helper or a non-component module
//    -> a store. A store is the only one of the four with a documented
//    lifecycle and an external API surface.
//
// The decisive question is the third: does anything outside a
// component need to read or write it? If yes, it is a store.

pro and a store are not in competition for the same problem. A store holds application state; a context provides a capability. A theme object with a setT function is a capability and belongs in a context. A cart is data several unrelated parts of the tree read, and belongs in a store. The strongest signal is a provider that has to be threaded through a layout chain only to reach a consumer two levels away, which is a store written with extra ceremony.

AspectComponent statepro / injModule-scope composablePinia store
Number of readers1One subtreeAnyAny
Scoped toThe instanceThe provider’s subtreeThe moduleThe application
Automatic disposalYes, on unmountYes, with the providerNoNo, and $dispose$dispose exists
Reachable outside a componentNoNo; inj needs an instanceYesYes
Devtools timelineComponent tree onlyComponent tree onlyNot registeredYes, with mutation log
Plugin surfaceNoneNoneNonepini
Test isolationPer mountPer mountManual reset requiredFresh pinia per test via createTes
Server-side renderingPer request, by constructionPer requestLeaks across requestsPer request via creat

Caveats. A module-scope composable with a module-scope r is a global singleton on a server. In server-side rendering every request shares it, so one user’s session is visible to the next. This is the single most serious defect in the list: it is invisible in development, where one request at a time hides the bug, and it is a data leak in production. A store avoids it only because a new pi instance is created per request, and only if that instance is created inside the request handler rather than at module scope.

Applies to. The Inject pattern used above is the typed form of pro and has been available since Vue 3.0. A setup store is a store whose definition is a function; see 7.2 for the difference in behaviour rather than only in style. The two are interchangeable within a project and are routinely mixed across a codebase.

defineStore: the options style and the setup style

A store is declared with defin, which takes an identifier and a definition. The definition is either an options object with st, get and act members, or a setup function that returns the same three kinds of thing. The two styles compile to nearly the same runtime, and the differences that matter are where th is available, what a getter may return, and whether the store can be reset.

// Installation. createPinia is created once and installed before any
// store is used. Calling a store outside an active pinia throws.
// src/main.ts
import { createApp } from 'vue'
import { createPinia } from 'pinia'
import App from './App.vue'

const app = createApp(App)
app.use(createPinia())   // must precede app.mount and any store call
app.mount('#app')

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (1) THE OPTIONS STYLE. Object members; `this` is the store.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/stores/cart.ts
import { defineStore } from 'pinia'

interface CartState {
  items: CartLine[]
  coupon: null | { code: string; percentOff: number }
  loading: boolean
}

export const useCartStore = defineStore('cart', {
  // state must be a function. An object literal is shared by every
  // instance of the store, which is the same defect as a literal
  // prop default (section 1.5).
  state: (): CartState => ({
    items: [],
    coupon: null,
    loading: false
  }),

  // Getters receive the state and every other getter as arguments
  // and are cached. They must be pure: no fetch, no writes.
  getters: {
    count: (state): number => state.items.length,
    subtotal: (state): number =>
      state.items.reduce((sum, i) => sum + i.price * i.qty, 0),
    hasItems(): boolean { return this.count > 0 },
    // A getter that needs another getter receives it as a second
    // argument, and using `this` instead works only in a
    // function shorthand, not in an arrow property.
    total(): number {
      const discounted = this.subtotal * (1 - (this.coupon?.percentOff ?? 0) / 100)
      return Math.round(discounted * 100) / 100
    }
  },

  // Actions are the only place state may be written. They may be
  // asynchronous, they may call other actions with `this`, and they
  // may be invoked from anywhere including outside a component.
  actions: {
    add(line: CartLine) {
      const existing = this.items.find(i => i.sku === line.sku)
      if (existing) existing.qty += line.qty
      else this.items.push({ ...line })
    },
    async checkout() {
      this.loading = true
      try {
        const order = await api.orders.create({ lines: this.items })
        this.items = []
        this.coupon = null
        return order
      } finally {
        this.loading = false
      }
    }
  }
})

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (2) THE SETUP STYLE. A function returning refs, computeds and
//     functions. No `this`; the closure is the scope.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/stores/cart.ts, setup form
import { defineStore } from 'pinia'
import { ref, computed } from 'vue'

export const useCartStore = defineStore('cart', () => {
  // Every returned ref becomes a state property.
  const items = ref<CartLine[]>([])
  const coupon = ref<null | { code: string; percentOff: number }>(null)
  const loading = ref(false)

  // Every returned computed becomes a getter. It is unwrapped on the
  // store instance, so a caller writes store.total, never .value.
  const count = computed(() => items.value.length)
  const subtotal = computed(() =>
    items.value.reduce((sum, i) => sum + i.price * i.qty, 0))
  const total = computed(() => {
    const discounted = subtotal.value * (1 - (coupon.value?.percentOff ?? 0) / 100)
    return Math.round(discounted * 100) / 100
  })

  // Every returned function becomes an action. Writing items.value
  // here is the same as writing this.items in the options style.
  function add(line: CartLine) {
    const existing = items.value.find(i => i.sku === line.sku)
    if (existing) existing.qty += line.qty
    else items.value.push({ ...line })
  }

  async function checkout() {
    loading.value = true
    try {
      const order = await api.orders.create({ lines: items.value })
      items.value = []
      coupon.value = null
      return order
    } finally {
      loading.value = false
    }
  }

  // EVERYTHING reachable from the returned refs must also be
  // returned, or it is private and SSR cannot serialise it.
  return { items, coupon, loading, count, subtotal, total, add, checkout }
})

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (3) Calling a store. The identifier is the same; only the shape of
//     the access differs.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/components/CartBadge.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { storeToRefs } from 'pinia'
import { useCartStore } from '../stores/cart'

// Options style: this.isAuthed is a getter property, not a function.
// Setup style: this.isAuthed is a computed, unwrapped on the instance.
// The two are indistinguishable from outside. That is deliberate.
const cart = useCartStore()

// Reading a getter, an action and a state property.
const n = cart.count                 // getter: a value, never cart.count()
cart.add({ sku: 'A1', price: 1200, qty: 2 })   // action: a call
const due = cart.total

// The store instance is reactive. In a template, no unwrapping
// write-up is needed for state or getters.
</script>
<template>
  <p>{{ cart.count }} items, {{ cart.total }} due</p>
  <button @click="cart.add({ sku: 'A1', price: 1200, qty: 1 })">Add</button>
</template>

// storeToRefs preserves reactivity for state and getters. See 7.4.
const { count, total, items } = storeToRefs(cart)
const { add, checkout } = cart   // actions: plain functions, safe

The single most consequential difference is that a setup store has no $reset$reset. There is no declared initial state for Pinia to restore, so a store defined as a function must implement its own reset if it needs one. A store defined with a st function gets $reset$reset for free, which is a real reason to prefer the options style in a codebase that resets state between tests or after a logout.

AspectOptions styleSetup style
DefinitionObject with st, get, actFunction returning refs, computeds and functions
Access to other membersth, available in getters and actionsClosure variables; no th
Private membersNot expressible; everything in the object is publicAny variable not returned is private
$reset$resetProvided, restores the st function’s resultDoes not exist; implement it and return it
Declare a non-serialisable fieldDifficult; everything in st is stateTrivial: do not return it
Get a typed thAutomaticNot applicable
Type inference through the helperGood, when the state type is annotatedGood, and usually simpler
Server-side serialisationSerialises the whole state objectSerialises the returned refs and computeds

Caveats. In a setup store, a r that is not returned is private, and a r that is returned is state. A plain variable holding a derived value is not returned and is not a getter, so a caller cannot read it; a comp that is not returned is also unreadable, and the computation is never subscribed. Returning a value that is neither a ref nor a function is a common mistake: it appears on the store as a plain property, is not reactive, and is a snapshot taken at store creation.

Caveats. A getter in the options style that uses th must be written as a method shorthand. Written as an arrow property, th is the enclosing module scope, and the getter silently reads an undefined property rather than throwing. Where two getters are involved, prefer the explicit argument form: total: (state, getters) => ...total: (state, getters) => ... is unambiguous in both notations.

Pick one style per project and hold the line. Mixing them inside one store is where the $reset$reset and private-member differences become confusing, because a reader cannot tell which convention a given store follows. The migration cost of a consistent codebase is one pass; the cost of a mixed one is a permanent review question.

State, getters and actions

A store has three kinds of member and each has a rule that is enforced by nothing but convention. State is writable and holds the truth. Getters are read-only, cached derivations. Actions are the only place a write may happen, and they may be asynchronous. The rules exist because the alternatives produce a store that cannot be reasoned about: a getter that fetches cannot be cached, and a state property that is written from a template cannot be reverted.

// The three member kinds, and the properties that follow from each.
export const useReportStore = defineStore('report', () => {
  // ---- STATE ----------------------------------------------------------
  // Writable. A store is a reactive object, so a nested mutation is
  // tracked exactly as deeply as `ref` is. Keep state denormalised
  // unless the duplication is a deliberate cache.
  const rows = ref<ReportRow[]>([])
  const filters = reactive({ status: 'open' as Status, from: null as string | null })
  const page = ref(1)
  const status = ref<'idle' | 'loading' | 'error' | 'ready'>('idle')
  const error = shallowRef<Error | null>(null)

  // ---- GETTERS --------------------------------------------------------
  // Pure, cached, no side effects. Each is recomputed only when a
  // reactive value it read has changed, and the result is shared
  // by every reader until then.
  const visible = computed(() =>
    rows.value.filter(r => filters.status === 'all' || r.status === filters.status))

  const pageCount = computed(() => Math.max(1, Math.ceil(rows.value.length / 50)))
  const openCount = computed(() => rows.value.filter(r => r.status === 'open').length)
  const hasNext = computed(() => page.value < pageCount.value)
  const isEmpty = computed(() => status.value === 'ready' && visible.value.length === 0)

  // A getter may return a function. The returned function is invoked
  // on every call and is NOT itself cached, which makes this the
  // right shape for a lookup that takes an argument.
  const byId = computed(() => {
    const index = new Map(rows.value.map(r => [r.id, r]))
    return (id: string) => index.get(id)
  })

  // ---- ACTIONS --------------------------------------------------------
  // The only permitted writes. Anything that is not a pure derivation
  // and is not state belongs here, including every async operation.
  async function load(pageNumber = 1) {
    status.value = 'loading'
    error.value = null
    try {
      const result = await api.reports.page({ page: pageNumber, ...filters })
      rows.value = result.rows
      page.value = pageNumber
      status.value = 'ready'
    } catch (e) {
      const err = e as ApiError
      if (err.status === 401) { status.value = 'error'; error.value = err; return }
      // A 4xx that is not an auth problem is an empty result, not an
      // error state. Distinguishing them here avoids a red banner
      // over a legitimately empty report.
      rows.value = []
      status.value = 'ready'
    }
  }

  function setStatus(next: Status) {
    filters.status = next
    // A filter change invalidates the page, not just the filter.
    void load(1)
  }

  async function archive(id: string) {
    // The write is optimistic; the rollback is in the catch. See 9.6
    // for the full pattern and for why the snapshot must be taken
    // before the mutation rather than reconstructed from it.
    const previous = await byId.value(id)
    if (previous) previous.status = 'archived'
    try {
      await api.reports.archive(id)
    } catch (e) {
      if (previous) previous.status = 'open'
      throw e
    }
  }

  return { rows, filters, page, status, error, visible, pageCount, openCount, hasNext, isEmpty, byId, load, setStatus, archive }
})

// Concurrency inside an action. Two overlapping loads race, and the
// slower one wins. A sequence token or an AbortController makes
// the last request win, which is the behaviour a user expects
// from a filter that is still changing.
let seq = 0
async function loadGuarded(pageNumber = 1) {
  const mine = ++seq
  status.value = 'loading'
  try {
    const result = await api.reports.page({ page: pageNumber, ...filters })
    if (mine !== seq) return          // a newer request superseded this one
    rows.value = result.rows
    status.value = 'ready'
  } catch (e) {
    if (mine !== seq) return
    error.value = e as ApiError
    status.value = 'error'
  }
}

// A state field that is not plain data. markRaw keeps the object out
// of the reactivity system; storing a Map, a Date or a class instance
// without it wraps the object in a Proxy and breaks identity
// comparisons inside the library it came from.
const chart = shallowRef<Chart>(null)          // shallowRef, not ref
// For a Map that must be reactive state, replace it with a plain
// object or an array, or hold it in a markRaw box and manage
// the invalidation yourself.

Whether a getter may be async is a question with a precise answer: it may not, in a way that a template can consume. An async getter returns a promise on every access, and a template renders the promise rather than the value. This is why the loading flag in the store is state rather than a getter, and why the pattern in chapter 9 returns a reactive wrapper with explicit da, er and pen members rather than an awaited derivation.

Member kindMay write stateMay be asyncMay take argumentsCachedReaches the devtools timeline
StateNo, except in an actionNoNoNo; it is the sourceYes, as a state change
GetterNoNoNo; return a function insteadYesNo
ActionYesYesYesNo; every call runsYes, as a labelled call

Caveats. A getter that is written as a side-effecting function is the most damaging mistake available in a store, because it looks correct. A getter that calls an API, writes to state or mutates a value it also reads will re-trigger itself, and Vue’s development build will report a recursive update; a getter that only performs a side effect will run once per invalidation and appear to work while duplicating requests. Getters are derivations and nothing else.

Caveats. Actions are not transactional. Two actions that read state, await, and then write can interleave, and the second writer overwrites the first. The sequence-token pattern above is the minimum defence; a pen guard on a button is a user-interface defence, not a correctness one, because the action remains callable from anywhere.

Applies to. shall and mar are Vue reactivity primitives and are the correct tools for non-plain values held in a store; they are described in section 3.6. The devtools integration requires the official browser extension, which reads store state and action calls from the Pinia instance rather than from any instrumentation inside the application.

storeToRefs, direct destructuring, $reset and $patch

Destructuring a store is the single most common Pinia mistake, and the reason it is a mistake is the unwrapping rule from section 3.2. A store is a reactive object whose properties are unwrapped; copying one with a destructuring assignment produces a plain value with no link back. The exception is actions, which are functions and need no unwrapping. store is the sanctioned escape, and it is a narrow one.

// The four forms, and exactly what each produces.
// src/components/CartBadge.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { storeToRefs } from 'pinia'
import { useCartStore } from '../stores/cart'
import { computed, watch } from 'vue'

const cart = useCartStore()

// (1) NO DESTRUCTURING. Always correct. Properties are read through
//     the store, so every read is a live unwrapped access.
const a = cart.count          // correct: a snapshot, refreshed by re-reading cart.count
watch(() => cart.count, cb)        // correct: a getter source
const b = computed(() => cart.total * 2)  // correct

// (2) storeToRefs FOR STATE AND GETTERS. Preserves the link.
const { count, total, items, loading } = storeToRefs(cart)
const doubled = computed(() => total.value * 2)
watch(count, cb)                    // correct: count is a Ref

// (3) PLAIN DESTRUCTURING. State and getters lose reactivity
//     permanently. This is the failure, spelled out.
const { count: brokenCount } = cart
brokenCount                          // a number, taken once. It never changes.

// (4) ACTIONS BY PLAIN DESTRUCTURING. Always correct: a function
//     needs no unwrapping, and it is bound to the store already.
const { add, checkout } = cart
add({ sku: 'A1', price: 1, qty: 1 })
// `this` inside the action is the store, because Pinia binds
// actions at store creation. Detaching one is therefore safe.
</script>

<script setup lang='ts'>
// In a template, the same rule with one difference: a store object
// bound to a top-level identifier IS unwrapped, so a template may
// write cart.count directly. Destructured refs are also unwrapped
// in a template, so `count` works there and needs no .value.
</script>
<template>
  <p>{{ count }} <!-- from storeToRefs: fine in a template --></p>
  <p>{{ count }} in script is {{ count }}</p>
  <<!-- in SCRIPT, `count` here is a Ref and needs .value -->
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// $reset and $patch. Two ways to put a store back.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
const cart = useCartStore()

// $reset exists ONLY on an options-style store. It calls the state
// function again and replaces the state with the result. Any
// value not produced by that function is discarded.
cart.$reset()

// $patch applies a partial state, deeply merged, and fires the
// subscriptions once rather than once per key. It is the correct
// form for a bulk edit and the wrong form for a form whose fields
// may be undefined.
cart.$patch({ items: [], coupon: null })
cart.$patch((state) => {
  state.items = state.items.filter(i => i.qty > 0)
  state.coupon = null
})

// $patch with an object merges. An array value REPLACES rather than
// merges, and an object value merges at the top level only.
cart.$patch({ items: [newLine] })   // items is now [newLine], not a merge

// A setup store has no $reset. Declare one and return it, so the
// capability is identical to the options style and callers do
// not have to know which style produced the store.
export const useCartStore = defineStore('cart', () => {
  const items = ref<CartLine[]>([])
  const coupon = ref<null | { code: string }>(null)

  function $reset() {
    items.value = []
    coupon.value = null
  }

  return { items, coupon, add, $reset }
})

// $subscribe and $dispose are the other two instance members.
// Both are covered in 7.6 and neither should be created inside a
// component without a matching cleanup: a store outlives every
// component, so a subscription registered in setup fires forever.
</script>

The template and the script disagree about the same identifier, and that is the whole source of the confusion. In a template, a r bound to a top-level name is unwrapped, so {{ count }}{{ count }} works whether co came from store or is a refref. In the script, the same name is a R and needs .value.value. A destructured getter from a store, however, is not a ref until store has wrapped it: cart. is a number, and co is a number too, which is why the failure is a value that never changes rather than an object that renders as [object Object][object Object].

Access formStateGettersActionsReactive
storestorestorestore.add()store.add()Yes
storeToRefs(store)storeToRefs(store)counttotalNot includedYes
const { count } = storeconst { count } = storeit is a snapshotto is a snapshota worksNo, for state and getters
const { add } = storeconst { add } = store——a worksYes; it is a function
toRefs(store)toRefs(store)WorksNot unwrapped correctly for the store shapeBecomes a ref to a functionYes, with caveats

Caveats. store is for state and getters only. Passing the result to a component as a whole object, or spreading it into a reactive object, reintroduces the snapshot problem one level up. A store that is passed across a component boundary should be passed as the store, not as its pieces.

Caveats. $patch$patch merges at the first level. $patch({ items: [...] })$patch({ items: [...] }) replaces the array; it does not merge it element-wise, and it does not append. A form that submits a partial object through $patch$patch will leave untouched fields at their current values rather than clearing them, which is usually the desired behaviour and occasionally the opposite.

Make the loss of reactivity impossible to write by never destructuring a store directly. A lint rule banning object destructuring of a value returned by useXx, with store as the only allowed exception, costs one rule and removes an entire class of defect that produces no diagnostic.

Multiple stores, cross-store use and stores outside components

A store is a module that exports a function returning the store instance for a given identifier and a given pinia. That signature is what makes a store usable from a router guard, an API interceptor or a test, because the call takes the active pinia as an implicit second argument. Splitting state by domain produces several stores that occasionally need each other, and the correct way to express that dependency is a call inside an action rather than an import of one store’s state into another’s.

// Splitting by domain. One store per bounded area of the application,
// not one per component and not one for the whole application.
//
//   session  -- who is signed in, and with what claims
//   cart     -- the basket, independent of the session
//   catalog  -- products and their categories
//   ui       -- modals, toasts, the sidebar, purely presentational
//
// A store that reaches across three domains is a store that has not
// been split yet, and a component that reaches across three stores is
// a component that is doing too much.

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// Cross-store use: call the other store from inside an action.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
import { defineStore } from 'pinia'
import { ref, computed } from 'vue'
import { useSessionStore } from './session'
import { useCartStore } from './cart'

export const useCheckoutStore = defineStore('checkout', () => {
  const step = ref(1)
  const placing = ref(false)

  async function place() {
    // Both stores are resolved inside the action. This is a hard
    // dependency, declared where it is used, and it is
    // evaluated at call time so it sees the active pinia.
    const session = useSessionStore()
    const cart = useCartStore()

    if (!session.isAuthed) throw new Error('not signed in')

    placing.value = true
    try {
      const order = await api.orders.create({
        accountId: session.accountId,
        lines: cart.items.map(i => ({ sku: i.sku, qty: i.qty }))
      })
      cart.$reset()   // options-style store; a setup store exposes its own
      step.value = 3
      return order
    } finally {
      placing.value = false
    }
  }

  return { step, placing, place }
})

// Cross-store state in a GETTER is harder, and is usually a sign
// that the derivation belongs in the component or in the store that
// owns both pieces. A computed may call another store, and it
// works, but the dependency is then invisible in both stores.
export const useCartStore = defineStore('cart', () => {
  const items = ref<CartLine[]>([])

  // Acceptable when the two states must always agree and the rule
  // is a property of the domain, not of one screen.
  const totalWithTax = computed(() => {
    const session = useSessionStore()
    const rate = session.taxRate
    return items.value.reduce((s, i) => s + i.price * i.qty * (1 + rate), 0)
  })

  return { items, totalWithTax }
})

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// Stores outside a component. Three cases, three correct forms.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------

// (a) A router guard. Injection is available, and it is the idiomatic
//     form, because the guard runs inside the application context.
router.beforeEach(() => {
  const session = useSessionStore()
  if (!session.isAuthed && session.routeNeedsAuth) return { name: 'login' }
  return true
})

// (b) A plain module, outside any injection context. The active pinia
//     must be supplied explicitly. In an application this is set at
//     boot with setActivePinia; in a test, with the test pinia.
import { setActivePinia } from 'pinia'
import { pinia } from '../pinia'

setActivePinia(pinia)                          // once, at boot
const session = useSessionStore()      // now resolvable anywhere

// (c) Passing the pinia explicitly. The most robust form, because it
//     depends on no ambient state at all. This is the form a
//     server-side entry point uses, once per request.
import { createPinia } from 'pinia'

export function createAppForRequest() {
  const pinia = createPinia()               // one per request
  const app = createSSRApp(App)
  app.use(pinia)
  return { app, pinia }
}

// A store called before createPinia or before setActivePinia throws:
// "[getActivePinia] was called but there was no active Pinia". The
// message names the fix, and the cause is always an ordering
// problem in the entry point.

Calling useCartStore()useCartStore() twice returns the same instance, not two stores, for a given pinia. The store is created on first use and cached by identifier, so the function name is a verb that reads as if it constructs something and is in fact a lookup. This is what allows a component to hold const cart = useCartStore()const cart = useCartStore() without worrying that it has a private copy, and it is also why a store identifier is a global namespace: two stores with the same identifier and the same pinia are one store.

ContextFormRequiresFails with
Inside a component or a guarduseCartStore()useCartStore()An installed or active piniaNothing
A module at bootsetActivePinia(pinia)setActivePinia(pinia) first, then the storeOne call at startupNamed Pinia error if the order is wrong
A module in a testsetActivePinia(createPinia())setActivePinia(createPinia()) in each testPer-test isolationState leaks between tests if omitted
Server-side, per requestapp.use(createPinia())app.use(createPinia()) per requestOne pinia per requestCross-request state leak if module-scoped
Explicit, no ambient stateuseCartStore(pinia)useCartStore(pinia)The pinia instanceNothing

Caveats. Two store modules that both call defin with the same identifier produce one store. There is no error and no warning: the second definition is never registered, the first one wins, and the failure appears much later as a state field that is mysteriously always the initial value. Identifiers are global, so they should be namespaced by domain in any project large enough for a collision to be possible.

Caveats. A store used from two stores creates an import cycle when the two stores import each other at module scope. The symptom is a store that is unde during initialisation, or an action that throws on its first call. Resolving the other store inside the action body, as above, defers the lookup past module evaluation and is the reason that form is preferred over a top-level import.

Plugins, $subscribe and $onAction

Three extension points exist on a store instance and one on the pinia instance. $subscribe$subscribe observes state changes, $onAction$onAction observes action calls, $dispose$dispose stops a store from being cached, and a Pinia plugin runs against every store as it is created. Together they are what a persistence layer, an analytics layer or a logging layer is built from, and each has a lifetime that must be managed explicitly because a store outlives every component.

// (1) $subscribe. Fires after a state mutation, with the whole state
//     and, if detached, the previous state. Two forms, and they are
//     not interchangeable.
// src/plugins/persistence.ts -- a module, not a component, so the
// scope cleanup below is a registration-time decision.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const cart = useCartStore()

// Direct: fires on a direct assignment to a state property. A nested
// mutation inside an action does NOT trigger it, because the state
// reference itself did not change.
const stopA = cart.$subscribe((mutation, state) => {
  // mutation.type is 'direct' | 'patch object' | 'patch function'
  // mutation.payload is the patch argument, for the patch cases.
  persist({ items: state.items, coupon: state.coupon })
})

// Detached: fires on ANY deep change, including a nested mutation,
// and the second argument is the state as it was before. This is
// what a persistence layer wants.
const stopB = cart.$subscribe((_mutation, state) => {
  localStorage.setItem('cart', JSON.stringify(state))
}, { detached: true })

// Callbacks registered on a store that lives longer than the
// component are never removed by unmounting. Registering one in
// setup without a stop is a permanent subscription that grows
// by one per mount.
onScopeDispose(stopA, true)
onScopeDispose(stopB, true)

// flush: 'pre' is the default, before the component re-renders.
// 'post' is required when the callback must observe the DOM, and
// 'sync' fires on every write with no batching. The three have the
// same meanings as the watcher flush option in section 3.4.
const stopC = cart.$subscribe(cb, { detached: true, flush: 'post' })

// A store's own effect scope disposes its $subscribe callbacks, so
// $dispose is the blunt instrument and scope disposal is the precise
// one. A store disposed this way is removed from the pinia cache and
// a later use creates a new one with fresh state.
cart.$dispose()
</script>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (2) $onAction. Fires around every action call, with a hook
//     per point in the lifecycle. The default form covers both.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/plugins/analytics.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
const cart = useCartStore()

const stop = cart.$onAction(
  ({ name, store, args, after, onError }) => {
    // `name` is the action name; `store` is the instance; `args` is
    // the call arguments array.
    // Invoking a hook is what makes this a middleware: returning
    // nothing passes the call through unchanged.
    const started = performance.now()

    after((returned) => {
      console.log(name, 'ok', Math.round(performance.now() - started), 'ms', returned)
    })

    onError((error) => {
      console.error(name, 'failed', error, 'args:', args)
    })
  },
  // A binding restricts the subscription to specific actions.
  { as: 'checkout', mode: 'trigger' }
)

onScopeDispose(stop, true)

// $onAction is the correct hook for analytics and for the
// persistence trigger. $subscribe is the correct hook for
// writing a serialised snapshot. Using $subscribe to detect that
// a checkout happened cannot work, because checkout may change no
// state at all.
</script>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (3) Pinia plugins. A function receiving the context, optionally
//     returning properties to add to every store.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/plugins/persist.ts
import type { PiniaPluginContext } from 'pinia'

// `options` lets a store opt out per store; `auto` is the store
// //id and `store.$id` is the identifier the store was declared with.
export function persistPlugin({ store, options }: PiniaPluginContext) {
  if (options?.persist === false) return
  if (!options?.persist) return

  const key = 'pinia:' + store.$id
  const stored = await loadAsync(key)      // may be async
  if (stored) store.$patch(stored)

  // Writing back is a detached subscription so that a nested
  // mutation is captured, and it is registered on the store's
  // own effect scope so $dispose removes it.
  store.$subscribe((_m, state) => {
    save(key, JSON.stringify(pick(state, options.keys as string[])))
  }, { detached: true })

  // Anything returned is merged into the store instance for every
  // store the plugin applies to. This is how a universal reset,
  // a timestamp or a debug flag is added to all stores at once.
  return {
    // $reset is only present on an options-style store, so a setup
    // store must provide its own before this is useful.
    async resetAndClear() {
      store.$reset()
      await remove(key)
    }
  }
}

// src/main.ts
import { createPinia } from 'pinia'
const pinia = createPinia()
pinia.use(persistPlugin)
app.use(pinia)

// Opting in per store, in the store's own options or as a third
// argument to defineStore.
defineStore('cart', { ... }, { persist: { keys: ['items', 'coupon'] } })
defineStore('session', { ... }, { persist: false })   // never persist a token to localStorage

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (4) A devtools plugin. Pinia stores already appear in the devtools
//     timeline; a plugin adds actions, mutations and a custom tab.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
export function devtoolsPlugin({ store }: PiniaPluginContext) {
  return {
    myAction() {
      console.log('custom action on', store.$id)
    }
  }
}

The distinction between the two $subscribe$subscribe forms is the practical part of this section. A direct subscription fires when store.items = [...]store.items = [...] is assigned and does not fire when store.items.push(x)store.items.push(x) is called, because the reference did not change. A detached subscription fires for both, at the cost of a deep traversal on every mutation. A persistence layer that forgets detached: truedetached: true therefore writes to storage on a checkout and never on an in-place line-item edit, and the bug is invisible until a user reports that their cart did not survive a reload.

Extension pointFires onSeesReturnsTypical use
$subscribe$subscribeA state assignmentMutation descriptor and new stateAn unsubscribe functionChange notification
$subscribe$subscribe with detaAny deep changePrevious and new stateAn unsubscribe functionPersistence, audit log
$onAction$onActionBefore and after every action, and on throwName, args, result, errorAn unsubscribe functionAnalytics, middleware, error reporting
$dispose$disposeNothing; it is a command——Removing a store from the cache
piniStore creationThe store and its optionsProperties merged into the storePersistence, universal concerns

Caveats. $subscribe$subscribe and $onAction$onAction callbacks registered inside a component are not removed when the component unmounts, because their lifetime belongs to the store. A page that mounts twenty instances of a component that registers one subscription leaks twenty subscriptions, each firing on every subsequent state change, and the symptom is duplicated persistence writes and analytics events multiplied by the navigation count. Every registration needs a matching stop, released in onScope as shown in section 4.6.

Caveats. A Pinia plugin that restores state from localS runs during store creation, and a plugin that returns properties is merged into every store it applies to. A plugin that throws during creation makes the store unusable, and the error surfaces at the call site of useXx rather than at the plugin, which is a long way from the cause. Wrap plugin logic that touches storage in its own error handling and log the store identifier.

Caveats. Persisting a store that holds a session token writes a credential to localS, where any script on the page can read it. Session state is the one store that should carry persist: falsepersist: false, or, if persistence is required by the architecture, an http cookie with a server-side session and no token in the client at all.

Exercises 7

  1. Create the same store twice, once as an options object and once as a setup function, with a getter that depends on a second getter. Record the behaviour of $reset$reset in each and implement it for the setup store.
  2. Write const { count } = storeconst { count } = store and a wa on co, then perform three state changes. Record what the watcher sees, then replace it with store and repeat.
  3. Assign a state property directly in one place and mutate it in place in another, and record which one triggers a plain $subscribe$subscribe. Repeat with detached: truedetached: true.
  4. Register a $onAction$onAction subscription inside a component, mount and unmount it twenty times, and count the invocations for one action. Add the stop and repeat.
  5. Write a persistence plugin with an opt-out, apply it to two stores, and record what a JSON round-trip does to a Da field held in state. Replace the field with an ISO string and record the difference.
  6. Create two store modules that import each other and call the other’s store from a top-level comp, and record the initialisation error. Move the call into the action body and confirm it resolves.
  7. Call a store from a module at boot without setActi, record the exact error, and then fix it in the two ways described in 7.5.

8 · Forms and Validation

8.1 The plain-input approach and where a library earns its place8.2 v-model modifiers and the modifier order8.3 A reusable input component with defineModel8.4 vee-validate: useForm, useField and schema validation8.5 Custom rules, error display, handleSubmit and touched/dirty8.6 Server-side errors, file inputs and accessible error output

A form is a binding problem, a validation problem and an accessibility problem, and the three are solved in that order. v-m solves the binding; a validation library solves the second; nothing in Vue solves the third, which is why the third is the part that is most often wrong. This chapter builds the binding from the native inputs, adds the modifiers that change its semantics, extracts a reusable input component, then introduces vee-validate with a Zod schema, and closes on the cases that are not native inputs and the cases the server owns.

The plain-input approach and where a library earns its place

The baseline is a reactive object bound with v-m and a submit handler that validates it. It is a complete solution for a login form, it needs no dependency, and it is the right answer more often than a library is. The point of establishing it precisely is that the failures people attribute to a missing library are usually one of four mechanical problems, and all four are visible in the baseline.

// The complete baseline. A reactive object, a rule set, a submit handler
// and an error map. No library.
// src/views/SignInView.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { reactive, computed, ref } from 'vue'

interface Form {
  email: string
  password: string
  remember: boolean
}

const form = reactive<Form>({ email: '', password: '', remember: false })

// Per-field state, kept in parallel maps. The four parallel collections
// in the next listing are the cost this approach incurs.
const errors = ref<Record<keyof Form, string>>({})
const touched = new Set<keyof Form>()
const submitted = ref(false)
const pending = ref(false)
const formError = ref<string | null>(null)

// The rules. A plain function from the whole form to a field-to-message
// map. Returning {} means valid.
function validate(f: Form): Record<keyof Form, string> {
  const out: Record<keyof Form, string> = {}
  if (!f.email) out.email = 'Email is required'
  else if (!EMAIL_RE.test(f.email)) out.email = 'Enter a valid email address'
  if (!f.password) out.password = 'Password is required'
  else if (f.password.length < 12) out.password = 'Use at least 12 characters'
  return out
}

const isValid = computed(() => Object.keys(validate(form)).length === 0)

// A field shows an error only once it has been touched, or once the
// form has been submitted. This predicate is the whole timing policy.
// A library provides it; the baseline must write it.
function errorFor(field: keyof Form): string | null {
  if (!submitted.value && !touched.has(field)) return null
  return errors.value[field] ?? null
}

function markTouched(field: keyof Form) {
  touched.add(field)
  errors.value = validate(form)
}

function onInput() {
  // Re-validate only what the user has already been shown. Validating
  // a field the user has not reached yet produces an error they have
  // no way to act on.
  if (submitted.value) errors.value = validate(form)
  else {
    const next = validate(form)
    for (const k of Object.keys(next)) {
      if (touched.has(k as keyof Form)) errors.value[k as keyof Form] = next[k]
    }
  }
  formError.value = null
}

async function onSubmit() {
  submitted.value = true
  errors.value = validate(form)
  if (!isValid.value) {
    // Focus the first invalid control. A screen reader user is
    // otherwise left at the top of a form they cannot see the
    // errors of.
    await focusFirstInvalid(errors.value)
    return
  }
  pending.value = true
  // pending is a guard, not a lock. A disabled button does not stop a
  // second submit from Enter in the password field.
  if (pending.value) return
  try {
    await api.auth.signIn({ ...form })
    await router.push({ name: 'home' })
  } catch (e) {
    // A 422 from the server is a field error and belongs in the map.
    // Anything else is a form-level message.
    const err = e as ApiError
    if (err.status === 422 && err.fields) errors.value = { ...errors.value, ...err.fields }
    else formError.value = err.message
  } finally {
    pending.value = false
  }
}
</script>

<template>
  <form novalidate @submit.prevent="onSubmit" @input="onInput"
        aria-busy="pending">
    <<!-- novalidate suppresses the browser's own bubbles so that the  -->
    <<!-- message and the styling come from one place. The trade-off  -->
    <<!-- is that messages must be reachable by assistive tech,    -->
    <<!-- which is what 8.6 sets out.                           -->

    <p v-if="formError" role="alert">{{ formError }}</p>

    <div>
      <label for="'email'">Email</label>
      <input id="'email'" v-model="form.email"
             type="'email'" autocomplete="'email'" required
             :aria-invalid="errorFor('email') ? 'true' : undefined"
             aria-describedby="errorFor('email') ? 'email-error' : undefined"
             @blur="markTouched('email')" />
      <p v-if="errorFor('email')" id="'email-error'">
        {{ errorFor('email') }}
      </p>
    </div>

    <div>
      <label for="'password'">Password</label>
      <input id="'password'" v-model="form.password"
             type="'password'" autocomplete="'current-password'" required
             :aria-invalid="errorFor('password') ? 'true' : undefined"
             aria-describedby="errorFor('password') ? 'password-error' : undefined"
             @blur="markTouched('password')" />
      <p v-if="errorFor('password')" id="'password-error'">
        {{ errorFor('password') }}
      </p>
    </div>

    <label>
      <input v-model="form.remember" type="'checkbox'" />
      Keep me signed in
    </label>

    <button type="'submit'" :disabled="pending">
      {{ pending ? 'Signing in…' : 'Sign in' }}
    </button>
  </form>
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// The four problems the baseline makes visible. Each is a reason to
// reach for a library, and none of them is "the form is long".
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
//
// 1. FOUR PARALLEL COLLECTIONS. form, errors, touched, submitted. A
//    field added to the form must be added to three more places, and
//    forgetting one produces an error that never appears or a stale
//    message that never clears.
//
// 2. THE TIMING POLICY IS WRITTEN BY HAND. errorFor() above is the
//    rule; it is the same rule in every form, and each hand-written copy
//    is a chance to get the interaction wrong.
//
// 3. CROSS-FIELD RULES NEED THE WHOLE OBJECT. "Confirm the password"
//    and "the end date must follow the start date" cannot be expressed
//    per field, so the field-oriented design below has to grow a
//    form-level escape hatch. One exists; it is not the main path.
//
// 4. NO SHARED SCHEME. The same rule must be written again on the
//    server, and a type-only contract between the two is a comment.
//    A schema library produces a validator AND an inferred type from one
//    declaration, which is the actual reason to use one.

The baseline is the right answer for a form with three fields, one rule each, and one error at a time. A library is justified by the count of fields and the presence of cross-field rules, not by the size of the form. The most common reason a project adopts a validation library is that it wanted the types to come from the schema, and the most common reason it regrets the adoption is that it adopted it for a form that did not need it and now has a schema file to maintain for two fields.

ConcernBaseline costLibrary costDecision
Field state bookkeepingThree parallel collections per formOne me object per fieldLibrary from roughly five fields upward
Error timing policyHand-written predicate per fieldConfiguration per field or per formLibrary whenever the policy is non-trivial
Cross-field rulesNatural; the whole form is in scopeNeeds a form-level schema or .confirmed.confirmedBaseline may be simpler
Type from the schemaNot availablez.i, shared with the serverThe strongest reason to adopt one
Server parityRules duplicated by handSchema imported, if the stack allowsAdopt with a shared schema
Bundle and learning costZeroA dependency and its APIJustified only by the above

Caveats. noval on the form suppresses the browser’s own constraint validation, which is what makes the custom messages possible and also removes the browser’s accessibility announcements. With noval the application owns the entire error-reporting obligation described in 8.6; without it, a browser bubble is transient, is not announced reliably, and cannot be styled. Suppressing it is correct, and suppressing it without replacing it is an accessibility regression.

Applies to. autoco attribute values follow the HTML autofill specification and are not advisory: current- and new-pa on a password field change what a password manager offers. Every input in a credential form should carry one, and a form that omits them is harder to use with a manager than the specification requires.

v-model modifiers and the modifier order

Four modifiers change what v-m does to a native input: la changes the event, num and tr change the value, and num additionally changes the binding type. They compose, they are applied left to right, and the order is observable. A fifth modifier, .prevent.prevent on the event, is a separate concern and is not part of the value pipeline.

// Each modifier, what it changes, and what it costs.
<template>
  <<!-- DEFAULT: v-model is `v-model:model-value` plus the `input`  -->
  <<!-- event. Every keystroke writes to the model.               -->
  <input v-model="a" />

  <<!-- .lazy: binds `change` instead of `input`. The model is    -->
  <<!-- written when the field loses focus or Enter is pressed.     -->
  <<!-- Cost: no live preview, and nothing to validate on a        -->
  <<!-- keystroke.                                                -->
  <input v-model.lazy="b" />

  <<!-- .number: the string is passed through parseFloat and the    -->
  <<!-- model becomes a number. A value that does not parse is     -->
  <<!-- assigned unchanged, i.e. the empty string stays a string.  -->
  <input v-model.number="n" />

  <<!-- .trim: leading and trailing whitespace is removed. The     -->
  <<!-- model is written on `input`, so the value in the field     -->
  <<!-- visibly jumps as the user types a trailing space.          -->
  <input v-model.trim="t" />

  <<!-- COMPOSITION. Left to right, outer to inner. `.trim.number` -->
  <<!-- trims first, then parses: ' 42 ' becomes 42.             -->
  <<!-- `.number.trim` parses first: ' 42 ' does not parse, so the  -->
  <<!-- trim never sees it and the field holds a string.           -->
  <input v-model.trim.number="q" />
  <input v-model.number.trim="r" />

  <<!-- On a textarea, lazy is redundant: a textarea has no `input` -->
  <<!-- semantics difference the user can perceive.                -->
  <textarea v-model.lazy="body" />

  <<!-- On a checkbox and a radio, the binding is the `checked`   -->
  <<!-- property, NOT the value, and no modifier applies.        -->
  <input v-model="agreed" type="'checkbox'" />
  <input v-model="choice" type="'radio'" value="'a'" />

  <<!-- On a select, the value is the option's `value` attribute,   -->
  <<!-- and a multiple select binds an array automatically.      -->
  <<!-- An option without a value attribute yields the option      -->
  <<!-- text, and an option whose value does not match any model   -->
  <<!-- value leaves the select showing nothing, with no warning. -->
  <select v-model="country">
    <option disabled value="''">Choose a country</option>
    <option v-for="c in countries" :key="c.code" :value="c.code">
      {{ c.name }}
    </option>
  </select>

  <select v-model="tags" multiple size=4>
    <option v-for="t in allTags" :key="t" :value="t">{{ t }}</option>
  </select>

  <<!-- The long form, which the modifiers compile to. Written out  -->
  <<!-- because the one case where a modifier is not enough is a  -->
  <<!-- date input, whose value is a string in a fixed format.      -->
  <input type="'date'" :value="isoDate"
         @input="onDateInput" />
  <input type="'range'" v-model.number="volume" min=0 max=100 />

  <<!-- .prevent is a MODIFIER ON THE EVENT, not on v-model. It   -->
  <<!-- does not compose with the value modifiers; it is written on -->
  <<!-- the same element as a separate attribute when both are      -->
  <<!-- needed, and the compiler handles both.                   -->
  <form @submit.prevent="onSubmit" @submit="onNativeSubmit" />
</template>

// The date case, handled explicitly. A date input yields 'YYYY-MM-DD'
// and nothing else; converting it to a Date in the middle introduces
// a timezone bug unless it is done in UTC.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const isoDate = ref('')
const asDate = computed(() => isoDate.value ? new Date(`${isoDate.value}T00:00:00Z`) : null)

function onDateInput(e: Event) {
  isoDate.value = (e.target as HTMLInputElement).value
}

// An empty date input yields '', which is falsy and which Number()
// turns into 0. Both are the reasons a date or number field appears
// to be filled with a zero value after a reset.
function onReset() {
  form.amount = null       // not 0, not ''
  form.dueOn = null       // not new Date(0)
}
</script>

// The modifier is not a coercion. `.number` calls parseFloat and
// keeps the raw string when the result is NaN, which is why a
// `type="number"` field holding a partial value like '-' or '1e'
// is a string in the model even with the modifier applied.
// If the model must always be a number, coerce in a computed and
// treat the invalid case explicitly rather than relying on the
// modifier to do it.

Modifier order is not a detail. .trim.number.trim.number and .number.trim.number.trim are both legal and behave differently, because each modifier wraps the previous one and the input travels through them in the order they are written. The rule that avoids the whole question is to use one modifier per field, and to do any non-trivial transformation in a comp where it can be read and tested.

ModifierApplies toEffectFailure it introduces
.lazy.lazyText inputs, textarea, select, and componentsWrites on cha rather than inNo live preview; nothing to validate per keystroke
.number.numberText and number inputsparse on the string; the model becomes a numberAn unparseable value stays a string, so the type is a union in practice
.trim.trimText inputs and textareaStrips leading and trailing whitespace on writeThe caret visibly jumps while typing a trailing space
.camel.camelComponents onlyCamel-cases the update:update: event nameNothing in practice; a component-model detail
.prop.propComponents onlyBinds with .prop.prop rather than .attr.attrNothing in practice; a fallthrough detail
.passive.passive, .capture.capture, .once.onceEvents, not v-mEvent registration changes from section 2.6Not composable with value modifiers in meaning

Caveats. A type="checkbox"type="checkbox" binding ignores va in the sense that matters: the model receives the boolean che state, not the attribute. A checkbox that must carry a value is a hidden input plus a labelled button, or a field in an array. A radio group binds the va attribute of the checked radio, and a va that is a number is converted to a string by the attribute binding, so a numeric model needs :value:value with a bound expression rather than a static attribute.

Caveats. .lazy.lazy on a form that validates on input defeats the validation: with the model written on cha, the input event fires with a model that has not been updated yet. A form that needs both must validate the event target rather than the model, or must not use .lazy.lazy on the field it validates.

A reusable input component with defineModel

A field is a label, a control, a description, an error and an id that connects them. Written inline five times it is five chances to get the f and the i out of step, and the error is then announced by nobody. Extracted into a component it is written once, and the component owns the accessibility contract rather than each caller.

// src/components/FormField.vue. A field wrapper: label, control slot,
// description and error, with the ids wired together.
//
// The component owns the accessibility contract. A caller cannot get
// the `for`/`id` pairing wrong, cannot forget `aria-invalid`, and
// cannot render an error that is not referenced by the control.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { computed, useId } from 'vue'

const props = withDefaults(defineProps<{
  label: string
  // The description is always rendered, whether or not it is
  // currently empty, so the `for` and the `aria-describedby`
  // pairing is stable across the empty-to-filled transition.
  description?: string
  error?: string | null
  required?: boolean
  // A help affordance next to the label, for a field whose format
  // is not obvious.
  hint?: string
}>(), { description: '', error: null, required: false, hint: '' })

// useId produces an id that is unique per instance and stable across
// renders, so the same field rendered twice on a page does not
// collide. Available from Vue 3.5.
const uid = useId()
const controlId  = computed(() => `${uid}-control`)
const descId    = computed(() => `${uid}-desc`)
const errorId   = computed(() => `${uid}-error`)

// The described-by list is computed once. aria-describedby accepts
// several ids, and a control may point at both a description and
// an error at the same time.
const describedBy = computed(() => {
  const ids = [props.description && descId.value, props.error && errorId.value].filter(typeof === 'string')
  return ids.length ? ids.join(' ') : undefined
})

interface Slots {
  default(props: { id: string; describedBy?: string; invalid: boolean }): any
}
defineSlots<Slots>()
</script>

<template>
  <div class="field" :class="{ 'field--invalid': error }">
    <label class="field__label" for="id">
      {{ label }}<span v-if="required" aria-hidden="'true'">*</span>
      <button v-if="hint" type="'button'" class="field__hint-btn'"
              :aria-describedby="`${id}-hint`">?</button>
    </label>

    <<!-- The slot scope hands the control its id and ARIA wiring.   -->
    <<!-- The caller writes a real control element, which is what     -->
    <<!-- allows the field to wrap an input, a select, a textarea    -->
    <<!-- or a custom widget.                                        -->
    <slot :id="controlId" :described-by="describedBy" :invalid="!!error" />

    <small v-if="description" :id="descId" class="field__desc">
      {{ description }}
    </small>

    <<!-- role="alert" is the assertive live region. It is announced  -->
    <<!-- on insertion, which is what makes a message that appears  -->
    <<!-- while the user is on the field reachable. It is NOT a      -->
    <<!-- substitute for aria-describedby, which is what makes the   -->
    <<!-- message available when the user returns to the field.      -->
    <p v-if="error" id="errorId" role="alert" class="field__error">
      {{ error }}
    </p>
  </div>
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// A typed input that owns its own model. defineModel makes the two-way
// contract one line, and the value can still be typed and validated
// in one place.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/components/TextInput.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { computed } from 'vue'

const model = defineModel<string>({
  required: true,
  // set runs on every assignment by the parent, so a normalisation
  // rule belongs here rather than in a watcher. It is emitted
  // upward, which means the parent decides whether to accept it.
  set: (v) => v.trim()
})

const props = withDefaults(defineProps<{
  // The id arrives from the wrapping field, so the label's `for`
  // and the control's `id` are the same string by construction.
  id?: string
  type?: 'text' | 'email' | 'password' | 'tel' | 'url'
  autocomplete?: string
  placeholder?: string
  disabled?: boolean
  invalid?: boolean
  describedBy?: string
  inputmode?: string
}>(), { type: 'text', disabled: false, invalid: false })

// Only ONE model is declared, so a caller cannot bind two models to
// this control by accident, and a second v-model is a compile error.
const hasDescription = computed(() => !!props.describedBy)
</script>
<template>
  <input v-model="model"
         class="'input'" :class="{ 'input--invalid': invalid }"
         :id="id"
         :type="type" :autocomplete="autocomplete"
         :placeholder="placeholder" :disabled="disabled || undefined"
         :inputmode="inputmode"
         :aria-invalid="invalid ? 'true' : undefined"
         aria-describedby="hasDescription ? describedBy : undefined" />
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// Using both, with the field wrapper supplying the accessibility.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/components/SignUpForm.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
const email = ref('')
const emailError = ref<string | null>(null)
</script>
<template>
  <FormField label="'Email address'" required
              description="'We send the confirmation to this address.'"
              :error="emailError" v-slot="{ id, describedBy, invalid }">
    <TextInput v-model="email" type="'email'"
                autocomplete="'email'" inputmode="'email'"
                :id="id" :described-by="describedBy" :invalid="invalid" />
  </FormField>
</template>

A wrapper and a control are separate components on purpose. Merging them into one Form that renders its own <label><label> forces every caller into the same markup, which is convenient until a field needs a different control: a <select><select>, a group of radios, a token input, a rich text editor.
