With a slot the label, description and error are shared and the control is supplied by the caller, so the same accessibility contract covers a native input and a custom widget.

ElementAttributeValuePurpose
lafThe control idGives the control an accessible name; also the click target
Controlaria-i"true""true", omitted otherwiseAnnounces the control as having a validation error
Controlaria-desOne or more element idsAssociates the description and the error text with the control
Error textrole="alert"role="alert"—Assertive live region; announces on insertion
Error textiReferenced by aria-desPersistent association while the message is on screen
Group of fieldsrole="group"role="group" plus aria-laThe group label idNames a fiel or a set of related controls
Live summaryrole="alert"role="alert" on a container—Announces a submit failure that is not tied to one field

Caveats. aria-des that points at a conditional element is a live reference, not a snapshot: if the description element is removed from the DOM the association is lost, and if it is not rendered at all the id is a dangling reference that a screen reader silently ignores. Rendering the description element unconditionally and leaving it empty, as Form does, keeps the association stable across a validation cycle.

Caveats. A s transform on a model cannot prevent a value; it can only change what is emitted. A parent that binds a r to a computed without a setter, or that ignores the update, will leave the control showing the value the user typed while the model holds the transformed one. Transformations that must be authoritative belong in a comp on the parent side with a visible setter, not in a model setter.

Applies to. us is available from Vue 3.5 and is the supported way to generate a per-instance identifier. Before 3.5 the equivalents are a hand-maintained counter or a crypto.r call, both of which are acceptable in a browser-only application and neither of which is available during server-side rendering without care.

vee-validate: useForm, useField and schema validation

vee-validate splits a form into a form object and a set of field objects. use owns the values, the errors, the submit handler and the form-level state; useF owns one field’s model binding, its error and its metadata. A schema is a plain validator object, and toType adapts Zod, Yup or a hand-written function to it, which is what makes the schema the single source of truth for both validation and the TypeScript type.

// The schema. It is the only declaration of the rules, and the type
// is inferred from it rather than written beside it.
// src/schemas/signUp.ts
import { z } from 'zod'

export const signUpSchema = z.object({
  email: z.email('Enter a valid email address'),
  password: z.string()
    .min(12, 'Use at least 12 characters')
    .regex(/[A-Z]/, 'Include an uppercase letter')
    .regex(/\d/, 'Include a digit'),
  confirm: z.string(),
  // A cross-field rule. refine receives the whole object, which is the
  // mechanism a field-oriented form library has to provide for this.
  terms: z.literal(true, { error: 'You must accept the terms' })
}).refine(
  (v) => v.password === v.confirm,
  { message: 'Passwords do not match', path: ['confirm'] }
)

// Zod 4 renamed the top-level format helpers. z.string().email() is
// deprecated in favour of z.email(), and the same applies to z.url(),
// z.uuid(), z.iso.date() and the rest of the format set. `path` names the
// field an error belongs to, which is how a form-level message is
// routed to a control rather than to the form.
export type SignUpInput = z.infer<typeof signUpSchema>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// The form. useForm takes the values, the schema, and the timing
// configuration; it returns everything a submit handler needs.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/components/SignUpForm.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { useForm, useField } from 'vee-validate'
import { toTypedSchema } from '@vee-validate/zod'
import { signUpSchema, type SignUpInput } from '../schemas/signUp'

const form = useForm<SignUpInput>({
  validationSchema: toTypedSchema(signUpSchema),
  initialValues: { email: '', password: '', confirm: '', terms: false },

  // THE TIMING POLICY, in one object. See 8.5 for the per-field
  // overrides and for what each option actually triggers.
  validateOnMount: false,   // no red fields before the user has typed
  validateOnBlur: true,     // a field is checked when it loses focus
  validateOnChange: true,    // and again on every subsequent edit
  validateOnInput: false,    // not on the keystroke that first fills it
  //
  // handleSubmit runs the whole-form validation first and calls the
  // handler only when it passes. It also owns isSubmitting for the
  // duration, including the time the handler spends awaiting.
  //
  // onInvalidSubmit receives the per-field error map, which is the
  // place to focus the first invalid control.
  onInvalidSubmit: async (fieldErrors) => {
    await focusField(Object.keys(fieldErrors)[0])
  }
})

// Destructured for the template. The form object above is kept as
// `form` because 8.6 and the catch handler need it whole.
const { values, errors, isSubmitting, isValid, validate, resetForm, setFieldError, defineField } = form

const onSubmit = handleSubmit(async (values) => {
  // `values` is typed from the schema. No cast, no hand-written
  // interface, and no possibility of the two drifting.
  await api.auth.signUp(values)
  await router.push({ name: 'verify-email', query: { email: values.email } })
})

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// Fields. useField binds one path in the values object to a control.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// Fields rendered in the template bind to `values` directly; useField
// is for a control that lives outside the template, or for the
// per-field metadata that the template does not need.
//
// The three-state contract a field reports, and what each means for
// rendering. This is what replaces the parallel collections of 8.1.
//   touched  -- the user has left the field at least once
//   dirty    -- the value differs from initialValues
//   valid    -- the last validation passed
// meta.touched is true for a field whose error has been SHOWN, which is
// not the same as "the user has focused and left it": a submit makes
// every field touched, and an untouched field with an error is a field
// the user is being nagged about before they have looked at it.
const { errorMessage: confirmError, meta: confirmMeta } = useField('confirm')

// A field can be defined without useField at all, for a control that
// is registered by a component library rather than by the template.
const [terms, termsAttrs] = defineField('terms', {
  type: 'checkbox',
  uncheckedValue: false,
  // A per-field timing override. This field validates only on blur,
  // because a checkbox has no partial value.
  validateOnBlur: true,
  validateOnChange: false
})

// Form-level access, for a field that is not rendered yet. The library
// cannot know about a field the template never created, so a
// hidden or conditional field must be declared explicitly or its
// rule will not run.
defineField('billingAddress', { validateOnBlur: false })

// A server error mapped back into the form. setFieldError writes the
// same map a client-side failure writes, so the display path is one.
catch (e) {
  const err = e as ApiError
  if (err.status === 409) {
    setFieldError('email', 'An account already exists for this address')
  } else if (err.status === 422 && err.fields) {
    for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(err.fields)) setFieldError(k, v)
  } else {
    throw e
  }
}

// Resetting. resetForm restores the values to the initial set, clears the
// errors, and resets the touched and dirty flags. With `force: true` it
// skips re-validating, which is what a user who pressed Cancel expects.
resetForm()
resetForm({ values: { ...values, email: '' } })
resetForm({ force: true })
</script>

<template>
  <form novalidate @submit.prevent="onSubmit" aria-busy="isSubmitting">
    <FormField label="'Email address'" required
                :error="errors.email" v-slot="{ id, describedBy, invalid }">
      <TextInput v-model="values.email" type="'email'"
                    :id="id" :described-by="describedBy" :invalid="invalid"
                    @blur="validate" />
    </FormField>

    <FormField label="'Password'" required
                description="'At least 12 characters, one uppercase, one digit.'"
                :error="errors.password" v-slot="{ id, describedBy, invalid }">
      <TextInput v-model="values.password" type="'password'"
                    autocomplete="'new-password'"
                    :id="id" :described-by="describedBy" :invalid="invalid"
                    @blur="validate" />
    </FormField>

    <label>
      <input v-model="terms" type="'checkbox'" />
      I accept the terms
      <span v-if="errors.terms" role="alert'">{{ errors.terms }}</span>
    </label>

    <button type="'submit'" :disabled="isSubmitting || !isValid">
      {{ isSubmitting ? 'Creating account…' : 'Create account' }}
    </button>
  </form>
</template>

The single most valuable property of the schema approach is that the type is derived, not declared. A hand-written inte beside a hand-written validator is two sources of truth, and a rule that grows a new field with a different shape will type-check against the interface and fail at runtime against the validator. z.i makes that class of drift unrepresentable, and it is the reason to adopt a schema library even in a form with two fields.

Returned memberTypeMeaningUsed for
valReactive typed objectThe current form valuesBinding fields, building the request
errReactive recordField name to first messageDisplay; also the path a Zod error is routed to
handleFunctionValidates, then calls the handlerThe submit event
isSubmRef of booleanTrue while the handler is pendingDisabling the button, aria
isVComputed of booleanWhether the last validation passedDisabling a submit that cannot succeed
valiFunctionRuns validation nowAn explicit blur or change handler
setFieFunctionWrites one errorServer-side error mapping
reseFunctionRestores values and clears stateAfter a successful submit, on Cancel
definFunctionDeclares a field with no controlHidden or conditional fields

Caveats. isV reflects the last validation that ran, not a live assessment. With validateOnInput: falsevalidateOnInput: false a field the user has never touched is not validated, so isV is initially tr for a form whose initial values are invalid. A submit button disabled on !isValid!isValid is therefore enabled on a form that cannot be submitted, and the user is told about the problem only by pressing it. Disable on isSubm alone and let the submit report the errors.

Caveats. vee-validate only knows about the fields the template created. A field that is conditionally rendered, or whose control is produced by a third-party component that does not call defin, is absent from the form and its rule never runs, so the form can submit successfully with a value the schema rejects. Declaring such fields with defin is the fix and the failure mode is silent.

Applies to. The API described here is vee-validate 4.x on the 4.15 line, with @vee-validate/zod@vee-validate/zod as the adapter. vee-validate 5 exists as a release candidate and is not the target of this guide. Zod 4 is the current major; the format helpers moved to the top level of the module namespace in that release and the chained forms are deprecated.

Custom rules, error display, handleSubmit and touched/dirty

A schema covers the rules that can be expressed as data. Four cases do not fit: a rule that needs an asynchronous lookup, a rule that depends on a runtime source, a rule that should run only on submit, and a rule that must be reused across several forms. Each has a first-class mechanism, and each mechanism has a timing consequence that must be chosen deliberately rather than inherited by default.

// (1) A custom rule. A function that returns true or a message, declared
//     globally or per field. The return value is the contract: true
//     passes, a string fails with that message, false fails silently.
import { defineRule, configure } from 'vee-validate'
import { z } from 'zod'

// A synchronous rule with a cross-field dependency. The parameters are
// the field value and the sibling-values object, so the whole form
// is reachable without declaring a schema-level refine.
defineRule('matches_field', (value, [otherFieldName]) => {
  const other = getFieldValue(typeof otherFieldName === 'string' ? otherFieldName : '')
  return value === other || 'The two values do not match'
})

// An asynchronous rule, for a uniqueness check. It returns a promise, and the
// library waits for it before resolving the validation. The field is marked
// pending for the duration, which is why the input should show an
// in-progress state rather than the previous result.
defineRule('available', async (value) => {
  if (!value) return true
  const res = await api.users.checkEmail(value)
  return res.available || 'This address is already registered'
})

// A rule that needs a source outside the form: a runtime configuration
// value, a feature flag, or a list fetched once at boot.
//
// configure sets the global behaviour. generateMessage overrides the
// default English text; leaving it unset means every rule needs its own
// message, which is a decision rather than an omission.
configure({
  generateMessage: (ctx) => {
    const { field, rule } = ctx
    return t(`validation.${rule}`, { field: t(`fields.${field}`) })
  },
  // How the library labels a result that is neither true nor a string.
  //
  // A debounce applies only to asynchronous rules, and it is what stops a
  // uniqueness check from firing on every keystroke.
  //
  // validateOnInput is the fallback for a field with no override.
  validateOnInput: false
})

// A field-level declaration, for a rule used in one place.
const { value, errorMessage } = useField('confirm', () => ({
  matches_field: ['password'],
  required: true
}))

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (2) The four timing options, and the state each produces.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
//
//   validateOnMount   run once when the field is registered. Useful for
//                   a field with a server-computed value; wrong for
//                   every other field, because the form is red on load.
//
//   validateOnInput   run on every input event. Immediate feedback; the
//                   reason a user cannot finish typing a value.
//
//   validateOnChange  run on the `change` event, which for a text input
//                   is blur. This is the default and it is correct.
//
//   validateOnBlur    run on the blur event specifically. Identical to
//                   validateOnChange for a text input; distinct for a
//                   select or a checkbox, whose change fires on every
//                   interaction.
//
// The library applies the strictest enabled option to a field, and the
// whole-form validation on submit always runs regardless of them. The
// characteristic behaviour is: the first failed validation turns on the
// strict timing for that field, so an untouched field is quiet and a
//                   field the user has got wrong nags them on every keystroke
//                   until it is right.

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (3) handleSubmit, and what it does before the handler runs.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
//
//   a. runs the schema against every registered field, not only the
//      visible ones;
//   b. populates `errors` and marks every field touched, which is what
//      makes every error render at once rather than field by field;
//   c. calls onInvalidSubmit with the error map and NOT the handler;
//   d. on success, sets isSubmitting, calls the handler, and clears
//      isSubmitting in a finally block;
//   e. on a thrown error, isSubmitting is still cleared and the error
//      propagates to the caller.

const onSubmit = handleSubmit(async (values) => {
  // The handler is not called on an invalid form. This is the whole
  // point of wrapping it, and it is why the handler body contains no
  // validation code at all.
  await api.auth.signUp(values)
})

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (4) touched, dirty and valid, and the single predicate that decides
//     whether an error is rendered.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
// The same `form` object as in 8.4; only the field is different.
const { errorMessage, meta } = useField('email')

// meta.touched is true once the field has been interacted with or
//                   once the form has been submitted. meta.dirty is true
//                   while the value differs from initialValues, and returns
//                   to false on reset. meta.valid reflects the last
//                   validation.

// The predicate. `touched && error` is the rule; everything else is a
//                   variation. Showing an error before the field has been
//                   touched is the single most common form defect.
const shown = computed(() => meta.value.touched ? (errorMessage.value ?? null) : null)

// A stricter variant: only show a message once the user has stopped
//                   trying, which is what validateOnInput false already does.
//                   The explicit form is useful when the two must diverge.
const strict = computed(() => (meta.value.touched && meta.value.dirty) ? errorMessage.value : null)
</script>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (5) Multi-step forms. The pattern is one useForm for the whole
//     thing plus a per-step validation call, because the schema
//     validates every registered field and a wizard must only
//     enforce the visible step.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
const step = ref(0)
const steps = [['email', 'password'], ['cardNumber', 'expiry']] as const
// Each step has its own schema, derived from the whole-form one by
// picking the relevant keys. The wizard owns the decomposition.
const stepSchemas = {
  account: signUpSchema.pick({ email: true, password: true, confirm: true }),
  payment: z.object({
    cardNumber: z.string().min(15, 'Enter the full card number'),
    expiry: z.string().regex(/^\d{2}\/\d{2}$/, 'Use MM/YY')
  })
}

async function next() {
  // validate() is whole-form, so calling it here would block the
  // user on a step three they have not reached. The per-step
  // check is a projection of the values against that step's
  // schema, and the wizard is responsible for the projection.
  const stepValues = Object.fromEntries(
    steps[step.value].map(k => [k, values[k]])
  ) as Pick<SignUpInput, keyof SignUpInput>

  const result = (step.value === 0 ? stepSchemas.account : stepSchemas.payment).safeParse(stepValues)
  if (!result.success) {
    // Route the per-step failures back into the same error map the
    // whole-form validation would have written, so the display
    // path is identical.
    for (const issue of result.error.issues) {
      setFieldError(String(issue.path[0]), issue.message)
    }
    return
  }
  step.value++
}
</script>

A custom rule declared with defin is global for the lifetime of the module, which means two forms that use the name avai for different checks will conflict. Namespacing a rule name is not supported, so the discipline has to come from the project: prefix by domain, or prefer a schema function over a global rule whenever the rule is used once.

Rule formCan be asyncSees sibling valuesScopeWhere the message lives
Schema function, Zod or YupYes, via a refineYes, the whole objectThe form, or a shared schemaIn the schema, next to the field path
definYes, the return may be a promiseYes, via the parametersGlobal for the moduleReturned by the rule, or by generat
Field rule objectYesYes, by nameOne fieldPer rule, as an argument
Inline validator functionYesYesOne fieldReturned by the function
setFie——One field, from outsideSupplied by the caller; for the server

Caveats. An asynchronous rule runs on every validation the timing options allow, and a uniqueness check is one request per keystroke unless it is debounced. The debounce applies to the rule, not to the schema, so a schema that includes an as refinement is not debounced and will generate a request per keystroke. When an async check is needed, express it as a defin so the debounce can be configured, and show a pending state on the field while it runs.

Caveats. A handle handler that throws leaves the form in a valid-looking state with isSubm cleared and no error anywhere. Every path out of the handler must either succeed, call setFie, or set a form-level message. A form-level message is a separate concern from the field map and needs its own live region.

Make the render predicate a single shared computed rather than repeating touched && errortouched && error in a template. One predicate means the timing policy is stated once, and a change to that policy is a one-line edit instead of an audit of every field in the form.

Server-side errors, file inputs and accessible error output

Three things a form must handle that a client-side schema cannot: a rejection only the server can make, a control that is not a text input, and the announcement of a failure to a user who cannot see it. Each has a defined mechanism, and each has a failure mode that produces no diagnostic at all.

// (1) Server-side errors. The contract, then the mapping.
//
// The server answers a validation failure with a 422 and a body whose
// keys are the form's field names. The client maps it into the same
// structure a client-side failure produces, so there is one display
// path and the field that failed is already marked touched.
catch (e) {
  const err = e as ApiError

  if (err.status === 422 && err.fields) {
    // The server is authoritative for field errors. Anything the
    // client already knows is overwritten, not merged, because a
    // server rule is the newer fact.
    for (const [field, message] of Object.entries(err.fields)) {
      setFieldError(field, message)
    }
    // Focus the first, so a screen reader user is told where the
    // problem is rather than being left at the top.
    await focusField(Object.keys(err.fields)[0])
    return
  }

  if (err.status === 409) {
    // A conflict is a field error in disguise: the message belongs
    // to the field that caused it, not to the form.
    setFieldError('email', 'An account already exists for this address')
    return
  }

  // A 5xx, a network failure or a rate limit is not the user's fault
  // and must not be phrased as if it were.
  formError.value = err.retryable
    ? 'We could not reach the server. Check your connection and try again.'
    : 'Something went wrong on our side. Your details were not submitted.'
}

// Clearing a server error as soon as the field is edited, so the
// message does not persist over a value that has changed. The
// field's own re-validation on blur replaces it if the new value is
// also wrong.
watch(() => values.email, () => {
  if (errors.value.email) setFieldError('email', undefined)
})

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (2) A file input. v-model binds a FileList, not a File, and the
//     value is not readable and is not serialisable. Two approaches.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
//
//   (a) Native submission, multipart. The file goes with the form and
//       the server returns a URL. Nothing about the File is read in
//       the browser, so nothing has to be validated client-side
//       beyond size and type.
const avatar = ref<File | null>(null)
const fileError = ref<string | null>(null)
const preview = ref<string | null>(null)

function onFileChange(e: Event) {
  // files[0] is undefined when the dialog is cancelled, which is not
  // an error and must not be reported as one.
  const file = (e.target as HTMLInputElement).files?.[0]
  if (!file) { avatar.value = null; return }

  // The accept attribute is a filter hint, not a constraint: it is
  // ignored by some browsers and bypassable entirely, so the check
  // is repeated here and again on the server.
  //
  // Reading the size and the type here is a courtesy that avoids a
  // wasted upload. The authoritative check happens after.
  if (file.size > 5 * 1024 * 1024) { fileError.value = 'The file must be under 5 MB'; return }
  if (!['image/png', 'image/jpeg'].includes(file.type)) { fileError.value = 'Use a PNG or JPEG image'; return }
  fileError.value = null
  avatar.value = file
}

async function upload() {
  if (!avatar.value) return
  // FormData is the only correct body for a multipart request. The
  // Content-Type header must NOT be set by hand: the boundary is
  // generated by the browser and a manual header produces a
  // body the server cannot parse.
  const body = new FormData()
  body.append('avatar', avatar.value, avatar.value.name)
  body.append('userId', String(userId))
  const { url } = await api.files.upload(body, { onProgress })
  // Revoke the previous object URL before creating the next one. A
  // preview that is never revoked holds the whole file in memory
  // for the life of the document.
  if (preview.value) URL.revokeObjectURL(preview.value)
  preview.value = URL.createObjectURL(avatar.value)
}

onScopeDispose(() => {
  if (preview.value) URL.revokeObjectURL(preview.value)
}, true)

//   (b) Presigned upload. The file goes straight to object storage, the
//       application never proxies the bytes, and the form
//       holds only the resulting key. This is the correct
//       shape for anything over a couple of megabytes, and
//       it is what makes an upload progress bar possible.
async function uploadPresigned() {
  const file = avatar.value
  if (!file) return
  const { url, key } = await api.files.presign({ name: file.name, type: file.type, size: file.size })
  await api.files.putTo(url, file, { onProgress })
  return key
}

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (3) Multi-value inputs. Three shapes, three binding forms.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
//
//     Checkboxes bound to a Set. The boolean is a computed with a setter
//     that mutates the set, and the set is a ref so mutation is tracked.
const features = ref(new Set<string>())
const hasInvoicing = computed({
  get: () => features.value.has('invoicing'),
  set: (on) => {
    const next = new Set(features.value)
    on ? next.add('invoicing') : next.delete('invoicing')
    features.value = next   // replace, so the computed re-runs
  }
})

//     A repeated field group bound to an array. The key must be stable and
//     must NOT be the index, or removing a row re-associates every
//     input below it and the values are transposed. See section 2.3.
const lines = ref<OrderLine[]>([])
const nextId = ref(1)
function addLine() {
  lines.value.push({ key: String(nextId.value++), sku: '', qty: 1 })
}
function removeLine(key: string) {
  lines.value = lines.value.filter(l => l.key !== key)
}

//     A token input. The array is the model, the draft string is local
//     state, and Enter commits a token. The draft must be cleared on
//     commit, or the next Enter re-adds the same value.
const tags = ref<string[]>([])
const draft = ref('')
function commitTag() {
  const v = draft.value.trim()
  if (!v || tags.value.includes(v)) { draft.value = ''; return }
  tags.value = [...tags.value, v]
  draft.value = ''
}
</script>

<template>
  <FormField label="'Avatar'"
              <!-- A file input is NOT a labelled text control: the browser  -->
              <!-- announces the filename, not the value, and the label names  -->
              <!-- the control rather than a text box.                    -->
              v-slot="{ id, describedBy, invalid }">
    <input type="'file'" :id="id"
           accept="'image/png,image/jpeg'"
           aria-describedby="describedBy"
           :aria-invalid="invalid ? 'true' : undefined"
           @change="onFileChange" />

  <<!-- A repeated group is a fieldset with a legend, not a div with a  -->
  <<!-- heading. The legend is what gives each row a name, which is   -->
  <<!-- what makes "SKU" and "Quantity" meaningful inside row 3.     -->
  <fieldset v-for="(line, i) in lines" :key="line.key">
    <legend>Line {{ i + 1 }}</legend>
    <FormField label="'SKU'" v-slot="{ id, describedBy }">
      <TextInput v-model="line.sku" :id="id" :described-by="describedBy" />
    </FormField>
    <FormField label="'Quantity'" v-slot="{ id, describedBy }">
      <TextInput v-model.number="line.qty" type="'number'"
                    :id="id" :described-by="describedBy" />
    </FormField>
    <button type="'button'" @click="removeLine(line.key)">Remove</button>
  </fieldset>
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (4) The form-level live region, for a failure with no field.
//     `role="alert"` is the assertive variant and announces as soon as
//     the text appears. It must be IN the DOM before the message, or
//     some screen readers will not notice the change; a rendered but
//     empty element is the reliable pattern.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<template>
  <<!-- The container is always present; only its text changes.   -->
  <p class="form-error" role="alert"
     :aria-live="'assertive'" aria-atomic="'true'">
    {{ formError }}
  </p>
</template>

//     The error-summary pattern, for a long form. A list of links to the
//     invalid controls, rendered at the top on a failed submit. The
//     links are what make it usable: a summary without links is a list
//     the user has to read and then act on from memory.
//     tabindex="-1" makes the container focusable so that the focus can
//     be moved to it, which is what announces the summary in full.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const summary = computed(() => Object.entries(errors.value).map(([field, message]) => ({ field, message })))

function jumpTo(field: string) {
  const el = document.getElementById(field)
  el?.focus()
}
</script>
<template>
  <div v-if="summary.length" role="alert" tabindex="-1">
    <h2>{{ summary.length }} problems to fix</h2>
    <ul>
      <li v-for="{ field, message } in summary" :key="field">
        <a :href="`#${field}`" @click="jumpTo(field)">{{ message }}</a>
      </li>
    </ul>
  </div>
</template>

// A note on `novalidate` and the browser's own bubbles. With
//     novalidate, aria-invalid and aria-describedby plus a live region
//     are the ONLY channel, and they are the channel that reaches a
//     screen reader. Without novalidate the two channels disagree and
//     the user gets a bubble that vanishes before it can be read.

A field error is a message that must be available in two ways: announced at the moment it appears, and available when the user returns to the field. role="alert"role="alert" provides the first and loses the content when the element is removed, and aria-des provides the second and says nothing at the moment of appearance. A form that uses only one of the two is inaccessible to somebody: role="alert"role="alert" alone loses the message when the field is fixed; aria-des alone is silent at the moment the error appears.

CaseBindingValidationAccessibility requirement
Single fileFi in a shallSize and MIME, in the browser and again on the serverThe file input needs a label; the filename is announced, not the input’s value
Several filesFile[]File[], appended through a watcher or a handlerCount, total size and per-file typeThe list of names is a list, and removal is a button per entry with an accessible name
Checkboxes as a groupS in a r, or an arrayPer-option and a group minimumA fiel with a leg, or a role="group"role="group"
Repeated field groupArray, keyed by a stable identifierPer row, and a rule about the array as a wholeA fiel and leg per row, with the index in the legend
Server-only fieldNot renderedDeclared with defin so the rule runsNot applicable; there is nothing to label
Form-level failureNot a fieldNot a ruleA persistent role="alert"role="alert" region, and a summary with working links

Caveats. Setting Content-Type: multipart/form-dataContent-Type: multipart/form-data on a Form request breaks it. The header must be left unset so that the browser can append the generated boundary; a manually set header advertises a boundary that is not in the body and the server cannot parse it. The same applies to a JSON body containing a Fi, which serialises to {}{} and produces a server-side error about a missing field rather than about the type.

Caveats. An object URL created for a file preview is held until it is revoked, and a document that creates one per selection without revoking holds every selected file in memory. A URL.revok in a scope-disposal hook is the minimum, and revoking the previous URL when a new file is chosen is what keeps the count at one.

Caveats. The acc attribute is a filter hint for the file dialog. It is not enforced: a file can be dropped onto the control, dragged from another application, or supplied by a script, and some platforms ignore it entirely. A client-side type check is a courtesy that prevents a wasted upload; the authoritative check is on the server, and it must not be omitted because the client checked.

Exercises 8

  1. Build a three-field form with the 8.1 baseline. Count every place a field name is written. Then convert it to use and record the difference in the number of places.
  2. Apply v-model.t and then v-model.n to a field that receives a value with a leading and a trailing space around 4242. Record the resulting value and the type in each case.
  3. Wrap a checkbox in Form and inspect the rendered markup in DevTools. Record the accessible name a screen reader would announce, and compare it with the native la wrapping the input.
  4. Add an asynchronous uniqueness rule to a Zod schema and type a character. Count the requests in the network panel, then convert the rule to a defin with a debounce and count again.
  5. Submit an invalid form and inspect the focus. Record where focus lands with and without an onInval handler, and with a disabled submit button bound to !isValid!isValid.
  6. Set validateOnInput: truevalidateOnInput: true and type an email address one character at a time. Record the message after each character, then set it to fa and repeat.
  7. Return a 422 from a test endpoint with a field-keyed body, map it with setFie, and confirm the field is marked touched and shows the message. Then confirm the message clears when the field is edited.
  8. Select a large image, record the preview URL, choose a second image, and check the memory report for retained files. Add a revoke and repeat.

9 · The Data Layer

9.1 Why fetch does not belong inside a component9.2 A typed API client and the VITE_ environment contract9.3 useFetch and useAsyncData: data, error, pending, refresh9.4 Cancellation, deduplication and caching9.5 Optimistic updates and the rollback discipline9.6 Pagination, infinite scroll, skeletons and error boundaries

Fetching is the part of a Vue application with the most moving parts and the least support from the core, and the reason is architectural: the framework deliberately contains no server. This chapter places the data layer in one module, describes the composables the Nuxt meta-framework provides and what a plain single-page application must supply in their place, and then covers the properties that distinguish a data layer from a sequence of fe calls: cancellation, deduplication, caching, optimistic updates, pagination, the loading state, and containment of failure. Every version is pinned, and the one that matters most here is the boundary between the Vue core and the meta-framework.

Why fetch does not belong inside a component

A fe call in a component is not wrong; it is unmanaged. It has no owner, so nothing cancels it, nothing deduplicates it, nothing caches it, and nothing normalises its failure. The component renders before the response arrives and renders again afterwards, and the state describing the request lives in whichever component happened to issue it, which is rarely the one that has to display it. The remedy is not a prohibition on fetching in components; it is that every request must be owned by something with a lifecycle.

// The unmanaged form. It works, and every property that makes a data
// layer worthwhile is absent.
// src/components/InvoiceList.vue -- NOT WHAT TO SHIP
<script setup lang='ts'>
const invoices = ref<Invoice[]>([])
const loading = ref(false)
const error = ref<null | Error>(null)

onMounted(async () => {
  loading.value = true
  try {
    const res = await fetch('/api/invoices')
    invoices.value = await res.json()
  } catch (e) {
    error.value = e as Error
  } finally {
    loading.value = false
  }
})
</script>

// What is wrong with it, precisely. Each item is a defect that a data
// layer removes and that no amount of care here fixes.
//
//  1. NO CANCELLATION. Navigate away during the request and the response
//     still arrives and still writes to a ref whose component is gone.
//
//  2. NO DEDUPLICATION. A list route with a table and a count badge in the
//     header issues two requests for one piece of data.
//
//  3. NO CACHE. Returning to the route re-fetches everything and the user
//     sees a spinner for data they have already seen.
//
//  4. NO ERROR NORMALISATION. res.json() on a 500 response body throws a
//     parse error, so a server failure is reported as a syntax error and
//     the status code is lost.
//
//  5. NO SHARED STATE. The badge and the table hold two copies of the same
//     list. They agree at one moment and differ at the next.
//
//  6. NO RACE PROTECTION. Two rapid changes of a filter issue two requests,
//     and whichever response arrives last wins, which is not necessarily
//     the most recent request.
//
//  7. NOT TESTABLE. The component cannot be mounted without a network, so
//     every test that renders it is a network test.

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// The managed form, in the smallest expression that has all seven: one
// request, owned by a scope, cancelled on disposal, race-protected, with
// a normalised error.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// src/composables/useResource.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { shallowRef, ref, onScopeDispose, type Ref } from 'vue'
import { toApiError, type ApiError } from '../api/client'

export interface ResourceState<T> {
  data: Ref<T | null>
  error: Ref<ApiError | null>
  pending: Ref<boolean>
  refresh: () => Promise<void>
}

// The composable DECLARES the request; the caller TRIGGERS it. That
// separation is what makes it safe to call from a watcher or from several
// places, because nothing is issued at call time.
export function useResource<T>(
  loader: (signal: AbortSignal) => Promise<T>
): ResourceState<T> {
  // shallowRef for the payload. An API response is a plain object that is
  // replaced wholesale, so deep reactivity on it buys nothing and costs a
  // traversal of the whole response on first access.
  const data = shallowRef<T | null>(null)
  const error = ref<ApiError | null>(null)
  const pending = ref(false)

  let controller: AbortController | undefined
  // The controller decides which request is still wanted; the sequence token
  // decides which response is allowed to write. They solve two different
  // problems and neither substitutes for the other, because an abort can
  // arrive after a response has already been read.
  let seq = 0

  async function refresh() {
    controller?.abort()
    controller = new AbortController()
    const mine = ++seq
    // A refresh does NOT clear the previous data. A refresh that blanks the
    // screen turns a background update into a visible outage.
    pending.value = true
    error.value = null
    try {
      const result = await loader(controller.signal)
      if (mine !== seq) return
      data.value = result
    } catch (e) {
      // An abort is a cancellation, not a failure. Recording it as an error is
      // what produces a red banner on every fast navigation.
      if (e instanceof DOMException && e.name === 'AbortError') return
      if (mine !== seq) return
      error.value = toApiError(e)
    } finally {
      if (mine === seq) pending.value = false
    }
  }

  // The owner. Whatever created this scope gets the cancellation, so a
  // component that unmounts needs no explicit teardown of its own.
  onScopeDispose(() => controller?.abort(), true)

  return { data, error, pending, refresh }
}
</script>

// The component declares what to load and how to render four states. It
// holds no request logic, and the same component works with any loader.
// src/views/InvoicesView.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { useResource } from '../composables/useResource'
import { api } from '../api/invoices'

const { data, error, pending, refresh } = useResource(
  (signal) => api.invoices.list({ signal })
)
void refresh()
</script>
<template>
  <!-- Four states, always all four. A successful response with zero rows  -->
  <!-- is neither an error nor a spinner.                                 -->
  <InvoiceSkeleton v-if="pending && !data" />
  <ErrorState v-else-if="error" :error="error" @retry="refresh" />
  <InvoiceTable v-else-if="data" :invoices="data" />
  <EmptyState v-else title="'No invoices'" />
</template>

The distinction that the template above makes explicit is the one that is usually collapsed: pen with no data is a first load and deserves a skeleton; pen with data is a background refresh and deserves nothing at all, or at most a thin progress indicator. A spinner shown during a refresh makes a working page look broken every time it updates.

PropertyProvided by a data layerAbsent when fetching in a component
Owner and lifetimeThe effect scope; cancelled on disposalNone; the response arrives regardless
Cancellation of a superseded requestAbortCo plus a sequence tokenBoth requests complete; the last arrival wins
DeduplicationA cache keyed by the requestOne request per component instance
Normalised errorThe client raises a typed error for a non-2xx responseres.json()res.json() throws a parse error on an error body
RetryA refresh function on the state objectA hand-written re-mount, or a remounting key
Stale-while-revalidateCached data shown while a refresh runsNothing; the view is empty until the response
TestabilityThe loader is injected and can be stubbedEvery test needs a network or a global mock

Caveats. A composable that issues its request at call time has the same defect as a component that does: it runs once per call, in whatever scope the caller happened to be in, and a caller that calls it inside a wa callback has no component scope to cancel against. Declaring the request and triggering it separately is what makes the composable safe, and it is the reason ref is returned rather than called internally.

Applies to. AbortCo, Abort and the DOMExc named Abort are platform APIs present in every browser that implements fetchfetch. The signal must be forwarded from the loader through the client to fe; a client that accepts a signal and ignores it makes the entire arrangement decorative, and the symptom is requests that appear cancelled in the network panel but whose responses still arrive.

A typed API client and the VITE_ environment contract

Every request passes through one module. That module owns the base URL, the headers, the credentials, the timeout, the status handling and the response parsing, and it exports one typed function per endpoint. Environment configuration is read through import., where the set of variables that reaches the client is fixed by the bundler at build time and is emphatically not a secret store.

// (1) The environment contract. Vite replaces import.meta.env with literal
// values at build time, and it exposes only variables whose name begins
// with VITE_. There is no runtime lookup and nothing to ask.
//
// .env.development
VITE_API_BASE_URL=http://localhost:8000/api
//
// .env.production
VITE_API_BASE_URL=https://api.billing.example.com/v1
VITE_SENTRY_DSN=https://abc123@o4507.ingest.sentry.io/1

// vite.config.ts
import { defineConfig } from 'vite'
import vue from '@vitejs/plugin-vue'

export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [vue()],
  // BASE_URL becomes import.meta.env.BASE_URL: the public base path of the
  // deployed application, which is what the router's createWebHistory needs
  // when the app is served from a sub-path. It is NOT the API URL.
  base: '/'
})

// THE EXPOSURE RULE. Everything prefixed VITE_ is inlined into the client
// bundle and is readable by anyone who opens the developer tools. A
// variable without the prefix is not present in import.meta.env at all, and
// a variable WITH the prefix is a published secret. Naming a key
// VITE_SECRET_KEY publishes the key.
//
// A typed accessor module, so a missing or malformed variable is one named
// error rather than an undefined at the first request.
// src/api/env.ts
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
interface Env {
  VITE_API_BASE_URL: string
  VITE_SENTRY_DSN: string
  MODE: string
  DEV: boolean
  PROD: boolean
  BASE_URL: string
}

const env = import.meta.env as Env

function required(name: keyof Env): string {
  const value = env[name]
  if (typeof value !== 'string' || value === '') {
    // Thrown during module evaluation, which is during the first import. The
    // BUILD does not catch this: a build with no .env.production succeeds
    // and produces a bundle that throws on load.
    throw new Error(`Missing environment variable ${name}`)
  }
  return value
}

export const API_BASE_URL = required('VITE_API_BASE_URL').replace(/\/$/, '')
export const SENTRY_DSN = env.VITE_SENTRY_DSN ?? ''
export const isDev = env.DEV

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (2) The client. The one place in the application that knows about HTTP.
// src/api/client.ts
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
import { API_BASE_URL } from './env'

export class ApiError extends Error {
  constructor(
    readonly status: number,
    readonly code: string,
    message: string,
    readonly fields?: Record<string, string>,
    readonly retryable = false,
    readonly body?: unknown
  ) {
    super(message)
    this.name = 'ApiError'
  }
}

export function toApiError(e: unknown): ApiError {
  if (e instanceof ApiError) return e
  if (e instanceof TypeError) {
    // A TypeError thrown BY fetch itself is a network failure, not a bad
    // request. Always retryable, never the user's fault, and never a
    // validation problem.
    return new ApiError(0, 'network_error', 'Could not reach the server', undefined, true)
  }
  return new ApiError(-1, 'unknown', 'Something went wrong')
}

export interface RequestOptions {
  method?: 'GET' | 'POST' | 'PUT' | 'PATCH' | 'DELETE'
  query?: Record<string, string | number | boolean | null | string[]>
  body?: unknown
  signal?: AbortSignal
  timeoutMs?: number
  // A FormData body must not receive a Content-Type header: the boundary is
  // generated by the browser and a manual header produces an unparseable
  // body. This flag is how the caller says so.
  formData?: true
  // A 204 has no body, and calling .json() on it is a parse error. The endpoint
  // declares that it returns nothing.
  empty?: true
  // Sent as an Accept header so the server can answer 406 rather than with an
  // HTML error page that cannot be parsed.
  accept?: string
}

const DEFAULT_TIMEOUT = 15000

function buildUrl(path: string, query?: RequestOptions['query']): string {
  const url = new URL(API_BASE_URL + path)
  if (query) {
    for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(query)) {
      if (v === undefined || v === null || v === '') continue
      // A repeated key for an array value, which is what the server expects for
      // a multi-select filter, rather than a joined string.
      if (Array.isArray(v)) v.forEach(item => url.searchParams.append(k, item))
      else url.searchParams.append(k, String(v))
    }
  }
  return url.toString()
}

function withTimeout(ms: number, signal?: AbortSignal): AbortSignal {
  // Composing the caller's signal with a timeout, so that either one cancels the
  // request and neither cancels the other. AbortSignal.any does this where it
  // exists; the fallback is the explicit form.
  const timeout = AbortSignal.timeout(ms)
  return signal ? AbortSignal.any([signal, timeout]) : timeout
}

async function request<T>(path: string, options: RequestOptions = {}): Promise<T> {
  const { method = 'GET', query, body, signal, timeoutMs = DEFAULT_TIMEOUT, formData, empty, accept = 'application/json' } = options

  const headers = new Headers({ Accept: accept })
  // A session cookie, not a bearer token in a header. The token never reaches
  // JavaScript, which is what makes a cross-site-scripting bug non-fatal for
  // the session. See the note below.
  headers.set('X-Requested-With', 'XMLHttpRequest')

  let payload: BodyInit | undefined
  if (formData) {
    payload = body as FormData
  } else if (body !== undefined) {
    headers.set('Content-Type', 'application/json')
    payload = JSON.stringify(body)
  }

  let res: Response
  try {
    res = await fetch(buildUrl(path, query), {
      method,
      headers,
      body: payload,
      // The credentials mode is what makes the session cookie travel on a
      // cross-origin request at all. 'same-origin' is the default and is the
      // reason a split-domain deployment silently loses its session.
      credentials: 'include',
      signal: withTimeout(timeoutMs, signal)
    })
  } catch (e) {
    // Re-throw the abort unchanged. toApiError would turn it into a network
    // error and the caller would report a cancellation as a failure.
    if (e instanceof DOMException && e.name === 'AbortError') throw e
    throw toApiError(e)
  }

  // A status check BEFORE the body is read. Reading the body first and
  // checking afterwards is what turns a 500 into a parse error.
  if (!res.ok) {
    const parsed = await readError(res)
    throw new ApiError(
      res.status,
      parsed.code ?? 'http_error',
      parsed.message ?? `Request failed with ${res.status}`,
      parsed.fields,
      res.status >= 500 || res.status === 429,
      parsed.body
    )
  }

  if (empty || res.status === 204) return undefined as T
  return (await res.json()) as T
}

async function readError(res: Response): Promise<{
  code?: string; message?: string; fields?: Record<string, string>; body?: unknown
}> {
  // An error body is not guaranteed to be JSON, and an HTML error page from a
  // proxy is not an error the user can act on. Anything that is not the
  // documented shape falls back to a status-derived message.
  try {
    const body = await res.json()
    return {
      code: body?.code,
      message: body?.message,
      fields: body?.errors ?? body?.fields,
      body
    }
  } catch {
    return { body: await res.text().catch(() => '') }
  }
}

export const http = {
  get:  <T>(path: string, o?: RequestOptions) => request<T>(path, { ...o, method: 'GET' }),
  post: <T>(path: string, body?: unknown, o?: RequestOptions) => request<T>(path, { ...o, method: 'POST', body }),
  put:  <T>(path: string, body?: unknown, o?: RequestOptions) => request<T>(path, { ...o, method: 'PUT', body }),
  patch: <T>(path: string, body?: unknown, o?: RequestOptions) => request<T>(path, { ...o, method: 'PATCH', body }),
  del:  <T>(path: string, o?: RequestOptions) => request<T>(path, { ...o, method: 'DELETE' })
}

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (3) One file per domain, one function per endpoint, typed at the
// boundary so a change to the server shape is a type error here.
// src/api/invoices.ts
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
import { http } from './client'

export interface Invoice {
  id: string
  number: string
  status: 'draft' | 'sent' | 'paid' | 'void'
  totalMinor: number
  currency: string
  issuedOn: string
}

export interface Page<T> {
  rows: T[]
  total: number
  nextCursor: string | null
}

export const api = {
  invoices: {
    list: (params: { status?: Invoice['status']; cursor?: string | null; limit?: number; signal?: AbortSignal }) =>
      http.get<Page<Invoice>>('/invoices', { query: params, signal: params.signal }),

    get: (id: string, signal?: AbortSignal) =>
      http.get<Invoice>(`/invoices/${encodeURIComponent(id)}`, { signal }),

    // A void endpoint: the `empty` flag stops the client calling .json() on a
    // 204 with no body.
    archive: (id: string) =>
      http.post<void>(`/invoices/${encodeURIComponent(id)}/archive`, undefined, { empty: true }),

    // The id is encoded. An id taken from a route param is untrusted and a
    // slash in it changes the path the request is sent to.
    create: (draft: InvoiceDraft) =>
      http.post<Invoice>('/invoices', draft)
  }
}

// The layer boundary. A view imports `api` and never `http`; a client
// function knows nothing about a component. Swapping the transport, or
// generating this file from an OpenAPI document, changes only this layer.
//
// TOTAL MINOR UNITS. Money is an integer count of the smallest unit, never
// a float, and the conversion to a display string happens once, in a
// formatter, so rounding cannot differ between two screens.
export const formatMoney = (minor: number, currency: string): string =>
  new Intl.NumberFormat(undefined, { style: 'currency', currency }).format(minor / 100)

Four decisions in that client are the ones that matter, and each prevents a class of defect rather than a bug. The status is checked before the body is read, so a 500 is a status rather than a parse error. The abort is re-thrown unchanged, so a cancellation is never reported as a failure. The timeout is composed with the caller’s signal rather than replacing it, so an unmount and a slow server are both handled. And the credentials mode is explicit, so a split-domain deployment fails loudly at the first request instead of silently losing its session.

Variable prefixReaches the client bundleUse for
VIYes, inlined literallyPublic configuration: API base URL, public analytics key, feature flags
NoneNo; not present in import.Server secrets, database URLs, private keys, signing material
None, read through a server routeIndirectly, at request timeAnything that must not be readable by the browser

Caveats. Anything prefixed VI is public, permanently and irreversibly for that build. A bundle that has shipped with a secret in it cannot be un-shipped: rotating the credential is the only remedy, and the rotation is urgent rather than planned. The convention that holds is that the client holds a session identifier in an http cookie set by the server, and no token is ever present in JavaScript.

Caveats. A credentials: 'include’credentials: 'include' request to a different origin requires the server to answer with Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: trueAccess-Control-Allow-Credentials: true and an explicit Access-Contro; the wildcard origin is rejected by the browser in that case. A cross-origin deployment therefore needs a specific origin, and a deployment that serves the application and the API from one origin needs nothing. The failure appears as a network error indistinguishable from a server outage, which is why the mode is explicit in the client rather than left to the default.

Requirements. A production build reads its .env.production.env.production at build time, not at start time, and a build performed on a workstation that also has a development .env.env produces a bundle pointing at localhost. The guard in env catches a missing variable; it cannot catch a wrong one, and the verification step is a request against the deployed origin before the release is announced.

useFetch and useAsyncData: data, error, pending, refresh

Two composables in Nuxt provide this behaviour, and neither is part of the Vue core. useAsy runs a handler during setup and returns a reactive wrapper; useF is a thin wrapper over it that takes a URL and options. A plain single-page application has neither, and the correct response is the composable from 9.1 rather than a copy of Nuxt’s. The section states both the contract and the difference, because the contract is what a project should be written against regardless of which implementation supplies it.

// (1) THE BOUNDARY. `useFetch` and `useAsyncData` are auto-imported
// composables from Nuxt 4.x. They are not exported by the `vue` package,
// and importing them from `vue` fails at build time.
//
// In a Nuxt application, this is the whole of a data fetch:
<script setup lang='ts'>
// Nuxt auto-imports. Available in a Nuxt component, nowhere else.
const { data, error, status, refresh, clear } = await useFetch<Page<Invoice>>(
  '/invoices',
  {
    query: { status: 'sent', limit: 25 },
    // The key identifies this request in the payload cache. Without one, the
    // key is derived from the URL and the options, so two calls with
    // different options are separate entries. An explicit key is required
    // when the same data must be shared by name.
    key: 'invoices:sent',

    // The composable resolves on the server during server rendering and is
    // transferred in the payload, so the client does not refetch on
    // hydration.
    server: true,

    // A 404 is data, not an exception. With the default the composable throws
    // and the nearest error boundary renders, which is correct for a route
    // that cannot exist and wrong for a row that is merely gone.
    ignoreResponseError: true,

    // retry controls a transient failure, not a permanent one: 0 disables it.
    retry: 1,

    // deep false stops the payload being walked when it is assigned to the ref,
    // which matters for a large response.
    deep: false
  }
)
</script>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (2) The contract both composables honour, and the four values a view
// actually uses. `status` is a string union rather than two booleans, which
// is what makes "loading with data" representable.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
type AsyncDataStatus =
  | 'idle'
  | 'pending'
  | 'success'
  | 'error'

//   data    Ref<T | null>          the payload, null until it arrives
//   error   Ref<Error | null>      normalised, or null
//   status  Ref<AsyncDataStatus>   the single source of truth for the four
//                                  states in a template
//   refresh (opts?) => Promise    re-run the handler, keeping the old data
//   clear   () => void             reset to the initial state
//
// The template that consumes it. This is the pattern to copy, in Nuxt or
// with the composable from 9.1.
<script setup lang='ts'>
const isFirstLoad = computed(() => status.value === 'pending' && !data.value)
const isRefreshing = computed(() => status.value === 'pending' && !!data.value)
</script>
<template>
  <!-- A first load is a block of skeleton rows. A refresh is a thin line  -->
  <!-- at the top and nothing else.                                       -->
  <div v-if="isRefreshing" class="'refresh-bar'" role="status"
       aria-live="'polite'">Updating</div>

  <InvoiceSkeleton v-if="isFirstLoad" :rows=25 />

  <ErrorState v-else-if="error" :error="error"
               @retry="() => refresh()" />

  <InvoiceTable v-else-if="data" :invoices="data.rows" />

  <EmptyState v-else title="'No invoices match this filter'"
              @reset="resetFilter" />
</template>

// (3) Reactive keys. The options object is watched, so changing the query
// re-runs the request and cancels the previous one. This is the single
// most valuable property of the composable and the reason a hand-rolled
// equivalent needs a watcher and a sequence token.
const page = ref(1)
const statusFilter = ref<Invoice['status'] | 'all'>('all')

const { data, pending, refresh } = await useFetch(
  () => `/invoices?status=${statusFilter.value}&page=${page.value}`,
  {
    // The URL is a function, so its dependencies are tracked. A plain
    // string is read once and never re-read.
    //
    // watch narrows what triggers a re-run; debounce coalesces a burst of
    // keystrokes into one request.
    watch: [statusFilter],
    debounce: 250
  }
)

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (4) The equivalent in a plain SPA, where no such composable exists. The
// watcher replaces the reactive key, useResource replaces the wrapper, and
// the wrapper exposes the same four values.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
const page = ref(1)
const statusFilter = ref<Invoice['status'] | 'all'>('all')

const { data, error, pending, refresh } = useResource(
  (signal) => api.invoices.list({ status: statusFilter.value, page: page.value, signal })
)

// flush: 'post' would let the request start after the render, which is
// marginally faster and flashes stale content. 'pre' is the right default
// for a request that drives what is on screen.
watch([page, statusFilter], () => { void refresh() })

// A burst of filter changes is one request. The pending handle is the returned
// value, and it must be cancelled on disposal or the last timer fires
// against a torn-down component.
const onQueryInput = useDebounceFn((q: string) => {
  statusFilter.value = q as Invoice['status']
}, 250)

onScopeDispose(() => cancelPending(onQueryInput), true)
</script>

// (5) One more difference that matters under server rendering. useAsyncData
// runs on the server, so a handler that touches `window`, a browser-only
// library or a `localStorage` read fails there. `import.meta.server` guards
// the browser-only half. In a pure single-page application the whole
// question does not arise, which is one of the substantive reasons to
// choose one architecture over the other.
const { data } = await useAsyncData('client-only', async () => {
  if (import.meta.server) return null
  const stored = await storage.get('recent-searches')
  return stored
})

// The Nuxt naming: `server` is the boolean that says "this is safe to run on
// the server", and `import.meta.server` is the compile-time constant that
// says "this code is currently executing on the server".

The contract is worth stating as an interface, because it is what a project should be written against and it is independent of who supplies it: a reactive payload, a reactive normalised error, a single reactive status from a four-value set, a refresh that preserves the previous payload, and a clear that restores the initial state. A view written against that interface runs unchanged on useF, on a hand-rolled composable, and on a caching library, and that portability is the reason to insist on the contract rather than on the library.

AspectNuxt 4 useFA plain SPA
AvailabilityAuto-imported in a Nuxt application onlyNot provided; written by the project, as in 9.1
Server renderingRuns on the server, transferred in the payload, no refetch on hydrationNot applicable
Return shapeda, er, sta, ref, clWhatever the project defines; should match the left column
Reactive keysA function URL or a wa list; re-runs the requestA wa calling refresh, written by hand
Caching across routesA payload cache keyed by the request keyNone, or a dedicated library
Error handlingThrows into the nearest error boundary unless suppressedReturns the error in the state object

Caveats. useAsy must be awaited during setup, and awaiting it inside a lifecycle hook or an event handler does nothing useful: the component has already rendered, so the data arrives after the first paint and the flash the await was meant to prevent reappears. A second useAsy in a component that runs in a loop produces one key per iteration, and the keys collide unless they are derived from the loop variable.

Caveats. A hand-written watcher that calls ref without a sequence token, or without cancelling the previous request, reproduces the race described in 9.1 in a new place. The composable from 9.1 is the correct place to solve it because it is the only place that knows about the request; solving it in the watcher means solving it in every watcher.

Applies to. The composables described here are from Nuxt 4.x on the 4.5 line and are auto-imported, which is why they are used without an import statement. useAsy and useF are also available from #app#app for explicit import, and their option names and return members are part of the documented Nuxt API rather than of the Vue API.

Cancellation, deduplication and caching

Three properties of a mature data layer, all of which are consequences of giving a request an identity. Cancellation follows from an owner. Deduplication follows from a key. Caching follows from a store keyed by that key. A request with no key can be neither deduplicated nor cached, which is why the key is the first thing to design and the last thing most implementations add.

// (1) CANCELLATION, in the three places it is needed and nowhere else.
//
// a. On unmount or scope disposal. The only mandatory case.
//    src/composables/useResource.ts, as in 9.1
onScopeDispose(() => controller?.abort(), true)

// b. When a newer request supersedes an older one for the same resource.
//    Aborting frees the connection immediately rather than waiting for the
//    server to notice it is no longer wanted.
async function refresh() {
  controller?.abort()
  controller = new AbortController()
  // ...
}

// c. On an explicit user action, such as a Cancel button on a search.
//    Here the abort is reported, not swallowed: the user asked for it.
//    controller.abort()

// What NOT to cancel: a mutation. A request that changes state on the
// server is not cancelled by navigating away, because the user has already
// been told it succeeded. Cancelling a mutation is how a form reports a
// failure for an operation that completed.

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (2) A cache with a key. The distinction between fresh, stale and failed
// is what makes a cached application feel instant rather than merely fast.
// src/api/cache.ts
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
interface CacheEntry<T> {
  data: T | null
  error: ApiError | null
  // updatedAt is the time the data was RECEIVED, not the time it was
  // requested. Freshness is a property of the payload.
  updatedAt: number
  // The in-flight promise for this key. Its presence IS the deduplication: a
  // second caller joins it instead of issuing a second request.
  inflight: Promise<T> | null
  // The shared controller. It is owned by the cache, not by a consumer,
  // because unmounting one consumer must not cancel a request that other
  // consumers are still waiting for.
  controller: AbortController | null
  // The number of consumers currently interested in this key.
  refs: number
}

const entries = new Map<string, CacheEntry<unknown>>()

// A key must be stable, order-independent and complete. `JSON.stringify` of an
// object is order-dependent, so sort the keys first: the same query built in a
// different property order is the same request.
export function cacheKey(parts: Record<string, unknown>): string {
  const sorted: Record<string, unknown> = {}
  for (const k of Object.keys(parts).sort()) sorted[k] = parts[k]
  return JSON.stringify(sorted)
}

export interface CachePolicy {
  // How long a payload is served without revalidation.
  staleMs: number
  // Whether a revalidation runs in the background while stale data is shown.
  revalidateOnStale: boolean
  // Whether a failed revalidation keeps the stale payload on screen. It should,
  // for a transient failure; a 404 should replace it.
  keepStaleOnError: boolean
}

export const DEFAULT_POLICY: CachePolicy = {
  staleMs: 30000,
  revalidateOnStale: true,
  keepStaleOnError: true
}

function entryFor<T>(key: string): CacheEntry<T> {
  let e = entries.get(key) as CacheEntry<T> | undefined
  if (!e) {
    e = { data: null, error: null, updatedAt: 0, inflight: null, controller: null, refs: 0 }
    entries.set(key, e)
  }
  return e
}

export function useQuery<T>(
  key: string,
  fetcher: (signal: AbortSignal) => Promise<T>,
  policy: CachePolicy = DEFAULT_POLICY
): ResourceState<T> {
  const entry = entryFor<T>(key)

  // The consumer-local state, seeded from the cache. A second consumer of the
  // same key starts with the first one's data already present.
  const data = shallowRef<T | null>(entry.data)
  const error = ref<ApiError | null>(entry.error)
  const pending = ref(false)

  entry.refs++

  async function refresh() {
    // DEDUPLICATION. A second caller for the same key joins the in-flight
    // request rather than issuing one. No locking is required: the check
    // and the assignment are atomic with respect to other microtasks in a
    // single-threaded runtime.
    if (entry.inflight) {
      pending.value = true
      await entry.inflight
      pending.value = false
      data.value = entry.data
      return
    }

    // FRESHNESS. A payload inside the window is served without a request. A
    // stale payload is shown while a revalidation runs in the background, so
    // the view never returns to an empty state for data it already had.
    const fresh = Date.now() - entry.updatedAt < policy.staleMs
    if (fresh && entry.data !== null) { pending.value = false; return }

    pending.value = true
    error.value = null

    entry.controller?.abort()
    entry.controller = new AbortController()

    entry.inflight = fetcher(entry.controller.signal)
      .then((result) => {
        entry.data = result
        entry.updatedAt = Date.now()
        entry.error = null
        data.value = result
        error.value = null
        return result
      })
      .catch((e) => {
        if (e instanceof DOMException && e.name === 'AbortError') {
          // A cancellation is not a failure: no error is recorded.
          entry.error = null
          return undefined as T
        }
        const err = toApiError(e)
        entry.error = err
        // A transient failure keeps the stale payload on screen and reports the
        // error alongside it. A 404 replaces it: the resource is gone and
        // showing yesterday's row is worse than showing nothing.
        if (policy.keepStaleOnError && entry.data !== null && err.status !== 404) {
          data.value = entry.data
          error.value = err
        } else {
          entry.data = null
          data.value = null
          error.value = err
        }
        return undefined as T
      })
      .finally(() => {
        entry.inflight = null
        entry.controller = null
        pending.value = false
      })

    return entry.inflight
  }

  function invalidate(prefix = '') {
    // Called after a mutation. Entries are marked stale rather than deleted,
    // which is what lets the screen keep showing the previous value while the
    // refetch happens.
    for (const [k, e] of entries) {
      if (!prefix || k.startsWith(prefix)) e.updatedAt = 0
    }
  }

  // The reference count. When the last consumer releases the key, an
  // in-flight request is abandoned and the entry is dropped once it is older
  // than the maximum retained age. This is the eviction policy that a cache
  // without one is missing.
  onScopeDispose(() => {
    if (--entry.refs > 0) return
    entry.controller?.abort()
    if (Date.now() - entry.updatedAt > policy.staleMs * 10) entries.delete(key)
  }, true)

  return { data, error, pending, refresh, invalidate }
}
</script>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (3) Pinia Colada. The data-fetching layer published by the author of
// Pinia, and the current answer to "which library". It is stable on the 1.x
// line and is built on Pinia, so it shares the store and the devtools with
// chapter 7. It supplies everything the listing above implements by hand:
// caching, deduplication, staleness, optimistic updates and garbage
// collection of unused keys.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
import { createPinia } from 'pinia'
import { PiniaColada, useQuery, useMutation, useQueryCache } from '@pinia/colada'

const pinia = createPinia()
app.use(pinia)
// Installed AFTER pinia, because it is a Pinia plugin.
app.use(PiniaColada)

// A query. The key is explicit and is what everything else addresses.
const { data, isPending, isError, error, refetch } = useQuery({
  key: () => ['invoices', route.params.id],
  query: () => api.invoices.get(route.params.id as string),
  staleTime: 30000
})

// A mutation, with an optimistic update and a rollback. The three callbacks
// are the mechanism, and the rollback is the one that is mandatory.
const { mutate: archive, isLoading } = useMutation({
  mutation: (id: string) => api.invoices.archive(id),
  onMutate: (id) => {
    // The previous value, captured BEFORE the write. Reconstructing it
    // afterwards is not the same thing and does not restore the row that was
    // on screen.
    const previous = queryCache.getQueryData(['invoices', id])
    queryCache.setQueryData(['invoices', id], (old: Invoice | undefined) =>
      old ? { ...old, status: 'void' as const } : old
    )
    return { previous }
  },
  onError: (_err, id, context) => {
    queryCache.setQueryData(['invoices', id], context?.previous)
  },
  onSettled: (_data, _err, id) => {
    queryCache.invalidateQueries({ key: ['invoices', id], exact: true })
  }
})

The subtlety in that listing is that a shared in-flight request cannot be cancelled by any single consumer’s unmount. Two components reading the same key, one of which unmounts, must not abort a request the other is still waiting for. The design that follows from that is a reference count, and it is why a hand-rolled cache of any sophistication converges on the design a mature library already has.

PropertyWithout a cacheWith a keyed cacheFailure if the key is wrong
DeduplicationOne request per consumerOne request per key, others join itTwo components show two copies that diverge
FreshnessAlways refetchedServed within the window, revalidated after itA user edits a record in another tab and never sees it
Background revalidationNot applicableStale data shown while a request runs—
Instant navigationA spinner every timeA cache hit paints immediately—
Error isolationOne failure blanks the screenStale data survives a transient failureA dead record is displayed as if live
InvalidationNothing to invalidateExplicit, after a mutationStale data is shown with no signal that it is stale

Caveats. A cache with no eviction policy is a memory leak with extra steps. A key that includes a filter the user has visited once stays in the map for the life of the document, and a key that includes a timestamp makes a new entry per request, which is a cache that never hits. A library that manages garbage collection is managing this, and a hand-rolled cache must too: an entry unused for a window, or beyond a maximum count, is dropped.

Caveats. A cached payload on a screen is a claim that it is current. Where that claim is expensive, the cache must be invalidated by the mutation that would falsify it, and an invalidation that is forgotten is not detectable: the interface shows the old value and nothing reports an error. Any endpoint that appears on more than one screen, and any endpoint whose mutation is visible elsewhere, needs an explicit invalidation path; a full invalidation after every mutation is a defensible first implementation.

Applies to. Pinia Colada is a separate package from Pinia and is installed after it. The API shown here is the 1.x line; the 0.x0.x line had a different option shape and code written against it will not compile unchanged. The cache is in memory by default, which means a full page load starts empty, and persistence is a plugin rather than a default.

Optimistic updates and the rollback discipline

An optimistic update writes the intended result locally, issues the mutation, and reverts if the server disagrees. It makes an interface feel immediate, and it is the correct choice for an operation the user is certain they want and the server is certain to accept. It is the wrong choice for anything the server computes, anything the client cannot know is permitted, and anything that cannot be undone. The pattern is not the difficult part; the rollback is, and the discipline below is what makes it safe.

// (1) The full pattern, in a store action, with the rollback that makes it
// correct. Every optimistic update is these six steps.
// src/stores/invoices.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { defineStore } from 'pinia'
import { ref } from 'vue'
import { api, type Invoice } from '../api/invoices'

export const useInvoiceStore = defineStore('invoices', () => {
  const byId = ref(new Map<string, Invoice>())
  const pendingIds = ref(new Set<string>())
  const actionError = ref<string | null>(null)

  const isPending = (id: string) => pendingIds.value.has(id)

  function markPending(id: string) {
    pendingIds.value = new Set(pendingIds.value).add(id)
  }
  function clearPending(id: string) {
    const next = new Set(pendingIds.value)
    next.delete(id)
    pendingIds.value = next
  }

  async function archive(id: string) {
    // 1. CAPTURE. The previous value, taken before any write. This is the only
    // copy of the truth the rollback can restore, and it must be a snapshot
    // rather than a reference to a mutable object.
    const before = byId.value.get(id)
    if (!before) throw new Error('unknown invoice')
    const snapshot: Invoice = { ...before }

    // 2. WRITE. Replace the Map rather than mutating it, so every reader updates
    // and the rollback is symmetric with the write.
    byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, { ...before, status: 'void' })
    markPending(id)
    actionError.value = null

    try {
      // 3. SEND. The only part that touches the network.
      const confirmed = await api.invoices.archive(id)

      // 4. RECONCILE. The server's version wins, always, and it is written even
      // when it equals the optimistic one, because it carries the fields the
      // client guessed: an audit stamp, a derived status, a version number.
      byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, confirmed)
    } catch (e) {
      // 5. ROLLBACK. Exactly the snapshot from step 1. Not a recomputed value: a
      // recomputation applies today's rules to today's data and is not the same
      // as what the user saw.
      byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, snapshot)
      // The error is reported, not swallowed. A silently reverted update is a
      // row that jumps back with no explanation.
      actionError.value = // set from the ApiError, see 9.2
      throw e
    } finally {
      // 6. RELEASE. In a finally, so a thrown error cannot leave the row
      // permanently marked as in flight.
      clearPending(id)
    }
  }
