  // (2) A second mutation on the same row while the first is in flight. This
  // is where naive optimistic updates corrupt state, and the fix is to queue
  // per key rather than to interleave them.
  const queue = new Map<string, Promise<void>>()

  function enqueue(id: string, task: () => Promise<void>): Promise<void> {
    // Chain onto the previous task for this key. Two rapid edits produce two
    // mutations in order, two captures and two rollbacks, and the second
    // rollback restores the result of the first rather than the state before
    // either.
    const next = (queue.get(id) ?? Promise.resolve()).then(task, task)
    queue.set(id, next)
    return next.finally(() => {
      if (queue.get(id) === next) queue.delete(id)
    })
  }

  async function rename(id: string, customer: string) {
    return enqueue(id, async () => {
      const before = byId.value.get(id)
      if (!before) throw new Error('unknown invoice')
      const snapshot = { ...before }

      byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, { ...before, customer })
      markPending(id)

      try {
        const confirmed = await api.invoices.update(id, { customer })
        byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, confirmed)
      } catch (e) {
        byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, snapshot)
        // A rejected rename is worth a specific message, and the text the user
        // typed is kept in the field so they can try again without retyping it.
        actionError.value = 'The customer name was not accepted'
        throw e
      } finally {
        clearPending(id)
      }
    })
  }

  // (3) The three cases where an optimistic update is wrong, and what to do
  // instead.
  //
  //   a. THE SERVER COMPUTES THE VALUE. A total with tax, a balance after a
  //      payment, a permission the caller does not have. Guessing it puts a
  //      number on screen that the server will contradict. Show the change as
  //      pending and take the value from the response.
  //
  //   b. THE OPERATION MAY BE REFUSED. A delete the user lacks the right to
  //      perform, an action outside a quota. A refusal removes a row the user
  //      was looking at and puts it back, which is worse than a brief delay.
  //      Confirm first, then remove.
  //
  //   c. THE OPERATION IS NOT REVERSIBLE. Sending a message, charging a card,
  //      anything that leaves the system. A rollback that cannot undo the server
  //      side is a local fiction, so the client must wait for the answer before
  //      claiming success.

  // (4) The reconciliation a cache needs, which is the part a hand-rolled
  // optimistic update leaves to chance: a mutation that changes a list must
  // update the list key AND invalidate every key whose contents depend on it,
  // such as a total that sums the list.
  async function pay(id: string, amountMinor: number) {
    const before = byId.value.get(id)
    if (!before) throw new Error('unknown invoice')
    const snapshot = { ...before }

    byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, { ...before, status: 'paid' })
    markPending(id)

    try {
      const confirmed = await api.invoices.pay(id, amountMinor)
      byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, confirmed)
      // The totals key depends on this row and is now stale.
      // totalsCache.invalidate('totals')
    } catch (e) {
      byId.value = new Map(byId.value).set(id, snapshot)
      throw e
    } finally {
      clearPending(id)
    }
  }

  return { byId, pendingIds, actionError, isPending, archive, rename, pay }
})

// (5) The template. A pending row is marked, not disabled: the user can still
// read it, and the row that jumps back is explained by a banner rather than
// by a row that silently reappears.
// src/components/InvoiceRowList.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
<script setup>
<template>
  <p v-if="store.actionError" role="alert"{{ store.actionError }}</p>

  <tr v-for="inv in rows" :key="inv.id"
      :aria-busy="store.isPending(inv.id)"
      :class="{ 'is-pending': store.isPending(inv.id) }">
    <td>{{ inv.number }}</td>
    <td>{{ formatMoney(inv.totalMinor, inv.currency) }}</td>
    <td>{{ inv.status }}</td>
    <td>
      <button type="'button'" :disabled="store.isPending(inv.id)"
              @click="store.archive(inv.id).catch(() => {})">
        Archive
      </button>
    </td>
  </tr>
</template>

Capture, write, send, reconcile, roll back, release. The order matters: the capture precedes the write, the rollback uses the captured snapshot rather than a recomputation, and the release is in a fin so a throw cannot strand a row as permanently pending. An implementation that satisfies all six is correct for the single-mutation case; per-key queueing is what makes it correct when the user is faster than the network, which they will be.

SituationOptimisticReason
Toggle a flag the server accepts or rejects identicallyYesThe value is a boolean the user already decided
Rename a record, move a row, mark as readYesReversible, and the failure is a visible reversion
Add a line to a listYes, with a temporary idReversible, and the id is reconciled from the response
Delete a recordConfirm firstA refusal is plausible and the reversion removes and restores a row
Anything the server computes: a total, a balance, a rankNoThe client cannot produce the right answer
Anything irreversible: a payment, a sent messageNoA local rollback cannot undo the server
Anything subject to a quota or a limitNoRefusal is expected, not exceptional

Caveats. An optimistic update and a pen flag are not a substitute for server-side serialisation. Two browser tabs can both apply the same change optimistically, and the second write must be rejected or merged by the server; a client that assumes its own state is authoritative will display a value the server never stored. Every mutation that changes a shared record needs a version or an idempotency key, and the client has to handle the conflict response rather than assuming it cannot occur.

Caveats. Reverting a row after the user has navigated away from it is a write to a store that no component is rendering, which is correct and silent. The user who triggered the mutation has left the screen, and the failure is only visible on their return. Where the mutation is significant enough that its failure matters, the error belongs in a notification that outlives the view rather than in a row.

Requirements. An optimistic update requires a fin that clears the pending marker, an error path that reports rather than swallows, and a reconciliation step that writes the server’s response even when it matches the guess. Omitting the third step is the most common defect: the client keeps its own value permanently, including for the fields it had no way to know.

Pagination, infinite scroll, skeletons and error boundaries

A data layer meets a user at three points: how a long result set is traversed, what is on screen while the first response is outstanding, and what happens when one part of a page cannot load. Each has a small number of defensible mechanisms, and each has a failure mode that produces no diagnostic. This section closes the chapter and the part of the guide that concerns data.

// (1) PAGINATION. Offset versus keyset, and the reason the choice is forced by
// the size of the table rather than by preference.
// src/api/invoices.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
export interface KeysetPage<T> {
  rows: T[]
  nextCursor: string | null
  hasMore: boolean
}

export const api = {
  invoices: {
    // OFFSET. Simple, and the only option that can jump to page 40 or say
    // "showing 1-25 of 4,182". Two costs, both of which grow with the table:
    //
    //   - The database must COUNT and then SKIP. OFFSET n makes the engine walk
    //     and discard n rows, so page 4,000 on a large table is far more
    //     expensive than page 1, and the cost is invisible to the client.
    //
    //   - It is not stable. A row inserted while the user is on page 2 shifts
    //     every later row by one, so a row the user just read appears again on
    //     the next page. A row deleted while paging shifts them the other way,
    //     and one is skipped entirely.
    //
    // Suitable to a few thousand rows, and to any list where a page number is
    // itself a requirement.
    listOffset: (p: { offset: number; limit: number; signal?: AbortSignal }) =>
      http.get<{ rows: Invoice[]; total: number }>('/invoices', {
        query: { offset: p.offset, limit: p.limit },
        signal: p.signal
      }),

    // KEYSET. The cursor is the sort key of the last row and the server returns
    // rows strictly after it. Three properties, all of them desirable:
    //
    //   - The cost is the same for every page, because the index seek is direct
    //     and no rows are skipped.
    //
    //   - It is stable under concurrent writes, for the same reason: an inserted
    //     row cannot appear between two pages the user visited.
    //
    //   - There is no page number, so there is no COUNT.
    //
    // The cost: the sort must be total and indexed, and a cursor is opaque, so a
    // deep link to a page requires the cursor in the URL.
    listKeyset: (p: { cursor?: string | null; limit: number; signal?: AbortSignal }) =>
      http.get<KeysetPage<Invoice>>('/invoices', {
        query: { cursor: p.cursor ?? undefined, limit: p.limit },
        signal: p.signal
      })
  }
}
</script>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (2) Offset pagination in a component, with the URL holding the page.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { computed, ref, watch } from 'vue'
import { useRoute, useRouter } from 'vue-router'

const route = useRoute()
const router = useRouter()
const PAGE_SIZE = 25

// The page lives in the URL, so it survives a reload, is shareable, and is the
// Back button's history. Local state would satisfy none of that.
const page = computed(() => {
  const n = Number(route.query.page ?? '1')
  return Number.isInteger(n) && n >= 1 ? n : 1
})

const { data, pending, error, refresh } = useResource(
  (signal) => api.invoices.listOffset({ offset: (page.value - 1) * PAGE_SIZE, limit: PAGE_SIZE, signal })
)
watch(page, () => { void refresh() })
void refresh()

const total = computed(() => data.value?.total ?? 0)
const pageCount = computed(() => Math.max(1, Math.ceil(total.value / PAGE_SIZE)))

function goTo(n: number) {
  // replace rather than push, so paging through a list does not fill the
  // history stack with twenty entries the user then has to press Back
  // twenty times to escape.
  const target = { ...route.query, page: n > 1 ? String(n) : undefined }
  router.replace({ name: route.name as string, query: target })
}
</script>
<template>
  <button :disabled="page <= 1 || pending" @click="goTo(page - 1)">Previous</button>
  <p aria-live="'polite'" :aria-busy="pending">
    Page {{ page }} of {{ pageCount }}, {{ total }} invoices
  </p>
  <button :disabled="page >= pageCount || pending" @click="goTo(page + 1)">Next</button>
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (3) INFINITE SCROLL. Three states beyond "more": the trigger, the
// in-flight append, and the end of the list.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
const rows = ref<Invoice[]>([])
const cursor = ref<string | null>(null)
const hasMore = ref(true)
const loadingMore = ref(false)
const loadMoreError = ref<ApiError | null>(null)
const sentinel = ref<HTMLElement | null>(null)

// A guard against re-entry. An IntersectionObserver fires when the sentinel
// becomes visible, which includes the moment it becomes visible AGAIN after
// an append, so without this the list loads several pages at once.
let loadingGuard = false

async function loadMore() {
  if (loadingGuard || !hasMore.value) return
  loadingGuard = true
  loadingMore.value = true
  loadMoreError.value = null
  try {
    // The cursor, not the offset. Appending is safe under concurrent writes
    // precisely because the position is a sort key rather than a count of
    // rows to skip.
    const page = await api.invoices.listKeyset({ cursor: cursor.value, limit: 25 })
    rows.value = [...rows.value, ...page.rows]
    cursor.value = page.nextCursor
    hasMore.value = page.hasMore
  } catch (e) {
    // A failed page must not discard the pages that already loaded. The error
    // is shown at the end of the list with a retry, and the rows above it
    // stay exactly as they are.
    loadMoreError.value = toApiError(e)
  } finally {
    loadingGuard = false
    loadingMore.value = false
  }
}

let observer: IntersectionObserver | undefined
watch(sentinel, (el) => {
  observer?.disconnect()
  if (!el) return
  // rootMargin preloads before the sentinel is actually on screen, which is the
  // difference between a scroll that feels continuous and one that visibly
  // stalls. The observer is disconnected on disposal, as every observer must
  // be (section 4.6).
  observer = new IntersectionObserver(([entry]) => {
    if (entry?.isIntersecting) void loadMore()
  }, { rootMargin: '400px' })
  observer.observe(el)
})
onScopeDispose(() => observer?.disconnect(), true)

void loadMore()
</script>
<template>
  <InvoiceTable :invoices="rows" />

  <!-- The three end states, all of which must exist.               -->
  <div ref="sentinel" aria-hidden="'true'" />

  <p v-if="loadingMore" role="status">Loading more…</p>

  <p v-else-if="loadMoreError" role="alert">
    Could not load more.
    <button type="'button'" @click="loadMore">Try again</button>
  </p>

  <p v-else-if="!hasMore && rows.length">End of list, {{ rows.length }} shown</p>

  <!-- An explicit Load more control is the accessible form, and is  -->
  <!-- preferable wherever the list is long enough that a user may want  -->
  <!-- to stop: infinite scroll has no end, so a keyboard user cannot    -->
  <!-- reach the footer by tabbing, and a screen reader announces the   -->
  <!-- number of appended rows without being asked.                   -->
  <button v-if="hasMore && !loadingMore" type="'button'"
          @click="loadMore">
    Load more
  </button>
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (4) SKELETONS. The shapes must match the real content, because a
// skeleton of the wrong shape produces a larger reflow than no skeleton
// at all. A skeleton is also a claim that the content is arriving; for a
// request that is slow because it is failing, it is waiting forever.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
const showSkeleton = computed(() => pending.value && !data.value)
const showInlineProgress = computed(() => pending.value && !!data.value)
</script>
<template>
  <InvoiceSkeleton v-if="showSkeleton" :rows=25 />
  <div v-else role="status" aria-live="'polite'"
       :aria-busy="showInlineProgress">
    <InvoiceTable :invoices="data?.rows ?? []" />
  </div>
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (5) ERROR BOUNDARIES. The Vue core has no error boundary component; this
// one captures a descendant's error and renders a fallback INSTEAD of
// unmounting the whole tree. That is the property that matters: a failed
// widget must not take the navigation with it.
// src/components/ErrorBoundary.vue
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, onErrorCaptured, type Component } from 'vue'

const props = defineProps<{
  // A fallback component, which is the practical form. Anything can be
  // rendered; the boundary only decides whether to render it.
  fallback?: Component
  // When true the error propagates to the parent boundary as well, so a
  // nested layout can escalate a failure it cannot handle.
  rethrow?: boolean
  // A label for the fallback, so a screen reader user knows what failed.
  label?: string
}>()

const error = ref<Error | null>(null)

onErrorCaptured((err, instance, info) => {
  error.value = err as Error
  reportError(err, { instance, info })
  // Returning false stops propagation. Returning nothing, or true, lets
  // the error reach the parent boundary too, which is what a nested layout
  // needs in order to escalate.
  return !props.rethrow
})
</script>
<template>
  <!-- The fallback replaces the subtree. It is not an overlay and not  -->
  <!-- a banner: the content is genuinely gone, and the layout must   -->
  <!-- not reserve space for it.                                   -->
  <slot v-if="!error" />
  <component v-else-if="fallback" :is="fallback" :error="error" />
  <WidgetError v-else :error="error" :label="label"
              @retry="() => (error = null)" />
</template>

// ------------------------------------------------------------------
// (6) Where the boundaries go, and what they must NOT catch.
// ------------------------------------------------------------------
//
// Around: each independent panel, the route outlet, the whole app. A
// failed revenue chart must not take the navigation with it, and a failed
// navigation must not take the shell with it.
//
// NOT around: anything that must succeed for the page to mean anything,
// such as the row that identifies the record being edited. A boundary there
// turns a required field into an error panel with a Retry button, which is
// a worse experience than a clear failure.
//
// NOT around: a request rejection. An error boundary catches a thrown error,
// not a rejected promise stored in a ref. A data layer that puts `error` in
// its state object is therefore not caught by any of these boundaries, which
// is correct: a failed request is a state, and the view renders it.
//
// A reset boundary must be resettable. Retrying by reloading the page is a
// last resort and a poor answer to a click that was available.
<template>
  <ErrorBoundary label="'revenue chart'">
    <RevenueChart :series="series" />
  </ErrorBoundary>
</template>

// A failed route chunk is a navigation failure rather than a component
// error, so it is caught here. On a redeploy the old asset no longer
// exists, and a reload is the only recovery.
router.onError((err) => {
  if (err.message.includes('Failed to fetch dynamically imported module')) {
    location.reload()
  }
})

A skeleton is a prediction about layout and about timing. Predict the layout from the real component’s dimensions and the timing from a bounded expectation: a request that has produced nothing after a stated interval is a failure, and a skeleton that has been on screen for ten seconds is a worse report of that failure than an error message. Both halves matter, and the second is the one that is usually missing.

MechanismScales to a large tableRandom accessStable under concurrent writesAccessible
Offset paginationNo; the cost grows with the offsetYes, to a page numberNo; rows shift between pagesYes, with a page list
Keyset paginationYes; constant cost per pageNo, forward onlyYesYes, with a Load more control
Infinite scrollYesNoYes, with a keyset cursorPoor without an explicit control
Full loadNoYesYesYes
Loading indicatorBest forFailure mode when misused
SpinnerA short, unpredictable wait: a button press, a saveAn indefinite spinner on a request that will not complete, which reports nothing
Skeleton matching the contentA first load that will render a known shapeA skeleton of the wrong shape, which causes a larger reflow than no placeholder
Inline progress on existing contentA background refresh with data already on screenA full-screen spinner over content the user was already reading
NothingA refresh the user did not ask for and does not need to seeUsed for a first load, which then appears to have failed

Caveats. Infinite scroll without a keyset cursor silently duplicates and omits rows, because a row inserted above the viewport shifts every later row into the next page. The symptom is a list in which the same invoice appears twice, which is a data-integrity impression rather than a cosmetic one, and it is invisible until a user reports it. Keyset or nothing.

Caveats. onError catches errors thrown during a descendant’s render, its lifecycle hooks and its watchers. It does not catch a rejected promise that was assigned to a ref, an error inside an event handler, or an error in an asynchronous callback that runs after the render. A data layer that rejects and is caught upstream therefore produces no boundary, which is the correct division of responsibility and the reason 9.1 puts the error in the state object rather than throwing it.

Applies to. onError is available from Vue 3.0 and is the framework’s only error-containment mechanism; there is no ErrorB component in the core, and the wrapper in 9.6 is the pattern every such component implements. Vue Router 4 adds router. for failures in a navigation, which is a separate channel from a component error and is the one that catches a failed route chunk.

Exercises 9

  1. Build the unmanaged fetch from 9.1, navigate away during the request, and record whether the response still writes. Add an AbortCo and a sequence token and repeat, then change a filter twice quickly and record which response wins.
  2. Request a URL that returns 500 with an HTML body, then one that returns 422 with a JSON error object, and record what res.json()res.json() throws in each case. Implement the status-first client from 9.2 and confirm both produce a typed ApiE with the right status.
  3. Set VITE_A in a .env.production.env.production file, build, and search the emitted bundle for the value. Then remove it and confirm the behaviour you should have relied on from the start.
  4. Two components request the same key. Count the requests in the network panel, add a cache with an in-flight join, and repeat. Then unmount one of them mid-flight and confirm the other still receives its data.
  5. Apply the optimistic pattern to an archive, then fail the request from a test endpoint. Confirm the row is restored from the captured snapshot and that a message is shown. Repeat with the snapshot replaced by a recomputed value and record the difference.
  6. Build a list with offset pagination, insert a row at the top from another session, and record what the next page does. Repeat with a keyset cursor and record the difference.
  7. Wrap two independent panels in a boundary, throw inside one, and confirm the other is unaffected and the shell survives. Then assign a rejected promise to a ref instead of throwing, and record which boundary catches it.
  8. Render a table with a skeleton whose row height differs from the real table, and measure the layout shift. Match the dimensions and measure again.

10 · Rendering Performance

10.1 The reactive effect, dependency tracking and the scheduler10.2 nextTick and when it is required10.3 The virtual DOM, static rendering, v-once and v-memo10.4 Shallow reactivity, large lists and windowing10.5 Code splitting, async components and lazy hydration10.6 Performance mistakes and the measuring discipline

Rendering performance is not a set of tricks; it is a consequence of four mechanisms that are worth stating exactly: the reactive effect that collects dependencies, the scheduler that batches the resulting work into one microtask, the virtual DOM that turns component output into a comparable tree, and the template compiler that marks the parts of that tree which cannot change. This chapter describes each mechanism, the two runtimes they belong to (the browser and the server), and the interventions available when the defaults are insufficient. Every intervention listed here trades correctness or maintainability for work avoided, and each one is stated with the condition under which it stops being an optimisation. Nothing in this chapter should be applied before a measurement exists; 10.6 ends with the measuring discipline for exactly that reason.

The reactive effect, dependency tracking and the scheduler

A reactive object does not know what depends on it. A running effect does, and it learns by executing. While an effect function runs, every reactive property it reads is recorded against that effect in the currently active dependency set; when the property is written, the effect is looked up and scheduled. This is the whole of the tracking model, and every performance question in this chapter is downstream of it.

// The effect, in isolation from components. This is what a render
// function, a computed getter and a watch callback each become.
// src/lib/effect.ts
import { reactive, watchEffect, effectScope } from 'vue'

const state = reactive({ count: 0, items: [] as string[] })

// The effect RUNS IMMEDIATELY, collecting everything reactive it
// reads. Reads are recorded, writes inside an effect are also tracked
// and cause self-invalidation, which is why an effect must be pure.
const stop = watchEffect(() => {
  console.log('count is', state.count, 'items', state.items.length)
})

state.count = 1   // the effect is QUEUED, not run
stop()                      // the effect is stopped; no further triggers

// An explicit scope lets a group of effects be disposed together.
// This is the primitive composable authors should use: it is why a
// composable can be created outside a component (see 11.4).
const scope = effectScope()
scope.run(() => {
  watchEffect(() => console.log('scoped', state.count))
})
scope.stop()                // disposes every effect created inside

// The queueing behaviour, observable. Four synchronous writes produce
// ONE render, because the flush is a microtask.
// src/components/Counter.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, onRenderTracked, onRenderTriggered } from 'vue'

const a = ref(0)
const b = ref(0)
const c = ref(0)

// Development-only hooks. They report what the last render READ,
// which is the only reliable way to find an over-broad dependency.
onRenderTracked(e => console.log('tracked', e.key))
onRenderTriggered(e => console.log('triggered', e.key, e.type, e.oldValue, e.newValue))

function bumpAll() {
  a.value = 1
  b.value = 1     // same tick: queued, not yet run
  c.value = 1
  // one render, containing all three new values
}
</script>

<template>
  <button class="counter" @click="bumpAll">{{ a }} / {{ b }} / {{ c }}</button>
</template>

The set of dependencies collected is per-run, not permanent. If a branch of the effect function is not taken this time, the properties read on the other branch are not dependencies this time. A conditional read inside a wa callback or an effect therefore makes the dependency set itself conditional, and the classic symptom is a watcher that stops firing after an unrelated code change.

StageWhat happensConsequence for the author
CollectAn effect runs; every reactive read is added to the active dependency set keyed by target.Reads are the cost. A function that reads 100,000 properties during a render pays for 100,000 tracked accesses.
InvalidateA write marks the target dirty and looks up its subscriber effects. A computed in the chain is marked dirty rather than re-run.Writes are cheap and lazy. Nothing recomputes until something reads.
QueueThe subscriber effect is added to the scheduler queue, deduplicated by its job id, and a flush is scheduled as a promise microtask if one is not already pending.Many writes in one tick produce one re-render, not many.
FlushThe queue is walked. Jobs are sorted so that a parent component renders before its children and a child before its parent on update.Component update order is deterministic, which is what makes props and emits safe to use synchronously.
PatchThe component re-runs its render function, producing a new virtual node, and the renderer diffs it against the previous one.A component re-renders when any tracked value it read during the last render changes. Nothing narrower is possible.

Applies to. Everything in this subsection describes the Vue 3.5 scheduler, which is shared by the browser runtime and by the server renderer. The effect API used here (watch, effec, the development-only onRende and onRender hooks) is stable from Vue 3.0. The onRende and onRender hooks compile away entirely in a production build and are callable only from inside setupsetup.

Caveats. Microtask batching is a correctness feature, not a performance feature, and it should not be relied upon to hide an expensive synchronous write. If a single write causes a hundred components to re-render, batching will not help: the flush is one microtask, but the work inside it is the same hundred renders. Reducing the number of components that re-render is a different problem, and 10.3 and 10.6 address it.

nextTick and when it is required

next returns a promise that resolves after the current flush completes. It is required whenever a statement must observe the DOM after a state change, and it is the single most common cause of a flaky assertion in a component test. The rule is short: DOM reads belong after await nextTick()await nextTick(), and state reads do not.

SituationIs the DOM updated?Correct tool
Read a reactive value immediately after assigning itNot relevant; state is synchronousRead it directly. No await.
Read element.t after assigning stateNoawait nextTick()await nextTick()
Read a prop or an emitted-event payload after a child updateNot relevant; the parent’s value is synchronousRead it directly.
Read the DOM after an awaited request changed stateNoawait nextTick()await nextTick() after the assignment
Act on a value produced inside a wa callbackDepends on the watcher flush timingSee the flush-timing note below
Read the DOM inside a wa with flush: 'post’flush: 'post'YesNo await needed
// src/components/QuantityStepper.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, nextTick, useTemplateRef } from 'vue'

const qty = ref(1)
const input = useTemplateRef('qty')

// WRONG. The DOM still shows the old value at this point.
function badReset() {
  qty.value = 0
  console.log(input.value?.value)   // still "1"
}

// RIGHT. nextTick resolves after the flush, so the DOM is current.
async function goodReset() {
  qty.value = 0
  await nextTick()
  console.log(input.value?.value)   // "0"
  input.value?.focus()
}
</script>

<template>
  <input ref="qty" v-model.number="qty" type="number" />
  <button @click="goodReset">Reset and focus</button>
</template>

// Watcher flush timing, and where DOM reads are legal.
// 'pre'   the default. Runs BEFORE the owner component re-renders,
//         in the scheduler queue, parent before child.
// 'post'  runs AFTER the component has re-rendered. The DOM is current.
// 'sync'  deprecated and removed. Do not use.
//
// src/components/Editor.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, watch, onMounted } from 'vue'

const draft = ref('')
const server = ref('')

// pre: right place to synchronise state, wrong place to read the DOM.
watch(draft, (value) => {
  server.value = value.toUpperCase()
})

// post: right place to measure or restore DOM state, because the
// component has already re-rendered when it runs.
watch(() => props.records.length, async () => {
  await nextTick()
  listEl.value?.scrollTo({ top: listEl.value.scrollHeight })
}, { flush: 'post' })
</script>

In tests the equivalent of await nextTick()await nextTick() is await flushPromises()await flushPromises() from @vue/test-utils@vue/test-utils, which additionally drains a macrotask. The rule in 11.3 is that any assertion following an asynchronous state change awaits flushP exactly once, and no more; a second flush usually hides a real ordering bug rather than fixing one.

The virtual DOM, static rendering, v-once and v-memo

A component’s render function returns a plain object tree: a virtual node per element, carrying the element’s type, its props and its children, plus a patch flag recording which of those props are dynamic. Two vnodes are patched only when they are of the same type and key, and when they are patched the renderer compares the two prop objects and touches only the properties that differ. The virtual DOM is therefore a change-detection mechanism, not a rendering optimisation in itself: it trades memory and a comparison pass for the removal of imperative DOM code.

// What the compiler emits, and what the patcher therefore knows.
// A template with one dynamic binding:
//
//   <div :id="uid" class="card" v-if="visible">{{ label }}</div>
//
// becomes, in substance:
//
//   createElementBlock("div", { id: uid, class: "card" },
//     toDisplayString(label), 1024 /* CLASS */)
//
// The trailing number is a patchFlag bitfield. The renderer uses it to
// decide which comparison to run. The important consequence: a prop
// that is a STATIC STRING in the template costs nothing to re-check,
// because the compiler hoists it.
//
// Three things therefore do not re-render, and this is free:
//
//   1. A component whose props are all unchanged. `shouldUpdateComponent`
//      compares every prop; equal means bail out entirely.
//   2. A static sub-tree. The compiler emits a cached hoisted vnode.
//   3. A sub-tree marked with v-once, cached by the renderer after the
//      first render and never re-rendered again.
//
// Everything else DOES re-render, and the only lever the author has
// over a sub-tree that reads reactive state is to remove the reads.

// v-once: render once, then never again. The vnode is cached; the whole
// sub-tree below it is skipped for the lifetime of the instance.
// It is irreversible: no state change can revive it.
<template>
  <p v-once>Rendered at {{ mountedAt }} for session {{ sessionId }}</p>

  <div v-once>
    <span v-for="c in categories" :key="c.id">{{ c.label }}</span>
  </div>
</template>

// v-memo (3.2+): cache keyed by an explicit dependency array, and only
// while that array is unchanged. Unlike v-once it can be undone,
// because the array itself is a reactive dependency.
<template>
  <!-- A 10,000 row table that only depends on three values. -->
  <div v-for="row in rows" :key="row.id"
       v-memo="[sortKey, sortDir, selectedId]">
    {{ row.a }} · {{ row.b }} · {{ row.c }}
  </div>
</template>

// Prop stability. This single change removes N child re-renders where N
// is the length of the list, and it requires no directive at all.
//
// BEFORE. Every row receives a new activeId whenever the selection
// changes, so every row re-renders.
<!--
<ListItem v-for="item in list" :key="item.id"
          :id="item.id" :active-id="activeId" />
</!--
//
// AFTER. active is a boolean derived in the parent. For every row
// except the two whose boolean flipped, the prop is unchanged and
// the child bails out of its update.
<!--
<ListItem v-for="item in list" :key="item.id"
          :id="item.id" :active="item.id === activeId" />
</!--
TechniqueCondition checkedRevocableUse when
Static sub-treeNothing: the compiler proved it is constantNot applicableAlways. It requires no author action.
Unchanged propsShallow equality of every propYes, per updateAlways. Design props so they stay stable: see the table below.
v-oNothing after the first renderNoContent that depends on runtime data and will never change: a session identifier, a generated watermark, a printed snapshot.
v-mThe supplied dependency array, by hasChYesA large list that depends on a small, explicit set of values.
Child component splitNothing: the parent never re-rendersYesA region that depends on state the parent also depends on. This is the general fix and the one that is easiest to apply correctly.

Caveats. v-m is a promise the author makes to the runtime: the sub-tree is guaranteed not to depend on anything not listed. Omit a dependency and the list silently stops updating when it should. Because a memoised vnode is reused, its children are not re-rendered, so anything with side effects, a r focus, a transition or a component that maintains its own state inside the memoised sub-tree will misbehave. Use it on presentational rows and nowhere else. v-o has the same class of hazard with no escape hatch at all.

Applies to. v-m requires Vue 3.2 or later and must be used on the element inside a v-, not on the v- element itself. v-o is available from Vue 3.0. Both are compiler-level features: they have no runtime options and no counterpart in the Options API.

Shallow reactivity, large lists and windowing

Reactivity is deep by default, which means every property access on a nested object is a proxy access with a dependency lookup. That is the right default for application state and the wrong one for a large structure that is written rarely and read in bulk: a 50,000-row dataset held in a deep r pays the tracking cost on every render that touches it. The shallow APIs make the root reactive and the interior inert, which requires the discipline of replacing the root rather than mutating the interior.

// The four ways to take a large structure out of deep tracking, and
// what each one costs.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, shallowRef, shallowReactive, markRaw, triggerRef } from 'vue'
import { parse } from './geojson'

// (1) shallowRef. The ROOT is tracked, the interior is not.
//     Reading .value is one access regardless of size. The canonical
//     use is a large, immutable, rarely-changing payload.
const regions = shallowRef<Region[]>([])

// (2) shallowReactive. Object form; property reads on the ROOT are
//     tracked, nested objects are returned as-is.
const cache = shallowReactive({ hits: 0, misses: 0 })
cache.hits = 1              // tracked: triggers a re-render
cache.entries[0].x = 1   // NOT tracked

// (3) markRaw. Excludes a value from the proxy system permanently.
//     Correct for a third-party class instance: Map, Set, a chart
//     library, a canvas context. Wrapping these in a proxy breaks them.
const engine = markRaw(new LayoutEngine())
const cacheMap = markRaw(new Map<string, Node>())

// (4) triggerRef. Escape hatch: notify watchers manually after a
//     mutation that shallowRef cannot see.
regions.value = parse(payload)
regions.value[0].name = 'renamed'   // no trigger
triggerRef(regions)                          // manual trigger
</script>

// List windowing. A container with a fixed row height, a spacer that
// preserves the scrollbar, and only the visible slice rendered.
// This is the minimum viable implementation; production use belongs in
// a library (see the note below), because variable row heights,
// focus management and accessibility are not solved here.
<!-- src/components/WindowedList.vue -->
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, computed, shallowRef, useTemplateRef } from 'vue'

const props = withDefaults(defineProps<Row[]>>(), { itemHeight: 32, overscan: 8 })
const rows = shallowRef<Row[]>([])
const scrollTop = ref(0)
const viewportHeight = ref(0)
const scroller = useTemplateRef('scroller')

function setRows(next: Row[]) {
  rows.value = next                     // replace, never push
}

const start = computed(() =>
  Math.max(0, Math.floor(scrollTop.value / props.itemHeight) - props.overscan))
const end = computed(() =>
  Math.min(rows.value.length,
           Math.ceil((scrollTop.value + viewportHeight.value) / props.itemHeight) + props.overscan))
const visible = computed(() => rows.value.slice(start.value, end.value))
const padTop = computed(() => start.value * props.itemHeight)
const padBottom = computed(() => (rows.value.length - end.value) * props.itemHeight)
</script>

<template>
  <div ref="scroller" class="scroller" @scroll="scrollTop = $event.target.scrollTop">
    <div :style="{ paddingTop: padTop + 'px', paddingBottom: padBottom + 'px' }">
      <div v-for="(row, i) in visible" :key="row.id"
           :style="{ height: itemHeight + 'px' }">
        <slot :row="row" :index="start + i" />
      </div>
    </div>
  </div>
</template>
</style scoped>
.scroller { height: 480px; overflow-y: auto; contain: strict; }
</style>
APITracked at the rootTracked insideUpdate method
ref(obj)ref(obj)YesYes, on every accessAny mutation
reactive(obj)reactive(obj)YesYes, on every accessAny mutation
shallYesNoReplace .value.value, or trigg
shallowYesNoReplace a root property, or trigg
marNoNoNothing triggers; it is inert by design

Caveats. Windowing changes the accessibility contract. A virtualised list removes rows from the accessibility tree, so a screen reader cannot navigate to them and Ctrl+EndCtrl+End no longer reaches the end of the data. Keyboard navigation, focus restoration when a row scrolls out and is replaced, and a non-visual indication of position must be implemented deliberately. contain: strictcontain: strict is what makes the scroll container cheap to lay out, but it also means a row taller than itemH will be clipped. Fixed-height windowing is therefore only correct for fixed-height rows; variable-height rows require measurement and a two-pass layout.

Requirements. For production use, prefer a maintained windowing library over the sketch above. The libraries that pair with Vue 3.5 are vue-virtua, vue-virtual and VVirtualListVVirtualList. All three require stable, unique keys on the rows and a predictable row height; none of them can virtualise content inside a native scroll container the browser has already laid out, so the scroll container must be an element the library owns.

Code splitting, async components and lazy hydration

Code splitting is a build-time decision that a component declaration makes possible. A static imp is a hard edge in the module graph and the bundler must place it in the initial chunk; a dynamic import()import() is a soft edge, and the bundler may emit it as a separate chunk fetched on demand. defineAsyn is the component-shaped wrapper over a dynamic import, and it adds a loading state, an error state, a timeout and a retry policy.

// The full option set, with the defaults written out, because the defaults
// are the part people get wrong.
// src/components/ReportView.ts
import {
  defineAsyncComponent, hydrateOnVisible, hydrateOnInteraction
} from 'vue'
import ReportSkeleton from './ReportSkeleton.vue'
import ReportError from './ReportError.vue'

const ReportView = defineAsyncComponent({
  loader: () => import('./ReportView.vue'),

  // delay: 200 by default. Do not set 0 unless the chunk is on the
  // same origin and already warm; otherwise every navigation shows a
  // flash of a loading state.
  delay: 120,
  loadingComponent: ReportSkeleton,
  errorComponent: ReportError,

  // timeout: Infinity by default, meaning a failed chunk load shows no
  // error component at all. A finite timeout is what turns a hung
  // network into a visible failure.
  timeout: 10000,

  // onError receives the error, a retry thunk, a fail thunk and the
  // attempt count. Retrying a dynamic import is safe: the module
  // registry caches the resolved module, so a retry after a
  // network failure re-requests, but a retry after success is a
  // cache hit.
  onError(error, retry, fail, attempts) {
    if (attempts <= 3 && error.name !== 'SyntaxError') retry()
    else fail()
  },

  // suspensible: true by default, which means the nearest Suspense
  // boundary owns the loading and error state instead of the options
  // above. Set false when the component must manage its own states.
  suspensible: false,

  // hydrate applies only to server-rendered markup. See below.
  hydrate: hydrateOnVisible({ rootMargin: '200px' })
})

// Lazy hydration (Vue 3.5+). On a server-rendered page the HTML arrives
// complete but the JavaScript for a sub-tree may not. These strategies
// decide WHEN that JavaScript runs, not whether it runs.
//
// hydrateOnVisible()           IntersectionObserver; the component hydrates
//                             when its element enters the viewport.
//                             Accepts an IntersectionObserverInit, so
//                             { rootMargin: '200px' } is valid.
// hydrateOnIdle()              requestIdleCallback; the default strategy
//                             for content below the fold.
// hydrateOnMediaQuery(query)   matchMedia; a mobile-only widget on a
//                             page that also has a desktop variant.
// hydrateOnInteraction(evs)    Hydrates on an event, then REPLAYS the
//                             event so the first click is not lost.
//                             Accepts a string or an array.
//
// A custom strategy receives (hydrate, forEachElement) and returns an
// optional teardown, which is what stops an IntersectionObserver
// from outliving an unmounted component.
//
// src/components/CommentsThread.ts
import { defineAsyncComponent, hydrateOnInteraction } from 'vue'

const Comments = defineAsyncComponent({
  loader: () => import('./CommentsThread.vue'),
  // The first click hydrates the component and is then re-dispatched
  // against the now-live handlers, so the user never clicks twice.
  hydrate: hydrateOnInteraction(['click', 'focus'])
})

A loading state costs a component tree of its own. If loadingC is a spinner with a fixed height and the real content is taller, the swap produces layout shift. Skeletons that match the final layout in height, width and line count are worth the extra markup, and 8.6 covers the same problem for data loading.

ScenarioToolNotes
A route component not needed on first paintVue Router lazy routePreferred over defineAsyn for routes: the router already resolves the chunk and exposes onE and a navigation error hook.
A heavy widget inside a routedefineAsynThe split point is the component, so its dependencies travel with it.
A heavy pure-JavaScript module, not a componentDynamic import()import() in a lifecycle hook or an event handlerNo wrapper needed; the module is simply not in the initial chunk.
Server-rendered page, below-the-fold islandhyd strategy3.5+. Requires SSR; on a client-rendered page there is nothing to hydrate.
A component that blocks its own subtreedefineAsyn with suspensible: truesuspensible: trueThe nearest Susp owns the fallback; the component’s own loadingC is ignored.

Applies to. The hyd option and the hydrateO, hydrat, hydrateOn and hydrateOnI strategy factories are Vue 3.5 additions. The strategies are separate named exports specifically so that a bundle that uses none of them can tree-shake all of them. The Hydratio type is exported for a custom strategy.

Caveats. Code splitting moves cost, it does not remove it. A route-level split that leaves the shared dependency in the initial chunk has made two requests instead of one. A deferred chunk that is requested on every navigation and never cached produces a worse experience than a larger initial bundle. And a dynamic import is a network request that can fail in a way a static import cannot, which is why tim and onE exist.

Performance mistakes and the measuring discipline

The recurring performance defects in Vue applications are not exotic. They are a watcher that duplicates work a computed already does, a deep watcher on a large object, an object literal constructed in a template expression, and a computed with a side effect. Each is cheap to recognise and each is cheap to fix; none of them is worth fixing speculatively.

// Five defects, each with its fix on the right. The left column is
// correct code that is slower than the right column for a reason that
// is invisible in the source.

// (1) A WATCHER WHERE A COMPUTED BELONGS. A watcher runs an effect and
//     then a render; a computed participates in the render and is lazy.
<!-- SLOW -->
<script setup>
const full = ref('')
watch([first, last], () => { full.value = `${first.value} ${last.value}` })
</script>

<!-- FAST -->
<script setup>
// computed is lazy and cached: it recomputes only if a dependency
// changed since the last read, and it does not wake a component
// until something reads it.
const full = computed(() => `${first.value} ${last.value}`)
</script>

// (2) A DEEP WATCHER USED AS A CHANGE NOTIFICATION. deep: true traverses
//     the whole object on every change to walk it. The callback then runs
//     for nested mutations that may not affect the consumer at all.
watch(state, () => save(state), { deep: true })

//     Fix: watch the one key the consumer cares about.
watch(() => state.draftText, () => save(state))

//     And in 3.5+, use onWatcherCleanup instead of a manual aborted flag.
watch(id, (newId) => {
  const controller = new AbortController()
  fetch(`/api/${newId}`, { signal: controller.signal })
  onWatcherCleanup(() => controller.abort())
})

// (3) AN OBJECT BUILT IN A TEMPLATE EXPRESSION. A template expression is
//     re-evaluated on every render, so this allocates a fresh object each
//     time and hands the child a prop that is never equal to the last.
<DataGrid :columns="[{ key: 'a' }, { key: 'b' }]" :rows="rows" />

<!-- Hoist it. The array is then a stable reference and the child -->
<!-- bails out of its update when rows is unchanged.          -->
<DataGrid :columns="columns" :rows="rows" />

// (4) A COMPUTED WITH A SIDE EFFECT. A computed must be pure. It may run
//     zero times if nothing reads it, and it may be re-run by the runtime.
const total = computed(() => {
  analytics.track('cart-viewed')   // fires an unknown
  // number of times
  return items.value.reduce(sum, 0)
})

//     Fix: the effect is a watchEffect, or an explicit call.
watchEffect(() => { analytics.track('cart-viewed', total.value) })

// (5) A RENDERLESS WRAPPER IN A HOT LOOP. A component instance costs far
//     more than a plain element. In a 2,000-row table, one unnecessary
//     wrapper per row is 2,000 instances for no benefit.

// The measuring discipline. Four tools, in the order they should be used.
//
// (1) Vue DevTools, Components panel. It shows, per component, the time
//     spent rendering, the number of renders, and whether the component
//     re-rendered. A component that re-renders on every keystroke but
//     does not read the input is the finding.
//
// (2) app.config.performance (3.5+). Emits Vue performance markers into
//     the browser's own performance timeline, so a Vue frame can be lined
//     up with layout, paint and long tasks.
// src/main.ts
import { createApp } from 'vue'
import App from './App.vue'

const app = createApp(App)

if (import.meta.env.DEV) {
  // Records the initial mount and every subsequent update with
  // user-timing marks, and warns when a render blocks the main
  // thread for more than the configured budget.
  app.config.performance = true
}
app.mount('#app')

// (3) The browser's own measurements, for page load rather than for
//     updates: Largest Contentful Paint and Interaction to Next Paint.
//     A fast framework with a 900 kB initial bundle still has a bad LCP.
//
// (4) A production build. A development build is not slower by a constant
//     factor: it disables the template pre-compilation, keeps the
//     reactivity debugger, adds validation to props, and does not
//     tree-shake. Profile the artefact, not the dev server.
SymptomMost likely causeFirst thing to check
Typing in a search box stuttersA deep watcher or a large computed recomputing per keystrokeDevTools: which component re-renders, and how long its render takes
A list scrolls poorlyToo many DOM nodes, or no con on the scroll containerCount of live elements; whether windowing is required at all
First load is slow, updates are fineInitial bundle size, not the framework14.6: bundle analysis and the size budget
A page flashes, then re-rendersClient-only rendering of content that should have been in the HTML14.4: the SSR and hydration boundary
One mutation triggers a full page re-renderState that lives above the component tree, or a store read in a high-level componentDevTools component tree: find the highest common ancestor that re-rendered
Nothing is slow but memory growsEffects or scopes not disposed; a watcher created outside a component12.x: onScope and effect scopes (see 11.4)

Before reaching for a directive, try the cheapest structural fix: move the state down. A value that only a leaf needs should live in the leaf. A value three levels of components below the one that changes should be provided or stored near its consumer. The virtual DOM will not save a component that genuinely re-renders, and no directive makes a re-render disappear; removing the dependency does.

Exercises 10

  1. Build a component with three refs and a render that reads all three. Assign to all three in one click handler and count the renders with a development-only counter. Repeat with the three assignments separated by an aw and record the render count.
  2. Attach onRende to a component and log every tracked key. Find a key you did not expect the component to depend on, remove that read, and confirm the trigger list shrinks.
  3. Write a component that logs an element’s textC immediately after changing state, then after await nextTick()await nextTick(). Explain the difference in terms of the scheduler, not in terms of the browser.
  4. Take a list of 200 rows and add an object literal to a child prop. Count re-renders on a selection change. Hoist the literal to a module constant and repeat.
  5. Add v-o to a sub-tree that reads a counter, then change the counter. Record what happens, and state the one situation in which v-o is the correct choice.
  6. Replace a computed-plus-watcher pair with a single computed and measure. Then replace a deep watcher with a single-key watcher and record how many times the callback fires under nested mutation.
  7. Put a 20,000-row array in a r and render a count in the template. Time the render. Repeat with shallowRefshallowRef. Then add windowing and record the change in both render time and maximum scroll height.
  8. Build an async component with a 200 ms artificial loader delay and a 0 ms delaydelay. Observe the flash. Set de to 200 and observe its absence. Record which one is correct for a chunk on the same origin.
  9. Run a production build with app.config.performance = trueapp.config.performance = true behind an environment check, and read the user-timing marks in the browser performance panel for a single route change. Compare against the development server and record the difference.
  10. Write a template that constructs an inline array in a binding and one that does not. Use the Vue DevTools component panel to show that the first re-renders the child on every parent render and the second does not.

11 · Testing

11.1 The test stack and the Vitest configuration11.2 mount and shallowMount: output, props and emitted events11.3 flushPromises and asynchronous assertions11.4 Composable testing with an effect scope11.5 Pinia, network and router in tests11.6 Playwright end to end and the division of labour

Vue has no test framework of its own. It has a component contract that can be exercised, and it has official libraries that make that exercise ergonomic. The stack pinned here is Vitest 3 as the runner, @vue/test-utils@vue/test-utils 2.4.6 for component mounting, js or happ for the DOM, and Playwright for the browser. This chapter covers configuration, the two mounting functions, the asynchronous assertion rule that decides whether a test is honest, composable testing without a component, the three integrations that break most suites (Pinia, fe and the router), and the boundary between unit and end-to-end coverage.

The test stack and the Vitest configuration

Vitest runs on top of the Vite configuration, so the plugin that compiles single-file components is already present and no separate build step is required for tests. Three environment choices exist: no for pure logic, js for a complete and standards-faithful DOM, and happ for a faster and deliberately incomplete one. The default for a Vue project should be one of the two DOM environments, declared globally, so that a test file never has to opt in.

// vite.config.ts -- one config for the dev server, the build and the
// tests. Vitest reads it, so the Vue plugin is already installed.
import { fileURLToPath, URL } from 'node:url'
import { defineConfig } from 'vite'
import vue from '@vitejs/plugin-vue'

export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [vue()],
  resolve: {
    alias: { '@': fileURLToPath(new URL('./src', import.meta.url)) }
  },
  test: {
    // 'jsdom' is the safe default. 'happy-dom' is faster and is a
    // reasonable choice for a suite that never touches layout,
    // ResizeObserver, IntersectionObserver or focus order.
    environment: 'jsdom',

    // Needed only for the auto-imported describe/it/expect. Explicit
    // imports from 'vitest' are preferred in a library; globals are
    // convenient in an application. Choose one and be consistent.
    globals: true,

    setupFiles: ['./test/setup.ts'],
    include: ['src/**/*.{test,spec}.{ts,tsx}'],
    exclude: ['node_modules', 'dist', 'e2e/**'],

    // jsdom does not implement these. Components that use them must
    // not crash on mount, so stub them once, globally.
    environmentOptions: {
      jsdom: { url: 'http://localhost/' }
    },

    coverage: {
      provider: 'v8',
      reporter: ['text', 'html'],
      include: ['src/**'],
      thresholds: { lines: 80, branches: 75 }
    }
  }
})

// test/setup.ts -- imported before every test file.
import { config } from '@vue/test-utils'
import { vi, afterEach } from 'vitest'
import { afterEach as vueAfterEach } from '@vue/test-utils'

// Unmount every wrapper after each test. Without this a leaked
// component keeps a scheduler job alive and the next test inherits
// its watchers, which is a genuinely baffling failure mode.
vueAfterEach(() => { config.global.stubs = {} })

afterEach(() => {
  vi.restoreAllMocks()
  vi.unstubAllGlobals()
  vi.useRealTimers()
})

// A fixed timezone and locale. A test that formats a date without
// pinning these fails on a machine, not in review.
process.env.TZ = 'UTC'

// jsdom gaps that must exist before any component mounts.
if (!('matchMedia' in window)) {
  window.matchMedia = vi.fn().mockImplementation((query: string) => ({
    matches: false, media: query, onchange: null,
    addEventListener: function() {}, removeEventListener: function() {},
    addListener: function() {}, removeListener: function() {},
    dispatchEvent: function() { return false }
  }))
}
if (!('ResizeObserver' in window)) {
  window.ResizeObserver = class {
    observe() {} unobserve() {} disconnect() {}
  } as unknown as typeof ResizeObserver
}

Requirements. environment: 'jsdom’environment: 'jsdom' requires the js package; 'happy-dom''happy-dom' requires happy-domhappy-dom. Vitest 3 does not bundle either. Both are declared as development dependencies. The te block in vite.co requires the vitest/configvitest/config type import rather than vi for editor support of the block; if the editor does not resolve it, create a separate vitest.c that imports define from vitest/configvitest/config and re-exports the same plugin and alias block.

EnvironmentFidelitySpeedChoose it when
noNo DOMFastestTesting a composable, a store, a pure function or an API client
jsClose; the reference implementationSlowerDefault for an application. Layout is still zero, so anything measured from layout is meaningless
happPartial; faster, smaller surfaceFastA large suite where no test depends on the missing APIs

mount and shallowMount: output, props and emitted events

A component test has three surfaces: the rendered DOM, the props the parent passes, and the events the component emits. mo renders the whole child tree; shallo renders the component under test and replaces every child component with a stub that records its props and events without rendering. The distinction is a trade between realism and specificity, and it should be made per test rather than per file.

// Output, props and events, with full mount.
// tests/components/InvoiceRow.spec.ts
import { mount } from '@vue/test-utils'
import InvoiceRow from '@/components/InvoiceRow.vue'
import { describe, it, expect } from 'vitest'

const invoice = {
  id: 'inv_1', number: 'INV-0001', total: 1200,
  currency: 'EUR', status: 'due' as const
}

describe('InvoiceRow', () => {
  it('renders the number and a formatted total', () => {
    const wrapper = mount(InvoiceRow, { props: { invoice, locale: 'de-DE' } })
    // get is a CSS query. Prefer the accessible queries below.
    expect(wrapper.get('[data-testid="total"]'').text()).toBe('1.200,00')
    expect(wrapper.text()).toContain('INV-0001')
  })

  it('emits select with the invoice id, not the object', async () => {
    const wrapper = mount(InvoiceRow, { props: { invoice } })
    await wrapper.get('button[data-action="select"]').trigger('click')

    // emitted() reads the recorded event log. It is synchronous with
    // respect to the DOM: the handler ran when trigger() was awaited.
    expect(wrapper.emitted('select')).toEqual([['inv_1']])
    expect(wrapper.emitted('select')![0]).toEqual(['inv_1'])
  })

  it('re-renders when the prop changes', async () => {
    const wrapper = mount(InvoiceRow, { props: { invoice } })
    await wrapper.setProps({ invoice: { ...invoice, total: 2400 } })
    expect(wrapper.get('[data-testid="total"]'').text()).toBe('2.400,00')
  })
})

// shallowMount replaces every child component with a stub. The parent's
// own markup is real; its children's is not.
// tests/components/InvoiceList.spec.ts
import { shallowMount } from '@vue/test-utils'
import InvoiceList from '@/components/InvoiceList.vue'

const wrapper = shallowMount(InvoiceList, {
  props: { rows: [1, 2, 3] }
})

// Each stub is findable by its registered name, and its props and
// emitted events are observable without rendering it.
const rows = wrapper.findAllComponents({ name: 'InvoiceRow' })
expect(rows).toHaveLength(3)
expect(rows[0].props('invoice'')).toMatchObject({ id: 1 })

// Emulate what a child would emit, and assert the parent's handler.
rows[0].vm.$emit('select', 1)
expect(wrapper.emitted('select')).toEqual([[1]])

// Keep a specific child real where its own output is the thing under
// test. This is the recommended pattern: shallow by default, one level
// deep where the behaviour lives.
const deep = shallowMount(InvoiceList, {
  props: { rows: [1] },
  global: { stubs: { InvoiceRow: false } }
})

// Query by role, not by class. The component's classes are free to
// change; its accessible name is a contract the test should share
// with the assistive-technology user.
//
// @vue/test-utils 2.4.6 does not bundle a role engine, so two routes
// exist. Either add Testing Library's DOM implementation, or keep a
// thin semantic layer:
//
//   npm i -D @testing-library/dom @testing-library/user-event
//
// and in the test:
import { screen } from '@testing-library/dom'
import userEvent from '@testing-library/user-event'

const wrapper = mount(InvoiceRow, { props: { invoice }, attachTo: document.body })
const user = userEvent.setup()

// Accessible name, then role. Not ".btn-primary:nth-child(2)".
await user.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Select invoice INV-0001' }))
expect(wrapper.emitted('select')).toEqual([['inv_1']])

// attachTo matters: user-event checks pointer-events and visibility,
// and a detached element reports zero dimensions in jsdom.
wrapper.unmount()
NeedAPIReturnsThrows if absent
One element by CSSgDOMWrYes
One element by CSS, may be absentfiDOMWrNo
Many by CSSfinDOMWrapper[]DOMWrapper[]No
A child component instancefindCo, findAllCVueWrOptional
The emitted event logemitted(name?)emitted(name?)Array of argument arraysNo
Async child resolutionfindCo (returns a promise-like)Promise of a wrapperNo
Global plugin or stubglobal., globalMount optionsNot applicable

Mount a component with exactly the props a user could produce. A test that mounts with ten props to satisfy one assertion will not fail when the other nine are wrong, and it will pass while the real parent cannot construct the component. If a prop is required, the mount helper should have no default for it.

flushPromises and asynchronous assertions

An assertion that follows an asynchronous state change is only valid if every promise the change depends on has settled and the scheduler has flushed. A test that omits this step is not a slow test; it is a test that asserts nothing, because the DOM it inspects is the pre-change DOM. The rule is that tri is awaited and flushP is awaited, exactly once, before reading the result.

// The three cases, and what each requires.
//
// (a) A synchronous handler. trigger() is enough.
await wrapper.get('button').trigger('click')
expect(wrapper.text()).toBe('1')

// (b) A handler that awaits a resolved promise. One flush.
async function onClick() {
  pending.value = true
  result.value = await api.load()      // a microtask
  pending.value = false
}
await wrapper.get('button').trigger('click')
await flushPromises()                  // required
expect(wrapper.text()).toBe('done')

// (c) A handler driven by fake timers. Advance timers AND flush.
vi.useFakeTimers()
await wrapper.get('button').trigger('click')
await vi.runAllTimersAsync()           // runs timers and
//                                                    their microtasks
expect(wrapper.text()).toBe('done')

// A complete asynchronous test, with the network stubbed at the global.
// tests/composables/useInvoices.spec.ts
import { flushPromises } from '@vue/test-utils'
import { ref } from 'vue'
import { describe, it, expect, vi, beforeEach } from 'vitest'
import { useInvoices } from '@/composables/useInvoices'

describe('useInvoices', () => {
  beforeEach(() => {
    // vi.stubGlobal installs the property and unstubAllGlobals
    // removes it, so no test can leak a fetch into the next.
    vi.stubGlobal('fetch', vi.fn())
  })

  it('exposes pending while the request is in flight', async () => {
    let release!: (v: unknown) => void
    vi.mocked(fetch).mockReturnValue(new Promise(r => { release = r }) as never)

    const { rows, pending, error, load } = useInvoices()

    const running = load()
    await flushPromises()                  // the fetch has
    //                                                    been called
    expect(pending.value).toBe(true)
    expect(rows.value).toEqual([])

    release({ ok: true, json: () => Promise.resolve([{ id: 'a' }]) })
    await running
    await flushPromises()

    expect(pending.value).toBe(false)
    expect(rows.value).toEqual([{ id: 'a' }])
    expect(error.value).toBe(null)
  })
})

Caveats. Adding a second await flushPromises()await flushPromises() to a failing assertion usually hides an ordering bug rather than fixing it: the component has two renders where one was intended, and the second flush is the test agreeing with the code. A failing test should be read as a description of what the component actually does; the fix belongs in the component unless the specification genuinely requires two ticks. flushP resolves after Vue’s scheduler has drained and a macrotask has elapsed, so under vi.useFakeTimers()vi.useFakeTimers() it must be combined with vi.advanceT or vi.runAllTimersAsyncvi.runAllTimersAsync.

Applies to. flushP is exported from @vue/test-utils@vue/test-utils 2.x, not from vuevue. It is a test utility with no production counterpart. vi.stu, vi.m and vi.runAllT are Vitest 3 APIs; Vitest 1 used vi.advanceT plus an explicit flush in a different order.

Composable testing with an effect scope

A composable that uses lifecycle hooks can only be called inside a component, which makes it awkward to test. A composable that uses onScope can be called inside any effec, which is the correct unit under test and produces a test with no component in it at all. The requirement on the composable author is therefore specific: register cleanup with onScope, never with onUnm alone, or the composable is untestable in isolation.

// A composable written so that it can be tested without a component.
// src/composables/usePolling.ts
import { ref, onScopeDispose, shallowRef } from 'vue'

export interface Polling {
  data: Ref<string | null>
  error: Ref<Error | null>
  start: () => void
  stop: () => void
}

export function usePolling(url: string, everyMs = 5000): Polling {
  const data = shallowRef<string | null>(null)
  const error = ref<Error | null>(null)
  let handle: ReturnType<typeof setInterval> | undefined

  async function tick() {
    try {
      const res = await fetch(url)
      if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`)
      data.value = await res.text()
      error.value = null
    } catch (e) {
      error.value = e as Error
    }
  }

  function start() { if (!handle) handle = setInterval(tick, everyMs) }
  function stop()  { if (handle) { clearInterval(handle); handle = undefined } }

  // Fires when the enclosing scope stops, whether that is a component
  // unmount or a test's scope.stop(). There is no component in this
  // file at all, so onUnmounted would have thrown.
  onScopeDispose(stop)

  return { data, error, start, stop }
}

// The test. A scope, a fake clock, and no mount().
// src/composables/usePolling.spec.ts
import { effectScope } from 'vue'
import { flushPromises } from '@vue/test-utils'
import { describe, it, expect, vi, afterEach } from 'vitest'
import { usePolling } from './usePolling'

describe('usePolling', () => {
  // Every test gets its own scope. Stopping it in afterEach is the
  // equivalent of unmounting a component.
  let scope: ReturnType<typeof effectScope>

  afterEach(() => scope.stop())

  function inScope() {
    scope = effectScope()
    return scope.run(() => usePolling('/api/health', 1000))!
  }

  it('polls on the interval and stores the body', async () => {
    vi.useFakeTimers()
    vi.stubGlobal('fetch', vi.fn().mockResolvedValue({
      ok: true, status: 200, text: () => Promise.resolve('ok')
    }))

    const polling = inScope()
    polling.start()

    await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(1000)
    await flushPromises()

    expect(fetch).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1)
    expect(polling.data.value).toBe('ok')
  })

  it('stops when the scope stops', async () => {
    vi.useFakeTimers()
    vi.stubGlobal('fetch', vi.fn().mockResolvedValue({ ok: true, text: () => 'ok' }))

    const polling = inScope()
    polling.start()
    scope.stop()

    await vi.advanceTimersByTimeAsync(5000)
    expect(fetch).not.toHaveBeenCalled()
  })
})
Cleanup registrationComponent context requiredTestable with an effect scopeUse this when
onScopeNoYesAlways, inside a composable
onUnmYesNo, it warns and does nothingOnly in a component that is a component
onBeforYesNoOnly for DOM work that must happen before removal
NothingNoYes, but leaksNever for a timer, listener or observer

A composable that returns refs and a composable that returns a reactive object behave differently under destructuring for the reason given in 7.4. Returning a plain object of refs is the safer default for a composable, because the caller can then use toR or store deliberately rather than accidentally.

Pinia, network and router in tests

Three dependencies break component tests in predictable ways: a store requires an active Pinia instance, fe must be replaced before the component mounts, and a component that calls useR outside an installed router throws on setup. Each has a canonical fixture, and the fixtures are small enough to write once in test/setup.tstest/setup.ts.

// Pinia. Two levels of control: stub the actions, or run them for real.
// tests/setup.ts
import { createPinia } from 'pinia'
import { createTestingPinia } from '@pinia/testing'

// (a) Default: a real Pinia. Actions run, state persists, getters work.
export function freshPinia() { return createPinia() }

// (b) stubActions: false keeps the action bodies but suppresses network
//     effects by replacing $patch. This is the setting to prefer when
//     the action's own logic is under test.
export function testingPinia(opts = {}) {
  return createTestingPinia({ createSpy: vi.fn, stubActions: false, ...opts })
}

// tests/stores/invoice.spec.ts
import { setActivePinia, createPinia } from 'pinia'

beforeEach(() => { setActivePinia(createPinia()) })   // required: without an
//                                                    active Pinia,
//                                                    useStore() throws
const store = useInvoiceStore()
store.items = [{ id: 'a', total: 10 }]
expect(store.count).toBe(1)                       // a real getter

// Inside a component test, pass the instance through global.plugins.
const wrapper = mount(InvoiceSummary, {
  global: { plugins: [createTestingPinia({ stubActions: true })] }
})
expect(wrapper.text()).toBe('0 items')            // actions are spies

// The network, two ways.
//
// (a) A global stub. Fine for a single test, for a component that
//     calls fetch directly, and for asserting the request itself.
vi.stubGlobal('fetch', vi.fn().mockResolvedValue(
  new Response(JSON.stringify([{ id: 'a' }]), {
    status: 200, headers: { 'content-type': 'application/json' }
  })
))
expect(fetch).toHaveBeenCalledWith('/api/invoices', expect.any(Object))

// (b) Mock Service Worker. Correct once more than one test needs a
//     different response for the same URL, because the handler is
//     declared once and intercepts at the network layer.
// test/server.ts
import { setupServer } from 'msw/node'
import { http, HttpResponse } from 'msw'

export const server = setupServer(
  http.get('*/api/invoices', () => HttpResponse.json([{ id: 'a' }])),
  http.post('*/api/invoices', async ({ request }) => {
    const body = await request.json()
    return HttpResponse.json({ id: 'new' }, { status: 201 })
  })
)

// test/setup.ts
beforeAll(() => server.listen({ onUnhandledRequest: 'error' }))
afterEach(() => server.resetHandlers())
afterAll(() => server.close())

// A per-test override, which is the main reason to use msw at all.
server.use(http.get('*/api/invoices', () =>
  HttpResponse.json({ message: 'boom' }, { status: 500 })))

// The router. A memory history is mandatory in a test: a browser history
// in jsdom cannot be navigated, and a real history pollutes the
// global URL between tests.
// tests/helpers/router.ts
import { createRouter, createMemoryHistory } from 'vue-router'
import Home from '@/views/HomeView.vue'
import Detail from '@/views/DetailView.vue'

export function makeRouter(initial = '/') {
  const router = createRouter({
    history: createMemoryHistory(),
    routes: [
      { path: '/', component: Home },
      { path: '/invoices/:id', name: 'invoice',
        component: Detail, props: true }
    ]
  })
  router.push(initial)
  return router
}

// A component that calls useRouter() must be mounted with the plugin
// installed AND with an initial navigation completed, or route
// props are undefined during the first render.
const router = makeRouter('/invoices/42')
await router.isReady()
const wrapper = mount(Detail, {
  global: { plugins: [router] },
  props: { id: '42' }
})
expect(wrapper.text()).toContain('Invoice 42')

Vue Routerglobal.plugins: [makeRouter(path)]global.plugins: [makeRouter(path)] and await router.isReady()await router.isReady()useRouter() requires an app contextuseRouter() requires an app context, or undefined route params on the first render
