Timersvi.useFakeTimers()vi.useFakeTimers() and vi.useRealTimers()vi.useRealTimers() in afteA later test that hangs because a real timer is pending

Playwright end to end and the division of labour

A unit test can prove that a component renders and emits. It cannot prove that the build works, that the router resolves a deep link, that the server sends the header the API needs, or that a cookie is attached. Those are end-to-end properties, and Playwright runs the real bundle in a real browser, which is the only place they can be observed. The division of labour follows from that: unit tests for logic and rendering, end-to-end tests for the handful of journeys whose failure would be an incident.

// playwright.config.ts
import { defineConfig, devices } from '@playwright/test'

export default defineConfig({
  testDir: './e2e',
  // Assertions retry until the timeout instead of failing on the first
// check. This is the mechanism that removes the sleep() from a
  // Playwright suite; there is no other correct way to wait.
  expect: { timeout: 5000 },
  fullyParallel: true,
  forbidOnly: !!process.env.CI,
  retries: process.env.CI ? 1 : 0,
  reporter: process.env.CI ? 'github' : 'list',

  use: {
    baseURL: process.env.E2E_BASE_URL ?? 'http://127.0.0.1:4173',
    trace: 'on-first-retry',
    // The production build, previewed. Never test the dev server:
    // HMR changes timing and the dev build is not the artefact.
    video: 'retain-on-failure'
  },

  projects: [
    { name: 'chromium', use: { ...devices['Desktop Chrome'] } },
    // A real mobile device profile is not a viewport resize. It changes
    // the user agent, the touch capability and the device pixel
    // ratio, all of which change application behaviour.
    { name: 'mobile', use: { ...devices['iPhone 14'] } }
  ],

  webServer: {
    command: 'npm run build && npm run preview',
    url: 'http://127.0.0.1:4173',
    reuseExistingServer: !process.env.CI,
    timeout: 120000
  }
})

// e2e/invoice.spec.ts -- one journey, asserted through the UI only.
import { test, expect } from '@playwright/test'

test.describe('invoice lifecycle', () => {
  test('creates an invoice and sees it in the list', async ({ page }) => {
    await page.goto('/login')
    await page.getByLabel('Email').fill('owner@example.test')
    await page.getByLabel('Password').fill('correct-horse')
    await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Sign in' }).click()

    // Role and accessible name, again. A data-testid is acceptable
    // only where no role conveys the element's purpose.
    await expect(page.getByRole('heading', { name: 'Invoices' })).toBeVisible()

    await page.getByRole('link', { name: 'New invoice' }).click()
    await page.getByLabel('Customer').fill('Acme GmbH')
    await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Save' }).click()

    await expect(page.getByRole('cell', { name: 'Acme GmbH' })).toBeVisible()

    // A deep link, which is a deployment property and not something a
    // unit test can assert.
    await page.reload()
    await expect(page.getByRole('cell', { name: 'Acme GmbH' })).toBeVisible()
  })
})
PropertyBest verified byReason
A computed derives the right valueUnit testCheap, precise, and the failure message names the function
A component renders and emitsComponent testNeeds a DOM but not a browser
A composable cleans upEffect-scope testNo component, no DOM, exact assertions on disposal
A form rejects invalid inputComponent test with a validation schemaThe schema is a pure function; test it directly and test the wiring once
The bundle builds and the chunk loadsEnd to endA chunk that 404s is invisible to a unit test
A deep link resolves on refreshEnd to endA server routing property; see 14.3
Authentication and authorisationEnd to endInvolves cookies, redirects and server responses
Nothing above is an incident if it failsNo automated testA journey that is neither critical nor stable is a maintenance cost with no return

Applies to. The pinned versions for this chapter are Vitest 3.2.4, @vue/test-utils@vue/test-utils 2.4.6, js 26.x, happ 17.x, @pinia/testing@pinia/testing 0.3.x, msw 2.x and Playwright 1.5x. createTes ships in @pinia/testing@pinia/testing and is not part of Pinia itself. Playwright browsers are installed once per machine with npx playwright installnpx playwright install, and the Playwright config must never start the Vite dev server as its webServerwebServer.

Caveats. A component test that asserts on a class name couples the test to the stylesheet, and a class rename becomes a red build with no behavioural change. A test that asserts on text produced by a computed date is a test that fails in a different timezone. A test that mocks the module under test proves nothing, because the mock is the implementation. And a coverage percentage is a description of the suite, not evidence that the suite is good: a suite can reach 95% by executing every line and asserting nothing.

Exercises 11

  1. Configure Vitest with environment: 'jsdom’environment: 'jsdom' and a setup file. Write a test that mounts a component using ResizeO without any stub, then add the stub and confirm the test passes with and without it.
  2. Write one test that omits await flushPromises()await flushPromises() after an async change and confirm it fails or passes vacuously. Add the flush and confirm the assertion is now meaningful. Add a second flush and record whether anything changes.
  3. Write a composable that registers an interval with onUnmountedonUnmounted. Call it inside an effec and record the warning. Convert it to onScope and confirm the scope stop cancels the interval.
  4. Mount a component that uses useR with and without await router.isReady()await router.isReady(). Record the difference in the first render and explain it in terms of when the route resolves.
  5. Set up msw with onUnhandledRequest: 'error’onUnhandledRequest: 'error'. Add a component that calls an endpoint you have not declared and record the failure. This is the behaviour you want in every test.
  6. Take a form component with a Zod 4 schema. Test the schema as a pure function with no component, then test the component’s wiring once with a single invalid input. Record how many component tests the first approach saves.
  7. Write a Playwright test that navigates to a deep link directly rather than by clicking, and confirm the history fallback of 14.3 is configured. Run it against the dev server and against the preview server and record any difference in behaviour.
  8. Add trace: 'on-first-retry’trace: 'on-first-retry' and force a failure. Open the trace and identify the network request that caused it. Note how this changes the cost of an end-to-end failure.

12 · TypeScript with Vue

12.1 Volar, script setup with lang="ts", and the retired Vetur12.2 Typing props, events and the Props type helper12.3 defineModel, ref, reactive and the UnwrapNestedRefs boundary12.4 Typing composables, template refs and PropType12.5 Generic components with the generic attribute12.6 vue-tsc, the common type errors and CI

TypeScript support in Vue is delivered by the language service, not by the framework: the framework compiles templates to functions, and a type checker that has never seen a single-file component cannot check a template. This chapter describes the toolchain that closes that gap, the compiler macros that carry types into the template, the specific unwrapping rules that make r and reac types surprising, generic components, and the command that must run in continuous integration for any of it to have value.

Volar, script setup with lang="ts", and the retired Vetur

Vetur was the original editor integration for Vue 2 and Vue 3. It has been retired; the successor is the language-tools project, which ships as vue for the command line and as @vue/language-tools@vue/language-tools for editors. The two are the same analysis: the editor calls it continuously, and vue calls it once in a build. A project that configures only the editor integration has no type checking at all.

// package.json -- the complete type-checking toolchain.
{
  "scripts": {
    "typecheck": "vue-tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.app.json",
    "build": "npm run typecheck && vite build"
  },
  "devDependencies": {
    "typescript": "5.9.2",
    "vue-tsc": "3.0.6"
  }
}

// Editors. VS Code needs nothing but the workspace TypeScript version,
// because vue-tsc and the language service share it. JetBrains
// needs the Vue plugin enabled. Both must be told NOT to use the
// bundled TypeScript, which is older and will disagree.
//
// .vscode/settings.json
{
  "typescript.tsdk": "node_modules/typescript/lib",
  "vue.server.hybridMode": true
}

// A single-file component with TypeScript. The lang attribute selects the
// language; everything else about the component is unchanged.
// src/components/PriceTag.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { computed } from 'vue'
import type { Currency } from '@/types/money'

const props = withDefaults(defineProps<{
  amount: number
  currency?: Currency
}>(), {
  currency: 'EUR'
})

// props.amount is `number`, not `number | undefined`: the default
// removes the optional flag for the properties that have one.
const formatted = computed(() =>
  new Intl.NumberFormat('de-DE', {
    style: 'currency', currency: props.currency
  }).format(props.amount)
)
</script>

<template>
  <span class="price">{{ formatted }}</span>
</template>
ToolPurposeStatus on the 3.5 line
vue 3.0.6Type-checks .vue.vue files from the command lineCurrent. Required in CI.
@vue/language-tools@vue/language-tools 2.1+The editor integration, of which vue is the headless formCurrent. Pinned to a version that supports the 3.5 reactive props destructure.
VeturThe original extensionRetired. It does not understand gen, defin typing or reactive props destructure, and it conflicts with language-tools when both are installed.
vue-tsc --watchvue-tsc --watchContinuous checking during developmentSupported. Useful on a second terminal; the editor already does it.

Requirements. vue requires TypeScript as a peer dependency and a tsconf that includes the .vue.vue extension. It is a type checker only: it emits nothing and must be combined with --noEmit--noEmit or with noEmit: truenoEmit: true in the configuration. Vite does not type-check. A build that runs vite buildvite build without a preceding vue will happily emit a bundle containing a type error.

Caveats. Editor inference and vue are the same engine and will agree, which means an error the editor does not report is an error vue will not report either. In particular, a template expression is only checked if the component’s props and bindings are themselves inferred correctly: a prop declared with a runtime object rather than a type gives up the template check for that component. A single wrong tsco target, an incorrect l or a missing ty entry produces errors that look like framework errors and are not.

Typing props, events and the Props type helper

A component’s type contract has three parts: the props it accepts, the events it emits, and the slots it offers. defin and defin are compiler macros, not imports, and they accept either a runtime declaration or a type argument, never both. With a type argument the compiler generates the runtime declaration by static analysis, so the runtime validation that section 1.4 described is retained.

// Props. Three forms, and the rule that decides between them.
// src/components/DataGrid.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import type { PropType } from 'vue'

interface Column {
  key: string
  label: string
  align?: 'left' | 'right'
}

export interface GridProps {
  rows: ReadonlyArray<Record<string, unknown>>
  columns: Column[]
  selectable?: boolean
  // A non-primitive prop. A plain `Function` type would be inferred by
  // the compiler as `Function`, which is not callable with arguments.
  // PropType supplies the generic, and the same construct is the
  // correct answer for a class instance in a prop.
  rowKey: (row: Record<string, unknown>) => string as PropType<(row: Record<string, unknown>) => string>
  renderer?: new (el: HTMLElement, row: unknown) => void
}

// Reactive props destructure (3.5+). The native default is compiled into
// the runtime prop options, so there is no withDefaults here, and
// `selectable` is `boolean` in the type, not `boolean | undefined`.
const { rows, columns, selectable = false, rowKey } = defineProps<GridProps>()

// Events. The named-tuple syntax (3.3+) is the preferred form; the call-
// signature form is the older equivalent and still valid.
<script setup lang="ts">
const emit = defineEmits<{
  // name: [payload types...]
  'row-click': [row: Record<string, unknown>, index: number]
  'selection-change': [ids: string[]]

  // The equivalent call-signature form.
  // (e: 'row-click', row: Row, index: number): void
}>()

emit('row-click', row, i)              // checked
emit('row-click', row, i, extra)      // error: arity
emit('row-dblclick', row)            // error: undeclared

// The Props type helper. When a parent needs a child's props type -- to
// type a wrapper, a store, or the argument of a function -- extract
// it rather than restating it. The declaration is hoisted to
// module scope by the compiler, so the type must be an import or a
// top-level type, never a value defined inside setup.
// src/components/DataGrid.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
export interface GridProps { /* ... */ }
const props = withDefaults(defineProps<GridProps>(), { selectable: false })
</script>

// The idiomatic way, using the helper the compiler provides.
// src/composables/useGridConfig.ts
<script setup lang="ts">
import type { Props } from '@/components/DataGrid.vue'

// ResolvedProps applies the defaults to the optional flags.
type GridConfig = Props<typeof DataGrid>
//          ^ Props is declared by the SFC compiler in .vue/shims-vue.d.ts
Prop shapeDeclarationWhy the naive form fails
strlabel: stringlabel: stringIt does not fail; the compiler maps it to the Str constructor
Callback proponPick: (id: string) => voidonPick: (id: string) => voidAn optional function is inferred as Func, which accepts any arity; a bare Func type is uncallable in TypeScript
Class instanceengine: Engine as PropType<Engine>engine: Engine as PropType<Engine>Eng is a type, not a value, so no runtime constructor exists to validate against
Constructor proprenderer?: new (el: HTMLElement) => voidrenderer?: new (el: HTMLElement) => voidNothing; the compiler infers Func, which is correct for a constructor
Object with a unionmode: 'grid' | 'list’mode: 'grid' | 'list'Nothing in the type, but the runtime validator the compiler generates for a union in 3.3+ is a string check and is a development aid only
Array of objectscolumns: Column[]columns: Column[]An imported interface is resolved for the runtime check only if TypeScript is installed as a peer dependency; otherwise the prop is emitted in the array format with no element validation

Applies to. Reactive props destructure is stable and enabled by default from Vue 3.5; on 3.4 and below it is a compiler option and defaults must be declared with withDefaultswithDefaults. The named-tuple emits syntax requires 3.3. Imported and complex types in the defin type position require 3.3. Conditional and mapped types cannot be used for the props object as a whole, because the conversion from type to runtime declaration is performed by syntactic analysis and does not evaluate types.

defineModel, ref, reactive and the UnwrapNestedRefs boundary

The template unwraps refs. The type system models that by wrapping the type of a reactive object in UnwrapNe, which removes R from the type of every property at every depth. This is exactly right for rendering and exactly wrong for a TypeScript programmer, because the same object behaves differently in script and in template. The rules below are the complete set; there is nothing further to learn here.

// defineModel: the typed, and the optional, two-way binding prop.
<script setup lang="ts">
import { watch } from 'vue'

// No argument: the prop is `modelValue` and the event is
// `update:modelValue`. The type is Ref<string | undefined> because
// the prop is optional without a default.
const query = defineModel<string>()

// A required model removes the undefined. This is the correct fix for
// the desynchronisation the Vue documentation warns about: a child
// with a default and a parent that passes undefined will disagree.
const page = defineModel<number>({ required: true })   // Ref<number>

// A named model: the prop is `count`, the event is `update:count`.
const count = defineModel<number>('count', { default: 0 })

// A default for a mutable type MUST be a factory, exactly as with
// withDefaults. An array literal would be shared by every instance.
const selected = defineModel<string[]>('selected', { default: () => [] })

// Modifiers: the second element of the destructure carries them, and
// the get/set transformers are where they are applied.
const [text, textModifiers] = defineModel<string, 'trim' | 'uppercase'>({
  set(v) { return textModifiers.trim ? v.trim() : v }
})
</script>

<!-- Parent side. The v-model argument must match the name. -->
<Pager v-model:page="page" v-model:selected="selected" />

// The unwrapping rules, exhaustively. Each line is what vue-tsc reports.
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref, reactive, type Ref, type UnwrapRef } from 'vue'

// (1) ref of a primitive: Ref<number>. In script, .value is number.
const n = ref(0)                 // Ref<number>
n.value = 1

// (2) ref of an object: the TYPE is UnwrapRef<T>, so a ref-of-ref
//     collapses to a plain value type even though at runtime it is
//     still a ref. This is the single most common surprise.
// const inner = ref(1)
// const outer = ref(inner)      // Ref<number>, not Ref<Ref<number>>
// outer.value.value = 2        // ERROR: Property 'value' does not exist
const explicit = ref<Ref<number>>(ref(1))  // the escape hatch

// (3) reactive: Ref<T> at every property. The object type is
//     UnwrapNestedRefs<T>, and it applies to nested objects too,
//     including ones the type author did not intend to be reactive.
const state = reactive({ count: 0, inner: { deep: 0 } })
state.count                     // number, no .value anywhere
state.inner.deep                 // number

// (4) An array of refs inside a reactive object: the element type keeps
//     its Ref only when the array is declared as an array of Ref.
// reactive({ items: [ref(1)] })   // items: Ref<number>[] -- unwrapped per element

// (5) A computed is unwrapped identically, so ComputedRef appears in a
//     return type but never in a template expression.
const doubled = computed(() => n.value * 2)  // ComputedRef<number>

// (6) Passing a ref where a plain value is expected is the error that
//     the UnwrapRef machinery exists to catch, and the fix is toValue()
//     -- never to write `.value` in a function that may receive either.

// The correct shape for a function that accepts "a value or a ref of it".
// Vue exports the type; the runtime function is toValue().
// src/composables/useDynamicLabel.ts
<script setup lang="ts">
import { toValue, type MaybeRefOrGetter } from 'vue'
import { computed } from 'vue'

// toValue accepts a value, a ref, a getter or a reactive property and
// returns the plain value, tracking it in the enclosing effect. This is
// the ONLY correct way to normalise such an argument, and it is what
// makes reactive props destructure usable inside a composable.
function useDynamicLabel(source: MaybeRefOrGetter<string>) {
  return computed(() => String(toValue(source)).toUpperCase())
}

// Callers: a plain value, a ref, a reactive prop, or an inline getter.
useDynamicLabel('fixed')
useDynamicLabel(count)
useDynamicLabel(() => props.query)      // required for a destructure

Caveats. reac returning UnwrapNe is not a defect; it is the price of the design and it is why section 3.2 recommends r for anything that will be assigned wholesale. The three concrete consequences to watch for are: a nested class instance placed in a reac object is returned as a reactive proxy, which breaks private fields and identity checks, so it must be markRawmarkRawed; an object with a property literally named va shadows nothing but reads ambiguously; and a reac array index assignment is not tracked, which is why arr[0] = xarr[0] = x on a raw array inside a reactive object is missed and arr.s is required.

Typing composables, template refs and PropType

A composable’s return type is its public interface, and the difference between an inferred return type and a declared one is the difference between a ref and a value for every caller. Declaring it explicitly, and declaring the input as MaybeRef rather than as a bare value, is the discipline that keeps a composable usable from a component, from another composable and from a test.

// An explicitly typed composable. Every return member is named, and the
// interface is exported so a test or a store can depend on it.
// src/composables/useSortable.ts
import { ref, computed, type Ref, type MaybeRefOrGetter } from 'vue'

export type SortDir = 'asc' | 'desc'

export interface UseSortable<T> {
  key: Ref<keyof T | null>
  dir: Ref<SortDir>
  sorted: Ref<T[]>
  toggle: (k: keyof T) => void
  reset: () => void
}

export function useSortable<T extends object>(
  rows: MaybeRefOrGetter<T[]>,
  initial: keyof T | null = null
): UseSortable<T> {
  const key = ref<keyof T | null>(initial) as Ref<keyof T | null>
  const dir = ref<SortDir>('asc')

  const sorted = computed(() => {
    const list = [...toValue(rows)]
    if (key.value === null) return list
    const k = key.value
    return list.sort((a, b) => {
      const av = a[k], bv = b[k]
      const cmp = av < bv ? -1 : av > bv ? 1 : 0
      return dir.value === 'asc' ? cmp : -cmp
    })
  })

  function toggle(k: keyof T) {
    if (key.value === k) dir.value = dir.value === 'asc' ? 'desc' : 'asc'
    else { key.value = k; dir.value = 'asc' }
  }
  function reset() { key.value = null; dir.value = 'asc' }

  return { key, dir, sorted, toggle, reset }
}

// Template refs. 3.5 introduced useTemplateRef, which matches by a
// runtime string and therefore supports a DYNAMIC ref binding that the
// compiler cannot see. The type is inferred from the ref attribute.
<script setup lang="ts">
import { useTemplateRef, onMounted } from 'vue'
import type ComponentExposed from 'vue-component-type-helpers'
import Editor from './Editor.vue'

// On <input ref="host">  ->  Ref<HTMLInputElement | null>
const host = useTemplateRef<HTMLInputElement>('host')

// On <Editor ref="editor" /> -> Ref<ComponentExposed<typeof Editor> | null>
// The type is the EXPOSED surface, so an unexposed member is an error.
const editor = useTemplateRef<ComponentExposed<typeof Editor>>('editor')

// Inside a v-for, the ref collects every matching element into an array.
const items = useTemplateRef<HTMLLIElement[]>('items')

onMounted(() => {
  host.value?.focus()                       // ?. is required: the
  //                                          element may be inside
  //                                          a v-if that is false
  editor.value?.insert('text')
})
</script>

<template>
  <input ref="host" />
  <Editor ref="editor" />
  <ul><li v-for="r in rows" ref="items" :key="r.id">{{ r }}</li></ul>
</template>

// The pre-3.5 pattern, still correct and still widely used. Two rules make
// it work: the variable name must match the ref attribute, and the
// type must include null because the element does not exist during the
// first render.
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref } from 'vue'
const input = ref<HTMLInputElement | null>(null)
</script>
<template><input ref="input" /></template>

// A function ref sidesteps the name-matching rule entirely and is the
// only approach that works with a computed ref name.
<script setup lang="ts">
import { shallowRef } from 'vue'
const el = shallowRef<HTMLElement | null>(null)
const setEl = (node: Element | null) => { el.value = node as HTMLElement | null }
// Called on every update; the argument is null when the element unmounts.
</script>
<template><div :ref="setEl">{{ label }}</div></template>

// InjectionKey, the typed provide/inject pair. The key and the
// consumer are both in the same module, so the type survives.
// src/session.ts
import { inject, type InjectionKey, type ComputedRef } from 'vue'

export interface Session {
  userId: ComputedRef<string | null>
  signOut: () => void
}

// A symbol, so no string key in the application can collide with it, and
// the second type argument makes inject() return Session, not
// Session | undefined, so a missing provider is a compile error.
export const SessionKey: InjectionKey<Session> = Symbol('session')

// src/components/SessionBadge.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import { inject } from 'vue'
import { SessionKey } from '@/session'
const session = inject(SessionKey)   // Session, non-nullable
</script>
NeedCorrect constructIncorrect form
A prop that is an array of a known typePropType<T[]>PropType<T[]>Object as PropType<object[]>Object as PropType<object[]>, which widens to object[]object[]
A prop that is a class instancePropType<InstanceType<typeof Engine>>PropType<InstanceType<typeof Engine>>Eng alone, which the compiler cannot convert
An injected valueInjectionKey<T>InjectionKey<T> with a sym keyA string key, which is untyped and collides
A DOM element refuseTemplateRef<HTMLElement>('name')useTemplateRef<HTMLElement>('name')ref()ref() with no type parameter, giving Ref<any>Ref<any>
A child component refComponentExposed<typeof Child>ComponentExposed<typeof Child>InstanceType<typeof Child>InstanceType<typeof Child>, which is a for a generic component
A store in a propAn Inject or a store identifier string, and useS insidePassing the store instance, which makes the component untestable in isolation

Generic components with the generic attribute

A generic component is one whose prop types depend on how it is used. The gen attribute on the script block declares the type parameters, using the same syntax as a TypeScript type parameter list, including constraints, defaults and multiple parameters. The parameters are in scope for every type position in the block, including the props and the emits declarations.

// A generic list. The element type is inferred from the `items` prop, and
// `render` receives a properly typed row rather than `any`.
// src/components/DataList.vue
<script setup lang="ts" generic="T extends { id: string | number }">
export interface ListSlots<T> {
  row: (props: { item: T; index: number }) => any
  empty: (props: { hint: string }) => any
}

const props = withDefaults(defineProps<{
  items: T[]
  selectable?: boolean
}>(), { selectable: false })

const emit = defineEmits<{
  pick: [item: T]
}>()

const selected = new Set<string | number>()

function onRow(item: T) {
  if (!props.selectable) return
  if (selected.has(item.id)) selected.delete(item.id)
  else selected.add(item.id)
  emit('pick', item)
}
</script>

<!--
  In the template, item is T. There is no cast and no `any`: the constraint on
  the generic parameter is what makes item.id legal.
-->
<template>
  <div v-if="items.length === 0"><slot name="empty" :hint="'No rows'" /></div>
  <div v-else v-for="(item, i) in items" :key="item.id" @click="onRow(item)">
    <slot name="row" :item="item" :index="i" />
  </div>
</template>

// Use site. T is inferred as Invoice, so the slot and the event are
// both typed. @pick is checked against Invoice.
<!--
<DataList :items="invoices" selectable @pick="onPick">
  <template #row="{ item }">
    {{ item.number }} · {{ item.total }}
    <span v-if="item.memo">{{ item.memo }}</span>
  </template>
</DataList>
</!--

// Two parameters, a constraint and a default. The attribute value is
// exactly a type parameter list, so anything legal there is legal here.
<script setup lang="ts" generic="T extends Item, U extends string = T['id']">
import type { Item } from '@/types/item'
defineProps<{ items: T[]; idField: U }>()
</script>

// A component that CANNOT be generic, and needs the override comment.
// Inference from props is impossible when the type comes from a remote
// schema, so the consumer states it explicitly with @vue-generic.
<!--
  <!-- @vue-generic {import('@/api').Actor} -->
  <ApiSelect v-model="peopleIds" endpoint="/api/actors" />
</!--

// Holding a generic component in a ref. InstanceType does not work: the
// component's type is a function of T, so there is no single instance
// type to extract. ComponentExposed from vue-component-type-helpers
// is the supported answer, installed as a dev dependency.
// npm i -D vue-component-type-helpers
//
// src/components/Toolbar.vue
<script setup lang="ts">
import type { ComponentExposed } from 'vue-component-type-helpers'
import DataList from './DataList.vue'
import { useTemplateRef } from 'vue'

// The exposed surface of a generic component is what is visible to the
// parent; if it exposes nothing, the ref is typed but empty.
const list = useTemplateRef<ComponentExposed<typeof DataList>>('list')
</script>

Caveats. A generic parameter is erased at runtime, so nothing in the compiled output differs between DataList<Invoice>DataList<Invoice> and DataList<User>DataList<User>: the generic is a compile-time contract only, and the component must not branch on it. The gen attribute cannot be used together with a plain scr block that exports options, because the two compile paths conflict. It also cannot be used with the s attribute, and it does not work on an in-DOM root component template. An unsatisfied constraint produces a template error at the use site, not at the declaration, which is why the error message must be read from the consuming file.

Applies to. The gen attribute and the @vue-generic@vue-generic comment require the language-tools feature set shipped with vue 2.x and later, and a vue version that matches the installed types major. A generic component’s slot typing additionally requires defin (3.3+) if the slot props are to be checked inside the component’s own template.

vue-tsc, the common type errors and CI

Type checking a Vue project means checking two things that a plain t cannot: the TypeScript inside .vue.vue files, and the template expressions. vue does both. The errors that recur are a small, enumerable set, and each has a single correct remedy; the table below is the index.

// tsconfig.app.json -- the configuration that actually enables template
// checking. The two entries that matter are `include` (the .vue
// extension) and `vueCompilerOptions.strictTemplates`.
{
  "extends": "@vue/tsconfig/tsconfig.dom.json",
  "compilerOptions": {
    "target": "ES2022",
    "module": "ESNext",
    "moduleResolution": "bundler",
    "strict": true,
    "noUncheckedIndexedAccess": true,
    "exactOptionalPropertyTypes": true,
    "noEmit": true,
    "types": ["vite/client"],
    "paths": { "@/*": ["./src/*"] },
    "baseUrl": "."
  },
  "include": ["src/**/*.ts", "src/**/*.vue", "env.d.ts"]
}

// env.d.ts -- the ambient declarations a Vue project needs. Without the
// .vue module declaration, importing a component is an error even
// though the file exists.
<!-- env.d.ts -->
<!-- <reference types="vite/client" />

declare module '*.vue' {
  import type { DefineComponent } from 'vue'
  // The older, weaker form. The SFC compiler provides a stronger one
  // that understands generics, so prefer it when available:
  //
  //   const define: DefineComponent<{}, {}, any>
  //   export default define
  const component: DefineComponent<{}, {}, any>
  export default component
}

// ImportMetaEnv, so import.meta.env.VITE_FOO is string, not any.
interface ImportMetaEnv {
  readonly MODE: string
  readonly DEV: boolean
  readonly readonly PROD: boolean
  readonly BASE_URL: string
  readonly VITE_API_BASE: string
}
interface ImportMeta {
  readonly env: ImportMetaEnv
}

// Continuous integration. Two jobs: type checking is fast and must not be
// allowed to fail silently, so it runs in parallel with the build.
// .github/workflows/ci.yml
name: ci
on: [push, pull_request]

jobs:
  typecheck:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with: { node-version: '20.19', cache: 'npm' }
      - run: npm ci
      - run: npm run typecheck      # vue-tsc --noEmit

  build-and-test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with: { node-version: '20.19', cache: 'npm' }
      - run: npm ci
      - run: npm run test -- --run   # vitest, single pass
      - run: npm run build           # which runs typecheck
        #                               first, so a type error
        #                               never reaches an artefact
      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with: { name: dist, path: dist }

Turn on strictT early, not late. It converts a whole class of silent template defects into compile errors, and the migration is a bounded amount of work: every component that already has a type-based defin passes immediately, and the ones that do not are the ones whose types were never really checked. Add noUncheckedI in the same change if the codebase is small; it is the flag that catches an array[i]array[i] that can be undefined, which in Vue templates is a render-time crash rather than a type error.

Exercises 12

  1. Add vue to a Vue 3.5 project and run it. Record the number of errors before and after setting strictTemplates: truestrictTemplates: true, and identify which components contributed the most.
  2. Declare one component’s props with a runtime object and one with a type. In the first, reference a misspelled prop in the template and record the absence of an error. Convert it and record the error’s wording.
  3. Write a prop typed as a callback, one typed with Prop, and one typed as a class instance. For each, record whether calling it with the wrong arity is a compile error and what message is produced.
  4. Write a r of a r and observe the inferred type. Then write it with an explicit Ref<number>Ref<number> type argument and observe the difference. Record when the ref-of-ref pattern is the right answer.
  5. Convert a composable that takes a plain array to one that takes MaybeRef with toValuetoValue. Confirm that a caller passing a ref, a plain array and an inline getter all work and all track.
  6. Build a generic Data with one constrained parameter, and consume it with two different item types. Then try to access a member that only one of the two types has and record the error’s file.
  7. Write a component with a template ref and read it in onMo without a null guard. Turn on strictNu and record the error. Convert to useTemp and record what inference it adds.
  8. Wire npm run typechecknpm run typecheck into a continuous integration job and confirm that a deliberate type error in a template fails the build, not only the editor.

13 · Security

13.1 Interpolation is safe by default and v-html is not13.2 URL schemes, javascript: and open redirects13.3 Prototype pollution and untrusted object merging13.4 CSRF, cookie attributes and the server boundary13.5 Environment variables, secrets and supply-chain risk13.6 CSP, clickjacking and defence in depth

Vue’s default behaviour is safe: text interpolation and attribute binding both escape, and they do so through textC and setAtt rather than through string concatenation, so a vulnerability in that path would have to be a vulnerability in the browser. Every security defect in a Vue application is therefore a defect at a sink where escaping is switched off, or a defect in code the framework does not control at all. This chapter enumerates the sinks, gives the sanitiser for each, and states plainly which of the remaining problems are not the frontend’s to solve.

Interpolation is safe by default and v-html is not

The single most important property of Vue’s rendering is that it does not build HTML strings. A template expression produces a text node or an attribute value, and the platform API that writes it performs the escaping. v-h is the documented escape from that property: it assigns a string to inne, which is a parse instruction, and the string is therefore code if it says so.

// The default, and why it is safe. No sanitiser is involved at all.
// src/components/CommentItem.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
const props = defineProps<{ author: string; body: string }>()
</script>

<!-- Given body = '<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>',
     the browser receives a text node containing that literal string.
     The angle brackets were escaped by Vue; the payload is displayed. -->
<p class="body">{{ props.body }}</p>

<!-- Given author = '" onmouseover="alert(1)',
     the quote is escaped by setAttribute, so the attribute is closed by
     Vue and not by the payload. -->
<span :title="props.author">{{ props.author }}</span>

// v-html is different in kind: it is an innerHTML assignment.
<!-- Given the same body, the img element is created and its
     onerror handler runs. This is stored cross-site scripting. -->
<div v-html="props.body"></div>
</template>

// The sanitiser. DOMPurify is the reference implementation: it parses the
// HTML with the browser's own parser and then re-serialises only the
// nodes it recognises as safe, which is why it knows about mXSS
// vectors that a regular expression over a string cannot see.
// npm i dompurify   (+ @types/dompurify if TypeScript cannot find types)
//
// src/composables/useSanitizedHtml.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { computed, type Ref } from 'vue'
import DOMPurify from 'dompurify'
import type { Config } from 'dompurify'

// A narrow allowlist is the difference between "an HTML sanitiser" and
// "a subset of HTML that this application is prepared to render".
const RICH_TEXT: Config = {
  ALLOWED_TAGS: ['p', 'br', 'strong', 'em', 'code', 'pre',
                 'ul', 'ol', 'li', 'a', 'blockquote'],
  ALLOWED_ATTR: ['href', 'title'],
  // Without this, DOMPurify permits data: images, which is a
  // known exfiltration and phishing vector.
  ALLOWED_URI_REGEXP: /^(?:(?:https?|mailto):|[^a-z]|[a-z+.-]+(?:[^a-z+.\-:]|$))/i
}

export function useSanitizedHtml(source: Ref<string>) {
  const html = computed(() => DOMPurify.sanitize(source.value, RICH_TEXT))
  return { html }
}
</script>

<!-- The only sanctioned place v-html may appear, and the value
     reaching it has been through an allowlist on every evaluation. -->
<article class="prose" v-html="html"></article>
SinkEscaped by VueCondition under which it is not
{{ text }}Yes, alwaysNever, for a string or a number. Only a value that is already a DOM node bypasses text insertion
:attr="value"Yes, alwaysNever for a string. A boolean or an object is normalised to a string first
v-htmlNoAlways unsanitised. It is innerHTML
innerHTML in a render functionNoSame as v-html; the JSX innerHTML prop likewise
Mounting a template from a stringNoThe template is compiled to JavaScript and executed. This is arbitrary code execution, not markup injection
<style>{{ value }}</style>RefusedVue’s compiler refuses to render a style element from a template binding. Bind an object to :style with an explicit property allowlist instead
:href="value"Attribute escaping onlyNot the URL scheme. A javascript: URL executes on click; see 13.2
target="_blank"NoThe opened document receives window.opener unless rel="noopener" is present

Caveats. Sanitising on the client is defence in depth and never the boundary. A sanitiser running in the browser can be bypassed by anyone who can modify the page, and the value it protects has already travelled from the server. The authoritative sanitisation belongs on the server, at the point the value is stored, so that every consumer of the API — including a native client and a server-rendered email — receives an already-safe value. Client-side sanitisation exists so that a value stored before the rule was introduced, or stored by a different writer, is still not executed.

Applies to. The safe-by-default property holds for the standard build of Vue 3.5 in the browser and in the server renderer. It does not hold for content mounted into a DOM node that already contains server-rendered, user-controlled markup: mounting over such a node makes the browser’s parser and Vue’s compiler disagree about the same text, and markup that is inert as HTML can be executable as a template. Mount on an empty element.

URL schemes, javascript: and open redirects

Attribute escaping prevents an attacker from breaking out of the hr attribute. It does not prevent the attacker from supplying the value of the attribute, and javascript:javascript: is a valid attribute value that the browser treats as a script. The remedy is a scheme allowlist applied to the value, and — as with HTML — applied on the server first.

// A URL sanitiser. The rule is a SCHEME allowlist, not a blocklist: a
// blocklist of `javascript:` and `data:` is defeated by obfuscation
// (embedded tabs and newlines, upper case, HTML entities already
// decoded by the parser, and so on).
// src/lib/safeUrl.ts
const SAFE_SCHEMES = new Set(['http', 'https', 'mailto', 'tel'])

export function safeUrl(raw: string, base = 'https://example.com'): string | null {
  const value = raw.trim()
  if (value === '') return null

  // Relative and fragment links are legitimate. Resolve them against a
  // base and then check the RESULT, so `//evil.test` (protocol-
  // relative, i.e. a cross-origin link) is caught too.
  let url: URL
  try { url = new URL(value, base) } catch { return null }

  if (!SAFE_SCHEMES.has(url.protocol.replace(':', ''))) return null

  // A relative link resolved above will have the base's protocol, which is
  // in the allowlist; returning the ORIGINAL string preserves the
  // relative form so the link keeps working under a router.
  return value
}

// Open redirect: a post-login destination supplied by a query parameter.
// The defect is that the application trusts a value it received from the
// request and hands it to a navigation primitive.
//
// WRONG. `?next=https://evil.test/login` sends the user, after a
// successful authentication, to a page that looks like yours.
<!--
<div v-if="error">{{ error }}</div>
<button :disabled="busy" @click="submit">Sign in</button>
//
// src/views/LoginView.vue
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref } from 'vue'
import { useRoute, useRouter } from 'vue-router'

const route = useRoute()
const router = useRouter()
const error = ref<string | null>(null)

// A path-only allowlist: the destination must be a path within this
// application. Rejecting the protocol-relative form is the part that
// matters; `new URL('/\\evil.test', base)` normalises to a cross-origin
// host, so a leading backslash must be rejected as well.
function safeNext(raw: unknown): string {
  if (typeof raw !== 'string') return '/'
  if (!raw.startsWith('/') || raw.startsWith('//') || raw.startsWith('/\\')) return '/'
  return raw
}

async function submit() {
  error.value = await auth.signIn(form.email.value, form.password.value)
  if (error.value) return
  await router.replace(safeNext(route.query.next))
}
</script>
InputInterpretation by the browserAccepted by safe
/invoices/42/invoices/42Path on this originYes
//evil.test/x//evil.test/xProtocol-relative, i.e. origin evilNo
/\evil.test/\evil.testNormalised by the URL parser to //evil.test//evil.testNo
https://evil.testhttps://evil.testAbsolute cross-originNo
javascript:alert(1)javascript:alert(1)Script, on clickNo, by the scheme allowlist
&#106;avascript:alert(1)&#106;avascript:alert(1)Entity-decoded by the parser, then scriptNo: the entity is decoded before the check, or the check runs on the decoded value
java&#9;script:alert(1)java&#9;script:alert(1)Tab inside the scheme, stripped by the parserNo: strip ASCII whitespace and control characters from the scheme before comparing

Requirements. Validate a URL on the server when it is accepted, not when it is rendered, and validate it again on the server when a redirect target is used. A sanitiser in the browser is bypassable by anyone who can run code in the page, which is precisely the attacker a sanitiser in the browser is supposed to stop. The Chrome DevTools console is enough to demonstrate this, which is the fastest argument for doing it in the right place.

Prototype pollution and untrusted object merging

Prototype pollution occurs when an attacker controls a key that a merge or an assign walks all the way down to Object.prototypeObject.prototype. In a Vue application the exposure is not in the framework — Vue’s reactivity system reads __proto____proto__ and const and refuses to proxy them — but in the application code that copies a response body into configuration, form state or a query object. The fix is a key allowlist or an explicit blocklist checked at every level of the recursion.

// The vulnerability, stated minimally. `deep` is the shape almost every
// merge utility takes, and almost none of them check the keys.
//
// Attacker input, arriving as a JSON body:
//   { "__proto__": { "isAdmin": true } }
//
// After `Object.assign({}, defaults, deep)`, EVERY object in the process
// inherits isAdmin, including the ones a later request creates.
//
// Note: a JSON.parse result has an own property named __proto__, and
// Object.assign invokes the setter on Object.prototype, so the pollution
// is real. A form-encoded body cannot express the key at all, which is
// why the content type changes the exposure.

// The guard. Reject the dangerous keys at every level, and own the copy
// so no setter is invoked on a shared prototype.
// src/lib/merge.ts
const FORBIDDEN = new Set(['__proto__', 'constructor', 'prototype'])

function isPlainObject(v: unknown): v is Record<string, unknown> {
  if (v === null || typeof v !== 'object') return false
  const proto = Object.getPrototypeOf(v)
  return proto === null || proto === Object.prototype
}

export function mergeConfig<T extends Record<string, unknown>>(
  target: T, source: unknown, allow: (keyof T)[] = []
): T {
  if (!isPlainObject(source)) return target

  // An explicit allowlist is stronger than a blocklist: a key that was not
  // anticipated cannot be introduced at all.
  for (const key of allow) {
    if (!Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(source, key)) continue
    const incoming = source[key]

    if (isPlainObject(incoming)) {
      target[key] = mergeConfig((target[key] ?? {}) as any, incoming, allow)
    } else if (FORBIDDEN.has(String(key))) {
      throw new Error('forbidden merge key')
    } else {
      target[key] = incoming
    }
  }
  return target
}

// Why the allowlist matters, and the three forms that defeat a naive one.
//
// (1) Blocklisting only the literal string "__proto__" is not enough when
//     the key arrives already decoded by a query parser, because a
//     parsed query turns "__proto__[isAdmin]=1" into a nested object whose
//     own key is "__proto__" but which has no prototype of its own.
//     Use Object.create(null) for the accumulator and check with
//     Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty, never with `in`.
//
// (2) A recursive spread {...a, ...b} over attacker-controlled b is the
//     same defect with different syntax. It must not be used on a body.
//
// (3) JSON.parse with a reviver is safe for this purpose, because the
//     reviver runs with the key available and the assignment is made by
//     the parser rather than by a setter on Object.prototype.

// The safe parse for a request body whose shape is known.
// src/lib/readBody.ts
export function readBody<T>(
  raw: string,
  allow: (new (v: T) => T)
): T {
  const parsed = JSON.parse(raw, (k, v) => (typeof k === 'string' && FORBIDDEN.has(k) ? undefined : v))
  return allow(parsed)
}

// A schema is the best form of allowlist, because it also enforces the
// types. Zod 4 strips unknown keys by default with .parse().
// src/lib/schemas.ts
import { z } from 'zod'

export const SettingsSchema = z.object({
  theme: z.enum(['light', 'dark', 'system']),
  pageSize: z.number().int().min(10).max(200),
  filters: z.object({ q: z.string().max(120).optional() }).optional()
})

// settings.value = SettingsSchema.parse(await res.json())
// Anything not in the schema is discarded, including __proto__.
Operation on untrusted dataSafeUnsafe
Reading a propertyobj.pa—
Copying one level{ ...known }{ ...known } with a fixed key listObject.assign({}, untrusted)Object.assign({}, untrusted)
Copying deeplyA recursive merge with a key allowlist or a schemastructu into shared state, or a recursive spread
Parsing a bodyz.object({...}).parse()z.object({...}).parse() or JSON. with a reviver that drops the keyObject.assign(state, await res.json())Object.assign(state, await res.json())
Building a query objectStarting from Object.create(null)Object.create(null) and assigning only known keysStarting from {}{} and assigning every parameter
Passing to a component propA typed prop with a declared shapeA prop typed as a or obj, spread into state

Set Object.create(null)Object.create(null) as the prototype of any long-lived object built from user input, and use Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(o, k)Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(o, k) rather than k in ok in o. The first makes a missing key return unde rather than something inherited; the second makes a polluted prototype visible as a key that was never set. Neither is a substitute for the allowlist, and both together make the failure mode legible when it occurs.

CSRF, cookie attributes and the server boundary

Cross-site request forgery is not a Vue problem and cannot be fixed in a Vue component. The browser attaches a cookie to a request based on the destination, not on the code that initiated it, so a page on another origin can cause a state-changing request to your API with the victim’s credentials. The mitigations are all server-side or transport-level: the Same cookie attribute, a token the other origin cannot read, and a request shape a simple form cannot produce.

// The client half. It is small, and it is only half of the control.
// src/api/client.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
let csrfToken: string | null = null

// A read of the token cookie. It must NOT be httpOnly, because the
// client has to read it and echo it in a header. The session cookie
// that identifies the user is a different cookie and IS httpOnly.
// The defence is that another origin cannot read this cookie at all.
function readCookie(name: string): string | null {
  const hit = document.cookie.split('; ').find(c => c.startsWith(name + '='))
  return hit ? decodeURIComponent(hit.slice(name.length + 1)) : null
}

// A custom header is the second half: a cross-origin HTML form cannot
// set one, so a request carrying it cannot have been initiated by a form
// on another origin. A cross-origin fetch could, but it would fail the
// preflight because the header is not in Access-Control-Allow-Headers.
export async function apiFetch<T>(input: string, init: RequestInit = {}): Promise<T> {
  csrfToken = readCookie('XSRF-TOKEN') ?? csrfToken

  const method = (init.method ?? 'GET').toUpperCase()
  const headers = new Headers(init.headers)
  if (method !== 'GET' && method !== 'HEAD' && csrfToken) {
    headers.set('X-XSRF-TOKEN', csrfToken)
  }

  const res = await fetch(input, {
    ...init, method, headers,
    // 'same-origin' sends the session cookie to this origin and not to
    // a cross-origin API. Use 'include' only when the API is on a
    // different origin AND the server sets SameSite=None; Secure
    // AND CORS with an explicit origin and credentials:true.
    credentials: 'same-origin'
  })
  if (res.status === 403 && res.headers.get('x-csrf-token')) {
    csrfToken = res.headers.get('x-csrf-token')!
    return apiFetch<T>(input, init)   // one retry, not a loop
  }
  if (!res.ok) throw new ApiError(res.status, await res.text())
  return res.json() as Promise<T>
}
</script>
AttributeValueEffect
SameStrThe cookie is not sent on any cross-site request, including a top-level navigation from a link. The most protective value; it will sign the user out of an inbound link from another site
SameLSent on top-level GET navigations but not on POST. The default in current browsers, and the reason a form-based CSRF against a mutating endpoint is already blocked
SameNoSent on all cross-site requests. Requires SecureSecure. Required when the session and the API are on different sites, which reintroduces the need for a token
HttppresentJavaScript cannot read the cookie, so an injected script cannot exfiltrate the session
SecpresentSent only over HTTPS. Mandatory for SameSite=NoneSameSite=None
Ori header—Set by the browser on every request and unforgeable by a page. A server can reject a mutating request whose Ori is not allowlisted, which requires no cookie read at all

Caveats. SameSite=LaxSameSite=Lax is a mitigation, not a control: it blocks the classic form-based attack, and it does nothing for a token or a header that an attacker’s page can obtain, nor for a top-level GET that has a side effect. Any endpoint whose GET changes state is a design defect that defeats it. A state-changing endpoint must additionally verify the request shape, so that a request that came from a plain HTML form is rejected before any handler runs. And a token stored in localS is worse than a cookie: it is readable by any injected script, whereas an Http cookie is not.

Applies to. Cross-site request forgery and cross-site script inclusion are backend concerns. Vue ships no protection for either, and the official security documentation says so explicitly. The frontend obligations are narrow: attach credentials deliberately, echo the token header the server expects, do not cache authenticated responses in a shared cache, and do not put authorisation decisions in a client-side guard.

Environment variables, secrets and supply-chain risk

A variable prefixed VI is substituted into the bundle at build time by textual replacement. It is not encrypted, not obfuscated and not hidden: the string appears in the emitted JavaScript, which anyone can download. This is a design property of a static build and it is not a defect in the tool, but it is the cause of the most common serious mistake in a Vue deployment.

// What the build does, and why the value is public.
//
//   .env.production
//     VITE_API_BASE=https://api.example.com
//     STRIPE_SECRET_KEY=sk_live_51H...
//
//   src/api/client.ts
//     const base = import.meta.env.VITE_API_BASE
//
//   dist/assets/index-9f2c1a.js, after the build
//     ...const t="https://api.example.com"...
//
// The STRIPE_SECRET_KEY line is not in the bundle, because it has no
// VITE_ prefix and nothing referenced it. The same line with a VITE_
// prefix would be in the bundle, verbatim, and would be in every
// browser cache and every proxy log along the way.
//
// The rule: a VITE_ variable is a PUBLIC constant. If its disclosure
// would be a security event, it must not be one.

// The correct shape. The client names an operation; the server decides
// whether it is allowed and with which credentials.
//
//   Client: POST /api/billing/checkout  (no key, session cookie only)
//   Server: validates the session, holds the secret, calls the provider
//
// The public publishable key is a different thing and IS meant to be in
// the client, which is why providers issue two and only one of them is
// safe to ship. Naming a variable PUBLISHABLE or SECRET makes the
// distinction visible in review.

// Runtime configuration, when the value genuinely differs per
// deployment and a rebuild is not acceptable. The server injects a
// script element; the value is still public, and is treated as such.
<!-- public/config.js, generated at container start, not baked in -->
window.__APP_CONFIG__ = { apiBase: "https://eu.api.example.com" }
</!--

// src/main.ts
declare global { interface Window { __APP_CONFIG__?: { apiBase: string } } }
const cfg = window.__APP_CONFIG__ ?? { apiBase: import.meta.env.VITE_API_BASE }

// Supply chain. The three habits, in the order they matter.
//
// (1) COMMIT THE LOCKFILE AND INSTALL WITH `npm ci`. `npm install` may
//     resolve a different version than the one recorded, and a transitive
//     dependency can change between two installs of the same range.
//     `npm ci` installs exactly what package-lock.json states and fails
//     if the lockfile and package.json disagree.
//
// (2) AUDIT THE ADVISORY FEED, ON A SCHEDULE, AND PIN EXACT VERSIONS.
//     A caret on a direct dependency admits every minor, and a minor can
//     be a compromised publish. The versions this guide pins are exact.
//
//     .github/workflows/audit.yml
//     on:
//       schedule: [{ cron: '17 6 * * 1' }]
//     jobs:
//       audit:
//         runs-on: ubuntu-latest
//         steps:
//           - uses: actions/checkout@v4
//           - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
//             with: { node-version: '20.19', cache: 'npm' }
//           - run: npm ci
//           - run: npm audit --audit-level=high
//           - run: npx vite build && npx source-map-explorer dist/assets/*.js
//
// (3) REVIEW WHAT A DEPENDENCY CAN REACH. A component library that runs
//     at module scope, or a build plugin that receives a shell, has the
//     privileges of the build. Prefer a dependency with a small surface, a
//     source you have read, and a release you can attribute to a commit.

// A build-time script injection worth knowing about, because it is a
// package.json field and not a package: a `postinstall` script runs with
// the developer's permissions the moment `npm install` completes.
// `npm ci --ignore-scripts` removes the class of risk entirely for a
// project with no native modules, at the cost of not building them.

Caveats. Nothing in this section makes a browser application secret-safe. Even with a perfect build, the session that identifies the user is present in the browser, and an attacker’s script runs with the same authority. The correct conclusion is architectural: authorisation is a server decision, the client only reflects it, and any capability the client can exercise is available to an attacker who has injected a script into that client. A content security policy (13.6) is the control that makes such an injection difficult; it is a mitigation, not a fix.

CSP, clickjacking and defence in depth

Two browser-level controls sit outside the application and are the last line of defence against the injection the previous sections discuss. A content security policy restricts what the page is permitted to load and execute, so an injected <script><script> element does not run. A frame policy prevents the page from being embedded in another page’s frame, which is the precondition for clickjacking. Both are response headers, and neither can be set from a component.

// A policy for a Vue application, stated in the form it would be sent.
//
// Content-Security-Policy:
//   default-src 'self';
//   script-src 'self' 'nonce-{RANDOM}' 'strict-dynamic';
//   style-src 'self' 'nonce-{RANDOM}';
//   img-src 'self' data: https:;
//   font-src 'self';
//   connect-src 'self' https://api.example.com;
//   object-src 'none';
//   base-uri 'none';
//   form-action 'self';
//   frame-ancestors 'none';
//   upgrade-insecure-requests
//
// Notes on each directive and the Vue-specific consequence.
//
//   script-src without 'unsafe-inline' means the inline bootstrap
//   <script> in index.html will not run. Two legitimate
//   solutions: emit a per-response nonce and have Vite's html plugin put
//   it on every emitted script tag, or move the bootstrap into a module.
//   'strict-dynamic' lets a nonce-trusted script import further modules
//   without a host allowlist, which is what makes the policy usable
//   with dynamically injected chunks.
//
//   style-src without 'unsafe-inline' conflicts with a style block in an
//   SFC, because the compiler injects that block with a <style> element at
//   runtime. Use a nonce (Vue 3.5 supports a nonce option for custom
//   elements, and the plugin ecosystem covers the app case) or accept
//   'unsafe-inline' for styles only. 'unsafe-eval' is never required for a
//   Vite build, because templates are pre-compiled; it is only needed by a
//   runtime-compiled build.
//
//   connect-src must list the API origin. This is where a VITE_API_BASE on
//   a different origin has to be declared, and forgetting it produces a
//   policy violation rather than a network error, which is harder to
//   diagnose.
//
//   form-action 'self' and base-uri 'none' are cheap and close real holes.
//   object-src 'none' disables plugins and the Flash-era <object> sink.
//
//   Report violations instead of only blocking them, so that a staging
//   deployment tells you what it would have blocked:
//     report-uri /csp-report; report-to csp-endpoint

// Clickjacking and window.opener, in the application code.
//
// X-Frame-Options: DENY            legacy, all browsers
// Content-Security-Policy: frame-ancestors 'none'   current, superset
//
// If a page MUST be embeddable, use frame-ancestors with an explicit list
// and never a wildcard: 'self' plus a partner origin, by name.
//
// A link that opens a new tab hands the opened document a reference to
// this one unless rel=noopener is present. The opened document can then
// navigate this page, which is a real phishing primitive.
//
// Correct, and the reason is a compiler detail worth knowing: a dynamic
// target binding cannot be analysed, so the framework cannot add
// the attribute for you and it must be written explicitly.
<!-- Component.vue -->
<a :href="doc.url" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">
  {{ doc.title }}
</a>

// And a component whose root element is an anchor, where the attribute
// must be declared once on the root because Vue 3.5 cannot infer it for
// a dynamic value.
//
// Other response headers worth setting on the same response, and why:
//
//   Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=63072000; includeSubDomains
//     Makes HTTPS sticky, so a downgrade is not possible even on the
//     first request. Only send it once HTTPS is confirmed working.
//
//   Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin
//     Stops the full path, including identifiers, from appearing in a
//     third party's logs.
//
//   X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff
//     Stops a browser from re-interpreting a response as a script type
//     the server did not declare. Cheap, and it breaks a real chain.
//
//   Permissions-Policy: geolocation=(), camera=(), microphone=()
//     Denies the powerful APIs to this document and to anything it embeds.
ControlWhere it is setWhat it stopsDoes it stop
Text interpolationThe component templateHTML injection through textAnything, for text. It is a platform guarantee
Attribute bindingThe component templateAttribute breakoutA malicious attribute VALUE, such as a javascript:javascript: URL
Server-side sanitisationThe APIEverything, for every consumerA defect in the sanitiser configuration
SameSite cookieThe API responseForm-based cross-site requestsA cross-origin request that carries a token
Token or Origin checkThe APICross-site state changesA request issued by the application’s own script
Content security policyThe web serverInjected script from an unexpected sourceScript from an allowlisted source, which includes a compromised dependency
Server-side authorisationThe APIEverything a client decidesA defect in the authorisation logic itself

Client validation is a courtesy to the user, never a control. A form that cannot be submitted with an invalid email address is a good form; it is not a validation, because the same request can be constructed with the developer tools in ten seconds, or with a script, or by the mobile client. Every rule expressed in a schema on the client must exist again on the server, and the server’s copy is the one that is authoritative. This is why chapter 8 ends with the schema being shared rather than duplicated: sharing a schema between the two runtimes keeps them in agreement, which is a different and much weaker claim than the client enforcing it.

Exercises 13

  1. Write a component that renders a user-supplied comment both with {{ }}{{ }} and with v-htmlv-html. Load a payload containing an i with an one handler and record which one executes. Then insert DOMPurify with a tag allowlist of three elements and record which parts survive.
  2. Bind a javascript:javascript: URL to an hr and confirm it is not escaped by Vue. Implement saf and pass the strings from the table in 13.2 through it, recording each result including the tab-inside-the-scheme case.
  3. Implement a post-login ne parameter and try the six inputs in the 13.2 table against it. Record which one succeeds, and add a test for each.
  4. Write a deep merge without a key check and merge the body { "__proto__": { "isAdmin": true } }{ "__proto__": { "isAdmin": true } } into an empty object. Test ({} as any).isAdmin({} as any).isAdmin and record the result. Convert the merge to use a Zod 4 schema and confirm the key is discarded.
  5. Set a VI variable to a recognisable string, build, and search di for it. Then set a non-prefixed variable to a second string and search again. Record both results and state the rule they demonstrate.
  6. Run npm auditnpm audit on a project with dependencies and record the advisories. Pin the direct dependencies to exact versions, re-run, and record what changed. Run npm ci --ignore-scriptsnpm ci --ignore-scripts and record which packages failed to build.
  7. Add a content security policy with script-src 'self’script-src 'self' and no nonce, load the application, and record the violation. Add a nonce and record what still fails, including whether a component with a scoped style block is affected.
