14 · Build, Deployment and the SSR Boundary

14.1 The production build and what code splitting does14.2 Environment handling per mode and the limits of a static bundle14.3 Static deployment and the history-fallback requirement14.4 SSR, hydration mismatch diagnostics and the Nuxt boundary14.5 CSR versus SSG versus SSR and what Nuxt adds14.6 Bundle analysis, caching and the pre-production checklist

A Vue application is compiled into a set of static files, and every question about deployment is a question about those files and about the server that returns them. This chapter describes what the production build emits, how each Vite mode selects configuration, what a static host must be told in order to resolve a deep link, where server-side rendering fits and why it is not a core feature, the decision table between the three rendering strategies, and the pre-production checklist that follows from all of it. Everything here assumes Vue 3.5.43 built with Vite 8.

The production build and what code splitting does

The development server and the production build are different programs. The development server serves individual modules on request, so a change to one file re-executes one module. The production build performs dependency analysis, eliminates the template compiler, enables tree-shaking across the module graph, minifies, scopes class names, hashes filenames and emits a static asset graph. Every performance number that matters is a property of the build output, and the development server cannot tell you any of them.

// What a build produces, and what each artefact is for.
//
//   dist/index.html
//     The shell. One HTML file. It contains the module preload hints and
//     the script tags for the entry chunk, and it is the only file a
//     static host must serve for the root path.
//
//   dist/assets/index-9f2c1a4b.js
//     The entry chunk: the runtime, the application entry, and whatever
//     is reachable from it by STATIC import. The hash is a content hash,
//     which is what makes it safe to cache forever; see 14.6.
//
//   dist/assets/InvoiceView-3d81ee07.js
//     A split chunk produced by one or more dynamic import() expressions.
//     The browser fetches it the first time the async component renders.
//
//   dist/assets/index-2c05f1a9.css
//     Extracted stylesheet(s). Extracted because a <style> element is
//     render-blocking while a stylesheet in the head is not.
//
//   dist/assets/index-9f2c1a4b.js.map
//     Source maps. Upload them to an error tracker; do not serve them from
//     the origin unless the code is public, because a map contains the
//     original sources verbatim.
//
//   dist/manifest.json  (with build.manifest: true)
//     Maps logical names to hashed files. Required by a plugin that owns
//     the asset pipeline, and by any server that generates a document
//     referencing the entry.

// vite.config.ts -- the build options that are worth setting deliberately.
export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [vue()],
  build: {
    target: 'es2022',
    sourcemap: 'hidden',          // emitted, no
    //                              // comment appended
    manifest: true,

    // Rollup's manualChunks. Use it only for a dependency that is
    // genuinely shared and genuinely stable, because a chunk
    // boundary is also a caching boundary: splitting a library
    // that changes often into its own chunk improves caching,
    // and splitting the application into many small chunks makes
    // every navigation a waterfall.
    rollupOptions: {
      output: {
        manualChunks(id) {
          if (id.includes('node_modules/date-fns')) return 'dates'
          if (id.includes('node_modules')) return 'vendor'
        }
      }
    }
  }
})

// The three kinds of code split, and the rule that decides between them.
//
// (1) ROUTE LEVEL. The single most valuable split in a Vue application.
//     Vue Router resolves the component as a dynamic import, so a route's
//     component, its sub-components and its exclusive dependencies form
//     their own chunk. Do it with a static import of the lazy form:
//
//   import { createRouter, createWebHistory } from 'vue-router'
//   const router = createRouter({
//     history: createWebHistory(),
//     routes: [
//       { path: '/', component: HomeView },           // no split: the entry
//       { path: '/invoices', component: () =>
//            import('../views/InvoiceListView.vue') },  // split
//     ]
//   })
//
//     The router already has a loading state, an error hook and a
//     navigation guard, so this is preferable to wrapping each view in
//     defineAsyncComponent by hand. See 10.5.
//
// (2) COMPONENT LEVEL. A heavy widget inside a route. defineAsyncComponent,
//     or a plain dynamic import in a ref() initialiser for a module:
//
//   const { default: Chart } = await import('heavy-charting-library')
//
// (3) VENDOR LEVEL. manualChunks, as above. Useful for exactly one
//     dependency: the one you upgrade on a different schedule from the
//     rest and which is large enough that re-downloading it per release is
//     the dominant cost of a deploy.
//
// What NOT to split: the framework, the router, the store library, and
// any module that every route needs. Splitting the runtime into a
// separate request buys nothing and costs a round trip on the critical
// path. Measure the request count before adding a split, not after.
Build decisionDefaultChange it whenCost of changing it
tarBaseline widely availableBrowser support genuinely requires itLowering it adds transpilation and, if it goes below ES2015, larger output and possible polyfill requirements
sourfaAlways for a production build you intend to debug: use 'hidden''hidden' so the files exist without a reference commentBuild time and disk. Never serve them from a public origin for private code
cssCodtrAlmost neverDisabling it produces one stylesheet that is needed for a single route and is downloaded for all of them
assetsIn4096 bytesA project with many small images or fontsRaising it embeds them as data URLs in the JavaScript, which delays discovery and blocks caching separately
chunkSizeW500 kBA project with an intentionally large vendor chunkOnly silences a warning. It is a prompt to investigate, not a property to configure away
manifaWhen a plugin or a server must reference the emitted entry by nameOne extra file

Environment handling per mode and the limits of a static bundle

Vite loads environment files by mode. Three files always participate, in increasing priority, and any number of mode-specific files are layered on top: .env.env, then .env.local.env.local, then .env.[mode].env.[mode], then .env.[mode].local.env.[mode].local. Only variables prefixed VI are exposed to the client, and they are exposed by literal substitution at build time. The consequence is stated in 13.5 and repeated here because it is a build property, not a security control: a Vite build produces static files, and a static file cannot keep a secret.

// The precedence, exactly. Higher wins. The .local variants are for
// machine-specific values and belong in .gitignore.
//
//   .env                     shared defaults, committed
//   .env.local               local overrides, NOT committed
//   .env.development          committed, used when mode is 'development'
//   .env.development.local    NOT committed
//   .env.production           committed, used when mode is 'production'
//   .env.test                 used by vitest when mode is 'test'
//   .env.production.local     NOT committed
//
// A custom mode reuses the .env.[mode] slot:
//   .env.staging   +  vite build --mode staging
//
// Note the consequence: `--mode staging` does not change `import.meta.env.DEV`
// semantics by accident. DEV is true whenever the mode is not 'production'.
// A staging build therefore has DEV true, and any `if (import.meta.env.DEV)`
// branch will be live in staging and absent in production. Choose the mode
// name deliberately or do not use a custom mode.

// src/config/env.ts -- the typed, validated accessor. The point of this
// module is to fail loudly at start-up rather than to produce undefined
// in a network call three minutes later.
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { z } from 'zod'

const schema = z.object({
  VITE_API_BASE: z.string().url(),
  VITE_APP_NAME: z.string().min(1),
  VITE_SENTRY_DSN: z.string().url().optional()
})

export const env = schema.parse({
  VITE_API_BASE: import.meta.env.VITE_API_BASE,
  VITE_APP_NAME: import.meta.env.VITE_APP_NAME,
  VITE_SENTRY_DSN: import.meta.env.VITE_SENTRY_DSN
})

// import.meta.env itself, for completeness:
//   MODE     'development' | 'production' | the --mode argument
//   BASE_URL the resolved base, from the `base` option, with a trailing /
//   PROD     true only when mode === 'production'
//   DEV      the negation of PROD
//   SSR      true when the module is being evaluated by a server renderer
NeedMechanismWhen the value is fixedHolds a secret
Different API host per deploymentVITE_A in a mode fileAt build timeNo
One artefact promoted across environmentsRuntime config injected into index by the serverAt container startNo
A feature flagRuntime config, or a fetched flag serviceAt load time, and changeable without a deployNo
A database password, an API keyThe server’s configurationNever reaches the browserYes, and only there
Public keys intended for the clientA VITE_P variableAt build timeYes — the value is publishable by design

Caveats. A custom --mode--mode whose name is not produ produces a bundle with development semantics: the reactivity debugger, prop validation and Vue’s own warnings are all present, and import.me is true. That is occasionally what you want for a staging environment and is never what you want by accident. Commit .env.development.env.development and .env.production.env.production because a build must be reproducible from the repository, and keep .env.*.local.env.*.local in .gitignore.gitignore because they exist precisely to hold the values a machine needs and a colleague does not.

Static deployment and the history-fallback requirement

A single-page application built with the HTML5 history mode is a set of files plus one rule the host must follow: for any path that is not a file in the output, return index and let the router resolve the route. Without that rule, every deep link is a 404. This is the most common deployment failure in a Vue application, and it is invisible in local development because the dev server implements the rule for you.

// The history mode router. createWebHistory() REQUIRES the fallback;
// createWebHashHistory() does not, at the cost of a fragment in every
// URL, a worse bookmark, and analytics that see one page.
//
// src/router/index.ts
import { createRouter, createWebHistory } from 'vue-router'

export const router = createRouter({
  history: createWebHistory(import.meta.env.BASE_URL),
  routes,
  scrollBehavior(to, from, saved) {
    // Restore a saved position on a back navigation, honour an anchor,
    // and otherwise start at the top. Returning false means "the
    // browser decides", which is not what scrollBehavior expects.
    return saved ?? (to.hash ? { el: to.hash, behavior: 'smooth' } : { top: 0 })
  }
})

// The fallback, in the dialect each common host expects. All four do the
// same thing and only one of them is correct for your host.
//
// Netlify -- public/_redirects. Note the 200 rewrite, not a redirect: a 301
// would change the URL and break the client router's history.
//   /*  /index.html  200
//
// Vercel -- vercel.json
//   { "rewrites": [{ "source": "/(.*)", "destination": "/index.html" }] }
//
// Cloudflare Pages -- public/_headers and _redirects, same as Netlify.
//
// Apache -- public/.htaccess
//   <IfModule mod_rewrite.c>
//     RewriteEngine On
//     RewriteBase /
//     RewriteCond %{REQUEST_FILENAME} !-f
//     RewriteCond %{REQUEST_FILENAME} !-d
//     RewriteRule ^ index.html [L]
//   </IfModule>
//
// nginx -- a location block, which must be OUTSIDE any try_files that
// would answer from a directory first:
//   location / {
//     try_files $uri $uri/ /index.html;
//   }
//   location /assets/ {
//     expires 1y;                       // hashed, see 14.6
//     add_header Cache-Control "public, immutable";
//   }
//
// Two refinements that are almost always required.
//
// (a) Deploying under a sub-path, e.g. https://host/admin/. Both the
//     router and the bundler must be told, or the base URL is wrong and
//     every dynamic import resolves 404.
//     vite.config.ts:  base: '/admin/'
//     router:         createWebHistory('/admin/')
//
// (b) A custom domain redirecting the bare apex to www (or the reverse).
//     Do it at the host, not in the router, so that the canonical URL is
//     decided before any HTML exists.
HostFallback mechanismDeep link resolvesAlso required
Netlifypublic/_redirectspublic/_redirects with a 200 rewriteYesA pub or build setting pointing at di
Vercelverce rewritesYesOutput directory set to di
Cloudflare Pages_redirects_redirectsYesNone
GitHub PagesA 404 page that redirects to /index.html/index.html, preserving the pathYes, with a visible flashRepository Pages must be served from a branch with the built output committed, or from an action
S3 plus CloudFrontAn error response document of index plus a 403 or 404 custom responseYesA CloudFront function for a clean rewrite
nginx or Apachetry_ or mod_rYesCompression and cache headers on the same block
A container imageWhatever serves the filesOnly if the server implements the ruleThe server must be configured; the build output is unaware of the host

Caveats. The fallback must not apply to paths that should be 404. A request for a missing asset under /assets//assets/ is a deployment error and returning index for it produces a confusing Unexpected token '<'Unexpected token '<' in the console instead of a 404, which is why the nginx example scopes the immutable cache block and, in a stricter configuration, an explicit error rule under /assets//assets/. A rewrite also defeats the browser’s speculative connection preload, so a 200 rewrite for a chunk that does not exist costs a full round trip. And a host that serves the site with a trailing slash on every asset will break the ba setting; check it in a real browser, not from a file path.

SSR, hydration mismatch diagnostics and the Nuxt boundary

Server-side rendering executes the same components on the server and serialises the resulting HTML, which the browser then hydrates. Vue’s core contains the renderer and the hydration logic; it does not contain a server, a router, a data layer or a build for a server target, and it is not intended to. The boundary is deliberate: a server-side rendered Vue application is built with a meta-framework, and on the pinned set that framework is Nuxt 4.x.

// WHY A HYDRATION MISMATCH HAPPENS. The client renders the component and
// compares its own output with the server's HTML. Any difference in
// structure, text, class, style or attribute is a mismatch: Vue logs a
// warning in development and, for most mismatch types, discards the
// server's nodes and patches the client tree in. The result is usually
// correct and always wasteful, and a structural mismatch can leave the
// page visibly wrong until the next interaction.
//
// The causes, exhaustively, in descending frequency:
//
//  1. Reading a value that differs on the two sides.
//     Date formatting, a random number, the browser width, a feature
//     detection result, a locale, a stored preference, a count of DOM
//     nodes. Anything derived from `window`, `localStorage` or the current
//     time is a mismatch until it is guarded.
//
//  2. Conditional rendering based on the same kind of value.
//     `if (isMobile) render A else render B` cannot agree on the server.
//
//  3. Invalid HTML nesting that the browser corrects, so the client's
//     tree differs from the intended one. A div inside a p, a tr outside a
//     tbody, a button inside a button. Vue logs this at build time as a
//     compile-time warning; the fix is to change the markup.
//
//  4. A browser extension that modifies the DOM before hydration.
//     Unfixable from the application; the mitigation is to hydrate on a
//     subtree the extension does not touch.
//
//  5. A browser extension aside, a class that differs because a
//     stylesheet or a class-ordering utility ran on the client only.
//
//  6. A value produced at module scope that is evaluated once per
//     request on the server and once per process in the browser, so the
//     second request on a server-rendered process sees a different value.
//     This is a cross-request leak as well as a mismatch, and it is why
//     per-request state must not live in a module scope.

// The fixes, in the order they should be tried.
//
// (1) useId (3.5+) for any identifier that must be stable across both
//     sides. A generated id is the most common mismatch in a form.
//
//   <script setup>
//   import { useId } from 'vue'
//   const id = useId()
//   </script>
//   <template>
//     <label :for="id">Name</label>
//     <input :id="id" />
//   </template>
//
// (2) data-allow-mismatch (3.5+) for the case where the difference is
//     genuinely unavoidable. Without a value it allows any type; with one
//     it limits the allowance to text, children, class, style or attribute.
//     Use it for a formatted date and nothing else.
//
//   <span data-allow-mismatch="text">{{ formatDate(d) }}</span>
//
// (3) Render the differing part on the client only, and hold the space so
//     that the layout does not shift.
//
//   <ClientOnly>
//     <div class="w-12 h-4 animate-pulse" />
//     <template #fallback><div class="w-12 h-4" /></template>
//   </ClientOnly>
//
// (4) Move the value into a prop supplied by the server, so both sides
//     receive the same one. This is what a data-fetching composable in a
//     meta-framework does automatically.
//
// (5) Fix the invalid nesting. There is no suppression for this; the
//     browser's parser has already changed the tree.

// Lazy hydration (3.5+) reduces the cost of a mismatch that is confined to
// a sub-tree: the sub-tree is not hydrated at all until its strategy
// fires, and if the mismatch is inside it, the damage is deferred. See
// 10.5. It does not fix a mismatch, and it does not apply to client-rendered
// pages.

Applies to. Everything in this subsection is about the core renderer’s behaviour and is documented for Vue 3.5.43. What is not part of the core is the server: renderT from vue/server-renderervue/server-renderer exists, but a production deployment needs an HTTP server, a router that knows the URL, a data layer that runs on the server, an asset pipeline that emits a server bundle, and a build that runs both halves. Nuxt 4.x supplies all of it. The other option is to hand-write that layer against renderT, which is a legitimate choice for a small, controlled application and is a significant ongoing cost for anything larger.

Caveats. Server-side rendering does not eliminate the client bundle; it emits HTML sooner and then downloads the same JavaScript to make it interactive. It also moves a class of problem to a place where it is harder to diagnose: a module-scope variable that differs per request is a data leak between users, and a win reference in a component’s setup function is a crash on the server that does not occur in development on the client. The reactivity system does not help here: nothing on the server is reactive after the first render, and a module-scope r is shared by every request the process serves.

CSR versus SSG versus SSR and what Nuxt adds

Three rendering strategies, three different answers to one question: when is the HTML produced. The choice follows from what the content is, who must see it before JavaScript runs, and whether the content differs per request. It does not follow from fashion, and a client-rendered single-page application is the correct answer more often than its detractors admit.

CriterionClient-side renderingStatic site generationServer-side rendering
When the HTML existsAfter the bundle downloads and executesAt build timePer request, before the response is sent
Time to first byteOne HTML file, then nothingOne HTML fileServer time plus render time
Time to meaningful contentBundle download, parse, execute, fetch, renderImmediate; the bundle is only needed for interactivityImmediate; the bundle is still needed for interactivity
Server costStatic files onlyStatic files onlyA process per request, or a serverless invocation
Per-request contentSupportedNot supported; the content is fixed at build timeSupported
IndexabilityPoor without prerendering; the crawler must execute the bundleExcellentExcellent, provided the response is the rendered HTML
Hydration riskNonePresent on every pagePresent on every page
Build timeFastGrows with the number of pages; a build per deployFast; pages are not known at build time
Best fitAn authenticated application behind a login, a dashboard, an internal toolDocumentation, marketing pages, a blog, a product catalogue that changes weeklyA public page whose content depends on the visitor, or a page where indexability and speed both matter
Tooling on the pinned setVite 8 plus Vue Router 4 aloneNuxt 4 with prer routes, or VitePressNuxt 4
// WHAT NUXT 4.x ADDS OVER A PLAIN SINGLE-PAGE APPLICATION. Each item
// below replaces a specific piece of code this guide has already built by
// hand, which is the clearest way to state what a meta-framework is.
//
// 1. FILE-BASED ROUTING. Replaces the src/router/index.ts of 6.1.
//
//   app/
//     pages/
//       index.vue              -> /
//       invoices/
//         index.vue            -> /invoices
//         [id].vue             -> /invoices/:id, and a typed route param
//         [[id]].vue           -> an optional parameter
//         [...slug].vue         -> a catch-all
//     layouts/default.vue      -> the shell, replacing App.vue's structure
//     middleware/auth.ts      -> a navigation guard as a module
//
// 2. SERVER ROUTES. Replaces the backend this guide has assumed exists.
//    A file under server/api/ becomes an endpoint with no framework.
//
//   server/api/invoices.get.ts
//   export default defineEventHandler(async (event) => {
//     const q = getQuery(event)
//     return await db.invoice.findMany({ where: { status: q.status } })
//   })
//
// 3. AUTO-IMPORTS. useFetch, useAsyncData, useState, defineEventHandler
//    and the Vue composables are available without an import statement.
//    This is a real reduction in boilerplate and also a real reduction in
//    greppability; a team that relies on it should agree an ESLint rule
//    that disallows the explicit import so the two do not diverge.
//
// 4. DATA FETCHING THAT RUNS ON BOTH SIDES. This is the reason to adopt
//    the framework. The payload is fetched during SSR, embedded in the HTML
//    as a serialised state payload, and transferred to the client so the
//    second render has the data and does not re-fetch. The component no
//    longer has a window in which it has no data.
//
//   <script setup>
//   const { data: invoices, pending, error, refresh } = await useFetch('/api/invoices',
//     { query: { status }, server: true, lazy: false, dedupe: 'defer' })
//   </script>
//
// 5. PAYLOAD EXTRACTION AND ROUTE RULES.
//    defineRouteRules in a page or nitro config: prerender for a static
//    page, swr for a cached-forever page, ssr: false to opt a route out of
//    the server render entirely.
//
// 6. ISLANDS. A component that hydrates on its own, inside otherwise
//    static markup, so the bundle for a mostly-static page contains only
//    the island. This is the .nuxt/components/nuxt-island element and it
//    is built on the lazy hydration strategies of 10.5.
//
// 7. BUILD TARGETS. One command produces a Node server, a serverless
//    function, or a fully prerendered static site, chosen by the deployment
//    preset rather than by rewriting configuration.

// WHEN TO ADOPT A META-FRAMEWORK, stated as a decision rather than a
//// preference. All three answers are correct; the question is which
// constraint is binding.
//
// ADOPT when at least one of these is true:
//   - The application must be indexable, or must show content before
//     JavaScript executes, and that content is not known at build time.
//   - There is no separate backend team, or the API is the same codebase.
//   - Several people would otherwise write the same client, the same
//     data-fetching composable and the same router configuration.
//
// DO NOT ADOPT when:
//   - The application is entirely behind an authentication wall. A crawler
//     cannot reach it, so server rendering buys nothing, and the
//     complexity is paid for a page load that a static shell handles.
//   - The team wants to keep the deployment as a set of files on a CDN with
//     no compute. A meta-framework with a server target is now a server
//     deployment; a meta-framework in full static mode is a prerender step
//     in the build and a lot of framework for a small application.
//   - The application has one route. The framework's value is proportional to
//     the number of routes and the number of developers.
//
// The middle path, and it is under-used: a single-page application for the
// application, prerendered static pages for the public ones, linked but
// separate. Two deployments, no shared session requirement, and the public
// pages cost nothing to serve.

// A fully prerendered Nuxt site, which is the configuration that produces a
// set of static files and therefore does need the 14.3 fallback.
// nuxt.config.ts
// export default defineNuxtConfig({
//   ssr: true,
//   nitro: {
//     preset: 'static',                  // emit HTML for every route
//     prerender: { crawlLinks: true, routes: ['/api/health'] }
//   },
//   routeRules: {
//     '/': { prerender: true },
//     '/docs/**': { prerender: true },        // built once, served as files
//     '/app/**': { ssr: false }                // a shell, hydrated on the client
//   }
// })

The decision is frequently made too late. Choosing a meta-framework after an application has grown means migrating the router, the data layer, the build and the deployment at once. Choosing it on the first page of a new application means paying for it immediately. The trigger worth watching for is not size; it is the appearance of the second developer, because the framework’s benefit is a shared set of answers rather than any single feature it provides.

Bundle analysis, caching and the pre-production checklist

Two numbers govern whether a page feels fast: the total bytes of JavaScript that must arrive before the application is interactive, and the number of sequential requests required to reach that point. Everything in this final section is about measuring the first and reducing the second, and about the caching that makes the next visit cheap.

// Bundle analysis. Three ways, in increasing order of cost.
//
// (1) The build output itself. Vite prints the gzip size of every chunk
//     and warns above chunkSizeWarningLimit. Read the first column of
//     that table; it is the sum of what the first paint depends on.
//
// (2) A visual report. rollup-plugin-visualizer emits a treemap whose
//     only purpose is to make an unexpected dependency impossible to miss.
//
//   npm i -D rollup-plugin-visualizer
//
//   vite.config.ts
//   import { visualizer } from 'rollup-plugin-visualizer'
//   plugins: [vue(), visualizer({ filename: 'stats.html', gzipSize: true })]
//
//     The finding that recurs is an entire date, query or charting library
//     imported for one function, because a default import of a barrel file
//     defeats tree-shaking. The fix is a deep import:
//
//       import { format } from 'date-fns/format'        // one function
//       import { debounce } from 'lodash-es/debounce'   // tree-shakable build
//
// (3) The network panel, on a cold cache, with the throttling set to a
//     real connection. This is the only measurement that includes the
//     server, the protocol and the user's network.
//
// A practical budget, stated as a number to argue about:
//
//   - Total JavaScript, gzipped, for the first route      <= 200 kB
//   - JavaScript before the application is interactive   <= 120 kB
//   - Requests on the critical path                      <= 10
//   - Any single dependency not written in house          <= 40 kB
//
// The last line is the one that has teeth: a dependency above the line
// is either not worth its functionality, is imported wrongly, or should
// be loaded on demand. All three are actionable, and none of them is
// "optimise later".

// Caching. The asset name is a content hash, so the file's URL changes
// whenever its content does. That makes an immutable, one-year cache
// correct, and it makes the deploy atomic: a client holding the old
// URL keeps using the old file, which is still there.
//
//   Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable   for /assets/*
//   Cache-Control: no-cache                               for /index.html
//
// `no-cache` on the document does NOT mean do not cache. It means
// "revalidate before use", and a 304 with an empty body is the correct
// and cheap answer: the entry is the one file whose content changes on
// every deploy, because it names the new hashed files.
//
// A CDN in front of a static host is close to free and close to
// mandatory: it removes the origin from the read path entirely, and the
// cache headers above make the whole asset tree edge-cacheable.
//
// Two settings that are usually forgotten.
//
//   Brotli or gzip on the wire. Vite does not compress; the host does.
//   Compression is decided on the CONTENT-TYPE, not the file extension,
//   and .json and .svg compress as well as .js and .css.
//
//   Modulepreload for the chunks the entry depends on. Vite emits the
//   hints by default; do not strip them with an HTML minifier, because
//   they are what allows the browser to discover the next chunk without
//   waiting for the first one to be parsed.

// The pre-production checklist. Every line is a property that cannot be
// checked by looking at the source, and every line corresponds to a
// failure that has been observed in production.
//
// THE BUILD
//   [ ] The lockfile is committed and the deploy runs `npm ci`.
//   [ ] Vue, Vite and every direct dependency are pinned to exact versions.
//   [ ] `vue-tsc --noEmit` runs before `vite build` and the pipeline fails
//       on a type error.
//   [ ] No development-only module is reachable from the entry: no raw
//       console of a payload, no `import.meta.env.DEV` branch holding a
//       debug endpoint, no source map reference in index.html.
//   [ ] The total gzipped JavaScript for the first route is inside the
//       budget in 14.6, and the reason for any exception is written down.
//
// CONFIGURATION
//   [ ] No VITE_ variable holds a secret. Search the built bundle for the
//       value of every one of them, as a release step.
//   [ ] The production .env file is committed and complete, so the build is
//       reproducible from the repository.
//   [ ] `base` matches the deployment path, and the router's history base
//       is the same string.
//
// ROUTING AND DEPLOYMENT
//   [ ] A deep link, typed directly into the address bar with a cold cache,
//       returns the application and not a 404.
//   [ ] A refresh on a nested route does the same.
//   [ ] A request for a missing asset under /assets/ returns 404 and not
//       index.html.
//   [ ] The content-type of every emitted extension is correct; a .js file
//       served as text/html is the classic misconfiguration.
//
// TRANSPORT SECURITY
//   [ ] HTTPS is enforced with a 301 from HTTP, and HSTS is set only after
//       HTTPS is confirmed working.
//   [ ] Every dynamic link to _blank carries rel="noopener".
//   [ ] The session cookie is HttpOnly, Secure, and SameSite=Lax or
//       Strict. SameSite=None only with a cross-site API, and then only
//       with a CSRF token.
//   [ ] A content security policy is present, its report-only rollout has
//       been completed, and the violation endpoint is monitored.
//   [ ] frame-ancestors is 'none' unless the application is deliberately
//       embeddable.
//
// CACHING
//   [ ] Hashed assets are immutable for a year; index.html revalidates.
//   [ ] A CDN serves the asset tree from the edge, and the origin is not on
//       the read path for a cached asset.
//   [ ] Compression is enabled for js, css, json, svg and html.
//
// QUALITY GATES
//   [ ] The unit and component suite passes on a clean checkout.
//   [ ] The end-to-end suite passes against the PREVIEW server, not the dev
//       server.
//   [ ] The console is clean on every route: no warnings, no unhandled
//       rejections, no failed requests. Vue's own warnings are the
//       cheapest defect detector in the framework.
//   [ ] The application is tested with a keyboard only, and with a screen
//       reader on the two flows that matter most.
//   [ ] The error boundary reports a Vue error to the tracker, and a
//       deliberately thrown error was confirmed to arrive.

The last item in each group is the one that fails silently. A missing type check produces a build that succeeds. A missing cache header produces a page that is slow only for the second and subsequent visitors. A missing rel="noopener"rel="noopener" produces nothing at all until someone exploits it. Each has a verification step that takes under a minute, and the verification step is the part that belongs in the release checklist rather than in a document nobody re-reads.

Exercises 14

  1. Run a production build and record the gzipped size of the entry chunk and of every split chunk. Identify the request count on the critical path for the first route and for a nested route.
  2. Import a date library with a default import and rebuild. Add rollup-plugi, switch to a deep import, and record both sizes and the difference in the treemap.
  3. Create .env.development.env.development and .env.production.env.production with different API bases. Build with --mode production--mode production and with --mode staging--mode staging, and confirm that the staging bundle has D semantics. Search both bundles for each value.
  4. Deploy to a static host without a fallback rule. Request a deep link directly and record the response. Add the rule in the host’s dialect and repeat. Then request a missing file under /assets//assets/ and confirm it is a 404 and not the application.
  5. Deploy under a sub-path. Record what breaks and which two settings must change together.
  6. Add a component that reads window.i and renders different markup above and below a breakpoint. Server-render the page and record the hydration warning. Fix it with Clien and with a prop supplied by the server, and record which is better for layout stability.
  7. Add a new Date()new Date() formatted string to a server-rendered page and record the mismatch. Replace it with us for an identifier and data-allow-mismatch="text"data-allow-mismatch="text" for the date, and record what each suppresses and what it does not.
  8. Write a module-scope variable in a server-rendered file and confirm that it persists between two requests. Move it into the request handler and confirm that it does not, and state the security implication of the original.
  9. Set Cache-Control: max-age=31536000, immutableCache-Control: max-age=31536000, immutable on index by mistake, deploy twice and observe the failure mode in a returning visitor. Revert to no-c and confirm a 304 is returned.
  10. Run the pre-production checklist against the application and record every line that fails. Rank the failures by the cost of the incident they would cause and fix the top one.

Appendix — Reference Material

A.1 app.config.ts and application-level configurationA.2 Directives: built-in and customA.3 Lifecycle hooks: quick referenceA.4 Composable naming and structure conventionsA.5 Migrating from the Options APIA.6 Official documentation and further reading

The appendix is the material that a reader returns to rather than reads once: the application configuration object, a single table of directives and another of lifecycle hooks, the conventions this guide has followed, a migration map from the Options API, and the canonical sources. It is reference material and is deliberately terse; every entry points back to the chapter that explains it.

app.config.ts and application-level configuration

The application configuration object is the only piece of state that is shared by definition: it is created once per crea call and injected into every component, a store and a plugin. It is typed, and it is the correct home for values that are configuration rather than state, because nothing in the reactivity system participates in it.

// src/app.config.ts
import type { AppConfig } from '@/types/app'

const config: AppConfig = {
  // Application identity
  title: 'Billing UI',
  version: __APP_VERSION__,

  // Feature switches. Read-only at runtime: a switch that changes at
  // runtime is a flag service, not configuration. See 14.2.
  features: {
    bulkInvoiceUpload: false,
    newInvoicePage: true,
    auditTrail: false
  },

  // Presentation defaults that every module should agree on.
  locale: 'de-DE',
  timezone: 'Europe/Vienna',
  dateFormat: 'YYYY-MM-DD',

  // Limits that are policy, not validation. The server enforces its
  // own; these keep the client from offering an option that will be
  // rejected.
  limits: {
    maxRowsPerPage: 200,
    maxUploadMb: 20
  },

  // Named links, so a route is not repeated as a string across the
  // codebase. See 6.2.
  routes: {
    invoices: '/invoices',
    invoice: (id: string) => `/invoices/${encodeURIComponent(id)}`
  },

  // Support and diagnostics, surfaced in a help panel.
  supportEmail: 'support@example.com',
  docsBase: 'https://docs.example.com'
}

export default config

// src/types/app.ts
import type config from '@/app.config'
export type AppConfig = typeof config

// Reading it. Three routes, and which one to use.
//
// (a) Injected, with a typed key. The cleanest option, and the reason
//     the InjectionKey pattern of 12.4 is not reserved for provides in
//     components.
// src/appConfig.ts
import { inject, type InjectionKey } from 'vue'
import type config from '@/app.config'

export const AppConfigKey: InjectionKey<typeof config> = Symbol('app-config')

// src/main.ts
//   app.provide(AppConfigKey, appConfig)
//
// src/components/LocaleBadge.vue
//   const config = inject(AppConfigKey)
//   // config.value is typed as AppConfig, non-nullable
//
// (b) The globalProperties escape hatch. Available in the Options API and
//     in templates as $config. Untyped in a template, so prefer (a).
//   app.config.globalProperties.$config = appConfig
//
// (c) A direct import. The simplest and the most testable, and acceptable
//     for values that are genuinely compile-time constants. It defeats any
//     attempt to override the configuration in a test or a storybook,
//     which is the reason the injection route exists.

// Runtime options that are NOT configuration and live on app.config:
//
//   app.config.performance       true enables Vue performance markers (3.5+)
//   app.config.warnHandler        replaces the warning function entirely;
//                                prefer app.config.warnHandler returning a
//                                string only for the warnings you accept
//   app.config.errorHandler      receives every uncaught component error
//   app.config.compilerOptions    per-app template compiler options; a
//                                runtime-compiled app only, see 14.4
//   app.config.globalProperties   the $name escape hatch
//   app.config.optionMergeStrategies   per-option merge rule for plugins
Belongs in app.configBelongs in a Pinia storeBelongs in a composable
Locale, timezone, format policyThe current user and their permissionsAnything a single component owns
Feature switches fixed at build timeServer state: the entity collection, the pending flag, the errorAnything computed from a single source, such as a sort or a selection
Route name constantsClient-only state: a draft, a selection, an open panelAnything with a lifecycle, such as a poll or a resize observer
Limits that are policyAnything persisted, such as a saved filterAnything that must be testable in isolation, which is all of it
Support and documentation links——
Anything that changes while the application is runningAnything two routes both needAnything one route needs

Caveats. app.co is not reactive. Assigning to it at runtime changes nothing that has already been read, and nothing in a component re-renders. It is also a module, so it is shared by every application instance in the same JavaScript context — which matters in a test that creates two apps, and which is the reason the injection route exists. And globalPr is untyped in a template and invisible to the language service, so a value placed there cannot be refactored safely. Use it for a small number of deliberately global, genuinely stable values and no more.

Directives: built-in and custom

A directive is a function that reacts to an element. It is the escape from the declarative model, and the correct reason to reach for one is an operation the template has no expression for: measuring an element, giving it keyboard behaviour, or integrating a third-party library that manages its own DOM. Anything expressible as a binding is better expressed as a binding.

DirectiveAvailable sinceEffectNote
v-3.0Conditionally renders, unmounting the alternativeA branch that is not rendered has no reactivity; the initial value is read once when the branch mounts
v-el, v-e3.0The remaining branches of one chainMust immediately follow the preceding element; the chain permits at most one v-
v-3.0Renders a listThe value is wrapped in a fragment, so the parent selector no longer applies directly; a k is required for correctness, not only for performance
v-s3.0Toggles disDoes not unmount, so the node stays in the DOM and its bindings keep working; use v- when the subtree is expensive or holds state
v-m2.0 / 3.0Two-way bindingOn a component it expands to a model prop and an update:modelValueupdate:modelValue listener; see 1.6 and 12.3
v-b2.0 / 3.0Attribute or property bindingThe shorthand ::; on a component it is a prop, on an element a property or an attribute
v-2.0 / 3.0Event listenerThe shorthand @@; a component listener with the same name as an emitted event is passed through as a fallthrough attribute
v-html2.0 / 3.0Sets inneUnsanitised by construction. See 13.1
v-t2.0 / 3.0Sets textCThe explicit form of {{ x }}{{ x }}; useful when a compiler flag disables interpolation
v-c2.0 / 3.0Hidden until the app mountsCosmetic; useful only for an un-hydrated page where the JavaScript is slow, and superseded by server rendering
v-o3.0Renders once and never againIrreversible. See 10.3
v-3.0Skips compilation of the element’s contentsEscapes {{ }}{{ }}; used for documentation samples inside a template
v-m3.2Skips the update while a dependency array is unchangedOn the element inside a v-forv-for. See 10.3
v-3.0Selects the component in a dynamic slot or a dynamic componentPrefer :is:is with a bound component reference in script setupscript setup
v-d3.5Defers a component’s parsing and execution to the next tickDefers parsing, not hydration. Pairs with the Teleport deferTeleport defer prop of the same release
// A custom directive. The hook set, in the order they are called, and what
// each receives. A directive is a plain object; Vue wraps it so that
// `this` is not relied upon.
// src/directives/intersect.ts
import type { ObjectDirective } from 'vue'

type IntersectOptions = {
  onEnter?: (el: HTMLElement, entry: IntersectionObserverEntry) => void
  once?: boolean
  rootMargin?: string
}

const vIntersect: ObjectDirective<HTMLElement, IntersectOptions> = {
  // beforeMount  the element exists; the component is not mounted
  // mounted     the element is in the document; the binding value is
  //            available. Everything that measures must be here.
  mounted(el, binding) {
    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(([entry]) => {
      if (!entry.isIntersecting) return
      binding.value?.onEnter?.(el, entry)
      if (binding.value?.once !== false) observer.disconnect()
    }, { rootMargin: binding.value?.rootMargin ?? '0px' })

    observer.observe(el)
    ;(el as any).__io = observer
  },

  // updated   the binding value changed. Compare oldValue to decide
  //            whether the observer must be rebuilt.
  updated(el, binding) {
    if (binding.value === binding.oldValue) return
    ;(el as any).__io?.disconnect()
    // re-create, or store the handler on the element and call it
  },

  // beforeUnmount  the element is still in the document. The
  //               counterpart of onUnmounted, and the right place to
  //               restore anything the directive changed.
  beforeUnmount(el) {
    ;(el as any).__io?.disconnect()
  }
}

export const Intersect = { mounted: vIntersect.mounted }

// src/main.ts -- global registration
//   app.directive('intersect', vIntersect)
//
// In script setup, a LOCAL directive needs no registration at all but
// must follow the vName naming convention; an imported one must be
// aliased to it.
//
//   <script setup>
//   import { vIntersect as vMyDirective } from './myDirective.js'
//   </script>
//   <template>
//     <div v-my-directive="{ onEnter: go }" />
//   </template>
//
// A custom ELEMENT modifier, declared in the name:
//   const vFocus = { mounted: (el, b) => b.value && el.focus() }
//   <input v-focus.trim />   ->   vFocus.mounted with the modifier list

Caveats. A directive that mutates an element directly fights the virtual DOM: if Vue later patches that element’s attributes, the two are writing to the same node with no coordination. A directive must therefore either own the element completely — a third-party widget it hands over and never touches again — or be read-only, measuring and observing. A directive that adds a class and a template that also binds that class will lose one of the two changes. Directives are also not composable: three directives on one element are three independent lifecycles with an order the framework does not document, which is a reason to prefer a single wrapper component.

Lifecycle hooks: quick reference

The Composition API names each hook after the moment it runs. The table gives the name, the moment, what exists at that point, and the operation the hook exists for. The ordering between a parent’s and a child’s hook is fixed and is the reason the after-mount and before-unmount pairs are ordered the way they are.

Composition APIOptions APIMomentWhat existsDo this, not that
onBefobeforBefore the first renderState, not DOMCompute initial state. Do not read an element; it is not in the document
onMomouAfter the first render, DOM insertedState and DOMMeasure, focus, attach a non-Vue listener, start an observer
onBeforbeforeBefore a re-renderState, old DOMSnapshot a value you will need after. Do not modify state here; it re-triggers the update
onUpupdAfter a re-render, DOM patchedState and new DOMRead the DOM, reposition a scroll, notify a library. Guard against an infinite loop
onBeforbeforeBefore removal, DOM presentEverything, DOM still thereStop a third-party library, restore a class, capture scroll position
onUnmunmoAfter removal, DOM goneState; element detachedRelease a reference, notify a store. Do not touch the element
onActactiOn insertion into a Keep cacheState and DOMResume polling, re-read a value that may be stale
onDeacdeactOn removal from a Keep cacheState, DOM detached but retainedPause polling. This is the hook most often forgotten, and the cause of a component that keeps requesting after the user has navigated away
onErrorerrorCOn an error in a descendant—Containment only. Return fa to stop propagation. See 9.6
onRendenoneDevelopment only, on a tracked readDuring the renderDiagnose an over-broad dependency. See 10.1
onRendernoneDevelopment only, on a tracked write—Diagnose which change caused a re-render. See 10.1
onServerserverPServer render only, before serialisationServer, awaiting allowedFetch data the first render needs. It runs once, on the server only
onWatchenoneBefore a watcher re-runs or stopsInside the watcherAbort the previous request, clear a timer. 3.5+; see 9.4
onScopenoneWhen the enclosing scope stopsAnywhere with an active scopeCleanup inside a composable. The only form testable in isolation; see 11.4
// The ordering, as an assertion. Every hook pushes its name onto an array;
// the expected sequence is the specification.
//
//   Parent   Child
//   onBeforeMount        onBeforeMount     // parent first, depth-first
//   onMounted           onMounted
//
//   onBeforeUpdate      onBeforeUpdate    // parent before child
//   onUpdated           onUpdated
//
//   onBeforeUnmount     onBeforeUnmount   // child before parent
//   onUnmounted         onUnmounted
//
// The rules this encodes:
//
//   1. Mounting is depth-first, top-down. A parent's onMounted runs after
//      every child's, so a ref to a child is populated.
//   2. Updating is parent before child, so a prop is already the new value
//      when the child's onUpdated runs.
//   3. Unmounting is child before parent, so a child's DOM is still present
//      when the parent's onBeforeUnmount runs.
//   4. No hook fires for a component that was never mounted: a v-if that is
//      false at first render has no beforeMount and no mounted, and gains
//      both when it becomes true.
//   5. Only the component that owns the hook runs it, except for
//      errorCaptured, which walks up the tree until a handler returns false.

// The three mistakes this table exists to prevent, restated:
//
//   onMounted that assigns state. The assignment triggers a second render
//   for a value that was known before mount; set it in setup instead.
//
//   onBeforeUpdate that assigns state. The write is tracked by the very
//   effect that is running, so the component re-renders again, and the hook
//   runs again, without a bound.
//
//   onUnmounted that touches the element. The element is detached; a style
//   write has no effect and a measurement returns zeros. Use onBeforeUnmount.

Composable naming and structure conventions

A composable is a function that uses the Composition API. The convention that makes a set of them composable is not stylistic: a u prefix, a return of refs, cleanup registered with onScope, and a directory whose layout says which module a composable belongs to. These are the conventions this guide has followed throughout, collected in one place.

// The directory layout. The rule is that a composable lives in the layer
// that owns it, and a consumer never reaches across a layer boundary.
//
// src/
//   app.config.ts
//   main.ts
//
//   components/          presentational; may import composables, not Pinia
//     ui/                 the design system: no domain knowledge at all
//     invoice/            domain components, grouped by entity
//
//   composables/         reusable, entity-agnostic behaviour
//     useFocusTrap.ts
//     useSortable.ts
//     useHotkeys.ts
//     index.ts             a barrel, so an import is never a deep path
//
//   features/            vertical slices: one directory per entity,
//     invoice/           containing that feature's components, its
//       components/       composables, its store and its api client
//       composables/
//       store.ts
//       api.ts
//       types.ts
//
//   stores/              cross-cutting stores only (session, preferences)
//   api/                 the typed HTTP client and its error type
//   lib/                 framework-agnostic helpers: no Vue import at all
//   directives/
//   types/
//   utils/
//
// A composable that is specific to one feature lives inside that feature.
// Promoting it to composables/ is a decision made when a second feature
// needs it, not before.

// The template every composable in this guide follows. Read it as a list of
// requirements, not as an example.
//
// src/composables/useHotkeys.ts
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { onMounted, onScopeDispose, ref, type Ref } from 'vue'
import type { MaybeRefOrGetter } from 'vue'

// 1. A `use` prefix, and a named export that is also a named export.
// 2. An exported interface for the return, so a caller and a test can
//    name the type without inferring it.
export interface HotkeyBinding {
  combo: string
  handler: (e: KeyboardEvent) => void
  description?: string
}

export interface UseHotkeys {
  register: (b: HotkeyBinding) => void
  unregister: (combo: string) => void
  active: Ref<ReadonlySet<string>>
}

// 3. Reactive arguments accepted as MaybeRefOrGetter, normalised inside.
// 4. No side effect on creation unless it is part of the contract: a
//    composable that starts a timer when it is called has a caller who
//    cannot decline it. onMounted makes the timing explicit.
// 5. Cleanup with onScopeDispose, never with onUnmounted alone.
// 6. Return refs, not a reactive object: a caller can then destructure
//    deliberately. See 7.4.
// 7. No store access, no router access, no fetch. A composable that needs
//    one takes it as an argument or is a feature composable instead.

export function useHotkeys(enabled: MaybeRefOrGetter<boolean = true): UseHotkeys {
  const bindings = new Map<string, HotkeyBinding>()
  const active = ref<Set<string>>(new Set()) as Ref<Set<string>>

  function onKey(e: KeyboardEvent) {
    if (!toValue(enabled)) return
    const combo = [e.ctrlKey && 'ctrl', e.shiftKey && 'shift', e.altKey && 'alt', e.key.toLowerCase()]
      .filter(Boolean).join('+')
    const hit = bindings.get(combo)
    if (!hit) return
    e.preventDefault()
    hit.handler(e)
  }

  onMounted(() => window.addEventListener('keydown', onKey))
  onScopeDispose(() => window.removeEventListener('keydown', onKey))

  return {
    // 8. Stable function identities where the caller will put them in a
    //    template or a watch source. These close over a Map, not a ref,
    //    so there is nothing reactive to lose.
    register: (b) => { bindings.set(b.combo, b) },
    unregister: (combo) => { bindings.delete(combo) },
    active
  }
}
</script>
ConventionRuleReason
Nameu plus a verb or a noun phraseDistinguishes a composable from a utility at a call site, and matches the community convention that makes a composable findable
FileOne composable per file, the file named after itA file exporting three composables cannot be tree-shaken by name in a partial build and cannot be read in isolation
ReturnAn object of refs and functionsThe caller controls destructuring, and store semantics from 7.4 are not needed
ArgumentsMaybeRef for anything that may be reactiveOne implementation serves a plain value, a ref, a reactive prop and an inline getter
Creation side effectNone, or one inside onMoA composable that cannot be declined cannot be composed
CleanuponScopeWorks in a component and in a test’s effect scope; see 11.4
DependenciesArguments, not imports, for anything domain-specificA composable that imports a store is a feature module, and a test of it needs a Pinia instance
ErrorsReturned in a ref, or thrown — never bothThe data layer of chapter 9 returns them in state because a component cannot catch a throw from a template
LocationIn the feature, until a second feature needs itPromoting a composable early produces a shared module with one consumer and a lifetime longer than the need

Migrating from the Options API

The Options API is fully supported on Vue 3.5 and nothing in this guide requires the Composition API. A migration is a refactoring with two hard rules: the component must behave identically at every point in the diff, and the two APIs must not be interleaved inside one component. The map below is the whole of it.

Options APIComposition APINote
data()data()r per field, or one reacChoose per field, not per component. See 3.2 for the rule that decides
prdefinRuntime form or type form; never both
emdefinSame
setup()setup()The body of script setupscript setupscript setupscript setup has no equivalent of th; see the migration table below
compcompIdentical semantics, including the value-equality short-circuit of 3.4 and later
metFunction declarationsA method on the instance is not reactive and is not tracked; a plain function is neither, and is not exposed
wawaThe Options API form takes an object of handlers; the Composition form takes a source, a callback and options
wa with immediate: trueimmediate: truewa, or compute the initial valueA watcher with imme that only computes a value is a computed with a misleading name
this.$useTemplateRef('x')useTemplateRef('x')3.5+. Before that, a r named xx
this, this.$, this.No equivalentThese are the calls that make a component untestable. Their presence is the strongest signal that a component needs migrating
this.useInvoiceStore()useInvoiceStore()And then store for the destructuring; see 7.4
this., this.$useRoute()useRoute(), useRouter()useRouter()The route object is reactive; route. read in a template or a computed tracks
this.const emit = defineEmitsconst emit = defineEmitsTyped from the declaration
this.useAttrs()useAttrs()Reactive, and not typed; mark the component inheritAttrs: falseinheritAttrs: false when using it deliberately
this.useSlots()useSlots()Slots are not reactive; capture the slot function, not its output
this.$nnextImported, not a method
A mixinA composableThe mapping is one to one: every mixin is a composable, plus a pro if it was sharing state
A mixin’s beforeThe top of the composable, or pro in a parentA hook that must run before da has nowhere to go in script setupscript setup; move the responsibility to the caller
filA plain function, or a computedRemoved in Vue 3. There is no replacement; call the function
functional: truefunctional: trueA function component, or a component returning a functionRemoved. Performance and th behaviour both change; read the migration note before converting
// One component, before and after. The behaviour is identical; the only
// differences are the ones the table above predicts.

// BEFORE -- src/components/InvoiceFilter.vue
<template>
  <form @submit.prevent="apply">
    <input v-model="form.q" />
    <select v-model="form.status">
      <option value="''">Any</option>
      <option v-for="s in statuses" :key="s" :value="s">{{ s }}</option>
    </select>
    <button type='submit'">Apply</button>
  </form>
</template>

<script>
export default {
  props: {
    initial: { type: Object, default: () => ({}) }
  },
  data() {
    return {
      form: { q: '', status: '' },
      statuses: ['due', 'paid', 'void']
    }
  },
  computed: {
    isEmpty() { return !this.form.q && !this.form.status }
  },
  watch: {
    initial: { handler(next) { this.form = { ...next, q: next.q ?? '', status: next.status ?? '' } },
              immediate: true }
  },
  methods: {
    apply() { this.$emit('filter', { ...this.form }) }
  }
}
</script>

// AFTER
<script setup lang='ts'>
import { ref, computed, watch } from 'vue'

const props = defineProps<{ initial?: Partial<InvoiceFilter> }>()
const emit = defineEmits<{ filter: [value: InvoiceFilter] }>()

// One ref per field: the two fields are replaced together, never
// individually, so one ref is the right container here.
const form = ref<Required<InvoiceFilter>>({ q: '', status: '' })
const statuses = ['due', 'paid', 'void'] as const

// The immediate watcher, kept as a watcher because it is genuinely
// async in effect: it reacts to a prop change, not to a dependency.
watch(() => props.initial, (next) => {
  form.value = { q: next?.q ?? '', status: next?.status ?? '' }
}, { immediate: true })

const isEmpty = computed(() => !form.value.q && !form.value.status)

function apply() { emit('filter', { ...form.value }) }

// Top-level bindings are exposed to the template; `statuses` and `form`
// are unwrapped in the template exactly as `this.statuses` and
// `this.form` were. No template change is required above.
</script>

Caveats. The two APIs must not be mixed inside one component. A script setupscript setup block’s bindings are not properties of the instance, so th is unavailable and a template cannot reach anything not declared at the top level. Combining the two is supported only in the narrow case described in the official documentation: an additional plain scr block for options that define cannot express. Everything else should be converted or left alone. A second hazard is order: da is initialised before comp, comp before met and wa with imme before mou; in script setupscript setup the order is the order of the statements, so an onMo placed above a r it reads will read it before it is initialised.

The canonical sources for everything in this guide, in the order they are worth reading. Each is a stable URL; the versions named here are the ones this guide describes, and the documentation tracks the current stable line rather than a pinned release, so a discrepancy between a page and this guide is a version difference until proven otherwise.

Applies to. Every URL above is current as of Vue 3.5.43. The Vue documentation follows the latest stable release, so a page that describes a 3.6 feature is not describing the version this guide is pinned to, and a page that describes a 3.5 feature may omit a limitation that a 3.4 page stated. When the two disagree, the pinned version and the changelog are the authorities and the prose page is the convenience.

Appendix Exercises

  1. Create src/app.config.tssrc/app.config.ts with a feature switch, read it through an Inject in a component, and confirm that changing the object at runtime does not re-render. Then replace the injection with a globalPr property and record what the language service stops knowing.
  2. Write a directive with a mou hook that attaches an Intersecti and no cleanup. Navigate away and back repeatedly and count the observers. Add before and repeat.
  3. Write a component that reads an element’s dimensions in onMo and records the value in onUpdatedonUpdated. Move the read to onBefor and to onUp, and record which one sees the new value and which sees the old one.
  4. Write a component that assigns state in onBeforeUpdateonBeforeUpdate. Observe the number of renders. Move the assignment into a wa and record the count.
  5. Take a mixin from an existing codebase and convert it to a composable. Record every place the conversion required a decision rather than a mechanical change, and state which of those decisions is the real work of a mixin migration.
  6. Convert an Options API component that uses this., this. and this. to script setupscript setup. Record every template change the conversion required; the answer should be none, and if it is not, the original was already relying on something script setupscript setup does not provide.
  7. Write a composable that takes a plain array and refuses to accept a ref. Then convert it to take MaybeRef with toV and confirm both callers work. Record the two lines that changed.
  8. Look up one behaviour in this guide that you can confirm or refute from the documentation listed in 14.6, and record the source. Repeat for a second, and record which of the two took longer to verify.

Vue 3 — The Professional Guide. Reference documentation for the Vue 3.5 framework: from the single-file component format and template directives through reactivity, the Composition API, component communication, Vue Router, Pinia, forms and the data layer, to rendering performance, testing, TypeScript, security, and the build, deployment and server-side rendering boundary. Version target: Vue 3.5.43. Fourteen chapters plus an appendix. Progress is saved in your browser (theme, last chapter). Open in any modern browser. No internet needed.
